Result: Applied to MNIST with four constraint usages (learning from constraints, improving adversarial robustness, correcting misclassifications, output relabeling) and to multi-commodity flow with neural routing predictors enforcing link-capacity constraints; experiments on MNIST and SNDLIB show CG4AI produces feasible predictors and outperforms single-model baselines in accuracy.

Conclusion: CG4AI offers a general, feasible framework for enforcing linear constraints on AI outputs without sacrificing performance, adaptable to classification and routing problems; future work may address scalability and non-linear constraint extensions.

Abstract: Standard machine-learning training minimizes a loss function over a dataset, but does not guarantee that the resulting model will satisfy predefined rules or constraints on its outputs. In many real-world applications, ranging from autonomous systems to network routing, such guarantees are essential. We propose CG4AI, a framework that builds a convex combination of AI models while enforcing linear constraints on the combined output. A master linear program (LP) determines the optimal mixture weights, while a pricing subproblem generates new models guided by LP dual variables, focusing attention on the most violated constraints. A cutting-plane procedure extends feasibility guarantees beyond the training set. We apply CG4AI to two problems: (i) digit classification on MNIST, where we demonstrate four distinct uses of constraints, learning from constraints alone, improving adversarial robustness, correcting misclassified examples, and enforcing output relabeling; and (ii) the multi-commodity flow problem, where link capacity constraints are enforced on neural-network routing predictors. Experiments on MNIST and standard SNDLIB benchmark networks show that CG4AI reliably produces feasible predictors while achieving better accuracy than single-model baselines.

</details>


### [102] [The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy: A Framework for Constructing Classifiers and Diagnosing Their Decisions, Applied to Prompt Injection Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26423)
*Jaturong Kongmanee,Smile Thanapattheerakul*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy (LDT): a framework to add a safeguard layer around classifiers and diagnose confident-but-brittle decisions using a dimensionality-optimized classifier, latent support vectors (≈29% of training prompts), and a diagnostic taxonomy to flag Safe, Heuristic Bias/Override, or Insufficient Context cases; applied to a prompt-injection dataset to reveal widespread brittleness and two failure modes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To improve the safety and reliability of NLP classifiers under prompt injection by identifying when confident predictions are not robust, and to provide an actionable taxonomy and remediation guidance.

Method: (i) Train a dimensionality-optimized classifier with embedding dimensionality selected by cross-validation; (ii) identify a compact set of latent support vectors (~29% of training examples) representing influential prompts for mislabeling; (iii) build a diagnostic taxonomy from tokens and their attack magnitudes to determine when to rely on, flag, or route predictions for human safety review; provide an end-to-end workflow.

Result: On a classifier trained with a public prompt-injection dataset, ~77% of confident decisions are brittle to removal of a single token, revealing two failure patterns: confidence calibration failure and genuinely exploitable shortcuts.

Conclusion: The Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy offers a practical, end-to-end framework for diagnosing and remediating prompts that undercut classifier reliability, and demonstrates how systematic analysis of influential prompts can guide safety interventions.

Abstract: This paper proposes a framework for constructing a classifier as a safeguard layer, and for developing a complementary diagnostic that identifies which of the classifier's confident decisions can be trusted. This framework, the Latent Diagnostic Taxonomy, consists of (i) constructing a dimensionality-optimized classifier, in which the embedding dimensionality is empirically selected via cross-validated performance rather than fixed a priori, (ii) locating a relatively small set of latent support vectors (~ 29% of total training examples) representing influential prompts for identifying tokens that alter the classifier's predicted labels, and (iii) utilizing such tokens and their associated attack magnitudes for constructing a diagnostic taxonomy. This diagnostic taxonomy provides an end-to-end guideline for flagging prompts that require different treatments: rely Safely on the classifier's decision; flag Heuristic Bias and Heuristic Override cases; route Insufficient Context cases for further human/safety review. Applying the framework to a classifier trained on a public prompt injection dataset, we find that a substantial fraction of its confident decisions (~ 77%) are not robust to removing a single token, and that this brittleness separates into two distinct failure patterns: a confidence calibration failure and a genuinely exploitable shortcut. For each zone of the taxonomy, we also recommend strategies for remediating diagnosed prompts. We illustrate the framework as a series of steps, demonstrating how each step operates.

</details>


### [103] [FedCMAPSS: A Benchmark for Federated Learning in Remaining Useful Life Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26433)
*Amelia Sorrenti,Matteo Pennisi,Concetto Spampinato,Simone Palazzo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes FedCMAPSS: a federated benchmarking suite for RUL estimation using NASA C-MAPSS; defines five tasks from IID to highly heterogeneous; benchmarks FL algorithms across multiple NN architectures; provides reproducible baselines and open-source data/splits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust RUL models suffer from limited run-to-failure data; FL enables cross-organization learning while preserving data privacy; yet there is no standardized evaluation for federated prognostics; a benchmark is needed for fair comparison and progress.

Method: Construct FedCMAPSS benchmark from C-MAPSS; design five tasks with varying data distribution heterogeneity; evaluate several state-of-the-art federated optimization algorithms on multiple neural architectures; release code and data splits for reproducibility.

Result: Establishes reproducible baselines and performance insights under different federated settings; demonstrates feasibility of FL for RUL estimation and provides a common evaluation framework; source code and splits publicly available.

Conclusion: FedCMAPSS provides a standard foundation for developing and comparing federated predictive maintenance solutions; encourages rigorous benchmarking and future research into FL methods for prognostics.

Abstract: Data-driven prognostics and health management has emerged as a key enabler for Industry 4.0, yet the development of robust remaining useful life (RUL) estimation models is often limited by the scarcity of run-to-failure data. While federated learning offers a promising paradigm to collaboratively train predictive models without sharing sensor data, research efforts have operated so far in the absence of a common evaluation framework. To address this gap, this paper introduces FedCMAPSS, a benchmark for federated RUL estimation based on the commonly-used NASA C-MAPSS dataset. We define a set of five standardized tasks designed to simulate real-world industrial challenges, ranging from ideal IID settings to extreme statistical heterogeneity, and conduct a systematic evaluation of state-of-the-art federated optimization algorithms across multiple neural architectures. By establishing reproducible baselines and making the source code and data splits publicly available, this work aims to provide a standard foundation for developing and comparing federated predictive maintenance solutions.

</details>


### [104] [NeoTriFuse: Reliability-Aware Multimodal Fusion under Missingness Heterogeneity for Neonatal Mortality Risk Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26436)
*Jiyuan Tian,Qincheng Shen,Ye Lin,Yu Gao,Haohui Lu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: NeoTriFuse proposes reliability-aware multimodal fusion for neonatal mortality prediction under missing data, integrating static perinatal variables, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level summaries via reliability-guided gating; achieves AUROC 0.945 and F1 0.674 with ablations underscoring the importance of the temporal architecture and summary branch.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle extreme class imbalance, heterogeneous neonatal risk factors, multi-scale temporal dynamics, and substantial missingness by treating missing data as a reliability signal rather than a preprocessing nuisance.

Method: Introduce NeoTriFuse: a reliability-guided multimodal fusion framework combining static perinatal features, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level summaries. Missingness acts as a reliability signal to modulate modality contributions via gating. Jointly optimizes mortality prediction and an auxiliary length-of-stay objective.

Result: Performance: F1 0.6736 ± 0.0216; AUROC 0.9454 ± 0.0056. Ablations show the local-global temporal architecture and patient-level summary branch drive most performance; reliability-aware gating yields gains on threshold-dependent metrics under heterogeneous observation. Sensitivity analyses indicate stability across nearby hyperparameters.

Conclusion: Reliability-aware multimodal fusion is a practical approach for neonatal mortality prediction under realistic missingness conditions, with explicit modeling of missingness as a reliability signal improving predictive robustness.

Abstract: Neonatal mortality risk prediction from bedside monitoring data remains challenging due to extreme class imbalance, heterogeneous clinical risk factors, multi-scale temporal dynamics, and substantial missingness. We propose NeoTriFuse, a reliability-aware multimodal fusion framework for missingness-heterogeneous neonatal monitoring data. Unlike conventional multimodal approaches that treat missingness primarily as a preprocessing issue, NeoTriFuse models missingness as an explicit reliability signal that dynamically modulates modality contributions during fusion. The framework integrates static perinatal variables, local-global temporal encoders, and patient-level statistical summaries through reliability-guided gating mechanisms, while jointly optimizing mortality prediction and an auxiliary length-of-stay objective. NeoTriFuse achieves competitive performance, with an F1 score of 0.6736 +/- 0.0216 and an AUROC of 0.9454 +/- 0.0056. Ablation studies indicate that the local-global temporal architecture and patient-level summary branch contribute most substantially to predictive performance, while reliability-aware gating provides additional improvements on threshold-dependent metrics under heterogeneous observation completeness. Sensitivity analyses further suggest stable performance across nearby hyperparameter settings. Overall, the findings support reliability-aware multimodal fusion as a practical approach for neonatal mortality prediction under realistic clinical missingness conditions.

</details>


### [105] [Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26437)
*Haohui Lu,jiyuan Tian,Fangyu Zhou,Shahadat Uddin*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: SF-GNN is a lightweight, architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias in GNNs by selectively filtering bias-prone edges during message passing, using a combination of sensitive homophily and structural propagation amplifiers, plus a warm-up regularizer; it achieves better fairness-accuracy trade-offs on five benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs propagate group-correlated signals via graph topology and message passing, potentially causing unfair outcomes even without explicit sensitive features; existing fairness methods act globally and do not target local propagation pathways. There is a need to control structural pathways that carry biased information.

Method: Identify bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers (hub participation, triadic closure); apply stochastic edge filtering in each message-passing step to downweight/remove these edges while preserving overall structure; train with a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule.

Result: SF-GNN yields consistent fairness improvements with competitive predictive performance across five benchmark datasets, achieving a favorable fairness–accuracy trade-off relative to recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

Conclusion: SF-GNN effectively mitigates structural bias at its source by subgraph-level edge filtering, offering a lightweight, architecture-agnostic approach that improves fairness without sacrificing much accuracy.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) can exhibit unfair behavior even when sensitive attributes are excluded from node features, because graph topology and message passing propagate group-correlated signals under sensitive homophily. Existing fairness-aware GNN methods mainly constrain representations or prediction distributions at a global level, without explicitly controlling the local structural pathways through which biased information propagates during aggregation. We propose Subgraph Filtering for Fair Graph Neural Networks (SF-GNN), a lightweight and architecture-agnostic framework that mitigates structural bias at its source. SF-GNN identifies bias-prone edges by combining sensitive homophily with structural propagation amplifiers, including hub participation and triadic closure. It then incorporates stochastic edge filtering into each message-passing step to selectively downweight or remove these edges while preserving the remaining graph structure. Training further incorporates a statistical-parity regularizer with a warm-up schedule to stabilize optimization. Experiments on five benchmark datasets show that SF-GNN achieves consistent fairness improvements while maintaining competitive predictive performance, leading to a better fairness--accuracy trade-off than recent fairness-aware GNN baselines.

</details>


### [106] [Toward Equitable Low-Carbon Mobility: Fairness-Aware Demand Prediction for Expanding Bike-Sharing Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26451)
*Man Luo,Yixuan Zhao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FairGIN is a fairness-aware GNN for expanding bike-sharing systems that addresses cold-start and equity via (1) Expansion-Simulated Increment Training, (2) Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer with station-adaptive temperature scaling and orthogonal embedding alignment, and (3) Fairness-Aware Optimization with income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score. It achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy and reduces income-based disparities in NYC and Seattle.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Expanding bike-sharing networks suffer from cold-start issues for new stations and potential fairness gaps since historical demand may reflect existing infrastructure inequities. A model trained on such data risks reinforcing disparities while underperforming in data-sparse areas.

Method: Three components: (1) Expansion-Simulated Increment Training to simulate network growth during training, reducing distribution shift between training and inference; (2) Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer with station-adaptive temperature scaling and orthogonal embedding alignment to transfer representations from data-rich stations to data-sparse new stations; (3) Fairness-Aware Optimization including income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score to guide fair station placement.

Result: Empirical evaluation on NYC and Seattle shows that FairGIN achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across diverse expansion scenarios while substantially reducing income-based disparities without sacrificing overall system efficiency.

Conclusion: FairGIN provides a unified framework addressing both cold-start and equity in expanding bike-sharing systems, offering a promising direction for fair graph-based demand prediction in evolving networks.

Abstract: Bike-sharing systems are an important component of low-carbon urban mobility, but continued expansion creates challenges in both cold-start prediction and equitable resource allocation. Newly deployed stations lack historical ridership records, causing a mismatch between training and inference for graph-based models on evolving networks. Historical demand may also encode structural inequalities, as lower ridership in low-income neighborhoods can reflect limited infrastructure access rather than weak latent demand. Models trained directly on such data may therefore reinforce existing mobility disparities. We propose FairGIN, a fairness-aware graph neural network for demand prediction in expanding bike-sharing systems. FairGIN integrates three components. Expansion-Simulated Increment Training stochastically simulates network expansion during training to reduce the cold-start distribution gap. Attention-Based Knowledge Transfer combines station-adaptive temperature scaling with orthogonal embedding alignment to transfer representations from data-rich existing stations to data-sparse new stations. Fairness-Aware Optimization introduces income-stratified regularization and an equity-calibrated deployment score to support more inclusive station placement. Experiments on NYC and Seattle demonstrate that FairGIN achieves state-of-the-art predictive accuracy across diverse expansion scenarios while substantially reducing income-based disparities without compromising overall system efficiency.

</details>


### [107] [Distributed Training using an Intelligent Network](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26453)
*Nihar Shah,Ben Blier*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: WAN-aware distributed training framework using multicast and in-line FPGAs to ease bandwidth bottlenecks, with an optimization-driven synchronization schedule (rotating cliques) tailored to network topology; demonstrated on a nine-city DoubleZero WAN to approach colocated training performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: WAN distributed training suffers from limited bandwidth, high latency, and topology heterogeneity. Making the network an active participant can unlock more efficient synchronization and information exchange than traditional point-to-point updates.

Method: Systemic: employ multicast to replicate outbound traffic and in-line FPGAs to aggregate inbound traffic, extending these WAN-optimized techniques beyond data centers. Algorithmic: develop an optimization framework that yields rich synchronization schedules (rotating cliques of islands) aligned with the physical topology and network capabilities. Evaluation on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero live WAN to observe schedule shifts as network capabilities change.

Result: The framework demonstrates that optimal synchronization schedules depend on network capabilities; as WAN features are enabled (multicast and FPGA aggregation), information exchange improves and schedules cluster islands more effectively, narrowing the gap to colocated training.

Conclusion: Network-aware training—coupling topology-aware synchronization with network-enabled primitives—can significantly improve WAN training efficiency and move training performance closer to the gold standard of colocated systems.

Abstract: Distributed training across a wide area network (WAN) is challenging, as continuous parameter exchange by islands of compute is constrained by limited bandwidth, high latency, and uneven topology. We propose making the network an active participant in training. On the systems side, such networks should leverage (i) multicast technology to replicate outbound traffic and (ii) in-line FPGAs to aggregate inbound traffic, to ease egress and ingress bottlenecks. These technologies are used for training across workers within a data center, but this paper extends them to the WAN. On the algorithms side, we develop an optimization framework that produces rich synchronization schedules (namely, rotating cliques of islands) around the underlying network topology and these technologies, to maximize information exchange. Finally, we illustrate this on a nine-city topology modeled on the DoubleZero network, a live programmable WAN equipped with both technologies, and show how the optimal schedules shift with the network's capabilities. Together, these can narrow the gap to the gold standard of colocated training.

</details>


### [108] [Diff Mining: Logit Differences Reveal Finetuning Objectives](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26462)
*Greg Kocher,Robert West,Clément Dumas,Julian Minder*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Diff Mining is a logit-difference framework for auditing finetuning by comparing finetuned and base model outputs; it aggregates token-level signals via Top-K or NMF to form interpretable finetuning fingerprints, achieving strong domain-detection and bias-identification while requiring only output logits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As models are finetuned, unintended behaviors may emerge; existing diffing methods often require access to internals and struggle to attribute learned objectives; a scalable, interface-light approach is needed to reveal what finetuning learned.

Method: Stage 1: compute per-context logit differences between finetuned and base models on a reference corpus. Stage 2: aggregate the differences into a token set. Aggregation methods include Top-K frequency and Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF) to separate multiple finetuning objectives into token clusters.

Result: Empirically, Diff Mining outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods in finetune-domain detection and boosts downstream interpretability-agent performance using the extracted token set; it identifies more than one third of injected biases without targeted probing.

Conclusion: Diff Mining offers a scalable, logits-only auditing tool for identifying finetuning objectives, capable of surfacing salient tokens and providing interpretable fingerprints of a model's finetuning.

Abstract: Finetuning has become the gold standard for refining existing behaviors and inducing new ones in language models, yet it often remains unclear exactly which behaviors emerge during this process. As models grow ever more capable, understanding finetuning better becomes increasingly important, particularly since unwanted behaviors may arise during finetuning. In this paper, we introduce Diff Mining, a simple yet effective framework for identifying what a finetuned model has learned by comparing its logits to those of its base model. Diff Mining effectively surfaces salient tokens that are amplified in the finetuned model, serving as a fingerprint of its training -- even on text unrelated to the finetuning domain. Unlike many existing model diffing methods which require model internals, Diff Mining only needs access to output logits and scales to large models. The framework consists of two modular stages: (i) extracting per-context logit differences between the finetuned and base models on a reference corpus, and (ii) aggregating the resulting signals to construct an interpretable token set representing the finetune. For aggregation, we explore both a simple Top-K frequency method and a Non-negative Matrix Factorization (NMF)-based approach for disentangling multiple finetuning objectives into distinct token clusters. Empirically, Diff Mining succeeds across diverse settings: on finetune domain detection, it significantly outperforms state-of-the-art model diffing methods both in identifying relevant tokens and in downstream performance when an interpretability agent is given access to the extracted token set; on models with injected biases, it identifies more than one third of the biases without targeted probing. Overall, our framework shows promise in developing auditing tools to detect finetuning objectives.

</details>


### [109] [Active Curriculum Refinement for Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26469)
*Zhenya Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PATH is a curriculum-learning framework for RL that explicitly models a directed acyclic graph (DAG) of prerequisite relationships between tasks and environments. It actively expands coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths and then reallocates training to regions that remain unmastered, yielding strong robustness and generalization across diverse environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many RL domains, curricula follow DAG-like prerequisite relations. Exploiting this structure explicitly can improve sample efficiency, training stability, and generalization, compared to implicitly leveraging curricula.

Method: PATH uses active learning over the curriculum graph. It first expands coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths through the DAG, then reallocates training resources toward regions of the graph that are not yet mastered.

Result: Empirical experiments across diverse environments show that PATH leverages the graph structure to achieve strong robustness and generalization.

Conclusion: Explicit modeling of the curriculum DAG with active path sampling improves RL performance in terms of robustness and generalization, suggesting broader applicability to curriculum-aware RL and potential extensions to more complex graph structures.

Abstract: In many reinforcement learning (RL) domains, environments are connected by prerequisite relations, such as difficulty-increasing edits or parameter increments, which induce a directed acyclic curriculum graph (DAG). Although this structure is often exploited only implicitly, explicitly modeling it can improve training. We introduce PATH, a curriculum-learning framework that performs active learning over the curriculum graph. PATH first expands coverage by sampling diverse curriculum paths and then reallocates training toward regions that remain unmastered. Experiments across diverse environments show that PATH explicitly leverages the graph structure to achieve strong robustness and generalization.

</details>


### [110] [Shared Actors Need Not Share Critics: Effects of Value Mismatch in Parallel Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26481)
*Zhenya Liu,Yang Meng,Zhuokai Zhao,Xuefeng Liu,Yuxin Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A shared critic across environments causes value target misalignment; conditioning the critic on environment index (multihead) improves stability and performance in parallel RL, with large gains on Procgen.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In parallel reinforcement learning across multiple environments of the same task, the critic lacks explicit environment information and must reconcile differing value targets, biasing updates and harming learning.

Method: Theoretical analysis using bandit models with multiple environments and a common optimal arm; comparison of oracle (no baseline), shared value, and environment-specific value; minimal intervention of providing an environment index to the critic; empirical evaluation on CartPole, MuJoCo, BipedalWalker, and Procgen settings with a multihead conditional critic.

Result: All processes converge to the same optimal policy in expectation, but learning trajectories differ due to value mismatch; conditioning the critic on environment index reduces value mismatch, yielding more stable learning and higher returns; across 16 Procgen games, aggregate normalized return on 600 unseen levels per game improves by 40.8%.

Conclusion: Value mismatch is a direct mechanism by which critic sharing degrades stochastic learning dynamics; conditioning the critic on environment index is broadly effective for parallel RL.

Abstract: When a single policy is trained in parallel across multiple environments of the same task, such as procedurally generated levels, randomized dynamics, or curricula, implementations commonly use one critic across all sampled environments. Yet different environments can assign different expected returns to the same input visible to the critic. A critic without environment information must then reconcile distinct value targets, systematically shifting the sampled advantages within individual environments. Using illustrative bandit models with multiple environments and a common optimal arm, we characterize how this value mismatch redistributes sampled policy updates, reinforcing unhelpful actions while attenuating or even reversing useful ones. The oracle processes using no baseline, the shared value, or the value specific to the sampled environment have the same mean logit update at a fixed policy and converge to the same optimal policy, yet their realized learning paths can differ sharply. The analysis motivates a minimal intervention: give only a logged environment index to the critic so that it can separate the value targets. Controlled CartPole and MuJoCo experiments expose the predicted shifted values, advantages, and performance gaps. In the more complex BipedalWalker and Procgen settings, the same intervention yields more stable learning and higher returns. Across all $16$ Procgen games, the multihead conditional critic improves aggregate normalized return on $600$ unseen levels per game by $40.8\%$. In conclusion, the theory identifies value mismatch as a direct mechanism through which critic sharing can degrade stochastic learning dynamics, not captured by scalar estimator variance alone, and the experiments show that conditioning on an index is broadly effective in parallel reinforcement learning.

</details>


### [111] [Bayesian methods and Markov chain Monte Carlo algorithms for curve reconstruction and point cloud data analysis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26490)
*Asir Intesar Tushar,Ioannis Sgouralis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A fully Bayesian framework for reconstructing closed curves from point clouds with uncertainty quantification, using non-parametric priors and MCMC.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Point-cloud data are noisy, large, and incomplete; standard pipelines yield a single estimate without uncertainty. A Bayesian approach can jointly model latent curve structure and observation noise to provide uncertainty estimates for the recovered curves.

Method: Model observed points as noisy perturbations of latent points constrained to lie on an underlying curve; regularize the latent curve with a non-parametric prior. Perform posterior inference via MCMC samplers tailored to point-cloud structure to recover closed curves and quantify uncertainty.

Result: Demonstrates accurate curve reconstructions and credible uncertainty intervals on synthetic data and real LiDAR datasets, indicating effective reconstruction and uncertainty quantification.

Conclusion: A principled Bayesian approach enables simultaneous curve reconstruction and uncertainty quantification for point-cloud data, with demonstrated effectiveness on synthetic and real-world LiDAR data.

Abstract: Point-cloud data routinely captured by modern imaging and sensor technologies provide detailed geometric descriptions of objects and environments, but their analysis is hindered by large data volumes, localization noise, and missing information. In addition, existing point-cloud reconstruction pipelines typically return a single best-fit structure without uncertainty quantification. We introduce a fully Bayesian framework for representing point-cloud data and reconstructing closed curves, in which observed points are modeled as noisy perturbations of latent locations constrained to lie on the underlying curve that is regularized by a non-parametric prior. Posterior inference in our framework is carried out using a series of Markov chain Monte Carlo samplers tailored to point-cloud characteristics. Numerical experiments, including synthetic examples and real-world LiDAR datasets, show accurate reconstructions and quantified uncertainty over the recovered curves.

</details>


### [112] [A Unified Framework for Fair and Personalized Decentralized Learning under Communication Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26493)
*Krishnendu S. Tharakan,Carlo Fischione*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A decentralized multi-task learning framework (DMFL-SQ) integrates graph-based personalization, agnostic fairness, and compressed event-triggered communication to achieve fairness-aware, communication-efficient learning with convergence guarantees in non-convex settings and PAC-Bayes generalization bounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Decentralized learning promises scalability, privacy, and robustness but suffers from statistical heterogeneity across clients, fairness disparities, and strict communication constraints.

Method: Introduce DMFL-SQ, a decentralized multi-task algorithm that couples personalized models over a communication graph with an agnostic mixture fairness objective, and employs sparsification, quantization, and event-triggered synchronization to reduce communication.

Result: Proves convergence with an O(T^{-1/2}) rate in expected squared Moreau-envelope stationarity under sparse/quantized/event-triggered communication; derives PAC-Bayes generalization guarantees for the fairness objective; experimental results on CIFAR-10 and MUSMET EEG show reduced communication with maintained accuracy and improved client fairness.

Conclusion: Personalization, fairness, and communication efficiency can be jointly achieved in decentralized learning without sacrificing the dominant convergence rate.

Abstract: Decentralized learning systems aim to collaboratively train models across multiple clients without relying on a central coordinator. While decentralization improves scalability, privacy, and robustness, it also exacerbates three fundamental challenges: statistical heterogeneity across clients, fairness in client-level performance, and stringent communication constraints. This raises a natural question: \emph{how fair can decentralized learning be under limited communication?} We address this question by presenting a unified framework for decentralized learning under communication constraints, bringing together graph-based personalization, agnostic fairness, and compressed event-triggered communication. Specifically, we propose a new algorithm DMFL-SQ, a decentralized multi-task learning algorithm that couples personalized model training over a communication graph with an agnostic mixture fairness objective, while reducing communication through sparsification, quantization, and event-triggered synchronization. We establish convergence guarantees for general non-convex objectives and show that DMFL-SQ achieves an $\mathcal{O}(T^{-1/2})$ rate in expected squared Moreau-envelope stationarity despite sparse, quantized, and event-triggered communication. We further derive PAC-Bayes generalization guarantees for the fairness-aware mixture objective. Experiments on CIFAR-10 and the real heterogeneous MUSMET EEG dataset demonstrate that DMFL-SQ substantially reduces communication while maintaining predictive performance and improving fairness across clients. Together, our theoretical and empirical results show that personalization, fairness, and communication efficiency can be jointly achieved in decentralized learning while preserving the dominant convergence rate.

</details>


### [113] [A Single Suffix to Break Them All: Basin-Aware Jailbreaks for Merged Model Families](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26506)
*Yu Zhe,Yixin Tan,Junhao Wei,Wang Chen*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: BAJ reveals a new jailbreak risk in model merging: transferable adversarial suffixes can generalize across merged models sharing the same pretrained backbone, even without access to exact merging coefficients or constituent checkpoints.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safety gaps in model merging by showing that the foundation model can introduce jailbreak risks that persist across independently aligned constituents; highlight the need to study cross-model transferability of jailbreak prompts in merged systems.

Method: Formulate jailbreak generation as a min–max optimization over the merging space to produce suffixes that transfer across merged model families sharing the same backbone; evaluate across diverse backbones and merging configurations; test against existing defenses.

Result: BAJ achieves high transfer success rates across various backbones and merging settings and remains effective under current defenses, indicating a robust, transferable jailbreak risk in merged models.

Conclusion: Merging safety vulnerabilities can originate from the pretrained foundation model; current defenses may be insufficient. The study demonstrates transferable, backbone-dependent jailbreaks (BAJ) and calls for new defenses tailored to merged-model threat models.

Abstract: Model merging enables combining multiple fine-tuned models without additional training, but its safety implications remain poorly understood. Prior work primarily attributes merging risks to unsafe constituent models, implicitly assuming that merging individually aligned models preserves safety. In contrast, we show that model merging reveals a previously overlooked jailbreak risk rooted in the pretrained foundation model, even when all constituent models are individually safety-aligned. Motivated by this observation, we study a new threat setting where an attacker constructs jailbreak prompts that generalize across merged models sharing the same pretrained backbone, without access to the exact merging coefficients or constituent checkpoints. To exploit this phenomenon, we propose \textbf{Basin-Aware Jailbreak (BAJ)}, which formulates jailbreak generation as a min--max optimization over the merging space to produce transferable adversarial suffixes across merged model families. Experiments across diverse backbones and merging settings show that BAJ achieves consistently high transfer success rates and remains effective under existing defenses.

</details>


### [114] [Algorithmic Principles For Multiclass Learning Are Hard To Come By: Limits of Regularization and Proper Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26516)
*Julian Asilis,Shaddin Dughmi,Vatsal Sharan,Alec Sun,Shang-Hua Teng,Chang Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Multiclass learning cannot be reduced to proper learning or captured by standard regularization; the paper proves three negative results and provides two positive SRM conditions with a representation criterion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify whether learning reduces to proper learning, and whether regularizers or SRM suffice for multiclass learning; address open problems in the theory.

Method: Construct explicit learnable multiclass problems with properties that defy proper-learning embeddings, force sublinear error rates in proper learners, and defeat SRM/local regularizers; alongside two positive SRM-sufficient conditions and a characterization of SRM via revealed-preference integrability.

Result: 1) A learnable multiclass problem cannot be embedded in any properly learnable class. 2) Proper learning may incur o(m) training errors; for some problems every sublinear target a_m=o(m) is necessary. 3) SRM and local regularizers are not universal learners; yet two sufficient SRM conditions exist and SRM representability links to integrability of revealed preferences.

Conclusion: The work maps concrete limits of reducing multiclass learning to proper learning and to regularization, while offering constructive SRM conditions and a representability criterion, thus guiding future theory beyond improper/proper reductions and SRM-based approaches.

Abstract: Two of the most fundamental questions in statistical learning theory are the following: which prediction problems are learnable, and how should they be learned? For the former, elegant answers often take the form of combinatorial dimensions. The latter question, however, has proved considerably more elusive: all known general-purpose multiclass learners rely on intricate orientations of exponentially large one-inclusion structures, and familiar algorithmic principles such as proper learning and regularization remain poorly understood. Motivated by prior work, we ask whether learning reduces to proper learning---possibly over a larger hypothesis class---and whether proper or improper multiclass learning can ultimately be captured by suitable regularizers.
  Our primary results answer both questions negatively, resolving three open problems from prior work. First, we exhibit a learnable multiclass problem that cannot be embedded in any properly learnable class, meaning learning cannot be reduced to proper learning by enlarging the hypothesis class. Second, we demonstrate that proper learning can require training error and characterize this phenomenon precisely: every properly learnable class admits a proper learner making $o(m)$ errors on samples of size $m$, but every prescribed sublinear scale $a_m=o(m)$ is necessary for some properly learnable problem. Third, regularization is not a general learner: we exhibit a properly learnable class that cannot be learned by any Structural Risk Minimization (SRM) learner, and a learnable class that cannot be learned by any local regularizer. We complement these impossibility results with a positive theory that gives two sufficient conditions for SRM learnability and characterizes SRM representability through integrability of revealed preferences.

</details>


### [115] [High Probability Derivative Bounds for Random tanh Neural Networks on a Hypercube](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26526)
*Josef Dick,Michael Feischl,Fabian Zehetgruber*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: High-probability bounds on mixed derivatives of wide random neural networks with tanh activations; depth-growth is avoided for first-order derivatives and becomes polynomial in depth for higher-order mixed derivatives, under Gaussian weights and Xavier init. Implications include Lipschitz and Sobolev bounds and relevance to quasi-MMC training.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understand regularity and derivative growth of wide random networks to enable probabilistic control of training dynamics and to connect derivative bounds with quasi-Monte Carlo (QMC) methods; improve over naive determinant-based depth growth by leveraging width and stochastic structure.

Method: Probabilistic analysis combining Euclidean operator-norm bounds with a deterministic decomposition that isolates the term linear in the highest-order derivative. For tanh with Xavier initialization, use finite nets to bound tangent directions and obtain high-probability estimates. Derive conditions on hidden width ensuring bounds hold with probability at least 1-η.

Result: For scalar-output tanh networks with Gaussian weights and Xavier init, there exist constants C, C0, C1>0 such that if the hidden width n satisfies n ≥ C( L^3 n0^2 (1+log n0) + L^2 (1+log(L/η)) ), then with probability ≥1-η, for every non-empty u ⊆ [n0] and x ∈ [0,1]^{n0}, |D^u R_{Φ^{(L)}}(x)| ≤ C0 |u|! (C1 L)^{|u|-1} ∏_{j∈u} β_j(η,n0). In particular, the first-order derivative bound is depth-independent, while a square-free mixed derivative of order |u| grows at most polynomially as L^{|u|-1} (up to coordinate factors).

Conclusion: The results yield high-probability bounds for Euclidean Lipschitz constants and weighted Sobolev norms of the network realization, linking derivative regularity to quasi-Monte Carlo analysis and suggesting how such regularity can benefit QMC-based training.

Abstract: We establish high-probability bounds for mixed input derivatives of wide random neural networks whose activation derivatives satisfy a factorial growth bound. Our main result specializes these estimates to $\tanh$ networks with Xavier initialization. A direct deterministic analysis based on Euclidean operator norms of the weight matrices yields derivative bounds that generally grow exponentially with the depth. We show that this growth can be substantially improved for sufficiently wide Gaussian networks by isolating the term that is linear in the highest-order derivative and controlling the corresponding tangent directions by measurable finite nets.
  For scalar-output $\tanh$ networks with Gaussian weights and Xavier initialization, we prove that there exist constants $C,C_0,C_1>0$ such that, whenever the common hidden width satisfies $n \geq C\left(L^3n_0^2(1+\log n_0)+L^2\left(1+\log(L/η)\right)\right)$, then, with probability at least $1-η$, the estimate $\left|D^u\mathcal{R}_{Φ^{(L)}}(x)\right| \leq C_0 |u|! (C_1L)^{|u|-1}\prod_{j\in u}β_j(η,n_0)$ holds simultaneously for every non-empty $u\subseteq[n_0]$ and every $x\in[0,1]^{n_0}$. Thus, the first-order derivative bound is independent of the depth, while a square-free mixed derivative of order $|u|$ grows at most polynomially as $L^{|u|-1}$, apart from the coordinate factors. As consequences, we obtain high-probability bounds for the Euclidean Lipschitz constant and for weighted Sobolev norms of the network realization. The latter connect the derivative estimates to quasi-Monte Carlo integration and indicate how such regularity can enter the analysis of QMC-based training.

</details>


### [116] [Predicting Quantifiability from Primary Screens to Prioritize Dose-Response Profiling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26538)
*Sean Lim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes a framework to predict quantifiability of potency in HTS and shows that predictability relies on response features rather than structure, enabling better triage for dose-response profiling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: High-throughput screening aims to identify active compounds, but many actives do not yield quantifiable potency; need to separate activity from measurability to optimize resource use.

Method: Develop a framework modeling quantifiability as a separate objective; assess predictors from low-cost screening data, including response-based features, across unseen scaffolds and assay-mechanism families; examine dependence on response amplitude and assay context.

Result: Quantifiability is strongly predictable from screening features, with most info from screening data; response-based predictors generalize to new scaffolds and assay mechanisms; quantifiability probability depends on response amplitude and assay context; demonstrates measurability as a predictable property.

Conclusion: Incorporating quantifiability into triage improves allocation of expensive dose-response profiling, establishing measurability as distinct from biological activity.

Abstract: High-throughput drug screening relies on low-cost primary assays to prioritize compounds for more expensive dose-response profiling, where potency is ultimately quantified. Current screening strategies largely focus on identifying compounds that will confirm biological activity on follow-up, implicitly assuming that confirmed activity will also yield a usable potency estimate. However, confirmed biological activity in screening does not necessarily translate into a quantifiable potency, because active compounds can still fail to produce a reportable dose-response estimate. We therefore present a framework for modeling quantifiability, whether follow-up testing will yield a usable potency estimate, as a distinct triage objective from biological activity. Quantifiability was strongly predictable from the preceding low-cost screen, with most predictive information arising from the observed screening features rather than molecular structure. Response-based predictors remained robust on previously unseen chemical scaffolds and generalized across held-out assay-mechanism families, while the probability of successful quantification varied strongly with response amplitude and assay context. These findings establish experimental measurability, distinct from biological activity, as a predictable property of screening outcomes and show that quantifiability-aware triage can improve the allocation of costly dose-response profiling capacity.

</details>


### [117] [Chart2SVG: Editable SVG Generation from Raster Chart Images](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26544)
*Jinning Cui,Lu Chen,Haoyan Shi,Yue He,Chenglong Wang,Mengyu Zhou,Weidong Huang,Yunhai Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Converts raster charts to semantically enriched, editable SVGs using a chart-aware LLM, supported by Beagle+ and a Chart Structure Graph.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable robust structural recovery of charts from raster images to support editing, reuse, and repurposing, addressing the semantic gaps of traditional raster-to-vector pipelines.

Method: Introduce Chart2SVG, a multimodal LLM with chart-specific semantic tokens, plus a rendering-aware post-training phase; build Beagle+ (33k canonicalized chart samples); and construct a Chart Structure Graph (CSG) to expose visual dependencies for interactive tasks.

Result: Chart2SVG achieves higher reconstruction fidelity and better downstream editing utility than baselines; Beagle+ provides diverse, structurally distilled training data; CSG enables interactive exploration, repurposing, and layout reuse.

Conclusion: This work advances intelligent, interactive visualization tools by delivering semantically aware, editable chart representations at scale.

Abstract: We present Chart2SVG, a multimodal large language model that converts static raster charts into structurally organized, semantically enriched SVGs that support programmatic editing. By incorporating chart-specific semantic tokens into a vision-language model, Chart2SVG captures both geometric primitives and their functional roles. To support robust structural recovery, we introduce Beagle+, a dataset of 33K canonicalized and structurally distilled chart samples. Our approach combines specialized training objectives with a rendering-aware post-training phase, producing SVGs that are both visually accurate and structurally consistent. To facilitate higher-level manipulations, we construct a Chart Structure Graph (CSG) that exposes visual dependencies, enabling tasks such as interactive exploration, chart repurposing, and layout reuse. Experiments show that Chart2SVG substantially outperforms baselines in reconstruction fidelity and downstream editing utility, advancing the development of intelligent and interactive visualization tools.

</details>


### [118] [Arrive and Survive: Scaling Safe Goal-Conditioned Policy Learning from One-Bit Failure Signals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26571)
*Guopeng Li,Yiyang Duan,Yiru Jiao,Chengcheng Xu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Safe-CRL corrects bias in contrastive RL under failure termination by mass-weighted InfoNCE and log-survival-mass scoring, improving safe goal-conditioned learning and outperforming Scaling-CRL across tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In failure-terminated MDPs, standard CRL ignores the probability mass removed by termination, causing overestimation of goal-reaching value and potential unsafe bootstrapping.

Method: Introduce two corrections: (1) mass-weighted InfoNCE to balance surviving futures in critic learning, (2) log-survival-mass score to account for missing survival mass in policy optimization; utilize only a one-bit failure signal; release code.

Result: Empirically, Safe-CRL improves survival and goal-reaching across twelve failure-prone robot navigation/ locomotion tasks and substantially outperforms the Scaling-CRL baseline; deep policies exhibit failure-avoidance behaviors.

Conclusion: Completes the CRL theory under failure termination and provides a scalable safe reinforcement learning framework.

Abstract: Contrastive reinforcement learning (CRL) scales effectively in goal-conditioned tasks by casting policy learning into a self-supervised contrastive objective. However, in a failure-terminated Markov decision process, established CRL considers pre-failure future goals only when constructing positive samples, without accounting for the probability mass removed by failure termination. Our theoretical analysis shows that this omission induces a systematic overestimation bias in goal-reaching values. Consequently, near-failure trajectories provide disproportionately strong supervision of success despite retaining little future occupancy. Unsafe actions can thereby be reinforced through catastrophic failure bootstrapping, leading to failed policy learning and unsustainable goal-reaching behaviours. To address this problem, we introduce two minimal yet strong corrections: mass-weighted InfoNCE corrects the overweighting of short surviving futures in critic learning, and a log-survival-mass score restores the missing survival mass in policy optimization. The resulting method, Safe Contrastive Reinforcement Learning (Safe-CRL), requires only the one-bit signal provided by failure termination to scale safe goal-conditioned policy learning. Across twelve failure-prone robot navigation and locomotion tasks, Safe-CRL consistently improves survival and substantially outperforms the Scaling-CRL baseline in goal-reaching performance. Additionally, deep Safe-CRL policies exhibit complex failure-avoidance behaviours. This study completes the CRL theory under failure termination and provides a scalable safe RL framework. The code is available via https://github.com/RomainLITUD/safe-crl.

</details>


### [119] [Activation Outliers Matter: Robust Recovery for Quantized Multimodal LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26581)
*Tanzila Rahman,Mehran Taghian Jazi,Yunke Peng,Zhuang Ma,Anandharaju Durai Raju,Yao Wang,Xing Huang,Hei Yi Mak,Shadan Golestan,Hoang Le,Yonghan Dong,Wei Guo,Yaoyuan Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Systematic evaluation of ultra-low-bit quantization (MXFP8, MXFP4, HiF4) on multimodal LLMs for video generation and reasoning; activation quantization is the main bottleneck; introduces Residual Fallback Quantization (RFQ) to reconstruct activations; RFQ improves 4-bit deployment with minimal overhead and no architecture changes, narrowing the gap to BF16 on Wan2.2 and Qwen3-VL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce compute and memory requirements of MLLMs with low-bit quantization in the context of practical hardware support (MXFP8–HiF4) and evaluate robustness across generation and reasoning tasks.

Method: Empirical study across representative MLLMs for video generation and reasoning; extensive ablations to identify bottlenecks; introduce RFQ—a lightweight activation reconstruction framework that adds a quantized residual pathway alongside the primary activation representation; no architectural modifications required; evaluate on Wan2.2 and Qwen3-VL.

Result: MXFP8 delivers near-lossless performance; aggressive 4-bit quantization degrades significantly with activation quantization identified as the dominant source of loss; RFQ recovers a substantial portion of 4-bit/HiF4 performance; negligible overhead; improves activation fidelity and narrows the gap to BF16 across generation and four reasoning benchmarks.

Conclusion: Activation quantization is the primary bottleneck in ultra-low-bit MLLMs; residual-based activation reconstruction (RFQ) is an effective, practical strategy for robust 4-bit deployment without changing model architecture or incurring cost, demonstrated on Wan2.2 and Qwen3-VL.

Abstract: Low-bit quantization offers a promising avenue for reducing the computational and memory demands of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). Recent hardware support for low-precision formats, ranging from MXFP8 to ultra-low-bit formats such as MXFP4 and HiF4, has accelerated research into efficient MLLM training and deployment. In this work, we present a systematic study of these quantization schemes in representative MLLMs that span both video generation and reasoning tasks. Our analysis shows that MXFP8 achieves near-lossless performance, whereas aggressive 4-bit quantization leads to significant degradation. Through extensive ablations, we identify activation quantization as the primary source of this performance loss, contributing substantially more than weight quantization. Motivated by this observation, we propose Residual Fallback Quantization (RFQ), a lightweight activation reconstruction framework that supplements the primary ulta-low-bit activation representation with an auxiliary quantized residual pathway. By explicitly modeling and compensating for quantization errors, RFQ improves activation fidelity while preserving the efficiency advantages of ultra-low-bit computation. RFQ requires no architectural modifications and incurs negligible computational overhead. Extensive experiments on Wan2.2 and Qwen3-VL demonstrate that RFQ consistently recovers a substantial portion of the performance lost under the quantization of MXFP4 and HiF4, significantly narrowing the gap to BF16 baselines across both generation and 4 reasoning benchmarks. Our findings establish activation quantization as the dominant bottleneck in ultra-low-bit MLLMs and highlight residual-based activation reconstruction as an effective and practical strategy for robust 4-bit deployment.

</details>


### [120] [J-Zero: Unified Challenger--Solver--Judge Co-Evolution from Zero Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26582)
*Gyouk Chu,Myeongho Jeon,Eunho Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: J-Zero introduces a unified Challenger–Solver–Judge co-evolution framework that enables self-improvement in both verifiable and unverifiable domains, surpassing baselines and scaling over at least ten iterations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To extend self-evolving language models into unverifiable domains, reduce human supervision, and achieve robust, sustained improvement through a triadic co-adaptation (Challenger, Solver, Judge) with known preference signals.

Method: A triad co-evolution: the Challenger generates increasingly difficult tasks, the Solver learns to provide higher-quality responses to these tasks, and the Judge adaptively aggregates preferences derived from the production process (Solver vs Challenger, and decomposed-vs-one-shot answers) rather than relying on its own scores. The approach is iterative and demonstrated over at least ten cycles, across both verifiable and unverifiable domains.

Result: J-Zero yields average gains of 4.2 points on verifiable domains and 8.0 points on unverifiable domains, with continued improvement through ten iterations, whereas baselines degrade after two iterations.

Conclusion: The J-Zero framework demonstrates robust, scalable self-improvement in both verifiable and unverifiable domains by leveraging adversarial co-evolution and preference-based judging that does not rely solely on the judge’s scored assessments.

Abstract: Self-evolving language models have recently emerged as a promising path toward superintelligence, with the advantage of reducing the cost of human supervision. While considerable progress has been made in verifiable domains, self-evolution in unverifiable domains remains substantially less explored. We propose Judge co-adaptation from Zero data (J-Zero), a unified Challenger--Solver--Judge co-evolution framework that supports self-improvement across both domains. The Challenger and Solver co-evolve through an adversarial interaction: the Challenger generates increasingly difficult tasks, while the Solver learns to produce higher-quality responses to them. In parallel, the Judge co-adapts using preference pairs whose ordering is known in advance from how each response was produced, i.e., the Solver's answer over the Challenger's, and its decomposed-and-recombined answer over its one-shot answer, rather than from the Judge's own scores. J-Zero outperforms the baselines by an average of 4.2 points on verifiable and 8.0 points on unverifiable domains, and continues to improve through at least ten iterations, whereas the baselines degrade after two.

</details>


### [121] [GRAS: Guided Reduced-Variance Proposals and Adaptive Selection for Training-Free Reward Alignment in Discrete Diffusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26585)
*Kwanyoung Kim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: GRAS combines gradient-guided and search-based steering for discrete diffusion models with variance reduction and adaptive resampling, achieving state-of-the-art training-free rewards in DNA/protein design without extra denoiser cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Training-free steering of discrete diffusion models toward downstream rewards faces two weaknesses: high gradient-estimate variance from single noisy samples and fixed-temperature resampling that ignores reward dispersion across denoising steps.

Method: Introduce Rao-Blackwellized reveal for differentiable rewards and leave-one-out baseline for non-differentiable rewards to reduce gradient variance; standardize per-step values into a group-relative advantage and derive an adaptive resampling temperature; the approach adds no denoiser cost.

Result: GRAS achieves the best training-free reward on regulatory DNA and protein design tasks, outperforming prior training-free methods and matching or surpassing reward-fine-tuned models; effective for non-differentiable rewards.

Conclusion: GRAS offers a simple, effective framework for training-free steering of discrete diffusion models by combining variance-reduced proposals with adaptive, group-relative resampling, delivering strong performance across differentiable and non-differentiable reward signals.

Abstract: Discrete diffusion models have become a strong, widely adopted class of generators for sequence data, and steering them toward a downstream reward at inference time, without any retraining, is increasingly important. Such training-free steering is done by gradient guidance, by search, or by combining the two. We study the combined regime and identify two weaknesses in how it is usually run: the guided proposal estimates its gradient from a single noisy sample, and the search then resamples particles at a fixed temperature that ignores how rewards spread across each denoising step. We address both with a small set of changes that add no denoiser cost. For the proposal, we lower the estimator variance with a Rao-Blackwellized reveal for differentiable rewards and a leave-one-out baseline for non-differentiable ones; for the search, we standardize the per-step values into a group-relative advantage and prove it collapses to a single active ingredient, an adaptive resampling temperature. We call the resulting method Guided Reduced-variance proposals and Adaptive Selection (GRAS). GRAS is simple yet effective: across regulatory DNA and protein design it attains the best training-free reward, outperforming prior training-free methods and matching or surpassing a reward-fine-tuned model, and it remains effective even for non-differentiable rewards.

</details>


### [122] [SimCast-S2S: An Efficient Generative Model for Subseasonal Precipitation Forecasting via Transfer Learning from Climate Simulations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26594)
*Hiep V. Dang,Antonios Mamalakis*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A diffusion-based latent-gen framework (SimCast-S2S) for probabilistic Subseasonal-to-Seasonal precipitation forecasts, using a latent space learned by VAEs and LoRA transfer learning to enable large ensembles from limited data. It yields competitive results vs. ECMWF-S2S with less input and no post-processing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: S2S precipitation forecasting suffers from weak signals, high uncertainty, and high computational costs. There is a need for probabilistic predictions, data-efficient training, and scalable ensemble generation.

Method: A generative latent-diffusion pipeline operating in a compact latent space learned by variational autoencoders. Conditional diffusion enables sampling from the predictive distribution. Large ensembles are generated efficiently in latent space. Transfer learning via low-rank adaptation (LoRA) pretrains on large climate ensembles, then fine-tunes on limited reanalysis data. Uses a subset of atmospheric inputs, no bias correction or post-processing.

Result: On reanalysis data, SimCast-S2S outperforms common DL baselines (CNN, U-Net) and is competitive with the ECMWF-S2S baseline, without requiring post-processing or full physics-based inputs. Demonstrates efficient large-ensemble generation.

Conclusion: Latent generative modeling paired with simulation-to-reanalysis transfer learning offers an efficient, scalable path for data-driven probabilistic S2S precipitation forecasting.

Abstract: Subseasonal-to-seasonal (S2S) precipitation forecasting has substantial financial and societal impact, yet remains challenging because of weak predictive signals, high associated uncertainty, and the computational cost of operational systems, which constrains simulation fidelity. We introduce SimCast-S2S, a generative latent-diffusion framework for probabilistic S2S precipitation forecasting that addresses three major bottlenecks in data-driven prediction. First, because S2S prediction requires uncertainty quantification rather than only deterministic point forecasts, SimCast-S2S is the first data-driven system that uses a diffusion-based generative pipeline for S2S prediction, enabling effective sampling from the underlying conditional distribution. Second, since generating large probabilistic ensembles is computationally costly in physical space, SimCast-S2S instead operates in a compact latent space learned by variational autoencoders, enabling efficient large-ensemble generation. Third, diffusion models typically require large training datasets; SimCast-S2S overcomes this via transfer learning with low-rank adaptation (LoRA), pretraining on large ensembles of climate simulations before fine-tuning on limited reanalysis data. On reanalysis data, SimCast-S2S outperforms deep learning baselines, including convolutional neural networks and U-Net architectures. Notably, despite using only a subset of atmospheric input variables and no post-processing, bias correction, or calibration, SimCast-S2S remains competitive with, and in many cases outperforms, state-of-the-art operational systems such as the ECMWF-S2S baseline. These results indicate that latent generative modeling combined with simulation-to-reanalysis transfer learning offers an efficient and scalable path toward data-driven probabilistic S2S precipitation forecasting.

</details>


### [123] [Technical Comparative Benchmarking Study: Advanced AI Hybrid Methods for Renewable Energy Farm Optimization and Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26613)
*Majid Masoumi,Asghar Dashtiy,Mohammad Dehghan,Mina Rajabi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Randomized tree ensembles (Extra Trees) excel on structured WEC data; RF BiLSTM hybrid yields best overall MAE; no single architecture dominates; graph networks capture spatial interactions; ensemble recurrent hybrids balance nonlinear tabular relations and temporal dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Benchmark a wide spectrum of AI architectures (conventional ML, ensemble methods, deep nets, recurrent models, Transformers, graph-based models, and hybrid ensembles) for surrogate forecasting in renewable energy under diverse WEC datasets and operational SCADA data.

Method: Systematic evaluation across three datasets (large-scale WEC, 16 WEC, and 10-minute SCADA from Penmanshiel wind farm) comparing conventional ML, ensemble, deep learning, recurrent networks, Transformers, graph models, and hybrid ensemble DL. Key models include Extra Trees, Random Forest, STGCN, MLP baseline, LSTM, BiLSTM, RF BiLSTM, and other hybrids; metrics are MAE and R.

Result: Extra Trees emerged as the strongest model for structured WEC data, achieving ~63.7% MAE reduction relative to the MLP baseline. STGCN achieved MAE ≈ 167.0 kW with R = 0.93 by learning spatial-temporal interactions. The RF BiLSTM hybrid yielded the best overall forecasting accuracy with MAE = 150.5 kW, about a 75% reduction versus standalone LSTM and ~10% better than STGCN.

Conclusion: No architecture is universally optimal. Randomized and boosted ensembles excel for structured WEC surrogate modeling, graph networks are advantageous when explicit spatial interactions dominate, and ensemble recurrent hybrids offer the strongest balance when nonlinear tabular relations and temporal dynamics co-exist.

Abstract: This study provides a comprehensive benchmarking of conventional machine learning (ML), ensemble learning, deep neural networks, recurrent architectures, Transformers, graph based models, and hybrid ensemble deep learning approaches under complementary renewable energy scenarios. Three datasets are considered: a large scale WEC dataset, a 16 WEC dataset, and operational 10 min SCADA measurements at the Penmanshiel wind farm. For structured WEC layout data, tree ensembles exhibited a clear advantage over conventional ML and neural predictors because randomized partitioning and boosting efficiently captured nonlinear layout power interactions without requiring explicit feature representation learning. The Extra Trees was the strongest model, achieving considerable results. Relative to the MLP baseline, this corresponds to an approximately 63.7% reduction in MAE, demonstrating the suitability of randomized tree ensembles for high dimensional structured WEC data. Also, STGCN reduced the MAE to approximately 167.0 kW and achieved R = 0.93 by explicitly learning spatial and temporal turbine interactions. The best overall forecasting accuracy was obtained by the RF BiLSTM hybrid, with an MAE=150.5 kW. Compared with standalone LSTM, this represents an approximately 75% reduction in MAE, while improving on STGCN by approximately 10.0%. Finally, the experiments reveal that no single AI architecture is universally optimal: randomized and boosted ensembles are particularly effective for structured WEC surrogate modeling, graph networks become advantageous when explicit spatial interactions dominate, and ensemble recurrent hybrids provide the strongest balance when nonlinear tabular relationships and temporal dynamics coexist.

</details>


### [124] [Robust Neural Stimulation Response Modeling Through Meta-Learning and Pretraining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26649)
*Matthew J Bryan,Daniel C Muir,Felix Schwock,Azadeh Yazdan-Shahmorad,Rajesh P N Rao*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Meta-learning with MAML pretraining improves cross-session neural stimulation response modeling, reducing catastrophic forecast failures and calibration time; demonstrates viability for closed-loop controllers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address two key obstacles to model-based closed-loop neural stimulation: (i) catastrophic forecast failures in unseen sessions, (ii) heavy per-session calibration incompatible with clinical constraints; exploit cross-session structure via meta-learning to enable robust, data-efficient models.

Method: Extend temporal basis function models (TBFMs) with cross-session pretraining using model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML); evaluate on 40 optogenetic stimulation sessions in primary sensorimotor cortex of two non-human primates.

Result: Substantial reduction in catastrophic forecast failure: test R^2<0.05 decreases from 16/40 with single-session training to 1 with MAML pretraining; prediction intervals narrower (p<0.05); calibration requirements reduced by 50-90% at matched accuracy.

Conclusion: Cross-session structure in stimulation responses is sufficiently consistent to support pretraining; first empirical evidence that meta-learning approaches are viable for neural stimulation; supports robustness and sample efficiency needed for deploying model-based closed-loop controllers.

Abstract: Objective: Model-based closed-loop neural stimulation holds promise for therapeutic applications ranging from Parkinson's disease to sensory restoration, but deployment has been limited by two obstacles: 1) forecasting models for predicting the consequences of stimulation fail catastrophically on a meaningful fraction of sessions, and 2) per-session calibration requirements are often incompatible with clinical constraints. We address both by demonstrating, for the first time, that meta-learning and pretraining can be applied to neural stimulation response modeling. Methods: Temporal basis function models (TBFMs) forecast state-dependent neural responses to stimulation. We extend TBFMs with cross-session pretraining using a novel architecture and algorithm based on model-agnostic meta-learning (MAML), evaluating them on 40 sessions of optogenetic stimulation in primary sensorimotor cortex of two non-human primates. Results: Meta-learning substantially reduces catastrophic forecast failure: for a 1k calibration set size, sessions with test R-squared < 0.05 drop from 16 of 40 (single-session training) to 1 (MAML-pretrained), and prediction intervals become significantly narrower (p < 0.05). Calibration requirements are reduced by 50-90% at matched accuracy, enabling experiments otherwise infeasible within clinical session-time constraints. Conclusion: Our results demonstrate that cross-session structure in stimulation responses is consistent enough to support pretraining, providing the first empirical evidence that meta-learning approaches are viable for neural stimulation. Significance: The robustness and sample efficiency gains directly address known obstacles to deploying model-based stimulation controllers. Our results motivate community efforts to assemble standardized multi-site stimulation datasets and to further explore meta-learning for robust closed-loop stimulation.

</details>


### [125] [When Privacy Hurts Mergeability: Geometry-Aware Model Merging under Differential Privacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26655)
*Jin Liu,Junkang Liu,Ning Xi,Yinbin Miao,Dawei Wei,Ke Cheng,Jianfeng Ma*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: DP-Merging: a geometry-aware method to improve mergeability of differentially private task models by addressing local sharpness and reference drift; uses a DP-compatible sharpness objective and a reference-based alignment regularizer; provides a merge-gap bound and shows improved performance across vision and language tasks under DP budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable private model merging of independently fine-tuned task models without data sharing, while preserving differential privacy and understanding how privacy affects mergeability.

Method: Introduce DP-Merging, which combines (1) a DP-compatible sharpness-aware objective to steer models toward flatter loss regions, and (2) a reference-based alignment regularizer to keep models close to the shared pretrained initialization. Derive a merge-gap upper bound showing how reducing local curvature and reference drift tightens the bound.

Result: Empirical evaluation on vision and language tasks across multiple DP budgets demonstrates that DP-Merging consistently improves the performance of private merged models while preserving the privacy guarantees of the DP fine-tuning procedures.

Conclusion: Geometry-aware DP merging reduces the detrimental effects of local sharpness and reference drift, leading to better private model merging and validated by theoretical bounds and cross-domain experiments.

Abstract: Model merging promises to construct a single multi-task model from independently fine-tuned task models without accessing the original task data. This makes it attractive when task data cannot be centralized, but released task models may still leak private fine-tuning data. Differential privacy (DP) provides a principled mechanism for limiting such leakage, yet its effect on model merging remains poorly understood. In this paper, we study the geometry of differentially private model merging and identify two geometric obstacles that make private task models difficult to merge: \emph{local sharpness}, which makes task losses sensitive to the parameter displacement induced by merging, and \emph{reference drift}, which measures the displacement of private task models from the shared pretrained initialization and amplifies cross-task interference. Based on these observations, we propose \textbf{DP-Merging}, a geometry-aware framework that improves the mergeability of differentially private task models. DP-Merging uses a DP-compatible sharpness-aware objective to guide each private task model toward flatter loss regions, and a reference-based alignment regularizer to keep task models close to the shared pretrained initialization. We derive a merge-gap upper bound showing that reducing local curvature and reference drift tightens the bound on the loss increase induced by merging. Experiments on vision and language tasks across multiple privacy budgets show that DP-Merging consistently improves private merged-model performance while preserving the privacy guarantees of the underlying DP fine-tuning procedures.

</details>


### [126] [Simple Actors and Deep Critics for Scalable Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26659)
*Guhyeon Kang,Jaehwi Lee,Minhae Kwon*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces LAC (Light Actor, deep Critic) for offline RL, assigning large capacity to a deep critic while keeping a lightweight deterministic actor; achieves competitive performance with significantly lower inference latency (up to 4x faster) compared to diffusion/flow-matching actors on OGBench.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Offline RL progress with expressive generative actors (diffusion/flow-matching) captures multimodal behavior but incurs high per-step computational costs. Since the critic is only needed during training, investing in the actor is inefficient for deployment. Deep critics in offline RL face instability, motivating a design that stabilizes deep critics and preserves inference efficiency.

Method: Use a residual MLP backbone for the critic, adopt n-step bootstrap targets, and apply a categorical cross-entropy loss to address instability and value-range drift. Maintain a lightweight deterministic actor. Train offline RL with these ingredients to form LAC (Light Actor, deep Critic). Evaluate on OGBench and test cross-compatibility of the critic across different actor parametrizations.

Result: LAC matches or exceeds the strongest diffusion- and flow-matching baselines on OGBench while delivering up to 4× lower inference latency, approaching the efficiency of distilled one-step policies without distillation. The critic recipe transfers across actor parametrizations, indicating robustness of the design.

Conclusion: Prioritizing capacity in the critic during offline RL is effective for inference-time efficiency without sacrificing performance. The proposed residual-MLP critic, together with n-step bootstrap targets and categorical loss, mitigates optimization, bootstrap-noise amplification, and value-range drift, yielding a practical, transferable blueprint (LAC) for scalable offline RL.

Abstract: Recent progress in offline reinforcement learning (RL) has been driven by expressive generative actors such as diffusion and flow-matching policies, which capture multimodal behavior in offline datasets.
