However, these actors require multiple denoising or integration steps per action and thus incur substantial overhead at every decision in deployment. In this work, we revisit where capacity should be invested in an offline actor--critic method. Since the critic is used only during training and is discarded at deployment while the actor runs at every decision step, allocating capacity to the critic rather than the actor is more favorable for inference-time efficiency. However, scaling MLP critics in offline RL is known to introduce several distinct instabilities that have, in practice, kept critics shallow. We identify three distinct failure modes that arise when critics are deepened in offline RL---optimization, bootstrap-noise amplification, and value-range drift---and address each with a corresponding ingredient: a residual MLP backbone, n-step bootstrap targets, and a categorical cross-entropy loss. Combining these ingredients with a lightweight deterministic actor, we propose LAC (Light Actor, deep Critic). On OGBench, LAC matches the strongest diffusion- and flow-matching baselines while achieving up to 4x lower inference latency, comparable to one-step distilled policies without distillation. Its critic recipe also transfers across actor parametrizations.

</details>


### [127] [Neural Regression with Embeddings for Numerical Attribute Prediction in Knowledge Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26729)
*Rupesh Sapkota,Louis Mozart Kamdem Teyou,Moshood Yekini,Caglar Demir,Axel-Cyrille Ngonga Ngomo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LitEm is a neural regression module that enables transductive KG embeddings to predict numerical attributes; a co-training framework with LitEm improves link prediction (notably for bilinear models) and enables attribute prediction; literal-aware evaluation suggests gains come from better attribute handling, not extra parameters; code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Knowledge graphs often include rich numerical attributes that traditional embedding methods neglect, hindering expressive power and downstream tasks. Incorporating attributes can enhance representation and prediction on link prediction and query answering.

Method: Introduce LitEm, a neural regression model to predict numerical attributes within transductive KG embeddings. Propose a co-training framework that jointly trains LitEm with state-of-the-art transductive KG embedding models. Evaluate on FB15K-237, YAGO15K, DB15K, and Mutagenesis; conduct literal-awareness evaluation and release code.

Result: LitEm achieves best or second-best results on most attributes across the evaluated datasets. The co-training framework improves link prediction performance mainly for bilinear models and enables numerical attribute prediction. Literal-awareness evaluation indicates gains stem from better encoding/exploitation of attribute information rather than added parameters.

Conclusion: LitEm provides an effective mechanism to incorporate numerical attributes into KG embeddings. The co-training approach further enhances link prediction and demonstrates the utility of literal-aware representations. Code is publicly released.

Abstract: In recent years, transductive knowledge graph embedding models have been applied to tasks such as link prediction and query answering. Although knowledge graphs often contain rich numerical attributes, most embedding models neglect them, limiting their ability to represent real-world knowledge graphs with diverse information. In this work, we propose a neural regression model (LitEm) that enables transductive knowledge graph embedding models to predict numerical attributes within knowledge graphs. Experimental results demonstrate that LitEm achieves the best or second-best results on most attributes across FB15K-237, YAGO15K, DB15K, and Mutagenesis. Furthermore, we propose a co-training framework that jointly trains state-of-the-art transductive knowledge graph embedding models with LitEm, which improves link prediction performance mainly for bilinear models and simultaneously enables them to predict numerical attributes. In addition, the literal-awareness evaluation demonstrates that co-training helps models to encode and exploit attribute information in a "literal-aware'' manner, suggesting that the observed gains are not merely due to additional parameters. We publicly release our implementation at https://github.com/dice-group/dice-embeddings.

</details>


### [128] [Rethinking Message Passing as Retrieval for Text-Attributed Graph Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26732)
*Jintang Li,Yuhong Chen,Ruofan Wu,Binli Luo,Jiayi Ji,Hui Li,Rongrong Ji*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Retrieval-augmented view of GNNs (RTA) replaces structural message passing with label-aware retrieval and an MLP, offering theoretical links to softmax-attention and robustness to mis-retrieval; achieves competitive efficiency and performance on text-attributed graphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: GNNs rely on expensive neighborhood aggregation and struggle with imperfect graphs; node-wise MLPs can underperform; a retrieval-based paradigm could provide robustness and efficiency.

Method: Propose RTA: each layer uses an MLP on node representations plus a permutation-invariant summary of retrieved graph context; retrieval replaces message passing; analyze connections to softmax-attention and robustness to outliers.

Result: RTA matches or surpasses strong GNN and graph LLM baselines on text-attributed graph benchmarks, with improved efficiency and robustness across diverse scenarios.

Conclusion: Retrieval-based context supervision is an effective, scalable alternative to traditional message-passing GNNs, balancing performance, efficiency, and robustness.

Abstract: Graph neural networks (GNNs) are typically conceptualized as message-passing neural networks, yet it remains unclear why neighborhood aggregation reliably outperforms node-wise multilayer perceptrons (MLPs). Despite its empirical success, this paradigm can be computationally expensive and sensitive to imperfect graph structures. In this work, we present a retrieval-augmented view of GNNs: each layer makes predictions by applying an MLP to a node representation together with a permutation-invariant summary of retrieved graph context. Motivated by this perspective, we propose RTA, a simple MLP-based framework that replaces structural message passing with label-aware retrieval and propagation. We provide theoretical insights that (i) connect retrieval-based aggregation to softmax-attention message passing, and (ii) establish the robustness of retrieved-context supervision to mis-retrieved outliers. Experiments on multiple text-attributed graph benchmarks show that RTA matches or even outperforms strong GNN and graph LLM baselines while improving efficiency and robustness across diverse scenarios.

</details>


### [129] [Self-Augmented Diffusion Guidance for Physics-Informed Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26748)
*Akira Osaka,Naoya Takeishi,Takehisa Yairi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Physics-informed diffusion guidance with self-generated data augmentation to enforce governing equations without per-iteration PDE solving, decoupling physics evaluation from diffusion training for faster, more faithful spatiotemporal samples.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard diffusion models lack physics constraints, causing samples to drift from true dynamics despite visual plausibility; there is a need to enforce governing equations efficiently for complex, computationally expensive simulations.

Method: Train a diffusion model conditioned on the deviation from physically correct dynamics using self-generated data augmentation; during sampling, enforce zero deviation. Decouple the evaluation of the governing equations from training and sampling, avoiding PDE solves at each denoising step, and enable compatibility with existing physics-constrained diffusion methods.

Result: Significant reduction in deviation from the true dynamics compared with standard diffusion models; additional improvements when combined with existing physics-constrained diffusion methods; enables faster sample generation due to decoupled physics evaluation.

Conclusion: A simple, effective plug-in for physics-informed diffusion that reduces physical deviations without incurring PDE solves during inference, suitable for computationally expensive simulations and compatible with other physics-constrained approaches.

Abstract: Diffusion models can be used to generate spatiotemporal signals of physical phenomena, such as time-series images of fluid dynamics. However, a major limitation of standard diffusion models is that they do not incorporate constraints derived from the underlying physical laws. Consequently, generated samples may appear visually plausible while deviating substantially from the true dynamics. In this study, we propose a simple yet effective physics-informed approach based on diffusion guidance with self-generated data augmentation. The proposed method learns the data distribution conditioned on the degree of deviation from the physically correct dynamics and generates samples by explicitly setting the deviation condition to be zero. The method decouples the evaluation of the governing equations from the diffusion model training and sampling processes, avoiding the need to solve the governing equations at every iteration of the denoising process. This design makes the method applicable to problems requiring computationally expensive numerical simulations and enables faster sample generation. Experimental results demonstrate that the proposed model not only significantly reduces the deviations compared with standard diffusion models but also achieves further reductions when combined with existing physics-constrained diffusion methods.

</details>


### [130] [Safety by Design: Realized-Cost Constraints for Contextual Bandits with Continuous Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26755)
*Spyros Dragazis,Aldo Pacchiano*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces High-Probability Constrained UCB for contextual bandits with stage-wise realized-cost safety; achieves near-optimal regret in linear settings ( Õ(d√T) ) and extends to general function classes via eluder dimension; demonstrated safety benefits empirically.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Safety is crucial in sequential decision-making; in heteroscedastic settings, actions affect both mean and variance of outcomes. Existing methods optimizing expected-cost can fail to prevent unsafe realized costs; the paper aims to enforce safety on realized costs while balancing exploration for reward.

Method: Proposes an optimistic-pessimistic UCB-style algorithm that optimistically selects actions for reward while conservatively estimating the safe action set using stage-wise high-probability constraints on realized cost. Analyzes the algorithm for linear reward and cost models to obtain tight Õ(d√T) regret; extends the theoretical framework to general function classes via the eluder dimension.

Result: The algorithm attains a tight regret bound of Õ(d√T) for linear settings and generalizes to broader function classes using eluder dimension metrics. Empirical results show substantial reduction in safety violations when enforcing realized-cost safety compared to baselines that constrain only expected cost.

Conclusion: High-Probability Constrained UCB provides a principled framework for safety-aware exploration in contextual bandits with realized-cost constraints, delivering strong theoretical guarantees and practical safety improvements, with a pathway to extension to general function classes through eluder dimension.

Abstract: Contextual bandits are a standard framework for sequential decision-making under uncertainty, with applications in clinical trials, dosage selection, recommendation systems, and autonomous systems. Safety is central in many of these applications, since a single unsafe decision in settings such as dosage selection or autonomous driving can have catastrophic consequences. A common way to model safety in bandit problems is to associate each action with both a reward signal and a cost signal, and to optimize reward subject to constraints on cost. Most existing safety-constrained bandit models enforce safety by requiring the expected cost of each action to remain below a prescribed threshold. However, this may be insufficient in heteroscedastic settings, where the chosen action affects not only the expected reward and cost, but also the variability of the observed outcomes. We study contextual bandits with one-dimensional continuous actions and stage-wise high-probability constraints on the realized cost. We propose High-Probability Constrained UCB, an optimistic-pessimistic algorithm that explores for reward while conservatively estimating the safe action set. For linear reward and cost models, we prove a tight $\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(d\sqrt{T})$ regret bound, and we extend the analysis to general function classes using the eluder dimension. Experiments show that enforcing realized-cost safety substantially reduces violations compared with expected-cost constrained baselines.

</details>


### [131] [Beyond Client Averaging: A Client-Independent Second-Order Stationary-Bias Component in Stochastic SCAFFOLD](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26765)
*Yi-Ping Tang,Guan-Ju Peng*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Identifies the first client-independent bias term in full-participation stochastic Scaffold with fixed local steps H; shows client averaging reduces the leading O(γ/N) bias but leaves a nonzero O(γ^2) term when the objective is nonquadratic; coefficient depends on third derivative and gradient noise; result holds for 1D homogeneous setting and is quadratic-objective degenerate.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address a gap in constant-step-size analysis for federated optimization: quantify the exact client-independent bias coefficient and explain how averaging interacts with local dynamics and gradient noise.

Method: One-dimensional, fully participatory (N clients) federated setting with fixed local-step count H and bounded additive gradient noise. Derive a uniform-in-N expansion of Eπ_{γ,N,H}[x]−x* for small γ, showing the leading γ/N bias and the next-order γ^2 term, plus a remainder O_H(γ^2/N+γ^3). The coefficient includes f'''(x*) and f''(x*).

Result: The stationary bias is: E[x]−x* = −(f'''(x*) σ^2)/(4 f''(x*)^2) · γ/N − (f'''(x*) σ^2)/(12 f''(x*)) · ((H−1)(5H−1)/H) · γ^2 + O_H( γ^2/N + γ^3 ). Hence client averaging suppresses the leading O(γ/N) bias but does not remove the client-independent O(γ^2) component when its coefficient is nonzero. The γ^2 coefficient vanishes for quadratic objectives; mechanism arises from gradient-noise-induced local second-moment corrections that nonquadratic curvature converts into stationary bias.

Conclusion: Client averaging reduces the leading γ/N bias but cannot eliminate the γ^2 bias in general; the γ^2 term is client-independent and persists unless the objective is quadratic. The mechanism is indirect, via second-moment effects within rounds. Results are supported numerically and restricted to the 1D homogeneous fixed-H setting; generalization to higher dimensions, heterogeneous clients, or varying H remains open.

Abstract: Existing constant-step analysis of stochastic \Scaf{} identifies a leading $O(γ/N)$ stationary mean bias and shows that higher-order bias can persist as the client count increases, but does not identify the first client-independent contribution at coefficient level. For full-participation stochastic \Scaf{} with one-dimensional homogeneous clients, fixed local-step count $H$, and bounded additive gradient noise, we prove, uniformly over $N\ge2$,
  $$ \begin{aligned} \mathbb{E}_{π_{γ,N,H}}[x]-x^\star ={}& -\frac{f'''(x^\star)σ^2}{4f''(x^\star)^2}\fracγ{N}\\ &- \frac{f'''(x^\star)σ^2}{12f''(x^\star)} \frac{(H-1)(5H-1)}{H}γ^2 +O_H\!\left(\frac{γ^2}{N}+γ^3\right). \end{aligned} $$
  Hence client averaging suppresses the leading $O(γ/N)$ bias but does not remove the client-independent $O(γ^2)$ component when its coefficient is nonzero.
  The mechanism is indirect: although the direct control contribution cancels pathwise in the linear global average, the controls still alter within-round local trajectories and their second moments. Fresh gradient noise and persistent control fluctuations therefore generate local second-moment corrections that nonquadratic curvature converts into stationary mean bias. The coefficient vanishes for quadratic objectives. Numerical experiments are consistent with the predicted coefficient, its persistence as client count increases, and the stated joint remainder. The result is restricted to the one-dimensional homogeneous fixed-$H$ setting.

</details>


### [132] [On the Indistinguishability of Human v/s AI Generated Text](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26797)
*Jaee Ponde,Aritra Das,Mihir More,Debayan Gupta*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Repeated paraphrasing of machine outputs using human samples can steer the machine distribution toward the human distribution, with explicit convergence rates and finite-sample scaling depending on human data and paraphrase rounds.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: With the rapid rise of LLMs, distinguishing AI-generated text from human writing is increasingly challenging, especially when paraphrasers are used. Understanding how access to human writing samples affects paraphrase alignment helps in detection, robustness, and policy design.

Method: Analytical study in a multi-sample setting: compare human and machine responses to the same prompts. Under simple mixing and stability assumptions, prove that iterative paraphrasing moves the machine distribution toward the empirical human distribution. derive an explicit convergence rate, extend to finite-sample scenarios, and characterize how the required number of human samples and paraphrasing rounds scale with the target error.

Result: Proves that repeated paraphrasing converges the machine-generated distribution toward the empirical human distribution under the stated conditions. Provides an explicit convergence rate, finite-sample extension, and scaling laws for sample complexity and number of paraphrasing rounds.

Conclusion: Strategic use of human-writing data and paraphrasing can align machine outputs with human-like distributions under clear conditions, yielding theoretical guarantees on convergence and informing practical limits and needs for detection, paraphasing defense, and data collection.

Abstract: The rapid improvement of LLMs has made distinguishing AI-generated text from human writing a pressing problem. This challenge is further amplified by paraphrasing tools designed to make machine-generated text appear more "human". We study how access to human writing samples can be used to strategically paraphrase machine-generated responses toward the human distribution. Under a multi-sample setting with human and machine responses to the same prompts, we show that repeated paraphrasing moves the machine distribution toward the empirical human distribution under simple mixing and stability conditions. Our results derive an explicit convergence rate, extend the analysis to a finite-sample setting, and characterize how the required number of human samples and paraphrasing rounds scale with the desired error.

</details>


### [133] [SAGE: Variate-Wise Semantic Augmentation for Vision-Language Time Series Forecasting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26829)
*Haizhao Fan,Xinyi Le*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A CLIP-based multimodal time series forecasting framework (SAGE) that leverages frequency-aware textual representations and variable-specific prompts, with a frozen vision encoder trained via contrastive objectives, to achieve state-of-the-art accuracy without involving large language models in the forecasting loop.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Time series forecasting lacks explicit semantic knowledge of variables and their dynamics. Existing approaches either incur heavy LLM inference costs or apply uniform, dataset-level prompts that ignore per-variable semantics.

Method: SAGE is an end-to-end framework that uses CLIP to jointly model temporal, cross-variable, textual, and visual information. The CLIP text encoder processes frequency-enhanced patches and variable tokens; gated residual connections inject variable-specific descriptions and statistical descriptors. A frozen CLIP vision encoder aligns rendered series with temporal representations through a training-only contrastive objective. No LLM sits in the forecasting loop.

Result: SAGE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy across eight long-term benchmarks and the M4 dataset. Ablation studies show complementary gains from multimodal alignment and variable-level knowledge.

Conclusion: Integrating multimodal semantic supervision via CLIP at both text and vision branches, with variable-aware prompts and residuals, yields significant accuracy gains in time series forecasting without relying on expensive LLMs in the loop.

Abstract: Time series forecasting models operate on raw numerical sequences, lacking the semantic knowledge that domain experts implicitly leverage, such as the physical meaning of each variable, its statistical behavior, and its temporal dynamics. Recent efforts to bridge this gap fall into two camps. Some rely on large language models at inference time, which is computationally expensive. Others apply uniform textual prompts at the dataset level, ignoring the heterogeneous semantics across individual variates. We propose SAGE (Seeing and Augmenting with Grounded Encoding), an end-to-end CLIP-based framework that jointly models temporal, cross-variable, textual, and visual information. The CLIP text encoder processes frequency-enhanced patches and variable tokens, while gated residual paths inject variable-specific descriptions and statistical descriptors. In parallel, the frozen CLIP vision encoder aligns rendered series with temporal representations through a training-only contrastive objective. This dual use of CLIP adds complementary semantic and visual supervision without placing an LLM in the forecasting loop. Across eight long-term benchmarks and M4, SAGE achieves state-of-the-art accuracy. Ablations confirm complementary gains from multimodal alignment and variable-level knowledge.

</details>


### [134] [Reinforcement Learning-Based Control of CAV Platoon Joining Maneuvers in Mixed Traffic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26860)
*Biao Yin,Abderrahmane Kasmi,Nadir Farhi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PPO-based DRL framework for CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic achieves high safety and efficiency (≈98% joining, <1% collisions) using risk penalties; external safety controller can further prevent collisions but may reduce efficiency; trade-off between safety, effectiveness, and decision time remains key.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address safe and efficient platoon joining under uncertainty and heterogeneous human-driven vehicle behaviors in mixed traffic; assess DRL algorithms (DQN, DDQN, PPO) within a realistic simulation setting and explore safety guarantees via penalties or an external safety controller.

Method: Agent-based modeling with SUMO to simulate CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic. Evaluate three DRL algorithms (DQN, DDQN, PPO) under different reward structures incorporating risk penalties; optionally use an external safety controller. Metrics include joining success rate, collision rate, and decision steps. Scenarios feature heterogeneity in longitudinal and lateral human-driver behaviors.

Result: PPO outperforms DQN and DDQN, achieving ~98% joining success and collision rate below 1%. Risk penalties in the reward function contribute to safety gains but require more decision steps, indicating a trade-off between safety/efficiency and decision cost. An external safety controller can prevent collisions but may reduce joining efficiency.

Conclusion: Jointly consider safety and efficiency in RL-based CAV platoon joining. Reward shaping with risk penalties and/or a safety shield are effective to ensure safe joining, but they introduce trade-offs in maneuver speed and decision steps. The framework provides a generic platform for evaluating DRL policies in mixed-traffic platooning and can inform design choices for real-world deployment.

Abstract: Connected and automated vehicle (CAV) platooning offers a promising approach to improving road safety and traffic capacity. However, platoon control in real-world traffic is challenging due to uncertainty and heterogeneous driving behaviors. Reinforcement learning (RL) has strong potential for addressing such control problems, but its practical deployment raises challenges related to safety and learning efficiency. This paper proposes a generic modeling and simulation framework for investigating CAV platoon joining maneuvers and comparing deep reinforcement learning (DRL)-based control algorithms. The problem is particularly challenging in mixed-traffic environments, where CAVs coexist with human-driven vehicles exhibiting heterogeneous longitudinal and lateral behaviors. The objective is to achieve safe and efficient joining maneuvers by either incorporating penalties for risky behaviors into the learning process or using an external safety controller to constrain the learned policy. An agent-based modeling framework coupled with the Simulation of Urban MObility (SUMO) simulator is used to evaluate Deep Q-Network (DQN), Double Deep Q-Network (DDQN), and Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO). Results show that PPO outperforms DQN and DDQN, achieving a joining success rate of approximately 98 % and a collision rate below 1 %, largely due to risk-related penalties incorporated into the reward function. However, this improved performance requires more decision steps to complete the maneuver, revealing a trade-off between safety, joining effectiveness, and decision efficiency. An external safety controller effectively prevents collisions, although its interventions may reduce joining efficiency. The results highlight the importance of jointly considering safety and efficiency when designing RL-based controllers for CAV platoon joining in mixed traffic.

</details>


### [135] [When Is the Sharp Covariance Envelope Tight? Feature-Only Geometry for Volume-Sampled Least Squares](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26877)
*Kihun Rhee*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A theoretical study of fixed-size volume sampling, deriving a globally sharp Loewner envelope for conditional coefficient covariance over full-rank pools, and introducing the spectral margin nu_A to characterize attainability and tightness under fixed budgets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To characterize the conditional coefficient covariance under ordinary indexed fixed-size volume sampling with a fixed pool and fixed budget, and to identify sharp spectral conditions that govern attainability, slack bounds, and geometry of the sampling process.

Method: Derives a Loewner envelope for centered coefficient covariance across full-rank pools under fixed-size volume sampling; analyzes residuals, introduces the feature-only margin nu_A, uses residual-augmented change of measure and support-saturation arguments, and examines equal-leverage geometry and lower certificates; employs Frobenius/Gram whitening concepts and conditional fixed-design analysis.

Result: Establishes a globally sharp Loewner envelope for centered coefficient covariance; nu_A > 0 iff the normalized spectral envelope is strict for every compatible residual, and nu_A = 0 iff some compatible residual is spectrally tight; provides slack bounds and attainment directions, and shows the results apply to conditional centered, full-Gram-whitened covariance rather than population generalization; introduces certificates and geometry interpretations.

Conclusion: The work advances understanding of fixed-size volume sampling by linking spectral margins and leverage geometry to sharp covariance envelopes, offering mechanisms for response-aware change of measure and conservative design decisions, while focusing on conditional covariance in fixed designs rather than generalization.

Abstract: Prior analyses by Derezinski and Warmuth established all-size sampling identities, selected-OLS unbiasedness, and inverse moments for ordinary volume sampling, while their exact arbitrary-fixed-response loss and prediction-covariance formulas are at the rank-size endpoint s=d. We establish a Loewner envelope for centered coefficient covariance for every full-rank fixed pool, response, and legal budget d <= s <= m under ordinary indexed fixed-size volume sampling followed by selected unweighted least squares; its coefficient is globally sharp over the full-rank class. Global sharpness does not determine attainability on the pool in hand. Under positive loss, strict-interior budgets, and no coloops, a feature-only margin nu_A gives the exact fixed-design spectral phase: nu_A > 0 if and only if the normalized spectral envelope is strict for every compatible residual, whereas nu_A = 0 if and only if some compatible residual is spectrally tight; the same zero-margin residual is tight at every strict-interior budget. A residual-augmented change of measure supplies the response-aware mechanism and a one-sided quantitative slack bound, while support saturation proves the attainment direction. Critical equal-leverage geometry interprets the boundary, and sound lower certificates yield conservative same-primitive cardinality decisions. Frozen-feature examples show that the certificate is nonvacuous and measure the fixed-pool cost of its authorized reduction. The claims concern conditional centered, full-Gram-whitened coefficient covariance, not population generalization.

</details>


### [136] [Mitigating Strong-Modality Collapse in Multimodal Learning via Inverted Asymmetric Fusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26879)
*Mary Ogbuka Kenneth,Foaad Khosmood,Abbas Edalat*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: IAF preserves the dominant modality in multimodal fusion by not forcing mutual cross-modal attention and strengthening weaker modalities via modality-aware KD; it mitigates strong-modality collapse and outperforms unimodal baselines across text- and audio-vision datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Multimodal fusion can degrade the dominant modality (strong-modality collapse), especially in symmetric types of fusion; need an approach that preserves strong signals while still leveraging weaker modalities.

Method: Introduce Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF): keep the dominant modality path unchanged during fusion; weaker modalities attend to this path as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, apply Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation to strengthen weaker modalities. Evaluate on three datasets with different modality hierarchies; perform pathway isolation analyses to measure modality preservation.

Result: IAF preserves the dominant modality's unimodal accuracy ceiling across configurations; symmetric fusion degrades it (up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE). IAF outperforms the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

Conclusion: IAF effectively mitigates strong-modality collapse and yields robust multimodal performance across datasets with varying modality hierarchies; asymmetric fusion with modality-aware distillation is a promising direction for reliable multimodal learning.

Abstract: Fusing multiple modalities is expected to improve model performance. However, on the MultiHuSE dataset, early, late, and symmetric attention fusion often fail to outperform the best unimodal baseline (text). Pathway isolation of a symmetric attention fusion model reveals that the text-pathway accuracy drops from 74.9% to 56.4% after fusion in one such setting, indicating that the dominant modality can be degraded during integration. We term this strong-modality collapse and argue that it helps explain why some multimodal models fail to surpass unimodal baselines. We propose Inverted Asymmetric Fusion (IAF), which avoids forcing mutual attention across modalities. The dominant modality is preserved by passing through fusion unchanged, while weaker modalities attend to it as a contextual anchor. Before fusion, weaker modalities are strengthened using Modality-Aware Knowledge Distillation. We evaluate IAF on three benchmarks with different modality hierarchies: text-dominant datasets (MultiHuSE, UR-FUNNY) and an audio-visual-dominant dataset (MUStARD). Pathway isolation shows that IAF preserves the dominant modality's internal accuracy at its unimodal ceiling across all tested configurations, whereas symmetric fusion degrades it by up to 18.5% on MultiHuSE. IAF improves over the strongest unimodal baseline by up to 8.25%.

</details>


### [137] [A Layer Importance Metric for Quantization Accounting for the Speed-Quality Trade-off in Autoregressive Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26926)
*Artem Safronov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Composite metric for sLLM quantization that combines information retention (normalized SQNR) and throughput gains (roofline-based latency) to prioritize blocks for acceleration, notably FFN blocks and embedding matrix; evaluated on Gemma 3 1B with ~4% speedup prediction error; outperforms some baselines and makes sLLM quantization more predictable.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accelerate autoregressive small language models on devices with limited memory and compute by intelligent, non-evaluated quantization decisions that balance quality and speed, avoiding the pitfalls of uniform quantization.

Method: Introduce a two-axis composite score: a normalized SQNR-based information-retention score and a normalized speed score from roofline latency modeling. Profile Gemma 3 1B to identify highly expressive blocks (FFN blocks and the embedding matrix) as primary targets. For each candidate, estimate a normalized quality score via simulated quantization and a normalized speed score via roofline-based modeling without executing the model. Combine scores into a composite priority coefficient to trade off speed and quality. Apply metric across blocks, projection sublayers, or transformer layers. Compare against baselines (evolutionary search, specialized accelerators, Shapley-value-based approaches).

Result: The methodology yields ~4% prediction error for accelerated speedup estimates. The approach tends to allocate more resources to the most expressive layers than competing methods, and it requires no actual hardware execution to rank targets, enabling a proactive, engineering-focused quantization process.

Conclusion: An analytical, generalizable metric enables predictable sLLM quantization, allowing practitioners to prioritize blocks or layers to optimize speed-quality trade-offs without expensive search or invasive profiling.

Abstract: Small language models (sLLMs) are nowadays hosted on devices with limited memory and computational budget. In an autoregressive setup, inference is memory-bandwidth bound: uniform quantization is often detrimental to such models, since their architecture has limited redundancies and only a few layers are not very sensitive to lower precision. We propose a composite metric that combines two orthogonal criteria: information retention (measured in terms of a normalized SQNR-based coefficient) and throughput gains (modeled using a roofline-based latency analysis). By profiling Gemma 3 1B, we find that Feed-Forward Network blocks and the embedding matrix are the most promising targets for acceleration. For each candidate, we estimate a normalized quality score based on simulated quantization and a normalized speed score based on roofline modeling with no actual execution needed. We combine the two scores in a composite priority coefficient, allowing us to tune the trade-off between speed and quality as needed. Our metric is general and can be used to prioritize individual blocks, their projection sublayers, or transformer layers as a whole. We evaluate our approach on several model architectures, showing that our estimates have at around 4% prediction error for the accelerated speedup. We find that our method generally allocates more resources to the most expressive layers compared to evolutionary search, specialized accelerators, or Shapley-value-based approaches that require expensive approximate inference. Our analytical approach makes sLLM quantization a predictable engineering task.

</details>


### [138] [Scaling Model-Generated Distillation Data Can Make Latent Teacher Traits More Recoverable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26958)
*Zhichen Dong,Zhixuan Liu,Yuyu Fan,Xiangtian Li,Shuyang Zhang,Chao Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Scaling model-generated data reveals teacher-specific signals in the trained student: larger off-task data amplifies target-trait transfer and can shift behavior toward the intended trait, necessitating trait-aware curation and evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand whether increasing the amount of generated distillation data not only improves coverage but also makes subtle, teacher-induced traits more detectable in the student, even when data appears off-task.

Method: Controlled setup inspired by subliminal learning: a teacher is induced to express a target trait via restricted off-task data (e.g., number-only completions). Students are trained with varying amounts of independent off-task data. Evaluation occurs in a separate domain with matched no-trait controls to isolate target-specific transfer. Analyses include learned LoRA updates and cross-model/trait/multi-trait transfer tests.

Result: Larger independent off-task data sets make the teacher’s induced trait more detectable in the student’s later behavior. This effect is robust across model families, trait types, multi-trait settings, and cross-model transfer. Scaling can amplify the target trait, or shift behavior toward it when competing signals exist; LoRA analyses show parallel trends.

Conclusion: Scaling generated distillation data should be paired with trait-aware curation and evaluation to guard against unintended trait transfer, even when the data appears off-task or benign.

Abstract: Scaling model-generated data is usually viewed as improving distillation: more examples should increase coverage, reduce noise, and produce stronger students. We show a second effect: larger datasets can make subtle teacher-specific signals easier to detect in the trained student, even when examples are off-task and never mention the trait. In a controlled setup inspired by subliminal learning, a teacher induced to express a target trait generates restricted off-task data, such as number-only completions. Students trained on different amounts of independent off-task data are evaluated in a separate domain, with matched no-trait controls isolating target-specific transfer. Our main finding is that larger independent datasets make the teacher's induced trait stand out more clearly in the student's later behavior. Other plausible traits may also strengthen with scale, but the target usually grows more. When the small-scale student already favors the target, scaling mainly amplifies that behavior; when it favors a related or salient alternative, more data can shift behavior toward the intended trait. Analyses of learned LoRA updates show a parallel trend. These effects appear across model families, trait types, multi-trait settings, and cross-model transfer. Our results suggest that scaling generated distillation data should be paired with trait-aware curation and evaluation, even when the data appears off-task or benign.

</details>


### [139] [Gromov-Monge Flow Matching for Equivariant Graph Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26961)
*Moritz Piening,Christian Wald*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces quotient-space aware flow matching for graphs, enabling equivariant couplings via efficient Gromov–Wasserstein-type relaxations; improves sample quality with permutation-equivariant models.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphs are invariant under node permutations; in flow matching, source–target coupling should respect this symmetry, leading to a natural quotient geometry (Wasserstein on the graph quotient space). Exact alignment is intractable, motivating tractable relaxations.

Method: Theoretically show that quotient couplings can be lifted to aligned representatives without extra cost; show that symmetrization yields equivariant flow-matching minimizers, including for categorical endpoints. Practically, build minibatch couplings using GW-type relaxations with inner node alignment bounds, possibly coupled with an outer graph assignment; the training coupling is the only change and remains compatible with permutation-equivariant architectures.

Result: Structure-aware couplings improve sample quality at small integration budgets for continuous graphs and categorical molecular generation; large-scale molecular models remain competitive under standard many-step sampling.

Conclusion: A quotient-space perspective aligns graph symmetry with flow matching, enabling equitable, efficient generation through relaxations that respect permutation invariance and are compatible with existing architectures.

Abstract: Graphs are invariant under node permutations, motivating the use of permutation-equivariant architectures in generative models. In flow matching, however, symmetry may also enter the source--target coupling: once graph pairs are compared up to node relabeling, the natural Wasserstein geometry is that of the graph quotient space. The Euclidean quotient metric of this space coincides with the Gromov--Monge distance, obtained by optimally relabeling the nodes. We develop this perspective theoretically, showing that quotient couplings can be lifted to aligned representatives without additional cost and that symmetrization yields equivariant flow-matching minimizers, including for categorical endpoint prediction. In practice, exact Gromov--Monge alignment is intractable, so we construct minibatch couplings using efficient Gromov--Wasserstein-type relaxations and lower bounds for the inner node alignment, optionally combined with an outer assignment between graphs. The resulting procedure changes only the training coupling and is compatible with standard permutation-equivariant architectures. Across continuous graph and categorical molecular generation, these structure-aware couplings substantially improve sample quality at small integration budgets, while our scaled-up molecular models remain competitive under conventional many-step sampling.

</details>


### [140] [Packora: Systematic Design for Generative Molecular Crystal Structure Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26962)
*Nayoung Kim,Kiyoung Seong,Sungsoo Ahn*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A flow-based generative model (Packora) for molecular crystal structure prediction (CSP) that jointly predicts atomic coordinates and lattice from molecular graphs, supporting multi-component and organometallic crystals with conditioning on conformers, stereochemical labels, and space-group information.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: CSP is critical in pharmaceutical, agrochemical, and organic electronic applications because small conformational/packing differences can drastically affect material properties; a flexible model that jointly predicts structure and lattice and handles complex crystals is needed.

Method: Packora uses a flow-based generative model that ingests molecular graphs and outputs atomic coordinates and lattice. It supports multi-component and organometallic crystals and can condition on subsets of conformers, stereochemical labels, and space-group data within a single model. The design emphasizes cacheable pairwise reasoning, training objectives, numerical solvers, conditioning dropout, and balanced scaling of pairwise and single representations.

Result: Packora outperforms baselines on both structure generation and ranking benchmarks, achieving best matched-budget coverage across six generation benchmarks, with higher experimental-form recovery, lower experimental-form ranks, and faster convergence in ranking.

Conclusion: Packora provides an effective, flexible, and scalable CSP approach that jointly predicts coordinates and lattice, accommodates complex crystals and conditioning, and delivers superior generation and ranking performance.

Abstract: Molecular crystal structure prediction (CSP) is important in pharmaceuticals, agrochemicals, and organic electronics, where subtle differences in molecular conformation and packing can strongly affect material properties. We present Packora, a flow-based generative model for molecular CSP that jointly predicts atomic coordinates and the lattice from molecular graphs. Packora supports multi-component and organometallic crystals and can condition on any subset of molecular conformers, stereochemical labels, and space-group information within a single model. Inspired by the CCDC CSP blind test, we evaluate generation and ranking separately, using generation to isolate generator quality and ranking to measure end-to-end performance under a common relaxation and ranking pipeline. We also systematically study architecture, training, conditioning, inference, and scaling, identifying an effective design based on cacheable pairwise reasoning, training objective and numerical solver choices, conditioning dropout, and balanced scaling of pairwise and single representations. Packora outperforms the baselines on both structure generation and ranking benchmarks, achieving the best matched-budget coverage across all six generation benchmarks, as well as higher experimental-form recovery, lower experimental-form ranks, and faster convergence in ranking.

</details>


### [141] [Adversarial Training Without Input Gradients via Low-Rank Householder Expansions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26963)
*Tiana C. Johnson,Donsub Rim*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: An LRHE-based adversarial training method that avoids inner input-gradient optimization by using a low-rank Householder expansion to identify perturbation directions, enabling cheaper regularization that matches or exceeds three-step PGD performance for small perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce the computational cost of adversarial training by exploiting small-norm adversarial examples arising from input instability and the near-linear regime, and by eliminating the need for inner gradient computations.

Method: Introduce low-rank Householder expansion (LRHE) to linearize the network's composed affine map and extract perturbation directions from activation patterns in the forward pass. Training uses only forward evaluations for perturbation generation and standard backward updates; inner maximization in the min-max formulation is removed entirely.

Result: The regularizer costs roughly 2.8 PGD steps per epoch, about 8.7x cheaper than 40-step PGD on MNIST. The resulting models match 3-step PGD adversarial training for relative l2 budgets ε ≤ 0.02 and 40-step training for ε ≤ 0.012; robustness degrades beyond these due to locality of the expansion.

Conclusion: It is feasible to perform effective adversarial training without input-gradient-based inner maximization by exploiting the local geometry captured by LRHE, yielding substantial computational savings while maintaining robustness for small perturbations.

Abstract: This work concerns adversarial training against the small-norm adversarial examples that arise from the inherent input instability of a trained deep neural network. Examples in this class are small as measured in the relative $\ell^2$-norm, and therefore lie in the neighborhood of the input on which the model acts approximately linearly, the regime in which the perturbation remains imperceptible. We first show that such examples can be computed directly from the trained network parameters, without input gradient iterations, by means of a linearization called the low-rank Householder expansion (LRHE). The expansion describes the composed affine map rather than any individual layer, and the directions it identifies are read from the activation pattern already available in the forward pass. We then propose a simple adversarial training scheme built on this construction. No differentiation with respect to the input is performed at any point: training requires only additional forward evaluations, with weight parameters updated by the standard backward pass, and the inner maximization of the usual min-max formulation is eliminated entirely. That such a regularizer exists is our main finding: the methods that dispense with the inner search all obtain their local geometry by differentiating with respect to the input, and we show this is not necessary. The regularizer costs the equivalent of $2.8$ PGD steps per epoch, an $8.7\times$ reduction relative to 40-step adversarial training on MNIST and below the cost of 3-step training. The resulting models match three-step PGD adversarial training for relative $\ell^2$ budgets $\varepsilon \le 0.02$ and 40-step training for $\varepsilon \le 0.012$, falling away beyond, consistent with the locality of the expansion.

</details>


### [142] [Graph-Based Pseudo-multimodal Contrastive Learning for 12-Lead ECG Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26964)
*Mengyu Wang,Kozo Okada,Takafumi Goto,Natsuko Jinba,Hiroki Yamaya,Kiyoshi Hibi,Tomoki Hamagami*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Graph-CMMC is a self-supervised, graph-based pseudo-multimodal framework for 12-lead ECG that uses Gramian Angular Difference Field (GADF) images to capture complementary information across leads; it aligns waveform and GADF representations and models inter-lead dependencies via a graph module, achieving competitive multi-label coronary occlusion classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing ECG analyses often treat leads independently or operate on 1D signals, failing to capture inter-lead relationships and global waveform patterns crucial for diagnosis. A pseudo-multimodal approach using GADF can provide complementary representations, while a graph model can encode inter-lead dependencies.

Method: Transform ECG leads into both waveform representations and GADF images; construct a 12-lead graph to model inter-lead relationships; employ self-supervised contrastive learning to align the waveform and GADF modalities across leads; use a graph-based relational module to enforce cross-lead structural consistency during learning.

Result: The framework achieves competitive performance against supervised methods on a multi-label coronary artery occlusion task, indicating that GADF is a useful complementary representation and that explicit inter-lead graph modeling improves learned ECG representations.

Conclusion: Graph-CMMC demonstrates the feasibility of pseudo-multimodal, graph-informed learning for 12-lead ECG and motivates further exploration of GADF representations and inter-lead graph structures in ECG analysis.

Abstract: 12-lead electrocardiogram (ECG) is a standard, non-invasive examination widely used for diagnosing coronary artery disease, where clinical interpretation relies on comparing waveform patterns across multiple leads. However, most existing ECG analysis methods focus on single-lead signals or treat each lead independently, and typically process ECG signals as one-dimensional time-series data using CNNs or RNNs. While effective in modeling local waveform changes, such approaches have difficulty capturing inter-lead dependency and global waveform patterns essential for clinical diagnosis.
  To address this limitation, we propose a graph-based pseudo-multimodal contrastive learning framework called Graph-CMMC. ECG waveforms are transformed into Gramian Angular Difference Field (GADF) images to construct complementary representations of the same cardiac activity, enabling a pseudo-multimodal learning setting. Using all 12 leads, Graph-CMMC aligns waveform and GADF representations in a self-supervised manner, while a graph-based relational module is employed to model inter-lead dependency and enforce structural consistency across leads during contrastive learning.
  Experimental results on a multi-label coronary artery occlusion classification task demonstrate that the proposed framework achieves competitive performance compared to supervised learning methods. These results further suggest the effectiveness of using GADF as a complementary representation and incorporating explicit graph-based modeling of inter-lead dependency for learning robust 12-lead ECG representations.

</details>


### [143] [ClusterAttention: A training-free speedup of bidirectional attention](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26965)
*Kasper Nordenram,Amelie Dittmann*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ClusterAttention provides a training-free, block-sparse bidirectional attention mechanism via fast recursive clustering, achieving significant speedups while preserving near-dense accuracy across unstructured inputs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce latency of attention in diverse domains without training or reliance on input structure, enabling single-pass, training-free inference on unstructured data.

Method: Fast recursive clustering per attention head partitions queries/keys into fixed-size clusters (power-of-two). Clusters yield block-sparse attention; compensation for excluded clusters via centroids; theoretical error expressions for sparse attention and centroid-compensated scenarios; integration of compensation into the method.

Result: On TabPFN-3 (tabular data): 2–6x speedups with at least 99% of dense accuracy. On Wan 2.1-14B T2V video generation: 1.8x speedup, closer to dense performance than SVOO (1.4x). No offline calibration needed.

Conclusion: Training-free clustering-based attention yields substantial speedups across unstructured inputs while preserving accuracy; fixed-size, power-of-two clusters align with GPU dense-attention latency and the method includes centroids compensation to control errors.

Abstract: This paper introduces ClusterAttention, a general training-free speedup of bidirectional attention layers. Existing sparse attention methods either rely on structure in the input, such as order in language or spatial proximity in images, or use slow clustering processes amortized over several forward passes. ClusterAttention instead uses a fast recursive clustering method that adapts to the geometry of the keys and queries in each attention head to produce useful clusters. This method allows setting the size of the clusters arbitrarily. We utilize this by setting all clusters to be a fixed size that is a power of two, allowing the block-sparse attention to run at the same latency per query-key interaction as dense attention on GPUs. We also derive an expression for the output error in sparse attention, that explains the counterintuitive experimental finding that tight clusters can lead to larger errors than random clusters. We then derive the error when excluded clusters are compensated through their centroids, and show that this error shrinks with tighter clusters. We integrate this compensation into the method.
  On large-scale tabular data ClusterAttention speeds up TabPFN-3 arXiv:2605.13986 by two to six times, while retaining at least 99% of the dense accuracy. To our knowledge, it is the first training-free method that can be successfully applied in the setting of unstructured input and a single forward pass. For video generation with Wan 2.1-14B T2V arXiv:2503.20314 , ClusterAttention achieves output closer to dense attention and a larger speedup (1.8x versus 1.4x) compared to SVOO arXiv:2603.18636 , a leading method developed specifically for this domain, both run without offline calibration.

</details>


### [144] [TEMPLAR Wales: A georeferenced environmental and toponymic dataset of Welsh settlements](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26970)
*Oktay Karakuş,Can Eyupoglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Georeferenced, reproducible toponymy-environment dataset for Wales linking lexical detections to multi-scale environmental attributes using stable identifiers, released as four relational tables with provenance and licensing; validated for integrity and reproducibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Provide a reproducible framework to study linguistic geography and landscape by separating mapped place names, lexical detections, and environmental measurements, enabling robust analysis without conflating etymology with annotations.

Method: Utilize a frozen registry of 24 Welsh place-name elements to derive 1,350 lexical detections across 1,294 settlements, preserving exact and prefix-token matches with provenance. Link detections to environmental attributes (river/coastal proximity, elevation, terrain context at multiple scales, land cover, woody cover) derived from independent elevation products. Data organized into four relational tables with a field-level data dictionary, source-provenance register, and licensing metadata.

Result: Release of TEMPLAR Wales dataset with documented environmental coverage, deterministic lexical reconstruction, strong agreement between independent terrain sources, and reproducible reconstruction of the frozen release.

Conclusion: Provides a reusable foundation for toponymy, linguistic geography, historical/environmental landscape studies, GIS research, with explicit caveats that lexical detections are not etymologies and environmental measurements are not historical landscape reconstructions.

Abstract: Place names provide persistent records of how landscapes have been described and organised, but their quantitative reuse requires explicit separation between mapped places, lexical annotations and environmental measurements. TEMPLAR Wales is a georeferenced environmental-toponymy dataset comprising 3,757 settlement records across Wales. The resource links a reproducible settlement frame to deterministic lexical screening and settlement-level environmental attributes through stable identifiers. It contains 1,350 lexical detections across 1,294 settlements, generated from a frozen registry of 24 Welsh place-name elements, while retaining exact- and prefix-token matches and their provenance separately. Environmental attributes describe river and coastal proximity, elevation and local terrain context at multiple spatial scales, land cover and neighbourhood woody cover, with parallel terrain measurements derived from independent elevation products. The dataset is distributed as four relational tables accompanied by a field-level data dictionary, source-provenance register and licensing metadata. Technical validation confirms relational integrity, deterministic lexical reconstruction, documented environmental coverage, strong agreement between independent terrain sources and reproducible reconstruction of the frozen release. TEMPLAR Wales provides a reusable foundation for research in toponymy, linguistic geography, historical and environmental landscape studies, GIS and spatial data analysis without treating computational lexical detections as verified etymologies or contemporary environmental measurements as historical landscape reconstructions.

</details>


### [145] [Terrain signatures in Welsh settlement names](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26978)
*Oktay Karakuş,Can Eyupoglu*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Welsh settlement names containing high-terrain elements encode measurable current terrain information beyond broad geography, as shown by elevation offsets and cross-validated models; effects are modest but robust within Wales and limited by residual structure and lack of external replication.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To test whether place names (toponyms) carry present-day environmental signals beyond coarse geographic structure, informing language-environment coupling and toponymy utility for geographic inference.

Method: Large sample (3,757 settlements) in Wales; 24-element lexical framework; preregistered outcome-specific models; geographically structured validation; compare 101 high-terrain vs 139 low-terrain name elements; measure elevation offsets relative to surroundings; replicate with an independent elevation source; assess terrain-name polarity; evaluate impact on held-out prediction error (RMSE) under 10-, 25-, 50-km spatial blocking; assess river- and woodland categories; report limitations including residual spatial structure and lack of external replication.

Result: High-terrain names associated with locations ~24.4 m higher than surroundings (95% CI 10.8–38.1 m; Holm-adjusted p = 0.00137). Positive across 1-, 2-, and 5-km neighborhoods; replicated with independent elevation source (~24.1 m; 95% CI 10.6–37.6). Adding terrain-name polarity to baseline reduced held-out RMSE by 4.63%, 6.22%, 7.30% for 10-, 25-, 50-km blocks respectively, though effects varied by region. River-related names weaker; woodland model non-estimable. Residual spatial structure and lack of independent external replication limit interpretation.

Conclusion: Selected Welsh settlement-name categories retain measurable information about present-day terrain within Wales, without establishing etymology, causal naming, historical memory, or transferability to other naming systems.

Abstract: Landscapes are named, but whether names retain measurable environmental information beyond broad geographic structure is rarely tested. We analysed 3,757 Welsh settlements using a frozen, source-audited 24-element lexical framework, preregistered outcome-specific models and geographically structured validation. The central comparison contrasted 101 settlements carrying high-terrain elements (\textit{bryn} or \textit{mynydd}) with 139 carrying low-terrain elements (\textit{cwm} or \textit{pant}). High-terrain names occupied locations 24.4 m higher relative to their 2-km surroundings (95\% CI, 10.8--38.1 m; Holm-adjusted $p$ = 0.00137). The association remained positive across prespecified 1-, 2- and 5-km neighbourhood definitions and was reproduced using an independently produced elevation source (24.1 m; 95\% CI, 10.6--37.6 m). Adding terrain-name polarity to a non-lexical spatial and settlement baseline reduced geographically held-out mean squared error by 4.63\%, 6.22\% and 7.30\% under 10-, 25- and 50-km spatial blocking, respectively, although improvement varied among held-out regions. River-related names provided weaker, directionally consistent evidence, while the preregistered woodland model was non-estimable. Residual spatial structure, unresolved name language and the absence of independent external replication limit interpretation. Selected Welsh settlement-name categories therefore retain measurable information about present-day terrain within Wales, without establishing individual etymology, causal naming, historical environmental memory or transferability to other naming systems.

</details>


### [146] [Decentralized Multitask Learning over Learned Task Graphs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26989)
*Zirui Wan,Stefan Vlaski*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Decentralized multitask learning with unknown task relationships; propose a two-phase approach: (i) estimate a generalized graph Laplacian from noisy distributed SGD iterates, (ii) use the learned graph to perform cooperative diffusion learning; analyze Laplacian estimation error and its impact on steady-state performance; introduce a topology sensitivity index; simulations show improved cooperation over non-cooperative learning and convergence toward true-graph performance with small stepsize.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In many practical networks, task relationships are unknown and must be inferred from distributed data. Exploiting task correlations via a learned graph can improve multitask performance without requiring a pre-specified structure.

Method: Phase 1: decentralized maximum likelihood estimation of a generalized graph Laplacian using noisy non-cooperative stochastic gradient iterates under a Gaussian Markov random field prior. Phase 2: diffusion-based multitask learning that exploits the learned Laplacian for cooperation. The Laplacian estimation and diffusion steps are analyzed for error propagation.

Result: The paper quantifies the Laplacian estimation error and its propagation to the steady-state performance of the diffusion recursion; introduces a topology sensitivity index to capture network-heterogeneity effects. Simulations corroborate theory, showing that learned-graph cooperation improves performance over non-cooperative learning and approaches the true-graph baseline as the estimation stepsize decreases.

Conclusion: Learning the task graph enables effective decentralized cooperation in multitask learning. The two-phase framework yields performance close to the true-graph benchmark when the Laplacian is accurately estimated (small stepsize), validating the approach and its theoretical analysis.

Abstract: This paper investigates decentralized multitask learning over networks when the underlying task relationships are unknown. While existing graph-regularized multitask frameworks typically assume a known structure, practical settings often require learning inter-task dependencies directly from distributed data. We propose a decentralized two-phase strategy that first estimates a generalized graph Laplacian from noisy non-cooperative stochastic gradient iterates, and subsequently exploits the learned graph to enable cooperative multitask diffusion learning. This framework is motivated by a Gaussian Markov random field prior, which gives rise to a decentralized maximum likelihood estimator for the graph Laplacian. The analysis quantifies the Laplacian estimation error and its propagation to the steady-state performance of the multitask diffusion recursion, and introduces a topology sensitivity index to capture the effect of network heterogeneity. Simulation results corroborate the theoretical findings and demonstrate that cooperation enabled by the learned task graph significantly improves performance over non-cooperative learning, while approaching the true-graph baseline when the estimation stepsize is sufficiently small.

</details>


### [147] [Benchmarking_Fast_Domain_Adaptation_for_Unsupervised_Speech_Units](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26992)
*Robin San Roman,Manel Khentout,Tu Anh Nguyen,Paul Michel,Yossi Adi,Emmanuel Dupoux*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A new ABX-Accent benchmark for accented speech with 10 English accents and a CPC-based baseline using adaptive domain normalization; achieves ~23.6% relative improvement in across-speaker ABX scores.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess robustness of representation learning to out-of-domain accents and feasibility of few-shot adaptation.

Method: Create ABX-Accent benchmark from AESRC with 10 accents; each accent has small unlabeled data (<10h); adapt a pretrained CPC model via adaptive domain normalization; evaluate with ABX metrics per accent.

Result: Avg across-speaker ABX improves by 23.6% relative over non-adapted CPC baselines.

Conclusion: Demonstrates effectiveness of domain-adaptive normalization for few-shot accent adaptation; data/metrics to be released upon acceptance.

Abstract: Representation learning has attracted great atten- tion and managed to reach good performances as a pretraining method for downstream tasks or as a first step towards unsu- pervised speech modeling. Yet, little is known about how such methods deal with out-of-domain speech and how could they be adapted in a few shot to new domains. This is important especially for accented speech where one observes a long tail of accents that diverge from the standard ones. We introduce ABX- Accent, a benchmark based on the AESRC dataset that features 10 different accents of English. It includes a small (< 10 hours) unlabelled training set in each of the accents and adaptations of the Zero Resources Challenge ABX evaluation metrics to each of the accents. We illustrate this benchmark with a baseline model that uses adaptive domain normalization to fine tune a pretrained Contrastive Predictive Coding model on the accents. This method is first developed on LibriSpeech using a male/female split. When applied to the new benchmark, the proposed method yields a relative improvement of 23.6% on across-speaker ABX scores on average compared to non adapted models. The data and metrics will be open sourced upon paper acceptance

</details>


### [148] [Disentangling Optimization Scale from Preference Scale in DPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27032)
*Ivan Kruzhilov*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: β in Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) entangles the inverse preference-noise scale with the optimization dynamics, causing non-monotone policy deviations across β and non-comparable losses; a centered-softplus reformulation decouples these effects and provides a β→0 linear-margin endpoint.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Clarify β's dual role in DPO, reduce hyperparameter sensitivity, and improve stability and cross-β comparability; enable principled learning-rate scheduling.

Method: Show that β couples noise scale and step size in DPO; propose a centered-softplus objective that is argmin-equivalent to DPO for β>0; normalize to separate inverse-noise-scale from learning-rate effects; derive a continuous β→0 endpoint.

Result: Decoupled control over inverse noise-scale and learning-rate effects; β becomes explicit and tunable; the β→0 limit yields a linear preference-margin; improved interpretability and potential for more stable LR scheduling; losses are more comparable across β due to normalization.

Conclusion: The centered-softplus reformulation provides a principled, tunable alternative to DPO that preserves optima for β>0 while clarifying β’s roles, enabling stable optimization and a smooth β→0 endpoint.

Abstract: Direct Preference Optimization (DPO) is a widely used objective for aligning language models from preference data, with the coefficient $β$ commonly interpreted as controlling the KL constraint to a reference policy. We show that $β$ entangles two distinct roles: it governs the effective inverse preference-noise scale and simultaneously rescales the optimization dynamics, coupling this scale with the effective step size. As a consequence, at a fixed learning rate the achieved policy deviation is non-monotone in $β$: it vanishes in a dead zone at small $β$, reaches a peak at an intermediate value, and decreases again for larger $β$. Moreover, standard DPO loss values are not comparable across $β$: runs with nearly identical loss curves can differ several-fold in KL divergence from the reference model. This entanglement obscures the role of $β$, increases sensitivity to hyperparameter choices, and complicates learning-rate scheduling. We propose a centered-softplus reformulation that is argmin-equivalent to DPO for $β>0$, while making the inverse preference-noise-scale and learning-rate effects explicit and independently tunable. The normalized centered-softplus objective also admits a continuous $β\to0$ endpoint that reduces to a linear preference-margin objective.

</details>


### [149] [Performance Foundations of Parallel & Distributed Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27046)
*Maciej Besta,Leonard Schmidt,Lara Nonino,Robert Gerstenberger,Pierre Pang,Patrik Okanovic,Ales Kubicek,Tiancheng Chen,Baraq Lipshitz,Torsten Hoefler*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A comprehensive systematization and compute-centric taxonomy for RL-for-LLMs, focusing on PPO/GRPO and inter-/intra-model parallelism, to enable scalable, fast, and cost-efficient Reasoning Language Models; provides guidelines and open research directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RL-style post-training (RL-for-LLMs) improves reasoning and long-horizon planning but incurs massive compute and tightly coupled pipelines. A compute-centric, scalable framework is needed to make RLMs practical.

Method: Systematically analyze the RL-for-LLM paradigm; compute-centric assessment of PPO, GRPO and variants; develop a taxonomy of intra- and inter-model parallelism (data, tensor, pipeline, sequence, context, expert, plus disaggregated placement, stage fusion, hybrid parallelism, asynchronous execution); employ the work-depth model to formalize insights; examine existing RLM frameworks and distill guidelines and open directions.

Result: Provides a rigorous taxonomy of parallelism strategies for RL-for-LLMs; assesses PPO/GRPO variants through a compute-centric lens; yields practical guidelines for scalable, fast, cost-effective RLM development and highlights open research directions.

Conclusion: RL-for-LLMs can be built to be scalable and cost-efficient through coordinated algorithmic and systems design; the paper offers actionable guidelines and identifies promising directions (e.g., disaggregated placement, stage fusion, asynchronous execution) for future work.

Abstract: Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) and other RL-style post-training paradigms have been used for aligning large language models (LLMs) with reasoning standards. The resulting recent Reasoning Language Models (RLMs) such as DeepSeek-R1, o3, and Kimi k1.5 show that such RL-style post-training ("RL-for-LLMs") can substantially improve chain-of-thought reasoning, long-horizon planning, and self-correction. However, the computational footprint of these systems is massive: state-of-the-art RLM training requires millions of GPU-hours and tightly coupled multi-model pipelines that stress modern hardware far beyond classical supervised LLM training. This makes RLM training as much a parallel and distributed systems problem as an algorithmic one. In this work, to facilitate developing RLMs that are simultaneously high-performance, scalable, and cost-effective, we first systematize the RL-for-LLM paradigm and provide a compute-centric analysis of prominent post-training algorithmic frameworks: Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO), Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO), as well as their variants. Second, we develop a taxonomy of intra- and inter-model parallelism strategies for RL-for-LLMs, covering both traditional techniques (data, tensor, pipeline, sequence, context, and expert parallelism) as well as novel forms of parallelism and optimization techniques for multi-model RLM training, for example disaggregated placement, stage fusion, hybrid parallelism, and asynchronous execution. We harness the work-depth model of parallel computing to make our taxonomy and its insights rigorous and portable. Finally, we analyze existing RLM frameworks and we distill practical guidelines and outline open research directions for building scalable, fast, and cost-effective RLMs.

</details>


### [150] [Soft Active Electromyography Interface for Machine Learning-Enabled Silent Speech Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27048)
*Yuta Kurotaki,Shusuke Yamakoshi,Reitaro Yoshida,Yutaka Isoda,Tamami Takano,Yuji Isano,Yusuke Miyake,Kentaro Kuribayashi,Hiroki Ota*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Soft, hand-worn EMG interface enables word-level silent speech recognition with high accuracy (97.2% on 30-word vocab) using fingertip lip-near signals, LM interconnects, and flexible electronics; demonstrates real-time drone control in private/noisy settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome privacy, attachment, and signal stability issues in conventional SSR by moving to a soft, wearable EMG interface that activates only when needed and can operate in privacy-sensitive environments.

Method: A fingertip EMG electrode placed near the lips captures signals while finger motion is allowed; uses liquid-metal interconnects, transparent flexible PCB electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation for mechanical stability; a deep neural network is trained on these stable signals to classify a 30-word vocabulary across 3 subjects; includes a real-time drone-control demonstration.

Result: Mean accuracy 97.2 ± 1.3% across three subjects for 30-word vocabulary; successful real-time drone control in challenging environments; demonstrates robust linguistic discrimination.

Conclusion: Soft, wearable EMG interfaces are viable, secure HMI for SSI/SSR, enabling high-accuracy word-level recognition with privacy-preserving operation and practical applications.

Abstract: Silent speech recognition (SSR) provides an alternative communication pathway in the absence of audible speech. However, conventional approaches are limited by the need for constant facial attachment, privacy concerns, and unstable signal acquisition. Here, we propose a soft, active electromyography (EMG) interface that enables word-level SSR using machine learning. Worn on the hand, the device uses a fingertip electrode that can be positioned near the lips to acquire EMG signals only when needed. The interface integrates liquid metal (LM) interconnects, transparent flexible printed circuit (FPC) electrodes, and elastomer encapsulation to ensure high mechanical stability during finger motion. A deep neural network trained on these stable signals achieved a mean accuracy of 97.2 $\pm$ 1.3% across three subjects in classifying a 30-word vocabulary, demonstrating robust linguistic discrimination. Furthermore, real-time drone control validates the practicality of this approach in noisy and privacy-sensitive environments where conventional voice recognition fails. This study highlights the potential of soft, wearable EMG systems as secure and intuitive human-machine interfaces.

</details>


### [151] [Unifying Detection and Adaptation in Task-Free Continual Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27070)
*Dezheng Han,Anbang Zhang,Zhihao Zhu,Shuaishuai Guo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: FiUni proposes a Fisher-guided, unified framework for batch-level task detection and parameter-efficient continual adaptation in task-free continual learning for LLMs. It leverages orthogonality of K-FAC-derived Fisher subspaces to decide when to reuse, expand, or create subspaces for LoRA adapters, enabling adaptive knowledge sharing with fewer trainable parameters and competitive performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address catastrophic forgetting in continual learning for large language models under task-free settings, where explicit task boundaries are unavailable; existing methods rely on task delineation and are parameter-inefficient.

Method: FiUni constructs FIM-derived frozen subspaces from a few downstream task samples using K-FAC. It matches the Fisher principal subspace of each incoming batch with historical subspaces to decide whether to reuse, expand, or create a subspace, guiding low-rank adaptation (LoRA). This yields batch-level task detection and dynamic, parameter-efficient adaptation.

Result: Empirical results show FiUni can infer latent batch-level task affiliations and achieves competitive performance against advanced task-aware CL methods while using fewer trainable parameters.

Conclusion: FiUni provides a practical, parameter-efficient solution for task-free continual learning by exploiting Fisher-subspace structure to balance knowledge sharing and isolation through adaptive subspace management and LoRA adaptation.
