Abstract: To mitigate catastrophic forgetting in downstream continual learning (CL) for large language models (LLMs), existing methods typically constrain parameter updates or introduce task-specific adaptation modules. However, these methods often rely on explicit task boundaries during training, limiting their applicability to realistic task-free scenarios. In this paper, we propose a \textbf{Fi}sher-guided \textbf{uni}fied (\textbf{FiUni}) framework for batch-level task detection and parameter-efficient continual adaptation. FiUni is motivated by a key observation about the Fisher information matrix (FIM) of pre-trained models: the orthogonality among the principal subspaces of its Kronecker-Factored Approximate Curvature (K-FAC) approximation, estimated from a small number of downstream task samples, can reflect the similarity between different tasks. Based on this observation, FiUni constructs FIM-derived frozen subspaces to guide low-rank adaptation (LoRA), while matching the Fisher principal subspace of each incoming batch window with historical subspaces. This enables FiUni to adaptively determine whether to reuse existing knowledge, expand a related subspace, or create a new subspace, dynamically balancing knowledge sharing and task isolation. Experiments show that FiUni can effectively infer latent batch-level task affiliations and achieve competitive performance against advanced task-aware CL methods with fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [152] [Emotional Preferences as Goal-Priority Regulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27072)
*Shiqi Liu,Yihua Tan,Hu Fu,Guanyu Qi*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Emergent emotional preferences in multi-objective RL via a hierarchical inner controller + outer preference generator; theoretical guarantees on optimality gap; empirical advantages over fixed/handcrafted preferences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Explain and implement how higher-level goals autonomously regulate lower-level objective priorities through emotion-like signals within reinforcement learning, particularly under changing environments and internal states.

Method: A framework with an inner multi-objective RL controller providing preference-conditioned policies and an outer preference generator that maps state to objective preferences, trained via RL on a high-level goal. The paper derives an upper bound on the optimality gap tied to the inner repertoire's representation error and analyzes the induced policy space. It also provides experiments in self-constructed multi-objective exploration environments.

Result: Derived an upper bound on the optimality gap; the gap vanishes if the optimal policy lies within the span of the preference-conditioned policies. Empirically, the learned preferences show contextual priority switching, graded trade-offs, and temporal persistence, outperforming fixed and handcrafted preferences.

Conclusion: Emergent, state-dependent emotional preferences provide a viable mechanism for dynamic prioritization of competing goals in RL, with theoretical guarantees and empirical advantages, given sufficient expressive inner policies.

Abstract: A core question in decision-making for agents is whether the relative priorities of competing lower-level objectives can be determined by emotional preferences autonomously generated by higher-level goals, rather than being externally prespecified. Under changing external environments and evolving internal states, emotions play an important functional role in regulating the relative priorities of competing goals. Inspired by the goal-directed theory of emotion, this paper studies how such preference regulation can be computationally realized through reinforcement learning. We first propose a conception of emergent emotional preference: a high-level goal autonomously induces state-dependent preferences over competing lower-level objectives. This conception is built upon a framework consisting of a multi-objective reinforcement learning inner controller and an outer preference generator. The inner controller provides a repertoire of preference-conditioned goal-directed behaviors, while the outer preference generator learns a mapping from the current state to objective preferences through reinforcement learning on a high-level goal. We operationalize emotional preference as a state-dependent regulation of relative goal priorities that emerges through optimization. Furthermore, we characterize the policy space induced by preference regulation and derive an upper bound on the optimality gap in terms of the representation error of the inner behavioral repertoire. We show that the gap vanishes when the optimal policy can be represented by the available preference-conditioned policies. Experiments in self-constructed multi-objective exploration environments show that the learned preference function exhibits contextual priority switching, graded trade-offs, and temporal persistence, and outperforms the evaluated fixed-preference and handcrafted-preference strategies.

</details>


### [153] [Tabular Deep Learning for Algorithmic Trading: Cross-Regime Bayesian Optimisation for Equity Signal Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27076)
*Joshua Le Grice*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Regime-informed hyperparameter optimization across five model classes improves out-of-sample robustness in equity trading; a XGBoost+TabNet hybrid achieves high risk-adjusted returns driven by stock selection.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the lack of regime robustness in hyperparameter selection for equity prediction models and evaluate whether regime-aware optimization improves generalization and trading performance.

Method: Train five model classes on daily data from ~300 large-cap US equities over 11 years. Use Bayesian optimization to optimize trading performance across three statistically distinct market regimes. Assess out-of-sample generalization, robustness to input noise, and compare against DL and gradient-boosted trees. Build a Hybrid ensemble by rank-aggregating XGBoost and TabNet. Report annualized return, Sharpe, and CAPM alpha (with p-values); examine beta. Explore involvement of alternative data; deploy interactive app for exploration.

Result: Regime-robust hyperparameter selection improves out-of-sample generalization; signal precision stays above random baseline across all four quarters of the test period. Portfolio performance degrades under simulated input noise only beyond a defined threshold. No individual tabular deep learning architecture outperforms gradient-boosted trees. The Hybrid ensemble (XGBoost + TabNet, rank-aggregated) achieves 51.26% annualized return, Sharpe 2.44, and CAPM alpha 0.423 (p=0.011), with near-zero beta indicating stock-selection-driven outperformance. Alternative data plays a secondary role after accounting for technical and fundamental features and contributes more on the short side; effects vary by model class. An interactive application enables real-time exploration; live data integration remains a next step for deployment.

Conclusion: Regime-robust hyperparameter optimization enhances out-of-sample robustness in equity trading. A Hybrid XGBoost–TabNet ensemble delivers strong, stock-picking–driven performance with favorable risk-adjusted metrics. Practical deployment hinges on integrating live data and providing interactive exploration tools.

Abstract: Algorithmic trading now represents a market exceeding $20 billion, where even marginal gains in signal robustness can translate into economically significant returns. Existing evaluations of equity prediction models do not explicitly target regime robustness during hyperparameter selection. Five model classes are trained on daily observations from approximately 300 large-cap US equities over eleven years, with Bayesian optimisation configured to target trading performance across three statistically different market regimes. Regime-robust hyperparameter selection is associated with out-of-sample generalisation, as signal precision remains above the random baseline across all four quarters of the test period, and portfolio performance slowly degrades under simulated input noise before collapsing beyond a defined threshold. No individual tabular deep learning architecture outperforms gradient-boosted trees, but combining XGBoost and TabNet using rank aggregation produces a Hybrid ensemble with an annualised return of 51.26%, a Sharpe ratio of 2.44, and a statistically significant CAPM alpha of 0.423 (p = 0.011). A near-zero beta indicates this outperformance is driven by stock selection, not market exposure. Alternative data plays a secondary role once technical and fundamental features are accounted for, as well as contributing more strongly on the short side than the long, and varies by model class. An interactive application makes these results explorable in real time, with live data integration the remaining step toward practical deployment.

</details>


### [154] [Cone Extended Rayleigh Quotients for Directed Graph Learning: Minimax Spectral Certificates, Sensitivity, and Adaptive Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27122)
*Yavdat Sh. Il'yasov,Nur F. Valeev*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A framework for training and certifying spectral properties of directed graphs using a two-sided cone Rayleigh approach on generalized pencils, enabling sensitivity analysis and control without symmetry. It yields computable bounds, differentiable surrogates with explicit errors, and shows practical spectral interventions on directed networks (e.g., Cora) with minimal accuracy impact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directed graph learning requires trainable nonsymmetric propagators with distinct left/right spectra. Existing symmetric/cone-preserving assumptions limit spectral control. The paper aims to provide a posteriori bounds, differentiable surrogates, and first-order sensitivity for interventions in a general, non-symmetric setting.

Method: Two-sided cone Rayleigh framework for generalized pencils B_θ - λG. In the positive orthant, computeable lower/upper cone bounds give a posteriori enclosure of a distinguished cone level; use smooth soft-min/max surrogates to preserve one-sided bounds with explicit approximation errors and differentiability in trainable parameters. For an interior level, right/left modes satisfy Dλ_C(B)[H] = v_C^T H u_C; enable first-order optimal graph interventions under perturbation budgets. Demonstrate robustness beyond cone-preserving operators; analyze signed perturbations unveiling transitions to boundary quasi-pairs; show spectral direction carries predictive information beyond symmetrization. Apply to directed Cora: adaptively recompute right–left sensitivity to reduce the spectral level by 21.5% under 0.5% edge-weight budget with no test-accuracy change.

Result: The framework provides practical, a posteriori cone-bound enclosures for a distinguished cone level and differentiable surrogates with explicit errors, enabling first-order sensitivity-based interventions in directed, nonsymmetric graph settings. Empirical results on Cora show meaningful spectral-level reduction with minimal performance impact; perturbation analysis reveals transitions to boundary/quasi-pairs and the importance of edge direction.

Conclusion: The approach yields a practical, certification-friendly method for spectral analysis/control of directed graphs without symmetry or cone preservation, with potential applicability beyond cone-preserving operators. It enables differentiable, budget-bounded interventions and reveals the significance of left–right spectral structure and edge direction in predictive performance.

Abstract: Directed graph learning naturally leads to trainable nonsymmetric propagation operators with distinct right and left spectral structures. Building on the two-sided cone Rayleigh framework for generalized pencils \[ B_θ-λG, \] we develop a learning-oriented methodology for spectral certification, sensitivity analysis, and control without requiring symmetry, nonnegativity, or cone preservation. In the positive-orthant setting, computable lower and upper cone bounds provide an a posteriori enclosure of a distinguished cone level, while smooth soft-min/max surrogates preserve rigorous one-sided bounds with explicit approximation errors and remain differentiable with respect to the trainable parameters.
  For a simple interior level, the right and left modes satisfy \[ Dλ_C(B)[H]=v_C^T H u_C, \] yielding first-order optimal graph-supported interventions under prescribed perturbation budgets and motivating adaptive spectral control. Numerical experiments demonstrate the applicability of the approach beyond cone-preserving operators and in directed learning settings. Signed nonsymmetric perturbations reveal a transition from interior eigenpairs to boundary complementary quasi-pairs, including non-spectral cone levels, while controlled experiments show that symmetrization can remove predictive information carried solely by edge direction.
  On the directed Cora citation network, adaptive recomputation of the right--left sensitivity reduces the distinguished spectral level by approximately $21.5\%$ under a cumulative edge-weight reduction budget of $0.5\%$, with no observed change in test accuracy for the trained model and data split considered.

</details>


### [155] [TRACE-CRC: Trajectory-Adaptive Conformal Risk Control for Multi-Step Channel State Information Prediction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27124)
*Kiarash Rezaei,Mehdi Sattari,Javad Aliakbari,Tommy Svensson,Paolo Monti,Carlos Natalino*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Trajectory-aware conformal risk control (TRACE-CRC) provides calibrated, trajectory-level uncertainty for multi-step CSI prediction by using Frobenius-norm uncertainty balls and learn-then-test risk control, outperforming per-step or naive trajectory methods.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reliable, calibrated uncertainty is essential for downstream decisions (beamforming, scheduling) in multi-step CSI forecasting, where trajectory-level failures can occur even if per-step accuracy is high; current approaches either provide only point predictions or fail to guarantee trajectory coverage.

Method: TRACE-CRC builds Frobenius-norm uncertainty balls around predicted CSI matrices. It integrates future-step-dependent error profiling, trajectory difficulty stratification, and learn-then-test (LTT) risk control to calibrate uncertainty across the entire trajectory rather than per step.

Result: The method achieves reliable trajectory-level coverage with substantially smaller uncertainty balls than conservative multi-step corrections and avoids trajectory undercoverage seen with compact stepwise and adaptive conformal baselines.

Conclusion: TRACE-CRC delivers efficient, trajectory-aware uncertainty quantification for multi-step CSI prediction, enabling more reliable downstream decisions in wireless systems.

Abstract: Reliable prediction of time-varying channel state information (CSI) is essential for efficient wireless communication. Each CSI frame is a matrix-valued representation of the wireless channel response, and a sequence of CSI frames forms a temporal channel trajectory. Modern deep learning-based CSI predictors, however, often provide only point predictions and lack calibrated uncertainty estimates. This limitation is particularly problematic in multi-step CSI prediction, where the target is a sequence of future CSI matrices, and downstream decisions such as beamforming or scheduling may fail if any part of the predicted trajectory is unreliable. We propose trajectory-adaptive calibration and error profiling with conformal risk control (TRACE-CRC), a method for trajectory-aware uncertainty quantification in multi-step CSI prediction. TRACE-CRC constructs Frobenius-norm uncertainty balls around predicted CSI matrices and controls the risk that at least one future frame is uncovered. Instead of calibrating each future step independently, TRACE-CRC combines future-step-dependent error profiling, trajectory difficulty stratification, and learn-then-test (LTT) risk control. Empirically, TRACE-CRC achieves reliable trajectory-level coverage with substantially smaller uncertainty balls than conservative multi-step corrections, while avoiding the trajectory undercoverage of compact stepwise and adaptive conformal baselines.

</details>


### [156] [Ultra Low-Power, Lightweight, Probabilistic RSS-Based Path Reconstruction: A System for Landscape-Scale Bee Tracking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27152)
*Christopher J. Noroozi,Joseph L. Woodgate,Michael Mangan,Michael T. Smith*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Presents a low-power RSS-based AoA localization method for ultra-light receivers using rotating high-gain transmitters and Gaussian-process-based path reconstruction, achieving ~10–15 m accuracy at sub-milliwatt to milliwatt scales over ~300 m, demonstrated on Bombus terrestris tracking.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable localization of extremely small, power-constrained devices (e.g., micro-robots, insects) in environments where GNSS is unavailable or impractical, by reducing measurement count and energy while maintaining accuracy.

Method: Minimal RSS measurements from rotating high-gain transmitters (range ~300 m) to infer AoA via probabilistic modeling; receiver path modeled as a Gaussian process; trajectory reconstructed with doubly stochastic variational inference.

Result: Accuracy ~15 m for 38 mg devices at <180 μW; improves to ~10 m at <600 μW with more RSS measurements; scalable to larger landscapes; validated by tracking Bombus terrestris nest return flights.

Conclusion: The approach offers a practical, ultra-low-power localization solution for tiny sensors in complex landscapes, with potential impact on ecological and IoT/robotics applications; scalability hinges on measurement strategy and robustness to multipath and non-line-of-sight conditions.

Abstract: Applications in fields such as movement ecology, Internet of Things or robotics share the need for systems that localize devices that are too small and power constrained to implement GNSS (Global Navigation Satellite Systems). Alternative low-power localization methods often rely on only measurements of RSS (Received Signal Strength) to infer the AoA (Angle of Arrival) of a transmitted radio frequency signal, but are limited by range and the power demand of the large number of RSS measurements required to infer an accurate AoA. In this paper we address these issues with a novel RSS-based method for tracking ultra lightweight and low-power moving receivers across a complex landscape, achieved by using a minimal number of RSS measurements from simple rotating high-gain transmitters with a range of 300m, and applying probabilistic modelling to infer their AoA. The receiver's movement path is then modelled using a Gaussian process and reconstructed using doubly stochastic variational inference, resulting in approximately 15m accuracy tracking of receivers weighing 38mg (including power source) over a scalable landscape range while consuming less than 180uW, increased to approximately 10m accuracy at less than 600uW by taking more RSS measurements. We anticipate that this method will support fields such as the behavioural study of flying insect species, which we demonstrate by applying the system to track Bombus terrestris nest return flights.

</details>


### [157] [Inductive Correlation Clustering with Graph Neural Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27153)
*Francesco Paolo Nerini,Francesco Bonchi,Arijit Khan,André Panisson*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Proposes Inductive Correlation Clustering via Graph Neural Networks to generalize CC to unseen graphs, achieving large speedups with competitive accuracy and enabling a learnable pooling mechanism for graph classification.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Correlation Clustering (CC) is scalable but inherently transductive and lacks generalization to new graph instances. An inductive CC framework would allow learning from graphs drawn from the same distribution and apply to unseen graphs with lower overhead.

Method: Train a Graph Neural Network to solve CC in an inductive setting, leveraging node features and graph structure to infer clustering on unseen graphs. The framework can be used as a learnable pooling layer for graph classification and demonstrates scalability and generalization across graphs from the same distribution.

Result: Experiments show inference-time speedups up to 5 orders of magnitude, while maintaining an approximation ratio within ~10% of the best baseline CC solutions. The approach is competitive with standard (transductive) CC benchmarks and improves performance when used as a pooling mechanism in GNNs for graph classification.

Conclusion: An effective, scalable inductive CC framework based on GNNs that generalizes to unseen graphs from the same distribution and can serve as a differentiable pooling layer to capture hierarchical graph structure.

Abstract: Correlation Clustering (CC) is a natural formulation of clustering in combinatorial optimization, which uses a graph representation of the input and does not require a pre-specified number of clusters. Given $n$ objects and a pairwise similarity function, the goal is to cluster the objects so that similar objects are put in the same cluster and dissimilar objects are put in different clusters. Despite its versatility, existing CC algorithms suffer from significant scalability issues and are inherently transductive: i.e., the algorithm must be executed from scratch for any new problem instance.
  In this work, we bridge this gap by leveraging Graph Neural Networks (GNNs) to solve Inductive Correlation Clustering, a novel generalization of the CC problem designed to handle unseen graph instances. By learning to exploit common structural patterns and node features during training, our framework generalizes to new graphs drawn from the same distribution with minimal computational overhead with respect to standard algorithms. We demonstrate the effectiveness and scalability of our approach through extensive experiments. Our framework not only excels in the inductive setting, e.g., lowering the inference time up to $5$ order of magnitude, while maintaining an approximation ratio within $~10\%$ of the best baseline solution, but also achieves competitive results on standard (transductive) CC benchmarks. Finally, we showcase a practical application of our framework as a learnable pooling mechanism for graph classification. Our results indicate that our method serves as an efficient pooling layer, enhancing the ability of GNNs to capture hierarchical structural information in networks.

</details>


### [158] [Diffusion Policies for Short-Horizon Planning in Robot Crowd Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27158)
*Wendong Li,Jochen Garcke*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: PDPO uses a diffusion policy to generate short-horizon action chunks for dense crowd navigation, improving success rate via offline pretraining and online PPO fine-tuning, and addresses boundary-violation artifacts in benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current RL methods produce single reactive actions, limiting diverse short-horizon avoidance strategies in dense, dynamic, multimodal human–robot interactions; there is a need for safe, efficient decision-making and methods to handle evaluation artifacts.

Method: Offline-to-online RL with a diffusion policy that denoises to produce five-step action chunks; pretrain on collision-avoidance demonstrations, then online PPO fine-tuning by treating denoising as internal decision process; execution uses a receding-horizon five-step chunk; introduces a benchmark variant where boundary violations are treated as collisions.

Result: PDPO achieves higher success rate than strong baselines; ablations show action chunks and the bounded benchmark setting contribute to performance.

Conclusion: Diffusion-based short-horizon action chunks with offline pretraining and PPO fine-tuning improve crowd navigation performance and mitigate evaluation artifacts; action chunking is especially beneficial in bounded benchmarks.

Abstract: Robot crowd navigation requires safe and efficient decision-making under dense, dynamic, and multimodal human--robot interactions. Existing reinforcement-learning methods typically output a single reactive action at each timestep, which limits their ability to represent diverse short-term avoidance strategies. We propose Planning Diffusion Policy Optimization (PDPO), an offline-to-online reinforcement-learning framework that uses a diffusion policy to generate short-horizon action chunks for crowd navigation. PDPO is first pretrained on collision-avoidance demonstrations and then fine-tuned online with PPO by treating the denoising process as an internal decision process. During execution, the policy generates a five-step action chunk and applies it in a receding-horizon manner. Furthermore, we observe an evaluation artifact in common crowd-navigation benchmarks: without explicit boundary constraints, learned agents may leave the valid domain and bypass dense crowds. To address this, we introduce a setting in which boundary violations are treated as collisions. Experiments show that PDPO obtains an improved success rate over strong baselines, and ablations demonstrate that action chunks are especially important for the modified bounded benchmark.

</details>


### [159] [TraceBench: Controlled Evaluation of LLM Agents for Time-Series Root-Cause Attribution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27182)
*Tommaso Bendinelli,Artur Dox,Christian Holz*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: TraceBench is a simulation-based framework to create controlled root-cause attribution tasks for time-series from dynamical systems and to systematically evaluate LLM agents’ anomaly detection/root-cause performance; findings indicate domain context helps, numeric outputs are favored over visualizations, and code-generation (Python mapping) underperforms direct predictions; results released via a leaderboard and dataset.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a lack of systematic, controlled evaluation of LLMs on time-series anomaly detection and root-cause analysis in dynamical systems, hindering understanding of their capabilities and failure modes.

Method: Three interpretable mechanical system simulators generate time-series data. In each task, an agent must decide whether a parameter was altered and which one. Four LLM agents are evaluated under controlled conditions. Metrics and trajectories are collected, with a leaderboard and datasets released.

Result: Domain context significantly improves performance; agents predominantly rely on numerical console output rather than visualizations; predicting root-causes directly outperforms generating Python scripts that map samples to labels; four agents evaluated and insights gained.

Conclusion: TraceBench provides a controlled framework for evaluating LLMs on dynamic-system time-series reasoning. Domain knowledge aids analysis; direct predictions outperform code-generation tasks; the work yields datasets and benchmarking resources for future research.

Abstract: LLM agents are increasingly applied to anomaly detection and root-cause analysis in time-series observations collected from real-world systems; however, their performance on these tasks has not been systematically evaluated under controlled conditions. We introduce TraceBench, a simulation-based framework for generating controlled root-cause attribution tasks. In each generated task, an agent receives time-series observations produced by simulating a physical dynamical system and must determine whether a system parameter was altered during the simulation and, if so, which one. Using TraceBench, we generate tasks from three interpretable mechanical systems and systematically evaluate four LLM agents across controlled experimental conditions, yielding new insights into how these agents analyze time-series observations from dynamical systems. Our results show that agents benefit substantially from domain context and explore data primarily through numerical console output rather than visualizations. We also find that agents generally perform worse when required to produce a Python script that maps each time-series sample to a predicted root-cause label than when they submit predictions directly. We release our datasets, agent trajectories, experimental results, and a leaderboard on our website, tracebench.github.io.

</details>


### [160] [When Interference Graphs Evolve: Doubly Robust Estimation of Dynamic Peer Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27187)
*Xiaojing Du*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces a controlled-contrast framework for dynamic networks and a Dynamic Network Doubly Robust estimator (DynaNet-DR) that yields consistent estimates if either the outcome model or propensity model is correct under a set of causal assumptions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To consistently estimate peer effects when interaction graphs evolve over time, where pre-assignment history, dynamic exposure, and post-assignment network changes each have distinct causal roles and confounding.

Method: Define potential outcomes indexed by own treatment, temporally aggregated peer exposure, and post-assignment network evolution; construct DynaNet-DR combining a temporally factorized propensity with normalized augmentation; implement with representative-score prediction, fixed clipping, and finite-sample stabilization.

Result: Semi-synthetic benchmarks on fixed real temporal graph sequences show favorable estimation accuracy for the proposed method relative to competitors that target the full exposure profile; benchmarks focus on summary-indexed contrasts rather than edge-counterfactuals; MathOverflow study offers observational illustration under the stated assumptions.

Conclusion: The framework enables multiple controlled contrasts (own-treatment, peer-exposure, network-evolution, and joint effects) with a robust DR estimator, with practical implementation to improve finite-sample performance.

Abstract: Peer effects are difficult to estimate when interaction graphs evolve because pre-assignment network history, dynamic peer exposure, and post-assignment network change have distinct causal roles. We introduce a controlled contrast framework that indexes potential outcomes by own treatment, temporally aggregated peer exposure, and a post-assignment evolution summary. Differences between the resulting means define own-treatment, peer-exposure, controlled network-evolution, and joint controlled contrasts rather than a mediation decomposition. We develop the Dynamic Network Doubly Robust estimator, DynaNet-DR, which combines a temporally factorized propensity with normalized augmentation. Under consistency, summary sufficiency, sequential exchangeability, positivity, nuisance convergence, and weak dependence, its canonical estimator is consistent when either the outcome regression or the propensity estimator is consistent. The reported implementation adds representative-score prediction, fixed clipping, and finite-sample stabilization. Semi-synthetic benchmarks on fixed real temporal graph sequences show favorable estimation accuracy among methods targeting the full profile. These benchmarks assess summary-indexed contrasts rather than counterfactual edge generation, and the MathOverflow study is an observational illustration under the stated assumptions.

</details>


### [161] [Common Geodesics Do Not Guarantee Fisher Consistency of the Structured SVM: Minimal Counterexamples and a Tree-Metric Classification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27203)
*Jintao Fei,Jiangying Luo*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Common-geodesic metric condition is not sufficient for argmax consistency of the canonical coordinate-wise SVM decoder. Complete classification shows argmax consistency iff the output-tree is a path; branching trees fail on boundary distributions, though full-support consistency holds. Five outputs are necessary for a full-counterexample under the common-geodesic condition; K_{2,3} is the smallest in an infinite family; a 3D Hamming cube also yields a full-support counterexample. All results have primal–dual certificates, revealing a decoder-gap between calibrated embeddings and argmax links on surrogate minimizers.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate Fisher consistency (Bayes consistency) of structured SVM with argmax decoding, clarifying the role of the common-geodesic condition and the geometry of the output space.

Method: Theoretical analysis using metric geometry and polyhedral methods; construction of explicit counterexamples on star units, trees, complete bipartite graphs K_{m,n}, and the 3D Hamming cube; derivation of primal–dual certificates to certify optimality.

Result: The common-geodesic condition is not sufficient for argmax consistency. Argmax consistency holds iff the output-space metric corresponds to a path tree. On branching trees, failures occur only on boundary distributions; full-support distributions preserve the argmax property for any tree. Five outputs are necessary and sufficient for a full-support counterexample under the common-geodesic condition; K_{2,3} is the smallest in an infinite K_{m,n} family. A full-support counterexample exists for the 3D Hamming cube. All optimality claims come with exact primal–dual certificates. The results reveal a decoder gap: embedding can guarantee a calibrated link without ensuring the argmax link is valid on every surrogate-risk minimizer.

Conclusion: These findings refine the criteria for Fisher consistency of structured SVMs by showing the insufficiency of the common-geodesic condition, and by providing a complete classification: only path-structured output spaces ensure argmax consistency. They also reveal practical implications for decoder design: calibration alone does not guarantee correct argmax correspondences, highlighting the need to verify argmax properties for each surrogate minimizer.

Abstract: A known necessary condition for Fisher consistency of the structured support vector machine requires the task loss to be a metric for which every output triple has a common geodesic point. We show that this condition is not sufficient for the canonical coordinate-wise argmax decoder. A four-output unit star admits an exactly optimal score vector whose maximizers are all strictly non-Bayes, and four outputs are minimal among metrics satisfying the condition. We then completely classify positively weighted tree metrics whose vertex set is the output space: argmax consistency holds if and only if the tree is a path. The failure on branching trees is confined to boundary distributions; every tree retains the argmax property at every full-support distribution. Among metrics satisfying the common-geodesic condition, five outputs are necessary and sufficient for a full-support counterexample; $K_{2,3}$ is the smallest member of an infinite $K_{m,n}$ family. We additionally give a full-support counterexample for the three-dimensional Hamming cube. All optimality claims have exact primal-dual certificates. The counterexamples expose a concrete decoder gap: in this polyhedral setting, an embedding can guarantee the existence of a calibrated link without validating a prescribed argmax link on every surrogate-risk minimizer.

</details>


### [162] [Profit based evaluation of machine learning for nitrogen recommendations in winter wheat](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27205)
*Xulong Wang,Po Yang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: ML underperforms as a replacement for nitrogen rate advice; profit-focused post-model corrections to standard practice recover profits; a damped correction plus standard advice yields robust performance; emissions pricing can be integrated; overall, ML is best used as a profit-aware correction rather than a replacement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To align nitrogen advisory systems with actual profitability under price and weather uncertainty, highlighting that predictive accuracy does not guarantee profit gains.

Method: Construct a bench using 892 yield-response curves from two long-running UK experiments. Sweep nitrogen-to-grain price ratios to cover all price scenarios. Compare machine learning models against standard UK advice. Apply profit-based corrections after model outputs, including a 'frozen' correction tested across sites. Extend analysis to include emissions pricing and evaluate profit impact.

Result: No model recovers the best N rate within farm tolerance; at normal prices, ML models lose to standard advice on profit. A simple correction after modeling reduces profit losses by ~25%; a frozen correction reduces losses by ~43% at the second site without retraining. A hybrid of standard advice plus a damped correction removes bias and mitigates rare large losses. Emissions pricing is feasible with costs similar to current carbon prices. Overall, ML pays as a profit-scored correction to standard advice rather than a replacement.

Conclusion: ML should be used as a profit-aware correction to standard nitrogen advice rather than as a replacement. The evaluation framework demonstrates practical profitability gains through correction strategies and highlights the potential to incorporate emission pricing.

Abstract: Nitrogen rates for winter wheat are set before the season, under unknown prices and weather. The standard UK advice does not respond to prices, yet recent price swings moved the most profitable rate by tens of kilograms per hectare. Machine learning is often proposed as the fix. However, it is usually judged on prediction accuracy, and accurate prediction does not by itself make the recommended rate more profitable. Our insight is to score nitrogen advice directly by the profit it forgoes on measured yield response curves. We build a test bench on 892 such curves from two long running UK experiments, and sweep the nitrogen to grain price ratio to cover all price scenarios. On this bench, machine learning fails as a predictor. No model recovers the best rate within farm tolerance, and the benchmark noise shows none can. At normal prices, every model also loses to the standard advice on profit. The gain sits elsewhere. A simple correction step applied after the model cuts profit losses by a quarter, while better models and extra features give no gain. The same frozen correction cuts losses by 43% at the second site without any retraining. A hybrid of standard advice plus a damped correction removes bias and trims rare large losses. The same price sweep also prices emission cuts, at a cost comparable to current carbon prices. Machine learning therefore pays as a profit scored correction to standard advice, not as its replacement.

</details>


### [163] [HALO: A Heterogeneity-Aware Language-Aligned IMU Foundation Model for Open-Set Human Activity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27233)
*Zihan Ding,Liyu Zhang,Xiaomin Ouyang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: HALO is a heterogeneity-aware IMU foundation model for HAR with a two-stage training pipeline that handles sensing heterogeneity and open-set generalization, achieving strong cross-dataset performance with ~35M params.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build a unified HAR model that generalizes across devices, sampling rates, sensor placements, and unseen label vocabularies, addressing sensing heterogeneity and open-set generalization.

Method: Stage 1: heterogeneity-aware self-supervised pretraining with adaptive-pooling tokenization, channel-independent features, and contextualized sensor conditioning using natural-language descriptions. Stage 2: synonym-aware soft contrastive learning to align IMU embeddings with text embeddings, enabling cosine-similarity open-set retrieval without per-dataset classifiers.

Result: Trained on 10 HAR datasets and evaluated on 7 held-out datasets; HALO outperforms five SOTA baselines on all 8 aggregate metrics; leads on 3 of 4 baseline-matched input settings; ~35M parameters (10x smaller than MOMENT 341.2M); improves zero-shot open-set accuracy across 87 labels by 13.7 percentage points. On two severely shifted datasets, all models collapse in zero-shot.

Conclusion: HALO demonstrates effective cross-domain HAR generalization with a compact model via heterogeneity-aware pretraining and text-alignment, enabling open-set recognition without per-dataset classifiers, though extreme distribution shifts can still break zero-shot performance.

Abstract: Human Activity Recognition (HAR) using inertial measurement units (IMUs) enables a wide range of applications, yet the field still lacks a unified model that can generalize across diverse subjects, devices, and activities. Training such a model is difficult due to two key challenges: sensing heterogeneity -- differences in sampling rates, channel configurations, and sensor placements -- and poor generalization to unseen activities and label vocabularies. We introduce HALO (Heterogeneity-Aware Language-aligned Open-set model), a domain-specific IMU foundation model that addresses both challenges through a two-stage training framework. Stage 1 pretrains the IMU encoder with heterogeneity-aware self-supervised learning, including adaptive-pooling tokenization, channel-independent feature extraction, and contextualized sensor conditioning that injects natural-language sensor descriptions into each channel embedding. Stage 2 aligns this IMU encoder with text embeddings via synonym-aware soft contrastive learning, enabling open-set recognition via cosine-similarity retrieval without per-dataset classifiers. Trained on 10 public HAR datasets and evaluated on 7 held-out datasets, HALO outperforms five state-of-the-art baselines on all 8 aggregate metrics, and still leads on 3 of 4 settings under baseline-matched inputs. Despite using only ~35M trainable parameters -- 10x fewer than the latest foundation model MOMENT (341.2M) -- HALO improves zero-shot open-set accuracy, measured over all 87 training labels, by 13.7 percentage points. On two further datasets with severe distribution shift, every model including HALO collapses zero-shot. A video demonstration of HALO's performance in real world is available at https://youtu.be/rooVKragtFU

</details>


### [164] [Importance Scoring of Transformer Attention Heads in Learning Tabular Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27241)
*Ahmad Jad Allah,Kazi F. Akhter,Md. Kamrozzaman Bhuiyan,Manar D. Samad*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Introduces an importance-scoring metric to interpret multi-head transformers on tabular data; finds robustness to dropping low-importance heads and diverse distribution of important heads across layers; enables pruning and efficiency, with code released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tabular data remains underexplored for transformer interpretability; understanding per-head contributions can improve efficiency and reliability and reveal redundancy.

Method: Compute a per-head importance score across six attention layers for 40 tabular datasets; perform ablation by removing heads in order of increasing importance; assess impact on classification performance; analyze distribution of important heads across layers and datasets; provide publicly available code.

Result: Low-importance heads can be removed with minimal performance loss in many cases (72.5% of examples); removing the most important head first yields the steepest performance drop; important heads are scattered across layers with no consistent layer-specific pattern; importance varies across datasets, indicating dataset-specific head utility; supports potential for efficiency gains via pruning; code for measuring head importance released.

Conclusion: Per-head importance scoring can guide pruning and efficiency improvements for tabular-data transformers; head contributions are dataset-dependent and distributed across layers, underscoring the need for data-aware interpretation; authors provide accessible tooling to measure head importance.

Abstract: Computationally demanding and opaque deep learning models can be better understood and optimized by analyzing how they transform data. While deep transformers have been widely studied in computer vision and natural language processing, their application in tabular data remains relatively underexplored. This paper presents one of the first applications of an importance-scoring metric to interpret multi-head transformer models in learning from tabular data. Experiments conducted on 40 diverse tabular datasets demonstrate robustness to head drops based on the proposed head importance score. In 72.5\% of experimental examples, the model remains most resilient to performance drops when heads with the lowest importance scores are gradually removed. In contrast, removing the most important attention head first results in the greatest reduction in classification performance. A closer look at individual head importance scores across six attention layers reveals that important heads are scattered across layers, with no consistent layer-specific trends. In contrast to the image and language domains, the importance of individual attention heads varies considerably across tabular datasets with different schemas and feature spaces. The proposed importance score can improve efficiency and redundancy within transformer architectures. We make the source code for measuring the importance of individual attention heads publicly available.

</details>


### [165] [Circuit Condensation: Post-Training that Concentrates a Behavior's Causal Circuit](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27254)
*Sai Adith Senthil Kumar*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A post-training method (Circuit Condensation) compresses mechanistic interpretation circuits by pruning low-attribution edges and training a low-rank adapter, producing substantially smaller, performance-preserving circuits across models and tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mechanistic interpretability via circuit diagrams is hampered by large, overlapping edge sets (frozen discoveries). A compact, verifiable sub-circuit would aid inspection, comparison, and verification of model behavior.

Method: Iteratively prune edges with low attribution and train a low-rank adapter to reproduce original behavior; retain pruning only if task performance and general capabilities survive. Evaluate across four behaviors and eight models, comparing to frozen baselines.

Result: Condensed circuits are smaller than the strongest frozen baseline in 30/32 settings, averaging 8.1x smaller and up to 316x. Re-running without weight updates yields larger circuits in 29/32 settings, showing weight updates drive reduction. In subset testing of 19 circuits, 11 cannot be reduced; remaining show removable edges. Pair ablations reveal dependencies between edges (not independent effects). For indirect object identification, condensation isolates 24 heads (17 documented roles) vs 61 heads (36 undocumented) in the frozen circuit, yielding a sufficient sub-circuit of the published mechanism that tracks the original next-token distribution and predicts errors.

Conclusion: Weight-updated condensation yields compact, faithful mechanistic subgraphs that facilitate interpretable, verifiable circuits. It exposes edge dependencies and robust sub-circuits, offering clearer mechanistic insight into model behavior and errors.

Abstract: One approach to mechanistic interpretability explains behavior through circuits: the components and connections that carry it. Frozen discovery often returns hundreds of edges, making them hard to inspect, compare, or verify exhaustively. We introduce Circuit Condensation, which post-trains models to concentrate behaviors into smaller causal graphs. Each round prunes low-attribution edges and trains a low-rank adapter to match the original through what remains, retaining the cut only if task performance and general capability survive. Across four behaviors and eight models, condensed circuits are smaller than the strongest frozen baseline in 30 of 32 settings, by $8.1\times$ on average and up to $316\times$. Repeating the search without weight updates produces larger circuits in 29 of 32 settings, showing that weight updates, rather than search alone, drive the reduction. Testing every subset of 19 circuits finds 11 that cannot be reduced and reveals removable edges in the rest. Pair ablations expose dependencies between edges, showing that their effects cannot be understood independently. On indirect object identification, condensation isolates 24 heads, 17 of them with documented roles, against 61 heads and 36 undocumented ones for the matched frozen circuit: a sufficient sub-circuit of the published mechanism rather than a reconstruction of it. The resulting circuit tracks the original model's next-token distribution and predicts its errors.

</details>


### [166] [Making Latent Evolution Explicit: Operator-Structured Transitions for World Action Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27259)
*Xiaoxiao Lu,Yunlong Dong,Jiahao Shi,Ye Yuan*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: LEON introduces a latent evolution operator for WAMs to model latent transitions via a context-modulated operator and additive forcing, grounded in controlled Koopman theory, improving closed-loop performance and robustness across WAM variants.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Treat transition realization as a distinct architectural choice in latent WAMs, moving beyond token-interaction predictors to model latent evolution structurally.

Method: Latent Evolution Operator Network (LEON) with context-dependent propagation around a shared evolution operator plus additive forcing; grounded in the controlled dynamical systems perspective via the Koopman generator; tested across two WAM formulations with different policy integration.

Result: Improved closed-loop performance and robustness; remains effective under full transition replacement; highlights architectural impact of transition modeling in latent WAMs.

Conclusion: Transition realization is a consequential architectural choice in latent WAMs; LEON offers a viable operator-based mechanism to model latent evolution with complementary propagation and forcing.

Abstract: World Action Models (WAMs) augment robot policies by predicting how task-relevant scene states may evolve under interaction. Recent WAMs increasingly perform such prediction in latent representation spaces, avoiding full appearance-level generation while preserving control-relevant information. Yet latent transitions are commonly realized with Transformer-based predictors whose inductive structure is centered on token interaction rather than temporal evolution. We study transition realization as an architectural choice distinct from predictive representation and prediction-policy coupling. We introduce the Latent Evolution Operator Network (LEON), which models latent evolution in a learned observable space through context-modulated operator-based propagation and additive forcing. Grounded in the controlled Koopman generator view of evolution, LEON organizes context-dependent transition variation around a shared evolution-operator structure while retaining a complementary path for additive change. Controlled dynamical systems verify the resulting evolution-specific inductive bias and the complementary roles of operator propagation and forcing. Across two WAM formulations that integrate latent prediction into the policy differently, LEON improves closed-loop performance and robustness while remaining effective under full transition replacement. These results establish transition realization as a consequential architectural choice in latent WAMs.

</details>


### [167] [MM-Spectrum: Multimodal Multi-spectral Molecular Structural Elucidation with a Stable MoE Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27286)
*Hai-tao Yu,Nan Min,Zheng Fang,Hongyu Zhan,Yusen Tan,Yuhan Wang,Jun Xia*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: MM-Spectrum uses a sparse Mixture-of-Experts with a modality-aware router to handle heterogeneous multimodal spectra, improving structure elucidation under multispectral imbalance and missing modalities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Directly concatenating multispectral sequences causes performance degradation due to pronounced heterogeneity and multimodal imbalance; a tailored architecture is needed to extract modality-unique and cross-modal information while suppressing noise.

Method: A sparsely-activated Mixture-of-Experts framework with modality-aware routing that uses spectral identity alongside token content, plus shared and interaction experts with heterogeneous capacities to capture modality-specific and cross-modal information across full, bimodal, and missing-modality settings.

Result: MM-Spectrum achieves consistent, substantial improvements across full-modality, bimodal, and missing-modality scenarios, with ablation and interpretability analyses supporting its effectiveness.

Conclusion: The modality-aware routing and diversified expert design effectively address multispectral imbalance and missing data, enabling robust, interpretable multispectral structure elucidation.

Abstract: Inferring molecular structures from multimodal spectroscopic measurements requires integrating complementary yet highly heterogeneous signals. However, the common paradigm of directly concatenating multispectral sequences can exhibit anomalous performance degradation, primarily due to pronounced heterogeneity and the resulting multimodal imbalance across modalities. As a remedy, we propose MM-Spectrum, a sparse Mixture-of-Experts framework tailored for multimodal multispectral spectra-to-structure elucidation. To better match the information characteristics under multispectral imbalance, MM-Spectrum introduces an explicit modality-aware routing mechanism that exposes spectral identity to the router in addition to token content representations. Moreover, it incorporates shared and interaction experts, together with heterogeneous expert capacities, to extract multispectral modality-unique and cross-modal synergistic information while suppressing noise-induced interference. Across full-modality, bimodal, and missing-modality settings on molecular structural elucidation, MM-Spectrum achieves consistent and substantial improvements, supported by ablation studies and interpretability analyses.

</details>


### [168] [QuantumBoostNet: A Hybrid Classical-Quantum Architecture for Enhanced Accuracy in Cardiac Ultrasound View Identification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27302)
*Mihai Udrescu-Milosav,Stefan-Alexandru Jura,Mihai Udrescu,Gerhard-Paul Diller*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: A two-head hybrid classical-quantum model (QuantumBoostNet) improves cardiac ultrasound view identification by combining a classical backbone with a 10-qubit quantum circuit head, trained in two stages with an adaptive head-switching mechanism. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods on its target task and on standard benchmarks, with notable robustness to noise.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Medical ultrasound imaging is noisy and specialized; traditional CNNs often underperform on view identification due to data noise and task-specific subtleties. The work proposes a hybrid quantum approach to enhance feature representation and generalization in a high-noise, low-sample regime.

Method: Architectural design: a classical backbone with two heads—one classical and one quantum (parametrized 10-qubit circuit). Training occurs in two stages, using a mixing parameter that adaptively transitions emphasis between heads based on loss dynamics. Evaluation includes cardiac ultrasound view identification and standard image classification benchmarks, with robustness to noise tests.

Result: QuantumBoostNet consistently outperforms leading classical and hybrid models in cardiac ultrasound view identification, achieving a relative improvement over the best competitor. It also shows superior performance on established image classification benchmarks and demonstrates robustness to noise, despite the limited number of qubits that can be simulated.

Conclusion: Hybrid classical-quantum architectures like QuantumBoostNet show promise for specialized medical imaging tasks, justifying further exploration and development of such models in clinical settings.

Abstract: Accurate identification of the correct view or angle in cardiac ultrasound (echocardiogram) is a critical component of cardiologic imaging. This step is essential for precise anatomical interpretation, reliable measurement, and the reduction of clinical errors. Although computer vision has advanced significantly, most state-of-the-art models perform well on standard benchmarks but often yield suboptimal results in specialized medical imaging tasks due to the high level of noise present in the data. QuantumBoostNet, a hybrid classical-quantum architecture, is introduced to address these challenges. This model integrates a classical backbone with two heads: one classical and one quantum, with the quantum head implemented as a parametrized 10-qubit quantum circuit. Training occurs in two stages, with an adaptive transition between heads governed by a mixing parameter that monitors loss dynamics. Extensive experiments indicate that, despite the limited number of qubits that can be simulated, QuantumBoostNet consistently outperforms state-of-the-art classical and hybrid classical-quantum models in cardiac ultrasound view identification, achieving a relative improvement over the best competitor. QuantumBoostNet also demonstrates superior performance on established image classification benchmarks and exhibits robustness to noise. These findings support the continued development of hybrid classical-quantum models for specialized medical imaging applications.

</details>


### [169] [Beyond Parallel Blindness: Information Floors and Model Gaps in Block Drafting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27339)
*Xinwei Qiang,Xiang Fang,Chang Chen,Yue Guan,Yufei Ding*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Separates information floor from model gap in all-parallel token drafting; floor constrains acceptance; realised tokens mitigate floor; current models still exceed the floor, indicating room to improve proposal quality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To disentangle two sources of rejection in all-parallel drafting: missing within-block path information and imperfect modelling of observable information; introduce an information floor (minimum expected rejection given conditioning order) to quantify them.

Method: Estimate both floor and model gap from target rollouts across four domains, four open-weight targets, and a frontier API target; assess per-slot acceptance and the effect of realising tokens; support with mutual-information analysis.

Result: All-parallel floor reaches 0.286 at the final slot on Qwen3-4B; maximum per-slot acceptance limited to 71%. Realising one token removes 86–100% of this floor, a locality also supported by mutual-information analysis. The final-slot model gap accounts for 43–64% of DFlash rejection and 85–92% of DSpark's oracle-conditioned rejection.

Conclusion: The study separates the value of short-range conditioning from proposal quality; despite reductions, current drafters remain above their floors, indicating substantial remaining gap attributable to proposal quality rather than observability alone.

Abstract: Block drafters propose several tokens in one forward pass, before earlier target tokens are realised. Their rejection mixes two losses: missing within-block path information and imperfect modelling of observable information. Accepted length cannot distinguish them. We separate the two with an information floor, the minimum expected rejection at a specified conditioning order; rejection above this floor is the model gap. Estimating both from target rollouts across four domains, four open-weight targets, and a frontier API target yields three findings. First, the all-parallel floor reaches $0.286$ at the final slot on Qwen3-4B, limiting even the best proposal to $71\%$ per-slot acceptance. Second, one realised token removes $86$--$100\%$ of this floor, a locality also recovered by an independent mutual-information analysis. Third, current drafters remain far above their floors: the final-slot model gap accounts for $43$--$64\%$ of DFlash rejection and $85$--$92\%$ of DSpark's oracle-conditioned rejection. These findings separate the value of short-range conditioning from proposal quality.

</details>


### [170] [Understanding Evolution Strategies for LLM Reasoning: Broader Reasoning Coverage than GRPO](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27351)
*Yunpeng Ba,Zhi Zheng,Yue Xie,Jiaqing Li,Xialiang Tong,Tao Zhong,Mingxuan Yuan,Zhichao Lu,Xuyang Wu,Zhenkun Wang*

Main category: cs.LG

TL;DR: Evolution Strategies (ES) offers a distinct, memory-efficient post-training paradigm for LLM reasoning with broader reasoning coverage and higher multi-step success (Pass@K) than Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO). ES shows dynamic, sparse but substantial parameter drift with no catastrophic forgetting and requires smaller population size on larger LLMs. A sequential GRPO-ES strategy can leverage GRPO’s Pass@1 gains and ES’s Pass@K gains. Hyperparameters matter, and ES is not merely a less effective alternative to GRPO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand the optimization dynamics of ES in LLM post-training, quantify its advantages over GRPO, and delineate when ES is preferable, including how hyperparameters and parameter drift relate to performance and stability.

Method: Theoretical analysis of verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity across ES populations; empirical comparisons between ES and GRPO on Pass@1 and Pass@K; analysis of parameter drift and the sparsity of functional changes; development of a sequential GRPO-ES training strategy; evaluation of hyperparameter effects, notably population size relative to LLM scale.

Result: ES yields broader reasoning coverage and higher Pass@K than GRPO; GRPO exhibits entropy collapse while ES maintains or increases Pass@1 and Pass@K; performance gains in ES arise from sparse, large-magnitude updates affecting a subset of parameters; no catastrophic forgetting observed in held-out tests; a sequential GRPO-ES approach benefits both Pass@1 and Pass@K; ES requires smaller populations on larger LLMs.

Conclusion: ES should be treated as a distinct reasoning post-training paradigm with unique dynamics and advantages, supplementing GRPO through a sequential strategy; hyperparameter design, particularly population size, is crucial, and ES can outperform GRPO in broader reasoning tasks while maintaining stability.

Abstract: Evolution Strategies (ES) have recently emerged as a memory-efficient post-training paradigm for LLM reasoning. However, the optimization behavior of ES remains understudied, making it hard to define its advantage scope compared to mainstream post-training paradigms (e.g., Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO)). By systematically investigating ES dynamics and mechanisms, this paper first identifies a performance advantage of ES over GRPO, theoretically and empirically showing that ES can lead to broader reasoning coverage, thereby better exploiting the reasoning capabilities of pretrained LLMs. Theoretically, we show that verifier-projected Jensen-Shannon diversity across the ES population is helpful to higher Pass@K performances. Empirically, unlike GRPO, which exhibits entropy collapse, ES improves Pass@1 while attaining higher Pass@K than GRPO. We further develop a sequential GRPO-ES training strategy that combines GRPO's strength in Pass@1 with ES's gains in Pass@K. Second, we find that despite substantial whole-model parameter drift, the task-performance gains of ES are only contributed to a sparse subset of larger-magnitude updates. This functional sparsity suggests that large parameter movement need not imply widespread functional change, and held-out evaluations further show that it does not necessarily lead to catastrophic forgetting. Finally, we study how hyperparameter design affects the effectiveness of ES, demonstrating that ES requires a smaller population size in a larger LLM. These findings position ES as a distinct reasoning post-training paradigm rather than a less effective, memory-efficient alternative to GRPO.

</details>


<div id='cs.RO'></div>

# cs.RO [[Back]](#toc)

### [171] [WALL-SS: Scaling Long-horizon World Models via Next-Scale Autoregression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26239)
*Maeve Zhang,Rain Sun,Xiang Wang,Cyril Zhang,Shalfun Li,Meng Cao,Howard Lu,Ethan Chen,Harry Jhou,KZ Zheng,Lights Shi,Regis Cheng,Lorenzin,Robert Wang,Victor Yao,Gody Li,Elise Mon,Yohann Tang,Ryan Yu,PS Zhang,Vincent Chen,Hang Su,Roy Gan,Hao Wang,Qian Wang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: WALL-SS is a scale-wise autoregressive world model for action-controllable, long-horizon robotic simulation that supports variable-length generation, streaming, and on-policy alignment to improve action following and reduce long-horizon inconsistency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To build a unified generative world model that links actions to future observations, supports flexible horizons and planning, and enables reward-driven optimization in robotics.

Method: Introduce WALL-SS, a world model that represents trajectories as causal sequences of interleaved observations and actions. It generates futures in a coarse-to-fine manner across three components: (1) action-conditioned next-scale prediction for improved action-future coupling, (2) scale-compressed long-horizon memory with distant observations/actions compressed and scale-wise dream forcing for robustness, and (3) on-policy alignment optimizing autoregressive dynamics with action-following and long-term consistency rewards while preserving the pretrained visual distribution. This yields variable-length generation, streaming extension via reusable causal states, and direct optimization through sequence probabilities.

Result: WALL-SS improves action following and trajectory accuracy, enables minute-long streaming rollouts under bounded memory, and benefits from on-policy alignment to reduce action drift and long-horizon inconsistency.

Conclusion: The proposed scale-wise autoregressive framework provides a coherent, action-conditioned, long-horizon visual world model that supports streaming, memory efficiency, and policy-optimization capabilities for robotic applications.

Abstract: Generative world models provide robots with predictive models of how the world evolves under interaction, with growing potential for simulation, planning, policy evaluation, and robot learning. Beyond clip-level future prediction, a unified generative formulation should relate actions to consequences, support flexible horizons and continuous interaction, and enable reward-driven optimization. We introduce WALL-SS, a world model that generates visual futures through Scale-wise autoregressive Scaling, enabling action-controllable and long-horizon robotic simulation. WALL-SS represents embodied trajectories as causal sequences of temporally interleaved observations and actions, making action-dependent state transitions explicit while naturally supporting variable-length generation, streaming extension through reusable causal states, and direct optimization through sequence probabilities. To make this formulation effective over long horizons, we generate each future observation in a coarse-to-fine manner and develop three complementary components within the same hierarchy. Action-conditioned next-scale prediction injects scale-aligned action representations to improve action-future coupling and model both successful and failed behaviors. Scale-compressed long-horizon memory retains recent interactions at fine resolution while compressing distant observations and actions, with scale-wise dream forcing enhancing robustness to self-generated context. Finally, on-policy alignment optimizes autoregressive visual dynamics with action-following and long-term consistency rewards while preserving the pretrained visual distribution. Experiments show that WALL-SS improves action following and trajectory accuracy, supports coherent minute-long streaming rollout under bounded memory, and consistently benefits from on-policy alignment in reducing action drift and long-horizon inconsistency.

</details>


### [172] [Constraint-Aware Physics-Informed Neural Networks for Static Shape Estimation of Co-Manipulative Continuum Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26273)
*Rana Danesh,Pari Qarehdaghi,Farrokh Janabi-Sharifi*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A constraint-aware PINN improves static shape estimation for tendon-driven co-manipulative continuum robots by enforcing equilibrium and loop-closure constraints, outperforming a purely data-driven ANN in simulation and real experiments with fast inference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static shape estimation of closed-chain CCRs is difficult due to static equilibrium and geometric loop-closure constraints; a physics-informed approach is needed for physically consistent and data-efficient estimation under noise.

Method: A PINN with a projected static equilibrium residual and a configuration-level geometric residual is applied to a tendon-driven CCR using the geometric variable strain formulation. Compared against a data-driven ANN under limited/noisy data; evaluated in simulation and with experimental fine-tuning.

Result: PINN reduces relative configuration error, equilibrium residual, and closed-chain residual by 67.88%, 67.35%, and 88.06% respectively (with 140 samples and 50% label noise). With full data, relative configuration error = 0.1597%, inference time = 0.1773 ms (vs 17.97 s for nonlinear solver). Experimental RMSE decreased from 2.657 mm to 0.497 mm; R2 improved from -0.788 to 0.937.

Conclusion: Physically constrained PINNs enable accurate, physically consistent, and computationally efficient static shape estimation for closed-chain CCRs, outperforming purely data-driven baselines and suitable for real-time applications.

Abstract: Static shape estimation of co-manipulative continuum robots (CCRs) is challenging because the continuum arms and manipulated flexible object form a closed chain that must satisfy both static equilibrium and geometric loop-closure constraints. This paper presents a constraint-aware physics-informed neural network (PINN) for static shape estimation of a tendon-driven CCR modeled using the geometric variable strain formulation. The proposed method incorporates a projected static equilibrium residual and a configuration-level geometric residual to enforce the governing mechanics and closed-chain geometry. In simulation, the PINN is compared with a purely data-driven artificial neural network (ANN) under limited and noisy training data. With 140 samples and 50% label noise, the PINN reduces the relative configuration error, equilibrium residual, and closed-chain residual by 67.88%, 67.35%, and 88.06%, respectively. Using the full dataset, the PINN achieves 0.1597% relative configuration error with an inference time of 0.1773 ms, compared with 17.97 s for an iterative nonlinear solver. Experimental fine-tuning reduces the marker RMSE from 2.657 mm to 0.497 mm and increases R2 from -0.788 to 0.937. These results demonstrate accurate, physically consistent, and computationally efficient static shape estimation of closed-chain CCRs.

</details>


### [173] [Dispersive Forward Tree Search for Optimal Control: Coverage, Complexity, and Computation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26314)
*Shashank A. Deshpande,Jonathan P. How*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Propagation-based kinodynamic planning with finite-sample near-optimal guarantees for differentially flat nonlinear systems via Dispersive Forward Tree search (DFT*); exponential worst-case tree size is mitigated by cost-conditioned pruning to polynomial in horizon; demonstrated on unicycle, trailer car, and quadrotor with parallelizable, real-time capable performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Steering-based boundary-value methods are often inaccessible for nonlinear platforms. Forward-propagation planners lack finite-sample guarantees and underperform in practice. The work aims to provide theory and practical algorithms with provable near-optimality and efficient implementation for a broad class of nonlinear systems.

Method: Develop dispersive forward tree search (DFT*). Construct dispersive command sets for control-affine systems. Prove that a forward tree can contain a near-optimal trajectory within a certified tree size, but covering the trajectory class may require exponential tree size. Introduce cost-conditioned dominance pruning to retain near-optimality with a tree size polynomial in the horizon. Implement BFS-style DFT* and design dispersive samplers for unicycle, trailer car, and quadrotor. Evaluate on challenging planning tasks and demonstrate real-time performance in receding-horizon planning on embedded hardware.

Result: Theoretical guarantees: near-optimal trajectory within certified tree; exponential lower bound on covering all trajectories without pruning, polynomial bound with pruning. Empirical results: DFT* matches or surpasses state-of-the-art kinodynamic planners in solution quality at similar times on embedded hardware; performance improves with parallelization; feasible real-time planning in dynamic environments using receding-horizon loop.

Conclusion: DFT* provides a scalable, near-optimal kinodynamic planner for a broad class of nonlinear systems with practical implementation on embedded hardware; the combination of dispersive command sets, pruning, and BFS enables parallelizable, real-time performance.

Abstract: Steering-based planners require solutions to state-to-state boundary value problems, which can be inaccessible for nonlinear platforms. Forward propagation evades the steering requirement, but the finite-sample behavior of the associated planners remains uncharacterized and their implementations underperform in practice. This paper develops a propagation-based kinodynamic planner with deterministic finite-sample near-optimality guarantees. We work within the large class of differentially flat nonlinear systems and show that a forward tree of locally dispersive control commands contains a near-optimal trajectory at a certified tree size. We provide a general mechanism to construct dispersive command sets for control-affine systems, which are necessary to implement the search algorithm prescribed by the theory. We show that covering the certified trajectory class irrespective of cost provably demands a tree exponentially sized in the problem horizon, and present a cost-conditioned dominance pruning procedure that retains near-optimality at a tree size polynomial in the horizon. We implement the resulting search algorithm, Dispersive Forward Tree search (DFT*), as breadth-first expansion of the forward tree, which maps naturally onto parallel hardware. We design efficient dispersive samplers for the unicycle, the trailer car, and the quadrotor and evaluate challenging planning tasks for these platforms. DFT* delivers consistently competitive and often substantially better solution quality than state-of-the-art kinodynamic planners at comparable solution times on embedded-tier processors, accelerating further as parallel compute is scaled. We also implement DFT* in a receding-horizon loop to demonstrate real-time planning in dynamic environments at embedded-tier compute budgets.

</details>


### [174] [Cross-Platform Benchmark of Neural 3D Reconstruction for Autonomous Laboratory Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26383)
*Yongho Kim,Mengjiao Han,Victor Mateevitsi,Silvio Rizzi,Michael E. Papka,Nicola Ferrier*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A compute-platform benchmark of neural 3D reconstruction for lab robotics, comparing NeRF, 3D Gaussian Splatting, and SAM3D; evaluates real-time viability across devices; GaS yields higher rendering quality but with heavier GPU cost; onboard compute cannot support interactive per-scene optimization; SAM3D provides plausible geometry within seconds but with detail mismatches; supports tiered pipelines combining lightweight real-time reconstruction with heavier offloads.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lab robotics require fast, accurate 3D scene representations within the latency budget of control loops; existing neural 3D reconstruction methods are not well characterized across common compute platforms used in labs.

Method: Systematic benchmark of NeRF and 3D Gaussian Splatting (training and rendering) on GPU-enabled devices ranging from single-board computers to server-class nodes; evaluate SAM3D single-image reconstruction on the same axes to quantify latency and fidelity gaps relative to scene-optimized methods.

Result: Gaussian Splatting yields higher rendering quality than NeRF but at greater GPU cost; onboard compute is insufficient for full per-scene optimization at interactive rates; SAM3D delivers plausible object geometry within seconds but exhibits detail mismatches that can affect downstream manipulation.

Conclusion: Suggest tiered pipelines: lightweight feed-forward reconstruction for real-time perception/tracking, with heavier neural reconstruction scheduled on suitable high-performance compute as needed.

Abstract: Autonomous robots performing laboratory tasks depend on 3D reconstruction pipelines that can turn raw camera streams into actionable object representations within the latency budget of a physical control loop. Neural 3D reconstruction methods have demonstrated high-quality view synthesis, but their real-time viability across the compute platforms on which laboratory robots actually run remains poorly characterized. In this work, we present a systematic compute-platform benchmark of neural 3D reconstruction methods, evaluating NeRF and 3D Gaussian Splatting training and rendering on GPU-enabled computing devices ranging from single-board computers to server-class nodes, and place Meta's SAM3D single-image reconstruction on the same axes to quantify its latency and fidelity gap relative to per-scene optimization. Our results show that Gaussian Splatting yields higher rendering quality than NeRF at greater GPU cost, and that onboard compute is insufficient for full per-scene optimization at interactive rates. Our preliminary assessment on SAM3D indicates that it delivers plausible object geometry within seconds, but with detail mismatches that can compromise downstream manipulation. Together, these findings motivate tiered pipelines in which lightweight feed-forward reconstruction sustains the real-time perception-and-tracking loop for laboratory robots, while heavier neural reconstruction is scheduled selectively on suitable compute.

</details>


### [175] [RTNav: Towards Real-Time Zero-Shot Object Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26496)
*Easop Lee,Lingyu Zhang,Boyuan Chen*

Main category: cs.RO
