TL;DR: RTNav introduces a real-time aware navigation architecture that accounts for inference latency, asynchronous environment stepping, and bounded compute to improve performance under wall-clock time constraints in unknown environments.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Zero-shot object navigation methods suffer performance degradation when wall-clock time counts; current architectures assume free inference time in synchronous simulators, leading to inefficiency in real-time deployment.

Method: Proposes RTNav, an architecture that explicitly models and optimizes for inference latency, asynchronous environment stepping, and bounded compute, enabling real-time operation.

Result: On real-time variants of HM3D (v1, v2, OVON), RTNav improves success rate by up to 11% and Success weighted by Completion Time (SWCT) by up to 5.1 points over prior work.

Conclusion: Incorporating latency-aware design and asynchronous execution into navigation agents yields tangible gains under real-time constraints, validating the need for time-aware architectures in open-world navigation tasks.

Abstract: Navigation in unknown environments to find unforeseen objects has become increasingly feasible with capable vision and language foundation models. However, these models also introduce non-negligible inference latency, which becomes an important concern when agents must operate continuously in the real world. Most state-of-the-art methods are still developed in synchronous simulators, where the environment waits for the agent to act and inference time is effectively free. As a result, agents are often designed around the sequential execution of perception, reasoning, and action, with little regard for time constraints. Under real-time execution, where wall-clock time counts towards the task budget, the inefficiencies of these architectures become clear. We show that recent zero-shot object navigation methods suffer consistent performance degradation under such realistic timing conditions. Motivated by this observation, we propose RTNav, a simple but effective architecture that treats inference latency, asynchronous environment stepping, and bounded compute as explicit design considerations. Evaluated on real-time variants of HM3D-v1, HM3D-v2, and HM3D-OVON, RTNav improves the success rate by up to 11% and the Success weighted by Completion Time by up to 5.1 points over prior work.

</details>


### [176] [Closing the Loop on the Poppy Humanoid: Bipedal Locomotion with Linear-Quadratic Control and Learned Cost Functions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26505)
*Xulin Chen,Borui He,Ruipeng Liu,Naveed Tahir,Zhenyu Gan,Garrett E. Katz*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A practical LQR-based closed-loop walking controller for the Poppy humanoid learned from nominal open-loop data, improving reliability over open-loop trajectories.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable reliable, unassisted bipedal locomotion on the low-cost Poppy robot for research and education.

Method: Record open-loop walking trajectory data, learn a quadratic cost for an LQR controller to track the trajectory, derive a closed-loop controller, validate empirically against open-loop performance.

Result: The learned LQR controller yields statistically significant improvements in walking performance (reliability) over open-loop playback.

Conclusion: LQR trajectory-tracking with a learned cost is an effective approach for enhancing bipedal locomotion on low-cost humanoids and can generalize to similar platforms.

Abstract: The Poppy Humanoid is an open-source, low-cost robot suitable for research and education in artificial intelligence. However, we are unaware of any published methodology that achieves reliable, unassisted bipedal locomotion on the standard Poppy hardware. This paper contributes a functional closed-loop walking controller for Poppy, based on the linear-quadratic regulator (LQR) framework for trajectory tracking. Starting with data collected from open-loop playback of a nominal walking trajectory, our proposed method learns a quadratic cost function for an LQR controller that substantially improves the reliability of the motion. The closed-loop controller is validated empirically, demonstrating statistically significant improvements in walking performance compared to open-loop trajectory playback.

</details>


### [177] [CLIPPER: Replayable Shortlisted Optimization for Repeated Spatial Coverage Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26819)
*Julian Teusch,Jörg Philipp Müller,Monika Sester*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: CLIPPER is a constraint-exact, low-latency planner for city-scale micromobility that uses bounded candidate pools and exact gain recomputation to enable rapid, replayable comparison of planning states while enforcing all constraints, with two variants (CLIPPER-F and CLIPPER-A) offering near-full-set performance with substantial speedups.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Full-set greedy planning for city-scale micromobility is prohibitively slow; there is a need for fast, repeatable planning that still exactly enforces all encoded constraints (geofenced exclusions, site retention, spacing, area caps).

Method: CLIPPER computes a bounded pool of candidates, recomputes exact gains, and enforces every active constraint before selecting the next proposal. The initial order is driven by each candidate's standalone coverage. Offline scans estimate omitted gains; online a conservative bound triggers pool expansion or audit. CLIPPER-F gives equal candidate slots per group; CLIPPER-A shares a single budget across groups.

Result: Across Braunschweig, Munich, and Berlin, CLIPPER’s mean coverage stays within 0.245 percentage points of full-set greedy under the same policy, with 13.6–28.9× lower mean rollout time (CLIPPER-F). CLIPPER-A achieves 9–15% of the full-set rollout time under the same policy, with mean coverage gaps of 1.82, 0.12, and 0.27 percentage points in Braunschweig, Munich, and Berlin, respectively.

Conclusion: CLIPPER enables rapid, replayable comparison of city-scale planning states while strictly enforcing all encoded model constraints.

Abstract: Operational requirements developed with the City of Braunschweig frame municipal micromobility planning under geofenced exclusions, mandatory retained sites, spacing rules, and area-level caps. Each policy edit requires a new feasible plan; full-set greedy takes tens of seconds per alternative at city scale. We present CLIPPER (Constraint-exact Low-latency Iterative Planning with Pooled Evaluation and Replay). It forms bounded candidate pools but recomputes exact current gains and checks every active constraint before selection. Coverage from each candidate alone sets the initial order. Offline full-set scans measure gains omitted by the pool; online, a conservative bound triggers expansion or audit. CLIPPER-F gives each proposal group the same number of candidate slots. Across Braunschweig, Munich, and Berlin, its mean coverage over complete chains stays within 0.245 percentage points of full-set greedy under the same policy, with 13.6--28.9 times lower mean rollout time. CLIPPER-A instead distributes one shared candidate budget across the groups. Under its coverage-prioritized policy, it uses 9--15% of full-set greedy's rollout time under the same policy, with mean gaps of 1.82 percentage points in Braunschweig, 0.12 in Munich, and 0.27 in Berlin. Together, CLIPPER enables rapid, replayable comparison of recorded city-scale planning states while enforcing every encoded model constraint.

</details>


### [178] [Memory Anchors for Continual Robot Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26545)
*Maximilian Du,Zhanyi Sun,Chen Xu,Paarth Shah,Masha Itkina,Shuran Song*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Memory Anchors are a small, critical subset of replay experiences that stabilize continual learning for robotic policies. Selecting or enriching replay buffers with these anchors significantly reduces catastrophic forgetting and improves learning across task sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robots deployed in the wild must continually acquire new tasks without forgetting prior ones. Standard replay buffers sampled uniformly can be inefficient; a small subset of experiences disproportionately anchors past performance.

Method: Identify Memory Anchors as experiences in which representations of new-task observations collapse onto old-task representations despite conflicting actions. Rehearsing data from these regions prevents destructive overwrite. Evaluate via ablation (removing 10% anchors) and buffer enrichment (adding anchors) on LIBERO benchmarks and real robot tasks.

Result: Removing only 10% of Memory Anchors from the replay buffer increases catastrophic forgetting by more than 4.5x on LIBERO benchmarks. Conversely, enriching the buffer with Memory Anchors reduces high-conflict forgetting by about 63% and enables continual learning over two task sequences on a real robot.

Conclusion: Memory Anchors are a pivotal subset of experiences for replay-based continual learning in robotics. Targeted rehearsal of these anchors yields substantial improvements in retaining past skills and enables more robust continual adaptation.

Abstract: Robot policies deployed in the wild should have the capability to continually learn new tasks without forgetting existing behaviors. A common approach to combat such catastrophic forgetting is to train on new task data with a replay buffer of previously learned task data. Although this buffer is commonly sampled randomly from all prior experiences, we show that a small set of these experiences contributes greatly in anchoring past performance. We call these experiences Memory Anchors. We identify Memory Anchors in regions where representations of new-task observations collapse onto those of old-task observations even though the tasks require conflicting actions, like when a familiar object must be manipulated in a new way. Rehearsing old data in this region plays a key role in preventing destructive overwriting of past task knowledge, serving as this critical Memory Anchor role. Excluding only 10% Memory Anchors before sampling the buffer leads to more than a 4.5x increase in catastrophic forgetting on the LIBERO benchmark suites. Conversely, enriching the replay buffer with Memory Anchors can decrease high-conflict task forgetting by 63% and enables successful continual learning of two task sequences on a real robot. Videos and additional visualizations can be found at https://robot-adaptation.github.io/MemoryAnchors

</details>


### [179] [TrapVLA: Trapping Vision-Language-Action Models in Configured Failure Modes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26578)
*Jun-Hui Liu,Kun-Yu Lin,Yi-Lin Wei,Xu-Han Chen,Yinghao Li,Zhuohao Li,Yuan-Ming Li,Qing Zhang,Xiaoyi Fan,Dongmei Jiang,Yan Li,Wei-Shi Zheng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces Configured Failure Trapping for Vision-Language-Action models, enabling trigger-controlled failure modes; provides data synthesis, evaluation suite, and benchmarks; presents TrapVLA to induce configured failures while preserving clean performance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond generic task failures toward controlled, stealthy failure modes in VLA systems, increasing realism of backdoor threats and evaluating defenses.

Method: Develop a data engine for synthesizing target trajectories; an automated suite to measure configured-failure fidelity; construct Trap-LIBERO and Trap-RoboTwin benchmarks across four failure modes; propose TrapVLA that learns trigger-induced action residuals to steer policies toward configured failures.

Result: TrapVLA effectively injects configured failure modes in VLA models across simulation and real-world settings, with limited impact on non-triggered (clean) performance, demonstrated on multiple benchmarks.

Conclusion: Establishes a novel, configurable backdoor paradigm for VLA models, supported by new datasets and evaluation tools; highlights need for robust defenses and further study of stealthy, task-specific backdoors in multimodal agents.

Abstract: This work introduces Configured Failure Trapping, a novel backdoor attack task against Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models, which aims to activate attacks through stealthy textual triggers and induce configured failure modes. Unlike prior backdoor attacks that treat any task failure as a successful attack, Configured Failure Trapping requires the attacker to control how the robot fails (e.g., causing the robot to grasp with a specified positional offset), making it substantially more challenging and hard to detect. To support the new task, we propose an effective data engine for synthesizing high-quality target trajectories and an automated suite for measuring configured-failure fidelity. Then, based on this foundation, we construct two new benchmarks, namely Trap-LIBERO and Trap-RoboTwin, that instantiate Configured Failure Trapping across four representative failure modes. To address this task, we identify sparse action deviation as a critical challenge and accordingly propose a novel method named TrapVLA, which explicitly learns trigger-induced action residuals to steer the policy toward the configured failure behavior. Extensive experiments across simulation benchmarks and real-world robotic settings show that TrapVLA effectively injects configured failure modes into VLA models while largely preserving performance on clean data. Project page: https://john-liua.github.io/TrapVLA/

</details>


### [180] [SOLO: Stable Omni-terrain Long-Horizon Perceptive Humanoid Locomotion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26583)
*Pihai Sun,Gang Han,Jingkai Sun,Jiahao Ma,Zeran Su,Zelin Tao,Peiran Liu,Shuai Shi,Wei Cui,Zifan Wang,Jialin Yu,Wen Zhao,Kangning Yin,Jiaxu Wang,Jiahang Cao,Lingfeng Zhang,Hao Cheng,Jian Tang,Yijie Guo,Qiang Zhang*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: SOLO is a unified framework for robust long-horizon locomotion on complex terrain, combining a Fourier-encoded Query Reconstructor and Trajectory-Aware MSE distillation to improve perception and policy credit assignment, achieving high stress-test success and zero-shot transfer.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Long-horizon fragility in perceptive policies arises from (1) dense terrain reconstructions that blur action-critical details and boundaries, and (2) imitation learning that lacks temporal credit assignment across trajectories.

Method: Query Reconstructor (QR) uses Fourier-encoded cell queries to extract spatially specific evidence from depth-proprioception tokens, preserving sharp terrain boundaries. Trajectory-Aware MSE (TA-MSE) distillation augments the PPO reward with next-state teacher-student disagreement, enabling Generalized Advantage Estimation to propagate future disagreement penalties to prior actions.

Result: In simulation, QR reduces height-map L1 error by 3.3–4.0x. TA-MSE outperforms PPO and MSE+PPO in curriculum progression. On stress-test terrains, SOLO achieves 97.5% mean traversal success and 96% stepping-stone success, vs. 75.0–75.6% and 0–3% for dense-reconstructor variants. Zero-shot deployment with only a chest-mounted depth camera and proprioception enables continuous 1.5-km outdoor routes and indoor mixed-terrain courses.

Conclusion: SOLO effectively mitigates long-horizon fragility by preserving action-critical terrain cues and by temporally crediting actions through TA-MSE, enabling robust, zero-shot locomotion on challenging terrains.

Abstract: Humans traverse complex terrain over long distances without losing balance, whereas perceptive humanoid policies become fragile as perception and control errors accumulate. We present SOLO, a unified framework addressing two compounding causes of this long-horizon fragility: dense terrain reconstruction smooths action-critical details, and pointwise imitation lacks temporal credit assignment. Its Query Reconstructor (QR) uses Fourier-encoded cell queries to retrieve spatially specific evidence from depth-proprioception tokens, preserving sharp terrain boundaries. Trajectory-Aware MSE (TA-MSE) Distillation adds next-state teacher-student disagreement to the PPO reward, enabling Generalized Advantage Estimation to propagate future disagreement penalties to preceding actions. In simulation, QR reduces height-map L1 error by factors of 3.3-4.0, while TA-MSE surpasses PPO and MSE+PPO in curriculum progression. On stress-test terrains, SOLO achieves 97.5% mean traversal success and 96% stepping-stone success, versus 75.0-75.6% and 0-3% for dense-reconstructor variants. Deployed zero-shot with only a chest-mounted depth camera and proprioception, SOLO completes a continuous 1.5-km outdoor route and an indoor mixed-terrain course. Project page: https://sunpihai-up.github.io/solo/

</details>


### [181] [Relaxation-Aware Multimodal Sensing of Soft Gripper Driven by Structure-Perception-Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26622)
*Yanzhe Wang,Hao Wu,Ziyi Zheng,Huixu Dong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A variable-stiffness, soft gripper with integrated vision and infrared sensing uses a temperature-coupled viscoelastic model and a physics-informed learning module to compensate force decay during grasp holding, achieving precise force maintenance and outperforming baselines in a 280 s test.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft polymers exhibit viscoelastic relaxation leading to force decay, undermining stable grasping. Inspired by human grasping, the work aims to preserve contact safety and stability by combining mechanism-driven stiffness modulation with continuous sensing and learning-based compensation.

Method: Develop a variable-stiffness soft gripper equipped with onboard vision and infrared thermography to track deformation and the temperature field in real time. Propose a temperature-coupled viscoelastic force representation and a physics-informed learning model to reconstruct the force trend and provide explicit holding-time compensation.

Result: In a 280 s force-controlled grasp-and-hold task, the approach maintained the desired force with a mean absolute error of 0.066 N, outperforming fixed-aperture and instantaneous-only baselines by 80% and 95%, respectively.

Conclusion: A mechanism–AI co-design framework where physical mechanisms define feasible interactions while learning mitigates residual viscoelastic uncertainties, enabling stable, sustained grasping.

Abstract: Achieving stable, sustained grasping with soft robotic hands remains a fundamental challenge. Compliance enables safe and adaptive contact, yet the intrinsic viscoelasticity of soft polymers leads to stress relaxation and a continuous decay of grasping force during holding. Inspired by human grasping, which combines phase-dependent stiffness regulation with continuous sensing and feedback, this paper presents an integrated structure--perception--learning framework. We develop a variable-stiffness soft gripper that uses onboard vision and infrared thermography to track deformation and the temperature field in real time, preserving continuous tracking of the interaction state. To mitigate relaxation-induced force decay, we propose a temperature-coupled viscoelastic force representation, together with a physics-informed learning model, to reconstruct the force trend and provide explicit compensation during holding. Experiments show that, in a 280s force-controlled grasp-and-hold task, the proposed method maintains the desired force with a mean absolute error of 0.066N, outperforming fixed-aperture and instantaneous-only baselines by 80% and 95%, respectively. Overall, the results support a mechanism--AI co-design view: mechanisms shape feasible interactions, while learning compensates remaining uncertainty in viscoelastic dynamics, together enabling stable, sustained grasping.

</details>


### [182] [FLARE: A Failure-Aware Framework for Autonomous Correction and Recovery in Visual-Language Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26645)
*Ganlong Zhao,Zijia Tang,Xingping Chen,Zhanghui Kuang,Ye Tian,Guanbin Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: FLARE adds offline/online error recovery to Vision-Language-Action models for long-horizon manipulation via a Retry mechanism that perturbs demonstrations to decouple pose from state, and a Reset pipeline using an offline MLLM to identify OOD states and learn object-centric reset skills, with an online monitor arbitrating between task execution and resets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: VLAs trained on monotonic, failure-free demonstrations are brittle and struggle to recover from execution errors in real-world manipulation. There is a need for explicit error recovery mechanisms that handle both typical deviations and state-breaking failures.

Method: Introduce a Retry mechanism that injects perturbation and bridging segments into demonstrations to decouple robot pose from environment state, enabling autonomous handling of execution deviations. Develop a Reset pipeline that uses offline failure analysis with an MLLM to identify OOD states from execution videos and collect a small library of object-centric Reset skills trained to restore the environment to a task-valid state. Deploy an online MLLM monitor to arbitrate between task execution and Reset actions during inference.

Result: Experimental validation on challenging, contact-rich manipulation tasks shows improved task success and robustness compared to baselines, demonstrating the efficacy of the Retry and Reset design in real-world-like scenarios.

Conclusion: Integrating Retry and Reset yields robust VLAs capable of autonomously handling execution deviations and state-breaking failures, enhancing reliability in long-horizon manipulation tasks.

Abstract: Vision-Language-Action Models~(VLAs) have demonstrated significant promise in generalizing to complex, long-horizon robotic manipulation tasks. However, their performance remains brittle, as they are typically trained on trajectory-monotonic, failure-free demonstrations. This reliance on ``perfect" data leaves them unable to recover from common execution errors, such as a missed grasp, a dropped object, or an unexpected collision. In this paper, we propose FLARE, a novel framework that endows VLAs with robust error recovery capabilities through a ``Retry" and ``Reset" paradigm. First, we introduce a ``Retry" mechanism by injecting perturbation and bridging segments that decouple robot pose from environment state into demonstrations, enabling the policy to autonomously handle execution deviations. Second, to address critical, state-breaking (OOD) failures, we introduce a ``Reset" pipeline. We leverage an MLLM for offline failure analysis to automatically identify OOD states from execution videos. This analysis enables the efficient, targeted collection of a small library of object-centric ``Reset" skills, which are trained to restore the environment to a task-valid state. Our full framework integrates these learned policies. At inference, an online MLLM monitor arbitrates between task execution and ``Reset" skills. Experiments on challenging, contact-rich manipulation tasks show our approach significantly improves task success and robustness.

</details>


### [183] [Beyond the Proving Ground: Independent Public-Road Testing of Assisted Lane Change Systems using LiDAR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26669)
*Marcello Cellina,Akos Kriston,Antonio Migneco,Davide Maggi,Stefano Favelli,Fabrizio Re,Fabrizio Minarini,Andrea Nuovo,Riccardo Dona,Biagio Ciuffo*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: On-road assessment of Assisted Lane Change (ALC) compliance with UNECE R79 using LiDAR-based sensing on public roads; 18/27 maneuvers completed, 9 suppressed; some noncompliant cases detected; first public-road campaign for this purpose; LiDAR viable for monitoring.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of proving ground testing and geo-fenced assessments by enabling independent, real-world evaluation of ALC systems for safety and regulatory surveillance.

Method: On-road campaign on the A31 motorway with a LiDAR-based detection/tracking system in a test vehicle. Varied inter-vehicle distances and speeds relative to a take-over vehicle. RTK GNSS used for detection/tracking accuracy. 27 predefined lane-change maneuvers; trajectories compared to UNECE Regulation 79 safety-distance requirements.

Result: 18 maneuvers completed; 9 suppressed. In 6 cases, the system allowed maneuvers not meeting minimum distance requirements. In 3 cases, deviation remained statistically significant after uncertainty consideration.

Conclusion: Demonstrates feasibility of LiDAR-based on-road testing for ALC compliance. First public-road campaign for R79 safety-distance evaluation. Supports market surveillance and regulatory updates by exposing real-world behaviors not captured in formal approvals.

Abstract: Testing of commercial Advanced Driver Assistance Systems is essential to ensure safety and compliance during type approval and in service operation. However, proving ground scenarios may not reflect real world driving complexity, while geo fencing can require manufacturer collaboration and limit assessment independence. This work presents a methodology for independently testing Assisted Lane Change systems on public roads. A campaign on the A31 French motorway used a test vehicle equipped with a LiDAR based vehicle detection and tracking system. Tests covered combinations of inter vehicle distance and speed between the test vehicle and the take over vehicle. Real time kinematic global navigation satellite system receivers assessed detection and tracking performance. Recorded lane change trajectories were compared with the lane change suppression requirements of UNECE Regulation Number 79. Of 27 predefined lane change manoeuvres, 18 were completed and 9 suppressed. In 6 cases, the system allowed manoeuvres that did not meet regulatory minimum distance requirements. In 3 cases, the deviation remained statistically significant after accounting for measurement uncertainty. To the authors knowledge, this is the first public road campaign designed to assess Assisted Lane Change compliance with Regulation Number 79 safety distance requirements. The results demonstrate the suitability of LiDAR based sensing for this purpose. The methodology can support market surveillance and future regulatory revisions by revealing real world behaviours not covered by approval procedures.

</details>


### [184] [PredVLA: A Sub-Million-Parameter Predictive-Coding Policy for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26673)
*Hiroki Sawada,Shunichi Kasahara*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Sub-million-parameter predictive-coding policy (PredVLA, 0.68M) achieves strong language-conditioned robot manipulation on LIBERO, outperforming parameter-matched baselines and enabling explicit online state correction via prediction errors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess how much model scale is necessary for language-conditioned control and whether compact predictive-coding architectures can compete with large pretrained models. Also quantify the contribution of observation-driven inference to online state correction.

Method: Hierarchical generative recurrent dynamics implementing a predictive-coding policy. Observations influence latent state only through prediction errors. 0.68M trainable parameters, norobot-data pretraining. Frozen front end, demonstrations, and action decoder used for evaluation; comparisons with parameter-matched Transformer and LSTM baselines on LIBERO (short- and long-horizon tasks).

Result: PredVLA achieves 86.9% mean success on three short-horizon suites and 75.4% when including the long-horizon suite. In controlled comparisons with the same front end and protocol, it attains 3.7x and 7.4x mean success rates over parameter-matched Transformer and LSTM policies, respectively. The architecture allows exact open-loop control when observation-driven inference is disabled, illustrating the measurable impact of prediction-error-based latent correction.

Conclusion: A sub-million-parameter recurrent generative policy can deliver strong language-conditioned manipulation performance and provides a transparent mechanism for online state correction driven by prediction errors, challenging the assumption that large-scale pretrained models are necessary for such tasks.

Abstract: Large pretrained vision-language-action models dominate modern robot-manipulation benchmarks, but it remains unclear how much model scale is necessary for strong language-conditioned control, or whether fundamentally different control architectures can remain competitive at much smaller parameter budgets. We present PredVLA, a language-conditioned predictive-coding policy with only 0.68 million trainable network parameters and no robot-data pretraining, whose hierarchical generative recurrent dynamics predict visual features and proprioception while observations influence latent state only through online inference from the resulting sensory prediction errors. On LIBERO, PredVLA achieves an 86.9% mean success rate across the three short-horizon suites and 75.4% when the long-horizon suite is included. Under a controlled comparison using the same frozen front end, demonstrations, action decoder, and evaluation protocol, PredVLA achieves 3.7x and 7.4x mean success rates of parameter-matched Transformer and LSTM policies, respectively. The predictive-coding formulation also makes the contribution of observation-driven correction directly measurable: because observations influence the recurrent state only through prediction-error-based latent inference, disabling this inference yields an exact open-loop control condition. Together, these results show that a sub-million-parameter recurrent generative policy can achieve strong performance on modern language-conditioned manipulation benchmarks while providing an explicit mechanism for prediction-error-driven online state correction.

</details>


### [185] [Residual Deep Reinforcement Learning-Based Computed Torque Control for a Cable-Driven Lower-Limb Rehabilitation Robot under Disturbances and Parametric Uncertainties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26739)
*Mohammad-Hossein Fakouri,Ali Keymasi-Khalaji*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Residual deep RL-enhanced computed torque control improves tracking and disturbance rejection for cable-driven rehabilitation robots while preserving an interpretable, constraint-aware model-based structure; demonstrated in simulation across multiple conditions; calls for experimental validation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Model uncertainty, disturbances, joint limits, and pull-only cable actuation degrade model-based controllers; need robust but interpretable control with constraint awareness.

Method: Compute torque control provides nominal command; a bounded Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG) policy supplies a compensating residual torque. Training and evaluation in simulation under nominal, uncertain, disturbed, and generalization conditions with trajectory-tracking and feasibility diagnostics.

Result: Residual controller outperforms pure computed torque control in tracking and disturbance rejection, while maintaining feasibility and interpretability; robustness persists across scenarios, though trajectory-dependent constraint limitations emerge.

Conclusion: Bounded residual learning is a practical robustness enhancement for simulation-based rehabilitation robot control, enabling constraint-aware improvements and motivating further experimental validation.

Abstract: Accurate trajectory tracking in cable-driven lower-limb rehabilitation robots is challenging because model uncertainty, external disturbances, joint constraints, and pull-only cable actuation can degrade nominal control performance. Conventional model-based controllers provide an interpretable control structure but remain sensitive to model mismatch, whereas fully learning-based control can reduce transparency and complicate constraint-aware operation. This study proposes a residual deep reinforcement learning-enhanced computed torque control framework in which computed torque control generates the nominal command and a bounded Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient policy supplies only an additional compensating torque. The approach is evaluated in simulation under nominal, uncertain, disturbed, combined, and generalization conditions, together with trajectory-tracking, joint-limit, cable-demand, workspace-feasibility, and cable-Jacobian diagnostics. Across the evaluated conditions, the residual controller improves tracking and disturbance rejection relative to computed torque control while preserving the interpretable model-based command structure and satisfying the reported feasibility checks in the representative evaluation. Broader tests indicate that tracking improvements can persist beyond the representative case while also exposing trajectory-dependent constraint limitations. These results support bounded residual learning as a practical robustness-enhancement strategy for simulation-based rehabilitation robot control and motivate further constraint-aware and experimental validation.

</details>


### [186] [MeshPriorDiT: Hierarchical Modeling for Action-Conditioned Cloth Dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26766)
*Zihang Wang,Jianming Hu,Shang Su,Hao Huang,Mengkai Shi,Jun Gao,Shuo Feng*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: MeshPriorDiT combines an action-conditioned mesh-GNN mesh prior with a residual DiT via conditional flow matching to predict cloth dynamics, achieving fewer errors than GNN-only and DiT-DDPM baselines on 15-step rollouts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome the local coordination limitation of mesh-GNNs due to finite message-passing, and the lack of explicit topology in transformer-based models, by decomposing motion into a topology-consistent mesh prior and a residual that captures unmodeled dynamics.

Method: 1) An action-conditioned mesh GNN predicts multi-step vertex displacements to form a topology-aware reference trajectory. 2) Conditional on history and planned actions, a Residual DiT uses conditional flow matching to generate the residual motion and then rescales/decodes it using material adjacency to coordinate neighboring vertices.

Result: Evaluated on 15-step autoregressive rollouts across three cloth manipulation tasks. On average, MeshPriorDiT reduces Global MSE by 43.42% relative to the GNN-Only baseline and by 75.03% relative to the DiT-DDPM baseline, while maintaining comparable Edge-strain MSE to GNN-Only.

Conclusion: A hierarchical approach that leverages a mesh topology-based prior and residual generative modeling yields substantial improvements in global pose accuracy while preserving local material consistency, outperforming both pure GNN and pure diffusion-based baselines.

Abstract: Action-conditioned cloth dynamics prediction requires both locally plausible deformation and long-range coordination. Existing approaches largely follow two paradigms. Mesh-based GNNs capture local physical responses through material connectivity. However, their finite message-passing range limits coordination between topologically distant regions, while autoregressive rollouts tend to accumulate prediction errors. Transformer-based dynamics models capture long-range interactions through global attention, but often operate without explicit material connectivity and must learn local topological responses directly from data. We propose MeshPriorDiT, a hierarchical dynamics model that decomposes future cloth motion into a structured mesh prior and a generative residual. An action-conditioned mesh GNN first predicts multi-step vertex displacements, yielding a reference trajectory that respects material topology and grasp constraints. Conditioned on historical states, planned actions, and the mesh prior, a Residual DiT then uses conditional flow matching to jointly generate the residual motion not captured by the prior. The generated residual is further rescaled and decoded using material adjacency to coordinate corrections across neighboring vertices. We evaluate MeshPriorDiT on 15-step autoregressive rollouts across three cloth manipulation tasks. Averaged over the three tasks, MeshPriorDiT reduces average Global MSE by 43.42% relative to the GNN-Only baseline and by 75.03% relative to the DiT-DDPM baseline, while maintaining a favorable Edge-strain MSE comparable to that of GNN-Only.

</details>


### [187] [Online Joint Calibration of Steering Offset and Planar LiDAR Extrinsics for Wheeled Mobile Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26789)
*Subodh Mishra,Arindam Dhar,Suprotim Majumdar,Naveen Arulselvan*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: EKF-based online estimation of steering offset and planar LiDAR extrinsics within a bicycle-kinematics model improves path tracking in warehouse mobile robots by replacing manual calibration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Manual, static calibration of steering zero and LiDAR extrinsics is error-prone and drifts after maintenance, compromising safety-critical WMR performance.

Method: Extended Kalman Filter estimating steering offset and 2D LiDAR extrinsics in a bicycle model, fusing wheel/steering measurements with LiDAR data.

Result: Real-world experiments show substantial reduction in cross-track error after steering offset correction, validating the approach.

Conclusion: Online EKF calibration is a principled alternative to manual procedures for WMR steering and LiDAR calibration, enabling more reliable operation.

Abstract: Accurate steering sensing and LiDAR-to-vehicle extrinsics are crucial for reliable path tracking in warehouse mobile robots (WMRs); miscalibration often leads to snaking, weaving, and elevated cross-track error (CTE). In practice, steering ``zero'' is commonly set manually (e.g., eyeballing straightness via a PS4 joystick), while LiDAR extrinsics are assumed from CAD and may drift after maintenance. Such static, manual procedures frequently cause miscalibration in safety-critical environments. This paper presents an Extended Kalman Filter (EKF)--based method for online estimation of steering offset and planar LiDAR extrinsics within a bicycle-kinematics model, providing a principled alternative to manual calibration. Experiments on real datasets show that correcting steering offset reduces CTE substantially, validating the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

</details>


### [188] [Rapid On-Robot Learning for Dynamic Manipulation Skills: Robot Juggling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26800)
*Taeyoon Lee,Chunpeng Wang,Christopher G. Atkeson,Alfred A. Rizzi,Nicolas Rojas*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An online, safety-aware, memory-augmented learning framework enables a bimanual robot with onboard vision to acquire five canonical three-ball juggling patterns on hardware in under 5 minutes, by combining a learned local model with a global prior and safe exploration.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge sim-to-real gaps by leveraging imperfect priors and continual on-robot learning, emphasizing learning upon current robot knowledge rather than replacement to improve data efficiency and stability.

Method: Regularized memory-based learning that maintains a local model learned from accumulated experience while retaining a global prior to extrapolate where data are sparse; safe mutually reachable state set to ensure transitions between throws/catches stay within joint/actuator limits; online learning on hardware with onboard vision enabling composition of patterns.

Result: Successfully learned five canonical three-ball juggling patterns (cascade, tennis, half-shower, shower, box) on real hardware in less than 5 minutes of interaction, despite significant sim2real gap.

Conclusion: Supports a paradigm where robots build upon imperfect prior knowledge and continually refine behavior through real-world experience, illustrating safe, data-efficient, continual learning for complex, multi-step manipulation tasks.

Abstract: We present an online learning framework that enables a bimanual robot to acquire diverse juggling patterns directly on physical hardware within minutes, even with a significant sim2real gap. One of the most important lessons from this work is that a model, even when far from reality, can be extremely useful for learning. This motivates a central philosophy of our approach: learning should build upon the robot's current knowledge rather than replace it. Our regularized memory-based learning puts this principle into practice by learning a local model from accumulated experience while retaining the global prior model to extrapolate where experience is sparse. This enables efficient and stable online learning from each new experience without resorting to uninformed exploration over a vast space of possible behaviors. Equally important to continual on-robot learning is safety, allowing the robot to repeatedly practice and improve in the real world. We construct a mutually reachable set that allows safe transitions between successive throws and catches, without driving either arm into a state from which its next action would require violating the robot's joint or actuator limits. Together, these ideas enable a bimanual robot with multi-fingered hands and onboard vision to safely learn and compose five canonical three-ball juggling patterns, including cascade, tennis, half-shower, shower, and box, within less than 5 minutes of real-world interaction. More broadly, this work points toward robots that build upon imperfect prior knowledge and continually refine their behavior through their own real-world experience.

</details>


### [189] [TemporalFlow-VLA: Learning Physically Grounded Execution History for Long-Horizon Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26821)
*Jiarui Yang,Yehao Lu,Yuning Su,Yu Zhong,Yufeng Xie,Yazhou Zhang,Haiyu Lan,Kaixiang Lu,Peiwen Lin,Chuang Wang,Junwei Liang,Enyu Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: TemporalFlow-VLA introduces a physically grounded, compact history representation for vision-language-action models by learning execution history through robot-surface temporal flow, improving long-horizon manipulation without motion estimation at deployment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Vision-language-action models often fail to capture recent physical changes with simple history frames, especially in multi-stage tasks where similar states require different actions based on prior steps. A compact, physically grounded history representation could improve decision-making and sample efficiency.

Method: Construct robot-surface temporal flow from recorded robot states, robot geometry, and calibrated cameras as a training-only target; supervise two execution-aligned temporal queries to provide structured history to the action expert; deploy without the geometric supervision path; use asynchronous feature caching to preserve single-frame latency; rely on temporal conditioning instead of explicit motion estimation.

Result: High success rates on benchmarks: 97.63% ±0.26% average on LIBERO (96.60% ±0.87% on LIBERO Long); 85.5%/84.2% Clean/Randomized across 12 RoboTwin tasks. Demonstrates clear advantage on longer-horizon, multi-stage manipulation; controlled history interventions show action prediction depends on both history content and temporal order.

Conclusion: TemporalFlow-VLA offers a compact, physically grounded interface to exploit ordered execution history for VLA-based robot control, achieving strong long-horizon performance while avoiding on-deployment geometric processing.

Abstract: Vision-language-action (VLA) models leverage pretrained vision-language representations for robot control, yet simply adding historical frames does not reliably capture recent physical change. This is especially problematic in multi-stage manipulation, where visually similar states may require different actions depending on prior execution. To address this challenge, we present TemporalFlow-VLA, which learns compact execution history through physically grounded temporal supervision. Using recorded robot states, robot geometry, and calibrated cameras, we construct robot-surface temporal flow as a training-only target and supervise two execution-aligned temporal queries that provide structured history to the action expert. The geometric supervision path is not evaluated at deployment. TemporalFlow-VLA achieves 97.63 +/- 0.26% average success on LIBERO, including 96.60 +/- 0.87% on LIBERO Long, and 85.5%/84.2% Clean/Randomized success across 12 RoboTwin tasks. It shows its clearest advantage over prior methods on longer-horizon, multi-stage manipulation. Controlled history interventions show that action prediction depends on both historical content and temporal order. With asynchronous feature caching, temporal conditioning maintains single-frame-level server-side sampling latency without additional historical-encoding overhead. Overall, TemporalFlow-VLA provides a compact, physically grounded interface for exploiting ordered execution history without explicit motion estimation or geometric processing at deployment.

</details>


### [190] [Active Surface-Driven Reconfigurable Gripper: Robust Grasping and Sequential Manipulation of Thin Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26883)
*Ziyi Zheng,Keqi Zhu,Hao Wu,Yanzhe Wang,Huixu Dong*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces an underactuated gripper with an active surface to robustly grasp thin objects (e.g., books) without precise control; demonstrates a kinematic model, parameter optimization, and extensive experiments showing robustness for flat and vertically packed books and long grasp–place sequences.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grasping thin objects is challenging due to fragility, low contact stability, and sensitivity to precise alignment; existing grippers require careful positioning and do not adapt well to various thin-object configurations.

Method: Design of an underactuated gripper with an active-surface thumb for in-hand repositioning; compliant contact via underactuated fingers; reconfigurable structure; development of a kinematic model for two grasp scenarios (flat and vertical); analysis of book–gripper–environment interaction to optimize structural parameters and grasping strategy; extensive experiments to validate performance.

Result: The gripper achieves stable, robust grasping of thin objects in flat configurations and shows high success for vertically packed books; it enables reliable long sequential grasp–place tasks and demonstrates adaptability across various thin objects (paper, fabric, plastic film, mouse pad).

Conclusion: Combining an active-surface mechanism with underactuated compliance yields robust thin-object grasping without heavy reliance on precise arm trajectories; the approach offers a versatile design principle potentially extendable to other lightweight, thin items.

Abstract: Robotic grippers face substantial challenges in grasping and manipulating thin objects. Most existing grippers rely on highly precise approach and grasp motions, which limits robustness and reduces applicability. This paper explores thin-object grasping using books as a representative example. Here, we propose a novel solution that integrates an active surface with underactuated compliance to achieve stable grasping of thin objects without complex control. First, an underactuated gripper with an active surface is designed. The active-surface thumb performs in-hand repositioning of the target book without requiring adjustments of the robot arm or the other fingers, while the underactuated fingers establish compliant contact conditions with the environment, and the reconfigurable structure enables reliable grasping of books under different configurations. Second, we establish a kinematic model of the gripper, and determine the initial grasp postures for two representative scenarios (books lying flat on a desktop and books vertically packed in a shelf). Third, by analyzing the physical model of a book lying on a table and its interaction with the gripper and the environment, we systematically optimize the structural parameters and grasping strategy. Finally, extensive experiments validate the effectiveness of the proposed gripper and strategy. The results demonstrate strong robustness and adaptability when grasping thin objects placed flat (including books, paper, fabric, plastic film, and mouse pad), as well as a high success rate when grasping vertically packed books. Moreover, the proposed gripper can reliably complete long sequential "grasp-place" tasks.

</details>


### [191] [Beyond Shallow-Water Photorealism: Physically and Sensor-Grounded Simulation for Deep-Sea Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26888)
*Michele Grimaldi,Enrico Di Maria,Ignacio Carlucho,Yvan R. Petillot*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Extends Stonefish with deep-sea physics and sensor models to improve realism for deep-ocean autonomous systems while preserving real-time capability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deep-sea operations demand accurate dynamics and sensor models; current underwater simulators emphasize visual realism in shallow water, leading to inaccuracies for long-duration navigation and learning-based autonomy.

Method: A physics- and sensor-grounded extension of Stonefish augmenting hydrodynamics with stochastic IMU/DVL drift, magnetometer disturbances, higher-order hydrodynamics, terramechanics, pressure-driven environmental variability, and physically based underwater optics, while maintaining real-time compatibility.

Result: Improved representation of forces and measurements relevant to deep-sea AUVs, ROVs, landers, ASVs, and gliders; supports more realistic navigation, perception, and autonomy research under deep-sea conditions.

Conclusion: While challenges remain (e.g., complex fluid-structure interactions and full environmental stochasticity), the framework provides a practical foundation for deep-sea navigation, perception, and autonomy research and is adaptable for real-time simulation.

Abstract: Many recent underwater simulators emphasize visual realism at the expense of physical fidelity, focusing on shallow-water effects with limited relevance in deep-water environments and high computational cost. In this work, we shift the focus toward deep-sea physical and sensor realism. We present a physics- and sensor-grounded extension of the Stonefish simulator that augments its hydrodynamic models with stochastic IMU and DVL drift, magnetometer disturbances, higher-order hydrodynamics, terramechanics, pressure-driven environmental variability, and physically based underwater optics. These additions are designed to better capture the forces and measurements shaping the behavior of deep-ocean AUVs, ROVs, landers, ASVs, and gliders, while remaining compatible with real-time simulation. This work advances underwater simulation toward more representative deep-sea operating conditions, which is particularly relevant for long-duration navigation and learning-based autonomy, where inaccurate sensor and environmental models introduce non-physical artifacts and overly optimistic performance. While challenges remain, including complex fluid-structure interactions and full environmental stochasticity, the proposed framework provides a practical foundation for navigation, perception, and autonomy research under deep-sea conditions.

</details>


### [192] [Contact-Aided Factor-Graph Localization for Underwater Sampling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26932)
*Michele Grimaldi,Yosaku Maeda,Hitoshi Kakami,Ignacio Carlucho,Yvan R. Petillot,Tomoya Inoue*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A contact-aided factor-graph localization approach for AUVs that uses suction-contact events as high-confidence loop closures to fuse adaptive visual odometry and learned detections, enabling online initialization and reducing drift in low-visibility seafloor scenarios.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome scale ambiguity and degeneracy of down-looking cameras on featureless seabeds and lack of drift correction in inertial-DVL fusion.

Method: Smoothing-based localization (factor graph) that integrates contact events as high-confidence constraints, visual odometry factors with uncertainty scaling by inlier statistics, landmark bearing-range factors, learned object detections, and onboard sensors; online initialization; implicit loop closures via contact; no appearance-based place recognition.

Result: Experimental evaluation in tanks, harbor, and simulation shows reduced drift and improved object revisit accuracy vs filtering-based nav and contact-free graph formulations.

Conclusion: Embodied physical interaction serves as a localization primitive in perception-degraded underwater environments.

Abstract: Accurate state estimation for autonomous underwater vehicles performing close-range seafloor sampling remains challenging. In low-altitude operation, down-looking cameras over featureless planar seabeds produce scale ambiguity, lateral degeneracy, and inconsistent feature tracking. Meanwhile, inertial-Doppler Velocity Log (DVL) fusion alone provides no mechanism for structural drift correction. We propose a Contact-Aided Factor-Graph Localization framework that treats physical interaction as an informative geometric constraint within a smoothing-based localization formulation. The method tightly fuses suction-based manipulator contact events with adaptive visual odometry, learned object detections, and on-board sensors. Visual odometry relative-pose factors and landmark bearing-range factors are uncertainty-scaled according to inlier statistics to prevent visually weak frames from destabilizing the estimator, while contact events are modeled as high-confidence factors that induce implicit loop closures without appearance-based place recognition. Furthermore, the system can fully initialize online during motion. Experimental evaluation in tanks, harbor, and simulation environments demonstrates that contact-induced constraints significantly reduce trajectory drift and improve object revisit accuracy compared to filtering-based navigation and contact-free graph formulations. These results highlight the role of embodied physical interaction as a localization primitive in perception-degraded underwater environments

</details>


### [193] [4DSynth: Controllable Procedural World Synthesis for Dynamic Embodied Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26947)
*Zehao Qi,Haochen Luo,Jia-Wang Bian,Zeyu Ma,Shuyang Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A controllable procedural system (4DSynth) that converts NL/mask/photo into editable 4D environments with explicit geometry, animated actors, and physics-ready state; it shares a geometry-grounded representation; introduces 4DSynth-Nav benchmark showing current vision-language models struggle; supports scalable embodied-agent research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Embodied agents require diverse, interactive, and time-varying environments; current procedural/4D tools are not easily editable or scalable; need a controllable pipeline and a benchmark to enable robust evaluation.

Method: 4DSynth pipeline converts natural-language instructions, blueprint masks, or a single photograph into a 4D scene with geometry, actors, trajectories, and physics state; uses a shared geometry-grounded representation across routes; supports animation, camera planning, rendering, and task generation; 4DSynth-Nav benchmark generated from these scenes; evaluation of two vision-language models across three difficulty tiers.

Result: A working controllable generation pipeline and a scalable 4D navigation benchmark; models fail on many tasks, demonstrating the difficulty and reproducibility/difficulty tunability; provides practical foundation for development/evaluation of embodied agents.

Conclusion: Presents a practical, controllable 4D scene generation system and a scalable benchmark, enabling systematic development and evaluation of embodied agents at scale.

Abstract: Embodied agents need environments that are visually diverse, physically interactive, and changing over time. Procedural simulators can generate large interactive scene collections, and recent 4D generators produce compelling visual dynamics. Combining these properties in one environment, however, still demands extensive manual effort, and the result is rarely editable or controllable enough to reuse at scale.
  We present 4DSynth, a controllable procedural system that turns a natural-language description, a blueprint mask, or a single photograph into an editable 4D environment with explicit geometry, animated actors, collision-free trajectories, and physics-ready simulation state. Multiple scene routes share one geometry-grounded representation, so the same pipeline handles animation, camera planning, rendering, and task generation.
  To validate the full pipeline, we construct 4DSynth-Nav, an interactive navigation benchmark generated entirely from 4DSynth's procedural scenes. Two vision-language models evaluated across three difficulty tiers both fail the majority of tasks and stall after early subtasks. The same procedural controllability that produces these environments also makes each failure reproducible and each difficulty axis independently tunable. This paper presents both a controllable generation pipeline and the scalable benchmark it enables, offering a practical foundation for developing and evaluating embodied agents.

</details>


### [194] [Arbitrary-Order Hermite Interpolation of Rigid-Motion Jets via Hyper-Multidual Quaternions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27000)
*Daniel Condurache*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Introduces hyper-multidual quaternions (HMD) for finite-order rigid-motion jet interpolation, analyzes holonomicity (whether the curve is the jet of its pose projection), and extends ScLERP to unit HMDs. Proposes a holonomic alternative via logarithmic coordinates and Hermite interpolation, with higher-order acceleration fields and SE(3) validation; direct HMD-ScLERP is generally non-holonomic, but a log-Hermite lift yields holonomic endpoint-derivative matching.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable accurate, high-order interpolation of rigid-body motions (SE(3)) using unit dual/quaternions while ensuring holonomicity (the interpolant matches prescribed pose derivatives). Address non-holonomic limitations of naive HMD-ScLERP and develop a practical holonomic construction.

Method: Define MD algebra and hyper-multidual (HMD) quaternions (coefficients in a truncated polynomial algebra). Analyze temporal HMD transforms, derive unitary constraints for a transform and its descriptor, and establish local realizability in admissible logarithm charts. Extend ScLERP algebraically to unit HMDs. Identify non-holonomic defects for arbitrary endpoint jets. Propose a holonomic approach by mapping endpoint transforms to logarithmic dual-quaternion coordinates, applying a degree-(2n+1) Hermite polynomial to match derivatives through order n, and lifting via the exponential. Validate via rotation and SE(3) tests up to second order and a third-order polynomial check.

Result: Temporal transforms and relative descriptors are unitary; derived coefficient constraints and local realizability results; identified holonomicity defects for direct HMD–ScLERP; introduced a holonomic alternative that preserves endpoint derivatives up to order n using a log-Hermite lift; HMD arithmetic yields higher-order acceleration fields without explicit dex/dexp differentiation; SE(3) tests up to second order (and a third-order polynomial check) reproduce the stated defects and endpoint jets.

Conclusion: HMD-based interpolation can be made holonomic via a log-coordinate Hermite lift, enabling accurate matching of finite-order rigid-motion jets. Direct HMD–ScLERP is generally non-holonomic for arbitrary endpoint jets, but the proposed holonomic construction provides a practical solution with higher-order derivative fidelity, validated by SE(3) tests.

Abstract: We study bilateral interpolation of finite-order rigid-motion jets represented by unit dual quaternions. An order-$n$ multidual (MD) algebra is the truncated polynomial algebra $\mathbb{R}[\varepsilon]/(\varepsilon^{n+1})$; hyper-multidual (HMD) quaternions are dual quaternions with coefficients in this algebra. Temporal HMD transforms encode a pose and its derivatives, whereas a generic HMD curve need not be the temporal jet of its pose projection; we call this requirement holonomicity. We show that a temporal transform and its relative descriptor are unitary and derive recursive coefficient constraints, together with a local realizability converse in an admissible logarithm chart. We then extend screw linear interpolation (ScLERP) algebraically to unit HMD quaternions. Although it matches complete endpoint transforms, direct HMD--ScLERP is generically non-holonomic for arbitrary endpoint jets. We give a coefficient criterion and explicit endpoint and first-order interior contact defects. A holonomic alternative is obtained by mapping endpoint transforms to logarithmic dual-quaternion coordinates, applying the degree-$(2n+1)$ Hermite polynomial that matches derivatives through order $n$, and lifting by the exponential. HMD arithmetic also recovers higher-order rigid-motion acceleration fields without explicit differentiation of $\mathrm{dexp}$. Rotation and full $\mathrm{SE}(3)$ tests through second order, with an additional third-order polynomial check, reproduce the stated defects and endpoint jets.

</details>


### [195] [Riemann-1.0: An Embodied World Action Model for Physical AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27033)
*Haofeng Sun,Jiangbo Pei,Fei Kang,Zexiang Liu,Yaokun Li,Boyi Jiang,Hua Xue,Cindy Zhou,Wei Li,Yichen Wei,Mengyin An,Fanliang Zhao,Biao Jiang,Zile Wang,Yang Liu,Yangguang Li*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A fully causal autoregressive World Action Model (Riemann-1.0) that unifies policy execution and world simulation, trained via progressive embodied pretraining on 200k+ hours, achieving state-of-the-art across multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Addresses fragmentation in embodied AI modeling by unifying action-conditioned world dynamics and executable policy within a single model, enabling learning from diverse data sources and improving generalization across tasks and embodiments.

Method: Models multi-view visual observations, robot states, and embodiment-specific actions as a single causal autoregressive sequence; treats world evolution and actions as causal state transitions; supports online policy execution and world simulation within one model; employs progressive embodied pretraining across egocentric human videos, handheld demonstrations, and robot trajectories under a shared World Action Modeling objective.

Result: State-of-the-art performance across benchmarks: RoboTwin2.0 94.3%; LIBERO 99.0%; RoboCasa-365 long-horizon 62.6%; long-horizon real-world SR 85.0% and PSR 94.4%; outperforms previous best by 8.4% on long-horizon tasks and exceeds strongest open-source baseline by 15% in SR.

Conclusion: Unified World Action Modeling with progressive embodied pretraining effectively converts large-scale embodied experience into generalizable robot manipulation capabilities, achieving strong performance in both simulation and real-world tasks.

Abstract: We introduce Riemann-1.0, a fully causal autoregressive World Action Model for embodied intelligence. Riemann-1.0 jointly models multi-view visual observations, robot states, and embodiment-specific actions within a unified causal autoregressive sequence, representing robot actions and world evolution as causal state transitions. Unlike existing WAMs based on joint generation, video-first prediction, or decoupled modeling paradigms, Riemann-1.0 unifies online robot policy execution and action-conditioned world simulation within a single model, enabling it to function as both an executable robot policy and a multi-embodiment visual world simulator. To scale embodied experience across heterogeneous data sources, we further develop a progressive embodied pretraining framework that unifies learning from egocentric human videos, handheld-gripper demonstrations, and heterogeneous robot trajectories under a shared World Action Modeling objective. Built upon 200K+ hours of interaction data, Riemann-1.0 progressively transfers large-scale embodied experience into executable robot manipulation capabilities. Riemann-1.0 achieves state-of-the-art performance across both simulation benchmarks and real-world manipulation tasks. It achieves success rates of 94.3% on RoboTwin2.0, 99.0% on LIBERO, and 62.6% on the long-horizon compositional benchmark RoboCasa-365, outperforming the previous best method by 8.4% On long-horizon real-world manipulation tasks, Riemann-1.0 achieves a Success Rate (SR) of 85.0% and a Progress Success Rate (PSR) of 94.4%, exceeding the strongest open-source baseline by 15% in SR. These results demonstrate that unified World Action Modeling together with progressive embodied pretraining effectively transforms large-scale embodied experience into generalizable robot manipulation capabilities.

</details>


### [196] [GRAFT: Grounded and Efficient Online Reinforcement Adaptation for Fine-Grained Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27079)
*Yibo Qiu,Haoliang Ye,Shu'ang Sun,Zan Huang,Ronald X Xu,Mingzhai Sun*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: GRAFT establishes an online adaptation framework for pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies in biomedical manipulation by grounding perception through region-level anchors and reusing cached language-action prefixes, enabling faster learning, lower computation, and a 25-point accuracy gain across four tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Online adaptation of VLA policies for fine-grained biomedical manipulation is hindered by view-dependent cues, sparse task rewards, and expensive online updates; effective grounding without costly region proposals at deployment is needed.

Method: Region-level supervision to learn view-specific visual anchors that focus perception on task-relevant cues; no region proposals required at deployment; combines single-step action generation with cached visual-language prefix reuse to accelerate online learning.

Result: Across four biomedical manipulation tasks, GRAFT improves success rates by 25 percentage points under matched adaptation budgets and reduces the computational overhead of online policy updates.

Conclusion: Grounded perception via region anchors and cached language-prefix reuse enables efficient online adaptation of VLA policies for biomedical manipulation.

Abstract: Pretrained vision-language-action (VLA) policies provide strong priors for robot manipulation, yet adapting them online to fine-grained biomedical tasks remains challenging. Task success often hinges on subtle, view-dependent visual cues, while task-level rewards provide little guidance about which regions matter, making it difficult to learn task-relevant visual grounding from limited real-robot interaction. Online adaptation is further constrained by the computational cost of VLA inference and replay-based updates. We introduce GRAFT (Grounded Reinforcement Adaptation for Fast Task Learning), a framework for efficient online VLA adaptation through grounded perception. GRAFT uses region-level supervision to learn view-specific visual anchors that focus perception on task-relevant local cues without requiring region proposals at deployment. It further combines single-step action generation with cached visual-language prefix reuse to accelerate online learning. Across four biomedical manipulation tasks, GRAFT improves success rates by 25 percentage points under matched adaptation budgets, while reducing the computational overhead of online policy updates.

</details>


### [197] [Pass the Bucket: Efficient, Robust, Local Load Balancing for Teams of Heterogeneous Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27085)
*Tobias Wallner,Dominik Krupke,Arne Schmidt,Sándor P. Fekete*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Decentralized bucket brigade load balancing for heterogeneous robot swarms in 1D space, using local tokens to stabilize and achieve robust throughput without central control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable scalable, robust load balancing among heterogeneous robots in distributed transport tasks without central coordination.

Method: Introduce bucket brigades for spatial task sharing in a confined 1D space; implement a token-based stabilization by temporarily decelerating robots after encounters; compare single boundary token to two-directional tokens and tune deceleration; use event-driven simulations to evaluate convergence and robustness.

Result: Convergence to stable throughput state; reduced oscillations; re-convergence under perturbations (robot deletion, jitter); faster convergence with token optimizations; shows robustness of local mechanism.

Conclusion: A practical, local mechanism for robust load balancing in heterogeneous robot teams, serving as a foundation for more complex multi-robot coordination.

Abstract: We study the problem of decentralized, self-organized task sharing for a swarm of heterogeneous robots that collaborate in transportation or other objectives that require coordinated motion planning. To this end, we present theoretical and practical results for the simple but effective mechanism of \emph{bucket brigades} for load balancing, in which a team of heterogenous robots share a spatial task in a confined, one-dimensional space, while only being able to sense collisions with neighbors or walls. The goal is to optimize throughput of the overall system, without central control or information, aiming at an interval partition proportional to robot velocities. We address possible chaotic system behavior by developing a stabilization mechanism based on simple local aid, a ``token'', that temporarily decelerates robots after an encounter. This purely local change eliminates persistent oscillations, resulting in convergence towards a stable system state. We accelerate system convergence by comparing a single boundary token to ubiquitous two-directional tokens and optimizing the deceleration factor. Event-driven simulations report convergence times and robustness: For a large variety of perturbations (such as robot deletion, position or velocity jittering), the system reliably re-converges. The results suggest a local, practical mechanism for robust load balancing for heterogeneous teams of robots that promises an effective tool as basis for more complex scenarios.

</details>


### [198] [Active sensing to characterize the heterogeneity of plant stress](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27088)
*Ayman Laaroussi,Peter Hanappe,David Colliaux*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: An autonomous robotic platform for targeted chlorophyll fluorescence measurements on plant leaves using dense 3D reconstruction and planning to enable collision-free, point-based sensing.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable active, spatially resolved physiological measurements and overcome the limitations of image-based phenotyping by integrating perception, geometry, and manipulation.

Method: Dense 3D plant model reconstruction from multi-view data; selection of candidate leaf surfaces based on orientation, accessibility, and sensing constraints; task-level planning to bring the end-effector to contact or near-contact positions for fluorescence acquisition; generation of collision-free trajectories for a robotic manipulator.

Result: Conceptual framework and autonomous operation demonstrated; enables automated, repeatable, and spatially resolved physiological measurements beyond passive imaging; shows integration of perception, geometry, and manipulation for high-resolution plant phenotyping.

Conclusion: Tightly coupling perception, geometric reasoning, and manipulation offers a robotics-driven path to advanced plant phenotyping and opens directions for autonomous agricultural inspection and plant-aware manipulation.

Abstract: While most phenotyping platforms rely primarily on image-based measurements, advanced plant characterization requires the integration of active physiological sensing modali- ties such as chlorophyll fluorescence. We present an autonomous robotic platform designed to perform targeted fluorescence measurements on plant leaves. The system combines 3D plant reconstruction, geometric analysis, and motion planning to localize suitable measurement points and generate collision-free trajectories for a robotic manipulator.
  A dense 3D model of the plant is reconstructed from multi-view data and used to extract candidate leaf surfaces based on orientation, accessibility, and sensing constraints. These targets are then integrated into a task-level planning framework that guides the end-effector to precise contact or near-contact configurations required for point-based fluorescence acquisition. The platform enables automated, repeatable, and spatially resolved physiological measurements that go beyond passive imaging. By tightly coupling perception, geometric reasoning, and manipulation, the proposed system provides a robotics-driven approach to high-resolution plant phenotyping and opens new directions for autonomous agricultural inspection and plant-aware manipulation.

</details>


### [199] [Planning a Shared Modular Fixture Layout Across Robotic Disassembly Stages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27151)
*Haohui Pan,Takuya Kiyokawa,Kensuke Harada*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: Unified vacuum-based fixturing with a shared layout across an entire disassembly sequence, enabled by a diffusion-model–driven initialization and Bayesian optimization refinement, yielding stable support evidenced by empirical margins (66.9% screwdriver, 81.6% shaver).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Disassembly changes in support surfaces, mass distribution, and loads make fixture reconfiguration impractical. A single, sequence-spanning fixture could simplify automation.

Method: Modular vacuum-based fixture; search across mixed continuous-discrete layout space using a denoising diffusion probabilistic model to generate physics-informed initial configurations, refined by Bayesian optimization. Experimental validation on screwdriver and shaver with robotic screw/ component-removal tasks and 11 directional-load tests.

Result: Demonstrated feasible disassembly with the planned shared layouts and quantified stability through empirical margins: 66.9% (screwdriver) and 81.6% (shaver).

Conclusion: A product-specific shared fixturing layout can maintain stable support throughout the complete robotic disassembly sequence, reducing or eliminating the need for fixture reconfiguration.

Abstract: Stable support remains challenging in robotic disassembly of irregularly shaped products. As components are progressively removed, the available support surfaces, mass distribution, and task loads change throughout the process. A fixture layout designed for one workpiece state may therefore become infeasible at later stages, motivating unified support planning over the complete disassembly sequence. This paper presents a modular vacuum-based fixturing system that plans one shared support configuration for the complete disassembly sequence of a screwdriver or shaver, allowing each sequence to proceed without fixture reconfiguration. To search the mixed continuous--discrete layout space under repeated cross-stage evaluation, a denoising diffusion probabilistic model generates physics-informed initial configurations that are refined through Bayesian optimization. Robotic screw and component-removal experiments verified the disassembly feasibility of the planned layouts, while 11 directional-load tests quantified their stability. Comparisons between the measured operational loads and directional responses yielded mean empirical stability margins of 66.9% for the screwdriver and 81.6% for the shaver. These results demonstrate that a product-specific shared layout can provide stable support throughout the tested robotic disassembly sequence.

</details>


### [200] [Task-space model-based control of pneumatic soft actuators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27186)
*Nithin S. Kumar,Joshua Gaston,D. Caleb Rucker,Eric J. Barth*

Main category: cs.RO

TL;DR: A real-time dynamic-model-based task-space control framework for planar soft pneumatic actuators using a non-minimal coordinate discrete elastic rod model with holonomic constraints, integrating a quasi-static inverse model, task-space PI control, and a dynamic observer; validated on three actuators across five tasks with mm-scale RMSE.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Soft actuators offer dexterous, compliant interaction but closed-loop task-space control is hindered by strong nonlinearities, distributed deformation, and uncertain dynamics; there is a need for real-time, accurate, scalable control methods that preserve distributed mechanics.

Method: Adopts a non-minimal coordinate discrete elastic rod model formulated in absolute coordinates with holonomic constraints, enabling sparse system matrices and real-time computation for up to 10 discretized rods. Combines a quasi-static feedforward inverse model with a task-space PI controller and a dynamic observer that fuses measurement residuals as virtual forces to achieve full-state estimation from sparse sensing.

Result: Experimentally validated on three planar pneumatic soft actuators with varying geometries across five tasks: drawing digits 0-9 at 3-18 mm/s tip speed, tracking periodic motion up to 37 cm/s, cross-platform generalization, operation under reduced sensing, and real-time user-defined references. Reported RMSEs are 1.5–2.3 mm for precision motions and 5.5–12.4 mm at 1–2 Hz.

Conclusion: Structured, non-minimal dynamic models can enable real-time, high-precision, moderate-bandwidth task-space control of planar soft pneumatic actuators in free space.

Abstract: Soft actuators enable dexterous and compliant interaction, but closed-loop task-space control remains challenging due to strong nonlinearities, distributed deformation, and uncertainty in their dynamics. This paper presents a real-time dynamic-model-based task-space feedback and estimation framework based on a non-minimal coordinate discrete elastic rod model formulated in absolute coordinates with holonomic constraints. The resulting structure preserves distributed mechanics while maintaining computational efficiency through sparse system matrices, enabling real-time control with up to 10 discretized rods. A quasi-static feedforward inverse model is combined with a task-space PI controller and a dynamic observer that fuses measurement residuals as virtual forces, enabling full-state estimation from sparse sensing. The approach is experimentally validated on three planar pneumatic soft actuators with varying geometries. Across five tasks, including drawing the digits 0-9 across the workspace (3-18 mm/s tip speed), tracking periodic motion (up to 37 cm/s), cross-platform generalization, reduced sensing conditions, and real-time user-defined references, our method achieves 1.5-2.3 mm root mean square error (RMSE) for precision motions and 5.5-12.4 mm RMSE at 1-2 Hz.
