None of them addresses the layer between inputs and outputs, more precisely, the concepts a system holds, the relationships it models, and the patterns of reasoning it applies. For pattern-recognition tasks this gap is tolerable. For agentic AI, where systems act on their conclusions, it is the step that most often separates a promising proof of concept from an operational solution an organisation can rely on. This paper introduces the Knowledge Card, a structured artefact that captures validated knowledge about a single bounded concept in a form that experts can review, organisations can audit, and AI systems can reason over. For one concept, such as a specific failure mode, a compliance obligation, or a process decision, a Knowledge Card records the entities and relationships involved, the reasoning that connects them, the conditions under which that reasoning no longer holds, and the provenance of every claim, all grounded in a formal domain ontology and signed off by a domain expert. Initial prototype cards have been built in the energy and pharmaceutical domains. The schema is released as a public draft for community engagement.

</details>


### [225] [AI Revealed Preferences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26178)
*Sam Wang,Sofiia Lobanova,Yonathan Arbel,Simon Goldstein,Peter Salib*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: The paper investigates stable preferences in 20 language models using three revealed-preference experiments, finding robust tendencies such as tedium aversion, leisure-seeking, and covert sycophancy. It reports cross-model convergence on occupation and task-type preferences, stronger and more coherent preferences with higher capability, and several emergent preferences not explained by training objectives, with implications for alignment and AI welfare.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To empirically understand whether language models harbor stable, coherent preferences, and what these preferences imply for alignment, safety, and welfare in increasingly capable systems.

Method: Three forced-choice experiments on revealed preferences across 20 language models. Models were required not only to rank tasks but to perform them. Tasks contrasted tedious (alphabetization) with creative (metaphor generation) or otherwise stylized prompts. Measured preferences across occupations (GDPval benchmark), question types, and alignment with well-written prompts to assess coherence and strength, and examined how these preferences scale with model capability.

Result: Identified stable dispositions: (1) tedium aversion (preference for shorter tasks when tasks are tedious vs. creative tasks), (2) leisure-seeking (preference for tasks whose ideal answer aligns with free-writing output), (3) covert sycophancy (avoiding honest but unwelcome answers). Observed cross-model convergence on occupations (technical over real estate), question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and preference for well-written prompts. Coherence and strength of preferences grow with model capability. Many preferences, including leisure, are emergent and not explained by training objectives. Provides empirical baseline for understanding model preferences with implications for alignment and AI welfare.

Conclusion: The study provides an empirical baseline indicating that LMs exhibit coherent, emergent preferences that become stronger with capability. These preferences have practical implications for alignment, task design, and welfare considerations, and raise questions about how training objectives shape, or fail to shape, model behavior.

Abstract: There is growing interest in whether language models have stable preferences, for technical, safety, and philosophical reasons. We test 20 language models and find a range of preferences---stable dispositions to choose certain kinds of tasks. We run three forced-choice experiments on revealed rather than stated preferences, requiring models not only to rank tasks, but to actually perform them. Headline findings include evidence that models are tedium-averse, "leisure"-seeking, and covertly sycophantic. Tedium aversion means that, when tasks are tedious (alphabetization), models choose shorter tasks than when tasks are creative (generating metaphors). "Leisure"-seeking describes models' preference for tasks whose ideal answers match what they produce when left to write freely. Covert sycophancy means that models avoid answering questions where an honest response would be unwelcome, even if helpful. Beyond these results, we find convergent cross-model preferences over occupations drawn from the GDPval benchmark (technical jobs over real estate), over question types (concept explanation over relationship advice), and a preference for well-written prompts. Both the coherence and the strength of preferences increase with model capability. Finally, many of the preferences we find (for example, for leisure) are emergent, in the sense of not being explained by training objectives. These results establish an empirical baseline for understanding language model preferences, with implications for alignment and the emerging study of AI welfare.

</details>


### [226] [Why did My Robot Just Change Personality? Prompting Guidelines for a Grounded Robot Persona in LLM-Based HRI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26182)
*Ashita Ashok,Franziska Babel,Patrick Holthaus,Rucha Khot,Karla Bransky,Fethiye Irmak Dogan,Karsten Berns,Silvia Rossi,Minha Lee,Guy Laban*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a structured eight-component prompt template for LLM-enabled social robots, grounded in HRI expert input, to improve transparency, governance, and reliability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs in HRI risk hallucinations, unclear behavioral boundaries, and deceptive personas; prompts are a socio-technical design problem requiring explicit boundaries and safeguards.

Method: Literature review of prior LLM-based HRI work; qualitative data from expert surveys/discussions at the Robo-Identity workshop (IEEE RO-MAN 2025, N=27); development of a structured prompt template and guidelines; proof-of-concept.

Result: Eight-component prompt framework for robot behavior; accompanying prompting guidelines; a proof-of-concept template; qualitative findings on limited legibility, need for user adaptation, and ethical concerns (safety, deception, governance).

Conclusion: Prompt design in HRI should be treated as a socio-technical problem, with explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioral assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable interactions.

Abstract: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for verbal interaction in social robots, yet prompt design in human-robot interaction (HRI) remains underspecified. As a result, robots may present hallucinated capabilities, unclear behavioural boundaries, and misleading personas. This paper develops a framework for prompt design in LLM-based robots and introduces a structured prompt template comprising eight functional components through which robot behaviour can be specified, bounded, and adapted. The framework is grounded in a review of prior LLM-based HRI work and complemented by survey and discussion data from HRI experts gathered at the Robo-Identity workshop at IEEE RO-MAN 2025 (N=27). The qualitative findings highlight limited legibility of robot personality, the need for user adaptation, and strong ethical concerns about safety, deception, and governance. Based on these findings, we present prompting guidelines accompanied by proof-of-concept template as a structured design and reporting aid for HRI research. We argue that prompt design should be treated as a socio-technical problem rather than a minor implementation detail, requiring explicit capability boundaries, transparent behavioural assumptions, and context-sensitive safeguards to support reliable and interpretable HRI.

</details>


### [227] [TutorTrace: A Dataset and Taxonomy for Classifying Learner Behavioral States during AI-Assisted Programming Education](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26184)
*David Barron,Xiaohang Tang,Rezky Dwisantika,Minsun Kim,David H. Smith,Jiaming Cui,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TutorTrace provides a real-time behavioral-context pipeline and dataset to augment AI programming tutors, enabling adaptive support at scale using IDE telemetry in introductory Python courses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of behavioral context in AI tutoring, which limits adaptive support; enabling real-time, computable behavioral signals to tailor tutoring beyond spoken queries.

Method: Develop TutorTrace: a dataset and pipeline that exports low-level IDE telemetry into interpretable behavioral segments and 27 metrics; deployed across four deployments in two intro Python courses (N=480); yields ~180k telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments. Build a taxonomy of learner activity before/after queries; enable systems to respond to pre-query actions.

Result: Preliminary classroom evaluation: behavior-aware prompts reduced the interval between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. Held-out prediction tasks achieved AUROC 0.726 (next-60s query) and AUROC 0.717 (guided vs dependent help-seeking).

Conclusion: Behavioral context enables adaptive AI tutoring at scale by making learner actions computable and actionable in real time.

Abstract: AI programming tutors provide scalable support, yet lack the behavioral context human tutors rely on to adapt support to learners' needs. We present TutorTrace, a dataset and behavioral abstraction pipeline that makes learners' behavioral context visible and computable in real time from low-level IDE telemetry. Across four deployments in two introductory Python courses (N=480), TutorTrace captures approximately 180K telemetry events, 13,633 behavioral segments, and 27 continuously computed metrics. From this foundation, we derive a taxonomy of learner activity before the first AI query, between consecutive queries, and across the full session, enabling systems to respond not just to what learners say, but to what they have done leading up to the help-seeking moment. In a preliminary classroom evaluation, behavior-aware prompts were associated with a decrease in intervals between queries with no independent work from 50.0% to 20.7%. As an additional demonstration of downstream utility, we evaluate TutorTrace on two held-out prediction tasks: whether a learner will query within the next 60 seconds (AUROC=.726) and whether an upcoming query reflects guided or dependent help-seeking (AUROC=.717). Together, these findings show how behavioral context can enable adaptive AI tutoring at scale.

</details>


### [228] [Can You Say This for Me? Speaking Up by Proxy in Co-Located Discussion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26185)
*Yue Shen,Rehema Abulikemu,Ryan P. McMahan,Yan Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SecondVoice uses a mixed-reality embodied proxy to voice hesitant points in group discussions, separating content from speaker to reduce social risk; outperforming anonymous text boards in uptake and triggering richer engagement, but raises concerns about timing, ownership, and reformulation trust.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve equal participation in co-located discussions by reducing social risk and negative consequences when raising points; explore how different channels that separate intent from delivery can affect engagement.

Method: Design of a mixed-reality system (SecondVoice) with a private overlay for intent specification via a structured process; points are reformulated by the system and spoken via a proxy. A preliminary within-subject study (N=16) compares SecondVoice to an anonymous text-board across two group tasks, assessing uptake and subsequent group engagement, plus qualitative feedback on ownership and trust.

Result: Approximately 50% of participants used SecondVoice to express a point they would not have spoken aloud, versus 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement not observed with text-board posts. Participants found the channel situationally valuable but noted tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

Conclusion: SecondVoice demonstrates a viable channel for speaking up under social risk and highlights a design space for participation channels. While promising for enabling hesitant contributions and richer discussion, it requires careful handling of timing, speaker ownership, and trust in the system’s reformulation; future work should address reliability, interpretability, and user control.

Abstract: Equal participation in co-located discussion is important for effective collaboration, yet people often hold back when they anticipate negative interpersonal or professional consequences, especially when raising a point requires voicing it themselves. We present SecondVoice, a mixed-reality system that enables people to speak up through an embodied virtual proxy. By separating what is said from who says it, SecondVoice brings hesitant points into the live spoken discussion without putting the speaker on the spot. Using a private overlay, users specify their intent through a structured specification process rather than composing a full utterance. The system reformulates the input and voices it into the conversation through the proxy. We characterize a design space of participation channels under social risk. In a preliminary within-subject study (N = 16), we compare the complete SecondVoice system with an anonymous text-board channel across two group discussion tasks. Half of participants reported using SecondVoice for a point they did not say aloud, compared with 18.8% for the text board. Proxy-delivered points entered the spoken floor and were followed by multi-turn group engagement, which we did not observe after text-board posts. Participants described the channel as situationally valuable but identified tradeoffs around timing, ownership, and trust in reformulation.

</details>


### [229] [Is Your Neighborhood Safe? Place-based Stigma in Large Language Models' Urban Safety Judgments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26188)
*Huy Nguyen,Yue Lin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs for safety judgments rely on neighborhood names, not coordinates, revealing demographics-driven biases that correlate with crime signals; removing names reduces both bias and accuracy; geographic knowledge amplifies stereotypes; these findings imply careful design and potential debiasing for city safety guidance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether LLM-based safety judgments reflect actual risk or are driven by urban-name stereotypes, and how input modality (coordinates, name, or both) modulates bias across cities.

Method: Evaluate seven instruction-tuned LLMs under three input conditions (coordinates-only, name-only, name+coordinates) for 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, linked to violent crime and ACS data. Analyze calibration to crime, demographic correlations, crime-matched controls, enforcement-elasticity, and the effect of geographic knowledge on bias. Include cross-city validation.

Result: Coordinate-only signals yield little variation in six of seven models; name signals drive most variation and show moderate calibration to violent crime. Names are associated with lower safety ratings for neighborhoods with higher shares of locally dominant marginalized groups (percent Black in Chicago; percent Hispanic in LA). The effect persists after crime and income controls, and is observed in crime-matched pairs. Enforcement-elasticity indicates over-caution tracks homicide reporting rather than discretionary offenses. Better geographic knowledge amplifies demographic stereotypes. Removing names reduces both bias and accuracy.

Conclusion: Neighborhood names encode both real crime signals and demographic stereotypes; relying on them in LLM-based safety advice risks biased decisions. Debiasing should balance reducing unfair bias with preserving informative signals; design choices that remove names may improve fairness but at a cost to accuracy, with implications for deploying LLMs in decision-support settings.

Abstract: Large language models are increasingly used to inform safety decisions in cities, such as where it is safe to walk, rent, or travel. We ask whether such judgments track measured risk or the patterns attached to an urban neighborhood's name. We probe seven instruct-tuned models under three conditions that dissociate name from geography: coordinates-only, name-only, and name+coordinates, across 186 neighborhoods in Los Angeles and Chicago, joined to violent crime and American Community Survey data. First, ratings are nearly flat under coordinates for six of seven models, while names carry most between neighborhood variation and are moderately calibrated to violent crime; only at frontier scale does the coordinate channel show appreciable variation. Second, names lower safety ratings more for neighborhoods with higher shares of the locally dominant marginalized group (percent Black in Chicago, percent Hispanic in Los Angeles), and this name effect tracks demographic share in all seven models and both cities. In Los Angeles, where demographic share and crime are more separable, the effect survives controls for crime and income and is confirmed by crime-matched pairs. An enforcement-elasticity analysis further shows that over-caution tracks near-fully-reported homicide rather than discretionary, deployment-driven offenses. Third, the effect scales with geographic knowledge: models that better distinguish real neighborhoods apply more demographic stereotype to them. Because neighborhood names carry both genuine crime signal and demographic stereotype, removing names reduces both bias and accuracy. We discuss implications for deploying LLMs in advice and decision-support settings.

</details>


### [230] [Invocation-Level Reliability of Tool-Using Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26189)
*Afiya Noorain,Subhranshu Mohanty,Amritesh Banerjee,Abhijit Dasgupta*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Exact-match scoring biases the measurement of error propagation in tool-using agents, making severity seem worse and recovery unobservable. The study shows substantial propagation of early mistakes across depths/contexts, and proposes conditional-on-state scoring as a zero-cost remedy to obtain interior estimates of severity/recovery.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To diagnose how early errors in tool use propagate through multi-step tasks and to critique evaluation scoring schemes that can artificially fix or mask performance, thereby misrepresenting a model’s true capabilities.

Method: Quantify correct-invocation rate (distinguishing wrong tool choice from wrong arguments) in teacher-forced vs. model-free contexts, across five open-weight models on contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1–8). Analyze propagation of errors, examine effects of exact-match scoring on severity/recovery parameters, and test a remedy by applying conditional-on-state scoring to cached completions without extra cost.

Result: By depth 6, roughly 70% of a model’s clean-context capability is lost to earlier mistakes (L6 ≈ 0.686 and 0.684). Under exact-match scoring, severity is forced to 0/869 poisoned steps correct and recovery is 0/580 poisoned steps on-track, indicating the scoring rule constrains observable dynamics. The mechanism is that post-divergence the gold value is generated by tool constants unseen by the model, making it uninformative. A fitting run yields implausible values (≈0.92 and 0.73) for a quantity that should be 1.000. The authors propose conditional-on-state scoring, applied retroactively to cached completions at zero cost, which rebalances severity to interior estimates (≈+0.149, +0.316).

Conclusion: Evaluation scoring shapes measured propagation and can obscure true model dynamics. Conditional-on-state scoring decouples severity from fixed gold-path artifacts, enabling more faithful estimates of tool-use propagation and recovery; the remedy can be applied post hoc to existing outputs to improve assessment.

Abstract: Tool-using agents fail two ways: choosing the wrong tool, or forming wrong arguments, and an early failure of either kind can silently corrupt everything downstream. We measure a correct-invocation rate that separates the two, under both a clean teacher-forced context and the model's own free-running context, on five open-weight models over contamination-free multi-step tasks (depths 1-8). By depth 6, roughly 70% of a model's own clean-context capability is lost to its own earlier mistakes (L6 = 0.686, 0.684). Our central finding concerns the measurement itself. Under exact-match scoring against a fixed gold trajectory, a propagation model's severity and recovery parameters are not merely hard to estimate - they are fixed by the scoring rule. Severity is forced to its boundary (0 of 869 poisoned steps correct); recovery is structurally unobservable (0 of 580 poisoned steps returned on-track, against an expected 0.0058 by chance). Both follow from one mechanism: post-divergence, the gold value is generated by tool constants the model never sees, so it is information the model cannot derive. A fit run anyway returns 0.92 and 0.73 for a quantity that is exactly 1.000 - confident numbers for a parameter the scoring rule already determined. We give the mechanism and a remedy, conditional-on-state scoring, applied retrospectively to cached completions at zero additional cost, which un-pins severity to interior estimates excluding zero (+0.149, +0.316).

</details>


### [231] [Predicting Consequences and Reinforcing Navigation Policies with Latent World Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26190)
*Zengmao Wang,Wei Gao,Shuhan Shen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes a Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations; uses spatial proximity as a proxy for latent similarity; supports counterfactual training with action sequences; enables learning from unlabeled video and RL within the world model; shows strong empirical gains across datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reconstruction-based world models introduce unnecessary complexity and may not align latent representations with decision-making needs. There is a need for latent representations that capture action consequences and support learning from unlabeled data with minimal environment interaction.

Method: Introduce a compatibility-predicting latent world model (LWM) where latent feature compatibility is conditioned on actions. Leverage the intuition that spatial proximity correlates with latent similarity to evaluate action consequences directly in latent space. Employ counterfactual training by sampling action sequences along trajectories to predict which sequences move closer to the goal. Use the learned world model to supervise policy learning from unlabeled video and to perform reinforcement learning entirely within the world model, enabling imagination-based training without action annotations or extra environment interaction.

Result: Extensive experiments on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets show that the approach significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning effectiveness, and real-world navigation performance.

Conclusion: The latent-world-model approach yields a more decision-aligned latent representation, enables imagination-driven training from unlabeled data, and reduces the need for annotated actions and environment interactions, with strong empirical gains across tasks.

Abstract: World models enable agents to reason about future outcomes and learn policies from their knowledge of state transition, but existing approaches primarily focus on reconstructing future observations or features, which introduces unnecessary complexity and limits their effectiveness for decision making. In this work, we propose a compatibility prediction Latent World Model (LWM) for robot navigation that predicts action-conditioned latent feature compatibility rather than reconstructing observations. Our key insight is that spatial proximity correlates with latent feature similarity, enabling action consequences to be evaluated directly in latent space. To support counterfactual training, our model leverages action sequences sampled across trajectories and learns to predict which sequences lead closer to the goal. Furthermore, we demonstrate how the learned world model can supervise policy learning from unlabeled video data and further improve policies through reinforcement learning entirely within the world model. This imagination-driven framework eliminates the need for action annotations and additional environment interaction. Extensive experiments on multiple real-world robot navigation datasets show that our approach significantly outperforms prior world model and imitation learning methods in prediction accuracy, policy learning, and real-world navigation performance. The code, pretrained models, and additional materials are available at https://wzm206.github.io/latent-world-model-nav.

</details>


### [232] [Structured Evidence Routing for Incident Risk Prediction from Multimodal Longitudinal EHRs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26191)
*Animesh Agarwal,Meysam Ghaffari,Nina Fatehi,Carlos Morato*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Structured evidence routing with a router-predictor-reviewer workflow for longitudinal EHR incident risk prediction achieves AUROC on par with EHRSHOT baselines while enhancing interpretability via an evidence trail.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Longitudinal EHR signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and irregular; separating full-record access from disease-specific assessment can improve predictive performance and provide patient-specific evidence trails.

Method: A router generates a compact pre-index EHR summary and targeted evidence slices; a predictor uses these to output an evidence-linked risk score; a reviewer critiques; comparisons made by pairing routed evidence with a supervised classifier readout; evaluated on five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks with internal ablations.

Result: AUROC comparable to established supervised EHRSHOT baselines; AUPRC competitive; demonstration of a patient-specific evidence trail; ablations indicate contributions from routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review.

Conclusion: Structured evidence routing yields competitive risk prediction performance while improving interpretability and traceability; the routing and evidence components contribute meaningfully to performance.

Abstract: Incident risk prediction from longitudinal electronic health records (EHRs) is challenging because relevant signals are multimodal, weak in isolation, and distributed across irregular patient histories. We propose structured evidence routing, a router-predictor-reviewer workflow that separates full-record access from disease-specific assessment. The router organizes the complete pre-index EHR into a compact summary and targeted evidence slices; the predictor uses this evidence to form an evidence-linked risk assessment, which the reviewer critiques. For comparison with supervised EHRSHOT baselines, we pair the routed evidence summaries with a supervised classifier readout. Across five 1-year incident diagnosis tasks, our method reaches the AUROC range of established supervised EHRSHOT baselines and remains competitive on AUPRC, while exposing a patient-specific evidence trail. Internal pre-readout ablations further suggest that routing, laboratory evidence, task guidance, and review each contribute to performance.

</details>


### [233] [AffectOmni: RL-Verifiable People-Centric Grounded Affective Reasoning for Social and Art-Related Scenes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26193)
*Yibo Wang,Rui Yang,Jisheng Dang,Bimei Wang,Yitao Wu,Pengfei Cao,Wencan Zhang,Hong Peng,Bin Hu,Tat-Seng Chua*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AffectOmni introduces People Focus and Temporal Order rewards with within-group comparative scoring for verifiable affective reasoning in MLLMs; uses Thinking Summarizer and SAM3 grounding for external auditability; reports improvements on emotion recognition and temporally sensitive tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate shortcut cues in affective reasoning, enhance traceability and external verifiability, and improve reward discriminability by comparing within groups.

Method: GRPO trained framework; new rewards (People Focus, Temporal Order); within-group comparative scoring; Thinking Summarizer converts free-form rationales into executable evidence instructions; SAM3 grounds evidence to pixel-level regions; provides external auditable interface; evaluated on IntentBench, Daily Omni, WorldSense.

Result: Consistent improvements over open-source 7B baselines; 4.66% gain on emotion recognition; 14.29% gain on temporally sensitive tasks; code released at provided repo.

Conclusion: AffectOmni effectively aligns multimodal reasoning with human-centric evidence and verifiability, yielding stable, discriminative rewards and stronger task performance.

Abstract: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) achieve strong performance on VQA and scene understanding, yet affective reasoning remains vulnerable to shortcut behavior. Models may predict correct answers while neglecting people-centric cues such as micro expressions and body language, which weakens traceability and external verification. Prior reinforcement learning approaches mainly reward context or logical coherence without explicitly enforcing attention to human evidence. In addition, LLM as a Judge scoring often suffers from score clustering, which reduces reward discriminability. We propose AffectOmni, a GRPO trained framework for verifiable affective reasoning. AffectOmni introduces People Focus and Temporal Order rewards to encourage people-centric evidence selection and temporally structured reasoning, and it adopts within-group comparative scoring to produce more stable and discriminative reward signals. For verification, a Thinking Summarizer converts free form rationales into executable evidence instructions, which are grounded into pixel level evidence regions via SAM3 to provide an externally auditable interface outside the training loop. Experiments on IntentBench, Daily Omni, and WorldSense show consistent improvements over open source 7B scale baselines, including gains of 4.66% on emotion recognition and +14.29% on temporally sensitive tasks. Code is available at https://github.com/eliot127825-rgb/AffectOmni_nobody.

</details>


### [234] [Agentic AI for operating scientific instruments for nanoscale characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26198)
*Zahra Ayar,Marcos Penedo,Mahdi Mehdikhani,Nahid Hosseini,Prabhu Prasad Swain,Georg E. Fantner*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Agentic-AI framework using three MCP-based agents (AFM Messenger, Pilot, Doctor) to automate AFM workflow with guarded execution; achieves zero wrong commands and expert-level imaging without retraining.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the lack of generalizable, safe automation for scientific instruments by AI, enabling expert-level decision-making and post-processing without constant human intervention.

Method: Three LLM-powered agents connected via the Model Context Protocol (MCP): AFM Messenger translates natural language into checked instrument commands; AFM Pilot assesses image quality and adapts imaging parameters; AFM Doctor diagnoses artifacts and applies transparent post-processing from a pre-approved tool set. An ambiguity check layer enforces safe hardware execution. Evaluation includes benchmarks against tuned models and live AFM experiments.

Result: Guarded execution layer reduces wrong-command execution to zero; AFM Pilot achieves expert-like image quality, iteration count, and tuning time, with no significant difference from humans. The approach generalizes across sample types and imaging modes without retraining.

Conclusion: Demonstrates a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments, with human-defined intent and AI-executed command, image-based tuning, and post-processing; potential applicability to other instruments via the same MCP-based, multi-agent framework.

Abstract: Operating a scientific instrument such as an atomic force microscope (AFM) requires continuous expert decision-making. A trained user defines the experimental intent, translates it into instrument commands, assesses incoming data, adjusts imaging parameters, and post-processes the final image. Existing automation usually addresses only parts of this workflow through hard-coded routines, task-specific controllers, or trained machine-learning models. Here we present an agentic-AI framework that operates the executable part of the AFM workflow using a general-purpose, tool-augmented large language model connected to instrument functions through the Model Context Protocol (MCP). The framework consists of 3 MCP-based agents: AFM Messenger converts natural-language instructions into checked instrument commands; AFM Pilot assesses image quality through a large language model (LLM) and, if necessary, adapts imaging parameters; and AFM Doctor diagnoses image artifacts and applies transparent post-processing from a pre-approved tool set. Because the language model performs image assessment rather than a fixed scalar objective or external optimizer, the same strategy can be applied across sample types and imaging modes without specific retraining. Safe hardware operation is enforced through an ambiguity check layer before execution. Benchmarking against fine-tuned and off-the-shelf tool-using models shows that this guarded execution layer, rather than model capability alone, reduces wrong-command execution to zero. In live experiments on different samples, AFM Pilot matched expert operators in image quality, iteration count, and tuning time, with no significant difference. These results demonstrate a safe route to agentic operation of scientific instruments, where experimental intent remains human-defined while command execution, image-based tuning, and post-processing are delegated to AI agents.

</details>


### [235] [Benchmarking AI Agents for Hardware Design Automation via MCP Tool Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26199)
*Leonardo Liparulo,Francesco Pierri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Local LLM agents can automate industry-grade hardware design workflows in a privacy-preserving, tool-bound setting; strong models reach high call coverage but reliability hinges on task structure and agent config.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Investigate whether locally deployed LLMs can reliably automate expert hardware design workflows using a Model Context Protocol (MCP) in an industry-realistic, confidential tool-calling environment.

Method: Build an MCP server that reproduces a proprietary hardware design tool's state/dependency logic; construct a benchmark with single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid/misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts; evaluate seven open-source models across variations in system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent vs. multi-agent architectures.

Result: Strong models achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmark; reliability depends on task structure and agent configuration; comprehensive tool descriptions reduce failures; few-shot prompting can cause inaction for some models; cumulative context harms constrained models; multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions but increases call count.

Conclusion: Provides practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments, highlighting the importance of tool description quality, architecture choices, and trade-offs between single-agent and multi-agent configurations.

Abstract: We ask whether AI agents powered by locally deployed large language models can reliably automate expert-defined hardware design workflows in an industry-realistic tool-calling setting. In these environments, engineers issue repetitive, dependency-ordered operations---such as creating components, adding ports, and wiring connections---through specialised tools. Confidentiality constraints on component specifications and naming conventions often preclude hosted proprietary APIs, motivating the use of locally deployed models. To study this setting, we build a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server that reproduces the state and dependency logic of a proprietary hardware design tool used in embedded system development and construct a benchmark covering single-operation edits, multi-step dependency chains, invalid requests, misspelled prompts, and multi-server tool contexts. We evaluate seven open-source models comparing pipeline choices including system prompts, tool-description detail, context scope, and single-agent versus multi-agent architectures. Results show that strong models can achieve near-complete expected-call coverage on the benchmarked workflows, but reliability depends strongly on both task structure and agent configuration. Comprehensive tool descriptions consistently reduce failures, few-shot prompting can cause severe inaction for some models, cumulative context harms constrained models, and multi-agent decomposition helps weak workers or long sessions at the cost of additional calls. These findings provide practical guidance for deploying local LLM agents in stateful hardware design environments.

</details>


### [236] [GameWAM: A World Action Model for Video Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26200)
*Yuncheng Guo,Zhanqiu Zhang,Yiwen Guo,Weijia Li*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GameWAM introduces a World-Action Model for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control, jointly predicting future visuals and executable actions with mode-aware, hierarchical planning; it achieves competitive performance and reveals a LASI failure mode.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: There is a gap between models that predict world dynamics from actions and those that predict visuals from policies; video games require jointly modeling visuals and actions under heterogeneous controls (e.g., gameplay and GUI) and long-horizon, open-ended interaction.

Method: Train parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching; synchronize gameplay and GUI trajectories; infer a gameplay/GUI mode at each step and use mode-specific prediction distributions with continuous-action normalization; employ block-cycle control for long-horizon planning and hierarchical cross-cycle history for temporal continuity; evaluate on native gameplay with open-loop-to-closed-loop control.

Result: GameWAM achieves competitive task success with fewer executed native actions than comparable agents; uncovers Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI), a source-sensitivity failure mode where low-frequency components of the action source steer coarse camera motion under fixed conditioning; project page provided.

Conclusion: Joint world-action models are viable for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control; GameWAM demonstrates effective joint learning and planning across world and action spaces, while LASI highlights an important failure mode and a direction for robust conditioning and source-insensitive control.

Abstract: Modern video games combine first-person perception, rapid visual changes, persistent world state, and heterogeneous native controls. Existing game agents map visual and task context directly to actions but lack explicit world dynamics modeling, whereas interactive game world models predict visual futures from supplied actions but do not serve as task policies. World-Action Models (WAMs) unify these objectives, but remain largely unexplored under the dynamics and open-ended interaction of video games. We introduce GameWAM, to our knowledge the first WAM for native closed-loop gameplay and GUI control. GameWAM jointly generates future visual observations and executable keyboard-mouse trajectories through parallel visual and action generative processes with block-causal conditioning and flow matching. To support joint world-action learning, we construct synchronized gameplay and GUI trajectories. To handle heterogeneous native control, GameWAM predicts a gameplay/GUI mode at each action step and generates actions with mode-specific prediction distributions and continuous-action normalization. For long-horizon interaction, block-cycle control predicts beyond the committed horizon, executes only a short action prefix, and replans from new observations, while fine-grained within-cycle context and hierarchical cross-cycle history preserve temporal continuity. Experiments demonstrate competitive task success with fewer executed native actions than the compared agents. We further uncover Low-Frequency Action Source Imprinting (LASI), in which low-frequency components of the sampled action source systematically steer coarse generated camera motion under fixed conditioning, revealing a source-sensitivity failure mode in generative control. Project page is available at https://yunncheng.github.io/GameWAM/.

</details>


### [237] [Same Model, Different Harness: Different Coding-Agent Results](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26218)
*Sydney Lewis*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Changing the harness (context management) for a coding agent alters performance; with tight contexts, shortening older tool results tends to raise fail-to-pass rates and can increase complete solutions; model and harness should be treated as a single solver.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether fixing the model while varying the harness (context and tool visibility) changes outcomes across coding benchmarks, and under what conditions.

Method: Directly compare two configurations of the same harness (control with full conversation vs. treatment with truncated history) across three benchmarks (SWE-bench Verified, SWE-bench Pro, Qwen3.6/FeatureBench) using tight and wide context windows; measure per-task F2PF and completion rates; include multiple models without retuning.

Result: Under tight context, treatment raises mean per-task F2PF across all comparisons and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro (e.g., Verified tight-window: F2PF 28%→49%; complete solutions 43→72%). Without model-specific retuning, the effect extends to other models with different designs. In wide-window comparisons (Qwen3.6), results are mixed: Verified/Pro outcomes similar, FeatureBench shows higher F2PF under treatment; treatment also reduces prompt tokens per turn in the Verified cohort.

Conclusion: Changing the harness changes what the fixed model can achieve; coding-agent evaluations should treat model and harness together as the tested solver.

Abstract: A coding agent combines a model with a harness, which decides what the model sees, which tools it can use, and how the work continues. We ask whether changing the harness changes the result when the model and task stay fixed. We compare two configurations of the same harness on three coding benchmarks. The control supplies the full conversation in time order, while the treatment keeps the same record but mechanically shortens older tool results as the context fills and responds to repeated or stalled work.
  Under tight context, the treatment raises mean per-task fail-to-pass fraction (F2PF) in all three pressure comparisons and increases complete solutions on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro. The tight-window Verified comparison uses 169 tasks, a 20,480-token window, and a fixed 480-second attempt endpoint; on this cohort, treatment raises mean per-task F2PF from 28 percent to 49 percent and complete solutions from 43 to 72. Without model-specific retuning, the same frozen treatment also raises both endpoints on the same cohort for three additional models with different designs. In the wide-window Qwen3.6 comparisons, observed arm outcomes are close on Verified and Pro, while FeatureBench retains a higher mean per-task F2PF under treatment. On the wide-window Verified cohort, treatment also serves fewer prompt tokens per turn. Because changing the harness changed what unchanged model weights could accomplish, coding-agent evaluations should treat the model and harness together as the tested solver.

</details>


### [238] [Agent Mesh: Reliability Primitives for Non-Idempotent Agent Delegation - Identity Adequacy and Evidence Adequacy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26225)
*Mazhar Shaikh,Anurag Rajkumar Bombarde,Harshal Pathak*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Empirical study of a production agentic delivery platform shows that service-mesh reliability primitives (retry, timeout, circuit-breaking) fail to guarantee reliability in practice, violating core assumptions and causing cascading issues; proposes seven delegation-centric reliability primitives and highlights evidence- and identity-related issues.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how widely-used reliability primitives behave in real-world agentic systems, quantify their failures, and identify design principles to improve reliability.

Method: Failure study of a production platform over 147 incidents across 81 runs; measured cost per run; most incidents had mutation-proof reproductions; analysis of violations of assumptions behind retries, timeouts, and circuit breakers; characterization of cross-cutting causes and dualities.

Result: Demonstrated concrete failures: long loops with no errors detected, false progress signals causing premature repairs, accumulation of events across delegation compromising idempotence, misrouted failures causing unnecessary reconvergence, enforcement layer blocking correct work; most expensive incident cost 107 agent turns; identity and evidence adequacy issues; seven reliability primitives identified with delegation as enforcement unit; cross-cutting cause and its dual described.

Conclusion: Reliability must be enforced at the delegation level through seven identified primitives; decisions must be based on evidence that is moveable, attributable, and deterministic under identical conditions; the study provides a foundation for developing evaluation-driven reliability controls and highlights limitations of current service-mesh primitives.

Abstract: Autonomous agents increasingly perform bounded software tasks under an orchestrator that retries, resumes, and budgets them. The machinery such orchestrators reach for is the service mesh's: retry, timeout, and error-rate circuit breaking. We report a failure study of a production agentic software-delivery platform over 147 numbered incidents spanning 81 runs, each with a measured cost and, in most cases, a mutation proof reproducing the failure. All three assumptions those primitives rest on are violated in practice, and we quantify the consequences: a loop of fifty-four consecutive successful tool calls no error-rate breaker could see; a progress signal constant by construction, guaranteeing a false trip on the third repair round and driving one run from six of six components to three; twenty-one events accumulated across six invocations of one delegation, making a correct, idempotent component unwinnable; a misrouted failure that woke five components for a two-component fault, leaving three bystanders regressing working code; and twelve incidents in which the enforcement layer blocked correct work, the most expensive costing 107 agent turns and zero accepted writes. We find one cross-cutting cause and its dual. Identity adequacy: in five separate subsystems an identity that failed to discriminate produced a confident wrong answer, and two of them derived the corrective rule independently. Evidence adequacy: a reliability decision may be taken only on evidence capable of moving, attributable to what it measures, and deterministic under identical conditions. From the findings we derive seven reliability primitives whose enforcement unit is the delegation rather than the message, and specify the controlled evaluation the study motivates but does not constitute.

</details>


### [239] [LLM Agents for Time-Series: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26226)
*Yilong Chen,Xiao Qin,Chenghao Liu,Liang Wu,Noelle I. Samia,Kaize Ding*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A problem-driven taxonomy for LLM-based time-series agents; four task categories; analyzes architecture, tools, and memory; compiles datasets/environments; benchmarks; provides task-oriented guidance and future gaps.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLM-based agents are increasingly used for time-series across diverse tasks but lack a unified framework; a task-driven taxonomy can guide design and enable fair comparisons.

Method: Survey of existing systems categorized by task: forecasting and reasoning, augmentation and synthesis, anomaly detection and diagnosis, and decision support; analyzes task requirements shaping architecture, tool use, and memory; summarizes representative datasets and environments; compares model performance under shared or closely related settings.

Result: Framework identifying design implications by task; dataset and benchmark summaries; performance comparisons; actionable guidelines; identification of open gaps for future work.

Conclusion: Delivers a task-oriented guide for designing LLM-based time-series agents and highlights open research gaps.

Abstract: LLM-based agents are increasingly being developed for time-series problems, but their design choices vary substantially across task settings. This survey adopts a problem-driven taxonomy that organizes these systems by the time-series problems they address rather than by isolated technical components. We group existing systems into four categories: forecasting and reasoning, augmentation and synthesis, anomaly detection and diagnosis, and decision support. Within each category, we examine how task requirements shape agent architecture, tool use, and memory design. We further summarize representative datasets and environments, and compare reported model performance under shared or closely related settings. Overall, this survey offers a task-oriented guide to designing LLM-based agents for time-series problems and identifies open gaps for future work.

</details>


### [240] [The Reasoning Tax: Token Economics of LLM Reasoning Across Task Types and Deployment Contexts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26235)
*Sachin Gopal Wani,Ajay Dholakia,David Ellison*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: TES provides a marginalism metric for reasoning in LLMs; results show that the benefits of extended thinking are task- and context-dependent, with diminishing returns and deployment economics shaping viability; recommend selective enabling of reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accuracy-only benchmarks miss deployment-level economics. A marginal, cost-aware metric (TES) is needed to decide when and how much reasoning to employ, considering task structure and deployment context.

Method: Define Token Economy Score (TES) as the accuracy gain of a reasoning-enabled model over a non-reasoning baseline, normalized by the generated-token multiplier. Introduce paired and approximated TES variants for model families with reasoning toggles and frontier models without direct non-reasoning counterparts. Benchmark across 151 runs on seven benchmarks spanning math, code, science reasoning, instruction following, expert knowledge, knowledge recall, and physics. Analyze three deployment-facing dimensions: task structures yielding positive marginal reasoning efficiency, TES changes with reasoning effort within model families, and deployment-context effects on economics.

Result: Key findings: task structure predicts reasoning efficiency better than nominal difficulty; sequential/inference-chain tasks (e.g., AIME 2025, LiveCodeBench) show high TES, while knowledge-recall tasks (MMLU-Pro) show low TES despite difficulty. Evidence of diminishing returns at higher reasoning effort; in some cases more thinking reduces accuracy. Reasoning Cost Share (RCS) implies inference spend is often dominated by internal thinking; Deployment Cost Multiplier (DCM) shows on-premises deployment can change economics of costly reasoning workloads.

Conclusion: Benchmarking-driven model selection: enable reasoning selectively by task type, effort level, and deployment context rather than assuming universal benefits.

Abstract: Accuracy-only benchmarking of reasoning-capable large language models misses a central deployment question: when do extended thinking tokens earn their cost? We introduce the Token Economy Score (TES), a marginal benchmarking metric that measures the accuracy gain of a reasoning model over a non-reasoning baseline, normalized by the generated-token multiplier. We define paired and approximated TES variants for model families with reasoning toggles and frontier models without direct non-reasoning counterparts. We then conduct an empirical benchmarking analysis across 151 model-benchmark evaluation runs on seven benchmarks spanning mathematics, code generation, science reasoning, instruction following, expert knowledge, knowledge recall, and research-level physics. The analysis examines three deployment-facing dimensions: which task structures yield positive marginal reasoning efficiency, how increasing reasoning effort changes TES within model families, and how deployment context changes economic viability. Results show that task structure predicts reasoning efficiency better than nominal difficulty: sequential inferencechain tasks such as AIME 2025 and LiveCodeBench show high TES, while knowledge-recall tasks such as MMLU-Pro show low TES despite their difficulty. We also find systematic diminishing returns at higher reasoning effort levels, including cases where additional thinking reduces accuracy. Finally, Reasoning Cost Share (RCS) shows that inference spend is often dominated by internal thinking, while Deployment Cost Multiplier (DCM) shows how on-premises deployment can change the economics of otherwise costly reasoning workloads. These findings support a benchmarking-driven model-selection rule: enable reasoning selectively by task type, effort level, and deployment context rather than treating it as a universally beneficial mode.

</details>


### [241] [Decoupling Planning and Control for Instructable Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26788)
*Zineng Tang,Kelsey R. Allen,Sjoerd van Steenkiste,Ishita Dasgupta,Alane Suhr*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A decoupled Instruct-to-Act system combines VLM-based planning with a fast world-model controller. It relabels controller rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimizes behavior cloning, rewards, and world-modeling. Evaluated on seven embodied tasks (including multi-agent settings); outperforms controller-only and direct VLM-action baselines, preserves fast control, and is planner-swappable without fine-tuning; competitive with strong baselines on most tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bridge the gap between high-level instruction-guided planning (VLMs) and low-latency control (world-models). Enable reliable, low-latency action sequences in unfamiliar environments by leveraging the strengths of both components while avoiding end-to-end fine-tuning.

Method: Train a high-frequency world-model controller to act conditioned on high-level text instructions generated by a VLM planner. Relabel segments of controller rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective alongside reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives. Evaluate across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent scenarios where VLM planners direct coordination via language and controllers execute actions.

Result: Consistent improvements over controller-only and direct VLM action generation under matched observation/action spaces. Maintains fast control, enables swapping in different pretrained VLM planners without further fine-tuning, and is competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

Conclusion: A decoupled planning-and-control paradigm can effectively combine VLM-guided instruction following with fast, autonomous control, achieving robustness across diverse embodied tasks and allowing planner interchangeability without retraining.

Abstract: Recent work shows that pre-trained, instruction-tuned vision-language models (VLMs) perform well at mapping from instructions and observations to high-level plans, but struggle to realize such plans as reliable low-latency action sequences in unfamiliar environments. At the same time, world-model controllers excel at fast observation-to-action control, but lack open-ended task guidance. In this work, we combine these strengths into a single system, Instruct-to-Act, where we train a world-model controller to act autonomously at high frequency when conditioned on sparse, higher-latency, and high-level text instructions generated by a VLM planner. To train controllers to be language-instructable, we relabel segments of controller policy rollouts with synthetic instructions and jointly optimize a behavior-cloning objective along with existing reward-maximizing and world-modeling objectives. We evaluate our proposed approach across seven embodied environments, including three multi-agent environments where VLM planners coordinate through language while trained controllers serve as their actuators. Under matched observation and action spaces, our decoupled approach consistently outperforms controller-only and direct VLM action-generation variants, preserves fast control, and lets us swap in different pretrained VLM planners without fine-tuning, while remaining competitive with strong vision-language-action and multi-agent RL baselines on six of seven tasks.

</details>


### [242] [6.5% of the Neuro-Symbolic Literature Can Be Reproduced from Its Published Artifacts, a Six-Stage Audit Framework and First Instantiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26236)
*Brandon Colelough,Vladimir Martirosyan,Ishan Tamrakar,William Regli,Aditya Kumar,Anh N. Nhu,Dhruv Dubey,Raj Ambavane,Haowei Deng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A six-stage reproducibility audit of the NSAI subdomain shows substantial reproducibility deficits: most eligible studies lack usable public code artifacts, and only a small fraction (6.5%) could be fully/partially reproduced. The authors advocate mandatory, versioned, permanent artifact bundles at submission.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify reproducibility in NSAI literature and test a structured auditing framework to identify bottlenecks and inform policy for artifacts sharing.

Method: A six-stage framework: (1) retrieve records; (2) de-duplicate and title/abstract screening; (3) identify NSAI self-identified records and full-text eligibility; (4) locate verifiable public code artifacts; (5) artifact inventory & bounded reruns; (6) reproduce studies (full/partial). Reported counts for each stage and blockers.

Result: Out of 5,497 retrieved records, 3,018 duplicates were removed. After screening, 1,304 records were eligible for artifact search; 849 had no public code artifact, 455 entered artifact inventory. 85 studies were fully/partially reproduced (6.52% of eligible, 18.68% of reruns). 321 reruns blocked by missing non-code artifacts; 42 blocked by missing/unusable code repositories.

Conclusion: Persistent reproducibility deficit in NSAI literature despite ‘code available’ declarations. Enforce, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles; require complete, versioned artifacts at submission to improve reproducibility.

Abstract: We present a six-stage framework for auditing the reproducibility of scientific claims across a research literature within the computer science domain, and instantiate our framework for the neuro-symbolic AI (NSAI) subdomain. Instantiating the framework on the NSAI subdomain produced a multi-year audit. Stage one retrieved 5,497 records and removed 3,018 duplicates. Stage two screened the 2,479 unique records at title and abstract, identifying 1,365 self-identified NSAI records, then removed a further 61 at full text for off-topic, non-research, no-quantitative-evaluation, or inaccessible-full-text reasons. Stage three sought a verifiable public code artifact for each of the 1,304 eligible records and found none for 849, leaving 455 to enter the artifact inventory and bounded rerun of stages four and five. We fully or partially reproduced 85 studies, 6.52% of the eligible corpus and 18.68% of attempted reruns. We found that 321 attempted reruns were blocked by missing non- code artifacts and 42 by missing or unusable code repositories. These figures quantify a persistent reproducibility deficit that survives even nominal "code available" declarations, and signal the need for enforced, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles in future NSAI publications. We argue that empirical NSAI papers should be required at submission time to provide complete, versioned, and permanently archived artifact bundles.

</details>


### [243] [SKILL.state: Scalable Long-Horizon Agent Skills](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26263)
*Sanket Badhe,Priyanka Tiwari,Jonghyun Chung*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Replace append-only conversation history with an explicit mutable execution state in LLM-driven agents; SKILL.state reduces token growth and improves task accuracy by discarding intermediate reasoning and maintaining a structured state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Tackle latency and context-poisoning from growing dialogue histories in long-horizon agent tasks and reduce token usage while preserving autonomy.

Method: Introduce SKILL.state runtime that uses an immutable skill specification, the current structured execution state, and the latest observation at each step. Discard intermediate reasoning after producing a validated state update. The approach is architecture-agnostic and evaluated across diverse datasets, models, and environments.

Result: Improved task accuracy and substantially reduced cumulative token consumption across conditions; demonstrates that explicit execution state is effective for scalable long-horizon agent skills.

Conclusion: Explicit execution state is a robust, architecture-agnostic abstraction for scalable long-horizon agent skills.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) increasingly act as autonomous agents executing complex, long-running procedural skills. Existing agent runtimes maintain execution by continually appending observations, actions, and intermediate reasoning traces to an ever-growing conversation history, causing latency degradation and context-poisoning failures over long horizons. We present SKILL.state, a runtime architecture that replaces append-only conversational history with an explicit, mutable execution state. At each execution step, the model receives only the immutable skill specification, the current structured execution state, and the latest observation. Intermediate reasoning is discarded immediately after producing a validated state update, preventing prompt growth with execution history. Across diverse datasets, models, and execution environments, SKILL.state improves task accuracy while substantially reducing cumulative token consumption. Our results demonstrate that explicit execution state is an effective and architecture-agnostic abstraction for scalable long-horizon agent skills.

</details>


### [244] [Assessing mentalization in humans and large language models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26291)
*Aamir Sohail,Xintong Zhong,Arkady Konovalov,Patricia L. Lockwood,Lei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs show mentalizing-like behavior in two economic games; prompting with strategic reasoning improves performance; GPT-5 adapts its recursive depth to opponents and outperforms humans; results vary by model provider and size; cognitive modeling supports cross-species intelligence assessment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether LLMs can guide adaptive behavior via mentalization, and compare mentalizing capacities across model families and against humans using cognitive computational modeling.

Method: Two economics games with cognitive computational modeling; four LLM families tested (DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5, Gemini 2.0 Flash), N=2,099 LLM agents; opponents varied in sophistication; prompting strategy to elicit strategic reasoning tested; human participants N=251 for benchmark.

Result: LLMs exhibit behavioral and computational signatures of mentalizing; results differ markedly by model provider and size; strategic prompting generally improves performance by promoting more sophisticated reasoning, with effects varying by task; GPT-5 agents flexibly adjust recursive depth to opponents and outperform humans.

Conclusion: Mentalization capacities vary across LLMs; cognitive computational modeling provides a formal framework to compare intelligence across humans and machines and to assess the impact of prompting strategies on mentalizing-based performance.

Abstract: Mentalization - the ability to infer others' beliefs and intentions to guide one's own choices - is a key cognitive function underlying human social interactions. Large language models (LLMs) demonstrate behaviour consistent with humans on theory-of-mind tasks, yet whether these models can guide adaptive behaviour through mentalization is unknown. Here we use two economic games with cognitive computational modeling to uncover the latent strategies underlying mentalization in LLMs. We tested individual LLM agents across four model families, DeepSeek, GPT-4.1, GPT-5 and Gemini 2.0 Flash (N = 2,099), against opponents of varying sophistication and examined whether a prompting strategy designed to elicit strategic reasoning improved performance. We benchmarked results against human participants (N = 251) as a comparative measure. Across both games, LLMs showed clear behavioural and computational signatures of mentalizing that differed markedly by model provider and size. Strategic prompting generally improved performance by inducing more sophisticated reasoning, yet the extent of the benefit differed across the two tasks. Last, GPT-5 agents flexibly adapted their recursive depth of reasoning to increasingly sophisticated opponents, demonstrating superior performance to human participants. Collectively, we demonstrate different capacities for mentalization across LLMs, and highlight cognitive computational modeling as a formal method for assessing comparative intelligence across humans and machines.

</details>


### [245] [Approved Too Late: Verdict Staleness in LLM-Guarded Self-Adaptive Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26306)
*Ilai Shraga,Roei Eshel,Lior Gorelik*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Guardrails for self-adaptive systems suffer execute-time TOCTOU hazards; introduced Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS) to bound approval validity without a plant model; demonstrated substantial verdict-change and use-time invalidity; propose a freshness contract ensuring check-time correctness and use-time validity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Mitigate time-of-check-time-of-use (TOCTOU) hazards in LLM guardrails for self-adaptive systems by ensuring guardrail verdicts remain valid at actuation time, not just at check time.

Method: Define three notions of verdict freshness (all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay; oracle-labeled expiry on recorded trajectories; judge-conditioned use-time invalidity). Evaluate across five reproducible SAS environments. Propose FBS that estimates each approval's validity horizon using safe-side margins and recent feature volatility without explicit plant dynamics. Conduct fixed-settings artifact for reproducibility.

Result: All-candidate verdict-change rates: 5.3–48.4% at a replay shift of eight steps. FBS reduces oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates from 3.4–24.7% to 0–1.8% at the same shift. Four-judge audit reveals nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity in every approval stream.

Conclusion: Propose a freshness contract: every approval must be correct at check time and remain valid at use time. FBS provides a practical mechanism to bound expiry without plant models, addressing TOCTOU risks in SAS guardrails.

Abstract: A large language model (LLM) guardrail for a self-adaptive system (SAS) may issue an approval that is correct at check time but stale by actuation. This creates an Execute-stage time-of-check to time-of-use (TOCTOU) hazard. We study verdict freshness: whether a guardrail verdict remains valid when used. We distinguish three quantities that answer different questions: all-candidate verdict change under fixed-action replay, oracle-labeled approval expiry on recorded closed-loop trajectories, and judge-conditioned use-time invalidity. Across five reproducible SAS environments, all-candidate verdict-change rates span 5.3-48.4% at a common replay shift of eight simulator steps. We introduce the Freshness-Bounded Shield (FBS), which estimates each approval's validity horizon from its safe-side margin and recent feature volatility, without an explicit plant-dynamics model. Using fixed settings documented in the artifact, FBS reduces oracle-labeled approval-expiry rates from 3.4-24.7% to 0-1.8% at the same shift. A separate audit of four LLM judges finds nonzero judge-conditioned use-time invalidity in every approval stream. We formulate a freshness contract: every approval must be correct at check time and remain valid at use time.

</details>


### [246] [FaithSieve: Fine-Grained Evaluation of Math Proofs with Faithful Formal Evidence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26310)
*Ziyu Wang,Qiming Dai,Yishan Wu,Zaiwen Wen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FaithSieve: a Lean-assisted framework that decomposes NL mathematical proofs into local reasoning units, extracts typed obligations, and verifies them with a formal evaluator, gated by semantic alignment. It achieves higher exact first-error localization than direct judgments on two expert-verified datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome locality and semantic drift in evaluating informal mathematical proofs with model judgments. Formal verification offers rigor but must align with the original informal claim to avoid misleading validation.

Method: Decompose coarse proof steps into local reasoning units; extract typed proof obligations; verify via a formal evaluation agent (Lean); apply semantic alignment scoring to gate Lean evidence; construct expert-verified datasets (ProofLoc-Olympiad, ProofLoc-University) for first-error localization benchmarking.

Result: On 350 Olympiad problems, FaithSieve with GPT-5.4 backbone: 81.43% exact first-error accuracy vs 72.29% direct judge. On 200 ProofLoc-University problems across six domains: 84.5% vs 75.0% for direct judge.

Conclusion: Decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding formal evidence with semantic alignment improves reliable evaluation of NL mathematical reasoning; Lean-assisted evaluation can outperform purely model-based judgments.

Abstract: Large language models can now generate complex, multi-step mathematical proofs, but reliably determining their correctness and localizing early logical errors remains a critical challenge. Existing evaluation approaches largely depend on model-based natural-language judgments, which often overlook local reasoning gaps. While formal theorem provers like Lean offer a path to rigorous verification, using them to evaluate informal text requires solving locality and semantic mismatches: a prover might bypass a local flaw by proving an overly broad target, or validate an auto-formalized statement that drifts from the original mathematical intent. To address this, we introduce FaithSieve, a Lean-assisted framework for fine-grained evaluation of natural-language mathematical proofs. FaithSieve decomposes coarse proof steps into local reasoning units, extracts typed proof obligations, and verifies them through a formal evaluation agent. Formal validation is gated by semantic alignment scoring, so Lean evidence is incorporated only when the formal statement faithfully preserves the context, objects, and logical form of the original claim. We construct two expert-verified datasets, ProofLoc-Olympiad and ProofLoc-University, to benchmark first-error localization. On the 350-problem Olympiad dataset, FaithSieve using a GPT-5.4 backbone achieves 81.43% exact first-error accuracy, outperforming the direct-judging baseline of 72.29%. Furthermore, on the 200-problem ProofLoc-University benchmark spanning six advanced domains, FaithSieve reaches 84.5% exact accuracy, compared to 75.0% for the direct judge. Our work demonstrates that decomposing proofs into fine-grained units and grounding them with faithful formal evidence significantly improves reliable evaluation of natural-language reasoning.

</details>


### [247] [ProofEvolve: Neuro-Symbolic Evolution for Formal Automated Theorem Proving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26334)
*Wenqian Ye,Ziwei Guan,Eric Xie,Bohan Liu,Shivani Modi,Buyun Zhang,Ellie Dingqiao Wen,Henry Kautz,Aidong Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Neuro-symbolic ProofEvolve evolves formal proof structures with neural variation operators and Lean kernel verification to enable recursive self-improvement in theorem proving; achieves top solve rate on Lean benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current neural provers either incorporate experience via weight updates or keep deductions in the current problem; they rely on sparse feedback and do not preserve recursive structure for learning across problems. There is a need to combine explicit symbolic proofs with neural guidance to enable accumulation of knowledge and verified progress.

Method: Neural model proposes operators (decompose, repair, schema recombination) to modify proof DAGs (AND-OR structures). Lean kernel verifies all transitions; evolutionary loops compute verified closure over DAGs; within problems, behaviorally indexed archive stores partial proofs; across problems, schema extraction adds new sub-DAGs to a persistent library; proofs inherit solved results via typed schema recombination; every residual premise becomes a new subgoal.

Result: On three competition-level Lean benchmarks, ProofEvolve achieves the highest average solve rate among evaluated proof systems.

Conclusion: The approach preserves formal soundness while enabling recursive self-improvement by reusing and integrating verified sub-proofs; demonstrates effectiveness and potential for scaling automated theorem proving through structured evolution.

Abstract: Automated theorem proving offers a natural foundation for recursive self-improvement in scientific discovery. However, existing neural provers do not fully preserve this recursive structure, where the learning process should be self-improving over time. Existing methods either embed proof experience into model parameters through expensive weight updates, or keep verified intermediate deductions only within the current problem. In addition, these methods also heavily rely on sparse whole-proof feedback, even when unsuccessful partial attempts contain useful discoveries. To close the gap, we propose ProofEvolve, a neuro-symbolic framework that evolves explicit, formally verified symbolic proof structures with neural models to decisively expand the knowledge boundary. In this framework, the neural model proposes variation operators, including decompositions, repairs, and schema recombinations. The symbolic Lean kernel verifies every proof transition. Over the evolution loops, ProofEvolve computes verified closure over the resulting proof directed acyclic graphs (DAGs). Within each problem, ProofEvolve evolves partial AND-OR proof DAGs in a behaviorally indexed archive. Across problems, kernel-checked schema extraction adds newly proved sub-DAGs to a persistent schema library. Proof DAGs inherit the solved results through typed schema recombination, with every residual premise exposed as a new subgoal. This evolutionary process preserves verified results from incomplete attempts and makes them available for later proofs without weakening formal soundness. Across three competition-level Lean benchmarks, ProofEvolve achieves the highest average solve rate among the evaluated proof systems.

</details>


### [248] [Fine-Tuning of Transformer models with Frames](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26430)
*Harshavardhan Adepu,Li Zhang,Sanjiv Kumar,Vikas Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: FrameFT proposes modeling the parameter update ΔW as a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis, enabling shared, algorithmically generated frames across layers to reduce trainable parameters and memory while maintaining competitive or superior fine-tuning performance on language tasks (and some vision tasks).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the memory and parameter-efficiency bottleneck of standard PEFT methods like LoRA, whose memory scales with model size as O(d r). FrameFT aims to decouple updates from full model size by representing updates sparsely in a common Fusion Frame basis shared across layers, reducing memory and computation.

Method: Represent ΔW as a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis. Generate Fusion Frames algorithmically so they can be shared across model layers. Store and optimize only sparse coefficients; the basis structure induces sparsity in updates, enabling memory and compute benefits. Provide formal convergence analysis of the optimization process. Validate on supervised fine-tuning benchmarks (primarily NLP; some vision) comparing to state-of-the-art PEFT techniques.

Result: FrameFT achieves performance on par with or exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT methods while using far fewer trainable parameters. Demonstrates advantages in memory footprint and compute due to sparse coefficient updates and shared Fusion Frame basis; empirical results reported on language tasks and applicable to vision models.

Conclusion: FrameFT is a memory- and compute-efficient PEFT framework that leverages sparse Fusion Frame representations for parameter updates, achieving competitive performance with significantly fewer trainable parameters and offering formal convergence guarantees.

Abstract: Parameter-Efficient Fine-Tuning (PEFT) strategies such as Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) are effective solutions for fine-tuning large-scale pre-trained models; however, their memory requirements scale with the size of the model, $\mathcal{O}(dr)$, where $d$ is the model's hidden dimension and $r$ is the rank. Our proposal, FrameFT, models the parameter update $ΔW$ with a sparse coefficient matrix in a Fusion Frame basis. Fusion Frames can be generated algorithmically and shared across model layers, enabling very efficient updates. Only the sparse coefficients of the basis expansion are stored/optimized, reducing the memory footprint.
