The sparse structure of the coefficient matrix in FrameFT and the sparsity in the Fusion Frames give large compute benefits, and our analysis provides formal convergence results. We evaluate the idea across a suite of supervised fine-tuning benchmarks, focusing on language tasks, but also report application to vision models. Our experiments show that FrameFT achieves performance on par with/exceeding state-of-the-art PEFT techniques, but needs far fewer trainable parameters.

</details>


### [249] [Don't Overthink, Don't Underthink: Toward Adaptive Reasoning in Agentic AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26442)
*Md Jueal Mia,M. Hadi Amini*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Adaptive reasoning allocation is necessary for agentic AI; misallocation manifests as over- or under-reasoning, with over-reasoning wasting resources without accuracy gains and under-reasoning causing errors; evaluation on MATH-500 and GAIA shows these patterns; supports developing dynamic reasoning controls.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fixed/inflexible reasoning controls fail in evolving planning, tool use, memory retrieval, and interactions; misallocated reasoning leads to inefficiency, latency, and incomplete solutions.

Method: Characterize over- and under-reasoning; evaluate on MATH-500 and GAIA public validation benchmark; metrics include tool-decision latency, token consumption, token-limit exhaustion, and answer correctness.

Result: Over-reasoning correlates with higher computation and little accuracy gain; under-reasoning correlates with incorrect or incomplete solutions; shows dynamic alignment is beneficial.

Conclusion: Encourage development of adaptive reasoning mechanisms for agentic AI to allocate reasoning according to task demands.

Abstract: Recent advances in Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown that increased inference-time reasoning can improve performance on complex tasks. However, many existing approaches rely on fixed or preallocated reasoning controls, such as fixed token budgets, pre-execution difficulty estimates, or activation-space interventions, and are often evaluated on standalone reasoning benchmarks rather than full agentic workflows. These assumptions may not hold in agentic AI systems, where reasoning requirements evolve dynamically through planning, tool use, memory retrieval, and agent-to-agent interactions. Consequently, reasoning can become either excessive or insufficient, resulting in unnecessary computation, increased latency, planning drift, excessive tool use, or incomplete solutions. We argue that a major challenge for next-generation agentic AI is not merely how much reasoning a language model should perform, but how it should allocate reasoning according to evolving task demands. We characterize over-reasoning and under-reasoning as recurring failure modes of misallocated reasoning and evaluate them on MATH-500 and the GAIA public validation benchmark. Using tool-decision latency, token consumption, token-limit exhaustion, and answer correctness, our results suggest that cases classified as over-reasoning are associated with higher computational cost without proportional accuracy gains, whereas cases classified as under-reasoning are consistently associated with incorrect or incomplete solutions. These findings motivate future research on adaptive reasoning mechanisms for agentic AI.

</details>


### [250] [PILOT in the Loop: Live Self-Improvement for Long-Horizon Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26530)
*Yang Xiao,Yusong Sun,Haoyi Wu,Wenyang Hui,Wen Da,Zhaokai Luo,Mu Chuan,Yao Hu,Wenjie Li,Chengyue Jiang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: PILOT enables live self-improvement through a supervisor-worker harness with live steering and live self-evolution for real-time redirection and memory/skill distillation, outperforming baselines in multiple benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address limitations of existing architectures where long-horizon experience from a running agent cannot influence the current run or be immediately validated; aim to enable real-time adaptation and reusable knowledge.

Method: Introduce PILOT as a two-part harness: (1) live steering to redirect or abort the active worker during execution; (2) live self-evolution to distill observed procedures and failure modes into reusable skills and memory; evaluate across two frozen backbones and three benchmarks against baseline harnesses.

Result: PILOT ranks first in five of six configurations; on Terminal-Bench 2.0 it outperforms baselines by up to 9.8 percentage points. In self-improvement settings, gains of 14.6 points (GLM-5.1) and 12.4 points (Kimi-K2.6); mean output tokens reduced by 42.9% and 47.4%; successful evaluations per million output tokens increased by 110.3% and 134.0%.

Conclusion: Live steering plus live self-evolution enable effective live self-improvement, yielding better task performance and more efficient outputs across multiple configurations; PILOT surpasses existing architectures in diverse benchmarks.

Abstract: Long-horizon agent runs generate experience that can improve both the current run and future work. Most self-improvement methods process this experience only after execution ends, so they cannot redirect the active run or immediately apply and validate lessons learned from it. We argue that self-improvement should instead be live, using emerging experience both to redirect the active run and to update the persistent harness. Existing agent architectures do not fully support this goal. Single-agent self-correction combines task execution and trajectory assessment within one context, while subagent delegation separates execution but typically cannot redirect an active subagent. We present PILOT, a supervisor-worker harness for live self-improvement through two coupled mechanisms: (1) live steering lets a separate supervisor redirect or abort the active worker during execution; and (2) live self-evolution distils procedures and failure modes revealed during execution into reusable skills and memory. Across two frozen backbones and three benchmarks, PILOT ranks first in five of six configurations. On Terminal-Bench 2.0, PILOT outperforms counterpart harnesses by up to 9.8 percentage points. In the self-improvement setting, PILOT gains 14.6 points with GLM-5.1 and 12.4 points with Kimi-K2.6. Mean output tokens fall by 42.9% and 47.4%, while successful evaluations per million output tokens rise by 110.3% and 134.0%, respectively.

</details>


### [251] [Multi2AV-Safety: Benchmarking Safety in Multimodal-to-Audio-Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26535)
*Kaichao Jiang,Changtao Miao,Baiqi Wu,Zhiyuan Lu,Kang Yang,Peiwei Zhao,Junchi Chen,Yunfeng Diao,He Liu,Qi Chu,Tao Gong,Nenghai Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: First safety benchmark for multimodal audio-video generation (11 non-singleton conditioning configurations; 11,024 attack instances) revealing weaknesses in current multimodal safety guards and highlighting compositional risk perception as a key gap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As audio-video generation becomes multimodal, safety evaluation must capture cross-modal and temporal interactions where harmful intent can emerge from benign inputs when combined; existing prompt-centric benchmarks fail to study these compositional risks.

Method: Construct 11 conditioning configurations across text, image, audio, and video that are non-singleton; generate 11,024 attack instances; evaluate representative multimodal safety guards; analyze failure modes and harm-evidence structures.

Result: Safety guards exhibit systematic weaknesses; two main failure modes: (i) harmful semantics emerge from combinations of individually benign inputs, (ii) explicit harmful cues are harder to detect when embedded in benign multimodal context; current guards struggle to integrate safety evidence across modalities and time.

Conclusion: Compositional risk perception is a central capability gap for safeguarding multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation; the dataset will be publicly released in October 2026.

Abstract: Audio-video generation is rapidly moving from prompt-driven synthesis toward multimodal conditioning, where text, images, audio, and video can jointly shape the generated output. This shift changes the nature of safety evaluation: harmful intent may no longer reside in any single input, but instead emerge from how otherwise benign or weakly harmful conditions interact across modalities and time. Existing safety benchmarks, however, remain largely prompt-centric or tied to fixed conditioning interfaces, leaving such compositional risks difficult to study systematically. To bridge this gap, we introduce Multi2AV-Safety, the first safety benchmark, to the best of our knowledge, to cover all 11 non-singleton T/I/A/V conditioning configurations for audio-video generation, comprising 11,024 attack instances. Evaluation on Multi2AV-Safety reveals systematic weaknesses in representative multimodal safety guards across attack mechanisms and harm-evidence structures. Our evaluation reveals two complementary failure modes: harmful semantics can emerge from the combination of individually benign inputs, while explicit harmful cues can become harder to detect when mixed with benign multimodal context. Together, these results identify \emph{compositional risk perception} as a central capability gap in safeguarding multimodal-conditioned audio-video generation: current safety guards fail to reliably integrate safety evidence across modalities and time, even when all conditioning inputs are observable. The dataset will be publicly released in October 2026.

</details>


### [252] [DuMateBench: Evaluating Autonomous Agents in Complex Real-World Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26546)
*Zechun Niu,Yukun Zhao,Jiaxin Zhang,Xu Shen,Jinhua Si,Han Tian,Can Xu,Yunfan Song,Jiaxin Mao,Yansong Gao,Yuchen Li,Jianmin Wu,Lingyong Yan,Shuaiqiang Wang,Dawei Yin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DuMateBench is a real-session benchmark for autonomous agents, built from anonymized production-user sessions, featuring 200 tasks across 8 scenarios and 17 capability categories. It introduces real-world environmental complexity (Insufficient, Unstable, Noisy) and a hybrid evaluation protocol, revealing substantial gaps across frameworks and LLMs. Code and data are publicly available.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Existing benchmarks often separate tasks by application or capability and operate in cleaner, more stable environments than those agents encounter in practice. There is a need for a realistic, end-to-end benchmark that preserves interaction history and workspace state to assess robustness and coordination across multiple capabilities and LLMs in real-world settings.

Method: Constructed from anonymized, privacy-screened user sessions, the benchmark preserves pre-solution interaction history, persistent configurations, and workspace state. Each of the 200 tasks is validated via human verification, spans 8 scenarios and 17 capability categories, and often requires multi-capability coordination. Tasks are executed in isolated Docker containers with three real-world perturbations (Insufficient, Unstable, Noisy). Evaluation uses a hybrid deterministic and LLM-as-Judge protocol. Experiments involve five autonomous-agent frameworks and four state-of-the-art LLMs.

Result: There are substantial gaps in strict task completion across frameworks and LLMs. Robustness, efficiency, and diagnostic analyses show that performance under environmental perturbations is jointly shaped by LLM capability and the surrounding agent framework.

Conclusion: DuMateBench provides a realistic, challenging benchmark for evaluating autonomous agents in real-world conditions and highlights the interplay between LLMs and agent frameworks in robustness and efficiency. The code and data are publicly available at the project site.

Abstract: Autonomous agents are increasingly adopted to complete complex, multi-tool workflows in real-world settings. However, existing benchmarks typically separate tasks by application or capability and evaluate agents in environments that are cleaner and more stable than those encountered in practice. We introduce DuMateBench, a real-session benchmark reconstructed from anonymized and privacy-screened user sessions collected from a large-scale production agent platform. Each task preserves the relevant pre-solution interaction history, persistent configurations, and workspace state, and is then validated through human verification. The resulting benchmark comprises 200 tasks spanning 8 broad scenarios and 17 fine-grained capability categories, with most tasks requiring multiple capability coordination. We execute these tasks in isolated Docker containers injected with three forms of real-world environmental complexity: Insufficient, Unstable, and Noisy, and assess performance using a hybrid deterministic and LLM-as-Judge evaluation protocol. Experiments across five representative autonomous-agent frameworks paired with four state-of-the-art LLMs reveal substantial gaps in strict task completion. Complementary robustness, efficiency, and diagnostic analyses further show that performance under environmental perturbations is jointly shaped by the capabilities of the LLM and the surrounding agent framework. The code and data are publicly available at https://dumatebench.com/.

</details>


### [253] [AgentJudgeBench: A Multi-Difficulty Benchmark for Evaluating LLM Judges on Agentic Tool-Calling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26623)
*Abhigya Verma,Amit Kumar Saha,Seganrasan Subramanian,Sai Harshitha Aluru*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLM judges show limited reliability for agentic tool-calling over DAG workflows; alignment declines with task difficulty and ground-truth exposure has mixed effects; structure rubrics help but do not generalize; a hard-task ceiling of ~77–82% emerges without ground truth; best judge choices depend on setting; study reveals fundamental limitations and practical guidelines for evaluation in agentic systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To systematically evaluate the reliability of LLM-based judges for agentic tool-calling tasks in structured, dependency-driven workflows (workflow DAGs), isolating it from generic text or preference judgments.

Method: 3,808 instances spanning six DAG topologies and three difficulty levels; five generators (3B–70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4) and six judges (20B up to frontier scale), evaluated with paired-with-ground-truth and without-ground-truth conditions; assessments of alignment; exploration of mitigation strategies: chain-of-thought (CoT), judge temperature, structured evaluation rubrics; ground-truth exposure; and a human validation study to identify human-aligned judges.

Result: Judge alignment degrades monotonically with task difficulty. Alignment is ~1.5× faster to degrade without ground truth; on hard queries without ground truth, all six judges converge to a narrow 77–82% band regardless of scale, indicating a structural ceiling driven mainly by task difficulty and partly by prompts. Ground-truth exposure is not universally beneficial, reducing alignment for GPT-5.4 and Gemini-2.5-Pro. Among mitigations, chain-of-thought reasoning and judge temperature have negligible effects; structured rubrics improve alignment by up to 6.5 percentage points but do not generalize across judge–generator pairs. With ground truth, QwQ-32B best matches the programmatic reference; a human-validation study identifies GPT-OSS-120B as the most human-aligned judge; without it, frontier judges perform only marginally better than the ceiling. These results reveal fundamental limits of current LLM judges and provide practical guidelines for reliable evaluation in agentic systems.

Conclusion: The study exposes fundamental limitations in current LLM judges for agentic tool-calling within structured workflows, showing a task-difficulty-driven alignment ceiling and limited benefit from many common mitigations; it offers practical guidelines and identifies best-performing configurations (under ground-truth or human validation) but cautions that reliability cannot be fully achieved by model scale alone.

Abstract: LLM judges are widely used to evaluate agentic tool-calling systems, yet their reliability on structured, dependency-driven workflows remains largely unexamined. We present AgentJudgeBench, the first benchmark to systematically study LLM-as-a-judge reliability for agentic tool-calling over workflow DAGs, as distinct from the broader LLM-as-a-judge task of open-ended text or preference evaluation. The benchmark comprises 3,808 instances spanning six DAG topologies and three difficulty tiers, evaluated with five generators (3B-70B open-weight models and GPT-5.4) and six judges (20B to frontier scale) under paired with- and without-ground-truth conditions. Judge alignment degrades monotonically with task difficulty, 1.5x faster without ground truth, and on hard queries without ground truth all six judges converge to a narrow 77-82% band regardless of scale, revealing a structural ceiling driven primarily by task difficulty, though its height is partly prompt-dependent for weaker generators, that model capacity alone cannot overcome. Ground-truth exposure is not uniformly beneficial: it reduces alignment for GPT-5.4 (1.5 pp) and Gemini-2.5-Pro (3.9 pp), consistent with over-anchoring. Among mitigation strategies, chain-of-thought reasoning and judge temperature both have negligible effect, while structured evaluation rubrics improve alignment by up to 6.5 pp but do not generalize uniformly across judge-generator pairs. With ground truth, QwQ-32B best matches the programmatic reference, while a human validation study identifies GPT-OSS-120B as the most human-aligned judge; without it, frontier judges lead only marginally within the shared ceiling. These results expose fundamental limitations of current LLM judges and yield practical guidelines for reliable evaluation in agentic systems.

</details>


### [254] [SIGMA: Structured Noise-Effect-Aware Grouped Multi-Agent Aggregation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26683)
*Li Mingqian*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SIGMA is a hierarchical MARL framework that uses density-based grouping to form adaptive local structures, intra-group consensus to preserve task-relevant shared information, and inter-group attention to integrate across groups, improving robustness to structured noise in observations while preserving performance in noise-free settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Robust coordination in cooperative MARL under observation disturbances is undermined by structure-induced noise effects, where disturbances create local, structured correlations aligned with cooperation structures. Existing methods rarely exploit this structure, limiting robustness.

Method: SIGMA constructs adaptive local groups via density-based clustering, performs intra-group consensus to smooth agent-specific representation deviations while keeping shared information, and uses inter-group attention to fuse information across groups to maintain global coordination with heterogeneous group contributions.

Result: Experiments on StarCraft II with noisy observations confirm structured noise effects and show SIGMA consistently improves robustness to observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

Conclusion: Exploiting cooperation structure through hierarchical grouping and attention improves robustness to structured observation noise in MARL without sacrificing performance in clean settings.

Abstract: Cooperative multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL) faces significant challenges in maintaining robust coordination under noisy observations. Although observation disturbances are often introduced independently across agents, their downstream effects on cooperative decision-making can become structured through underlying cooperation structures. We characterize this phenomenon as structured noise effects, where noise-induced decision effects exhibit local correlation among agents with stronger task-related dependencies while remaining globally heterogeneous across different agents and local structures. Existing robust MARL methods, however, rarely explicitly characterize or exploit such structure-dependent noise effects. To address this limitation, we propose SIGMA, a hierarchical collaboration framework that exploits cooperation structures to learn robust representations under noisy observations. SIGMA first organizes agents into adaptive local structures through density-based grouping and performs intra-group consensus aggregation to preserve shared task-relevant information while smoothing agent-specific representation deviations. Inter-group attention then adaptively integrates information across different groups to preserve global coordination while accommodating their heterogeneous contributions. Experiments on noisy-observation tasks in StarCraft II empirically validate the structured noise effects and demonstrate that SIGMA consistently improves robustness under observation noise while maintaining competitive performance in noise-free environments.

</details>


### [255] [Relational Over-Regularization: Graph-Based AI-Generated Text Detection via Sentence Transition Deviation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26694)
*Hyeonchu Park,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A relational graph approach (CSFG) detects AI-generated text by modeling sentence-pair transition variance (Relational Over-Regularization). It achieves high binary detection accuracy and strong generalization, with limitations when transition variance is near human baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current token-level or stylometric detectors overfit to specific generators and fail under distribution shift. A structural, sentence-pair level signal—inflated inter-sentence transition variance—offers a robust signal across generators.

Method: Formalize Relational Over-Regularization (ROR) as a relational problem. Implement CSFG (Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph) that encodes positional, sequential, semantic, and transition-deviation signals as learnable GNN edge features. Use per-edge signed deviation δ_ij as a false-positive calibrator, avoiding hand-crafted thresholds.

Result: Validation across four benchmarks with p<0.001. CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy in binary detection, outperforming the strongest graph-based baseline by 11.14 percentage points, with a false-positive rate of 1.57% and robust generalization to unseen LLMs in the inflated-variance regime. Detection degrades when generator transition variance approaches human baseline.

Conclusion: The central contribution is the relational problem formulation (ROR); CSFG is a concrete instantiation that effectively leverages sentence-level relational signals for robust AIGT detection, though performance may drop for generators that mimic human-like transition variance.

Abstract: Detecting AI-generated text (AIGT) remains challenging because existing approaches rely on token-level statistical signals or independent stylometric features, causing them to overfit to specific generators and fail under distribution shift. We identify a structural signal at the sentence-pair level: LLMs produce inter-sentence transition variance that deviates from human writing through inflated variance driven by recurring similarity bursts at paragraph boundaries and templated transitions. We formalize this as Relational Over-Regularization (ROR) and validate it across four benchmarks (p < 0.001). The central contribution is this relational problem formulation, not a novel GNN architecture; CSFG is one concrete instantiation for operationalizing ROR. To exploit this signal, we propose the Cross-Source Stylometric Fingerprint Graph (CSFG), a graph-based framework that encodes positional, sequential, semantic, and transition deviation signals as learnable GNN edge features. The per-edge signed deviation δ_ij operationalizes ROR without hand-crafted thresholds and acts as a false-positive calibrator. CSFG achieves 97.14% accuracy under binary detection, outperforming the strongest graph-based baseline by 11.14 pp, with a false-positive rate of 1.57% and robust generalization to unseen LLMs in the inflated-variance regime; detection degrades for generators whose transition variance falls at or below the human baseline.

</details>


### [256] [Five Primitives for Governing Autonomous AI Agents at Runtime](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26696)
*Jiten Oswal,John Cadeddu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Runtime governance is needed for enterprise autonomous agents; five primitives—discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain—address runtime control gaps not solved by model design; implemented as policy-mediated actions with per-tenant vocab and a verifiable, hash-linked ledger; four primitives implemented in private pilots, the fifth as separate tooling; described more as a codebase description than a taxonomy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enterprise autonomous agents operate with ephemeral principals, self-directed actions, and discoverable populations, undermining traditional human-centric control models. The need is for runtime governance that can answer where, who/what, what rules, how actions are attested, and how supply chains are secured.

Method: Conceptual decomposition into five primitives and an architectural design where actions are mediated against policy on the request path, tied to per-tenant vocabularies, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger verifiable by a vendor-independent third party. The paper reports private pilot implementations for four primitives and separate tooling for the fifth, with explicit cost and architecture trade-offs.

Result: A concrete, runnable architecture for runtime governance with an emphasis on verifiable accountability and tamper-evident logging. Demonstrates practical progress (four primitives in pilots) and clarifies integration challenges and performance costs (enforcement latency, per-workload sidecars, fail-closed behavior).

Conclusion: The five primitives reflect a codebase-derived decomposition rather than a taxonomy; runtime governance is essential for enterprise AI agents, but full integration is still in progress. The work highlights actionable design points and trade-offs while acknowledging partial adoption status.

Abstract: Enterprise deployments of autonomous AI agents inherit a control model built for human users and long-lived services, and the fit fails in three specific ways: agent principals are ephemeral, appearing and vanishing faster than provisioning; their actions are selected by a model rather than programmed, so the set of things they may attempt is not known in advance; and the population is discovered rather than provisioned, because anyone who can call an API can create one. We argue that governing such agents is a runtime problem -- not a model-alignment problem and not a build-time problem -- and we derive five primitives from the questions that must be answered before an action takes effect and after it has: discovery, identity, governance, attestation, and supply chain. For each we state what fails if it is absent and why the others cannot structurally supply it. We describe an implementation in which an agent's action is mediated against policy before it takes effect, authorised against a per-tenant action vocabulary, and recorded in a hash-linked signed ledger a third party can verify with the vendor out of the loop. We report what the architecture costs: the enforcement point sits on the request's critical path, identity requires a sidecar per workload, and fail-closed mediation converts availability incidents into denial. We are explicit about implementation status: four primitives are built and running in private pilots, and the fifth is built as separate tooling and not yet integrated into the request path. We keep it in the set deliberately: a five-part decomposition that exactly matches what its authors happened to build is not a taxonomy but a description of a codebase.

</details>


### [257] [Accelerating Scientific Research with Gemini in the Real-World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26701)
*Samuel Schmidgall,Xiaokai Zhu,Marian Shaw,Lin Yang,Valentin Liévin,Jingyun Yang,Yuchen Zhuang,Tim Strother,Alex Bijamov,Min Woo Sun,Anil Palepu,Justin Chen,David Steiner,Jacqueline Shreibati,Wei-Hung Weng,Yilin Zhao,Xingjian Hu,Nicholas Zahn,Sadhya Garg,Julia Kirby,Yuxiang Gan,Jiaoli Li,Divy Thakkar,Shekoofeh Azizi,David Racz,Juraj Gottweis,Vivek Natarajan,Chenglin Wu,Tal Danino,Keran Rong,Haozhe Wang,Benoit Schillings,Yong Cheng,Quoc V. Le,Tao Tu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Extends Co-Scientist, a Gemini-based multi-agent system, into execution-grounded, closed-loop scientific workflows spanning materials science, biology, and computer science, with real-world validation and safety measures demonstrating improved reliability and reduced hallucination; evidence includes experimental materials synthesis, lab-in-the-loop growth, phenotype prediction, autonomous architecture discovery, and a double-blind human evaluation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To move beyond hypothesis generation toward end-to-end, validated scientific automation that can operate safely in real-world laboratories across multiple domains.

Method: Integrates Co-Scientist with experimental hardware (semi-automated CVD reactor for MXenes), leverages Gemini 3 Deep Think for rapid lab-in-the-loop execution, predicts phenotypes from sparse data in biology, autonomously discovers scalable inference-time architectures, and evaluates generated papers via a double-blind study with domain experts; includes reliability modules to mitigate hallucinations and plagiarism.

Result: Experimental MXene precursor route design yielded lamellar 2D material with lattice similarities to Ti3C2Tx; single-attempt growth of monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, WS2; emergent E. coli swarming phenotypes predicted from IPTG gradients with quantitative alignment to wet-lab data; architecture outperformed six frontier models on HealthBench while reducing potential clinical harm; double-blind expert review indicates reduced hallucination/plagiarism and improved research safety.

Conclusion: Shows tangible progress toward closed-loop, multi-agent AI systems that can accelerate real-world discovery, while highlighting areas needing further verification (atomic structure confirmation, broader generalizability, and safety validation).

Abstract: We present an extension and comprehensive real-world validation of Co-Scientist, a Gemini-based multi-agent system designed to accelerate end-to-end scientific research across hypothesis generation, experimentation, and manuscript generation. Moving beyond in silico hypothesis generation, this specialized configuration transitions Co-Scientist into an execution-grounded research partner advancing closed-loop scientific workflows across materials science, biology, and computer science. In materials science, Co-Scientist interfaced with a semi-automated chemical vapor deposition reactor to design a safe precursor route for MXenes; experimental execution produced a lamellar 2D material sharing key structural similarities with the Ti3C2Tx MXene lattice, although further experiments are needed to confirm the atomic structure. Leveraging Gemini 3 Deep Think for rapid, lab-in-the-loop execution, it also tailored growth recipes to laboratory constraints in minutes, enabling single-attempt growth of monolayer MoS2, MoSe2, and WS2 semiconductors. In biology, Co-Scientist predicted emergent swarming phenotypes of engineered E. coli across inducer (IPTG) gradients from sparse imaging data, quantitatively matching unpublished wet-lab morphological measurements. In computer science, Co-Scientist autonomously discovered an inference-time scaling architecture that outperformed six frontier models on HealthBench (Hard and Professional) while reducing potential clinical harm under blinded physician evaluation. Finally, a double-blind study of end-to-end generated papers with 30 domain experts across 450 reviews demonstrates that Co-Scientist's reliability modules reduce hallucination and plagiarism while improving research safety. Together, these results demonstrate progress toward closed-loop multi-agent scientific AI systems capable of accelerating real-world scientific discovery.

</details>


### [258] [Style as a Confound: False Positives in AI Detection of Non-Native Academic Writing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26710)
*Hyeonchu Park,Gahye Jeong,Bugeun Kim*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Professional editing changes are a major confound for AI text detectors, variably affecting detector scores and challenging claims that detectors reliably distinguish authorship from linguistic polish.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To determine whether AI detectors reflect AI authorship or broader linguistic features by exploiting a natural experiment where editing preserves content and authorship.

Method: Analyzed 135,389 document pairs from a professional English editing service (2018–2025): non-native manuscripts and their native-edited versions, across 13 detectors. Measured false-positive rates (FPRs) for human-written texts, detector score changes due to edits, and correlation with editing extent.

Result: FPRs for human-written texts varied 0.0%–100.0% across detectors. Editing produced detector-specific responses: some detectors increased AI scores, others decreased them after edits. Magnitude of score changes correlated with the extent of editing.

Conclusion: Professional editing style is a key confound in AI detector outputs, undermining the ability to separate text origin from linguistic style and raising fairness and reliability concerns in academic settings.

Abstract: AI text detectors are increasingly employed in academic settings, but it remains unclear whether their outputs reflect AI authorship itself or broader linguistic features associated with polished academic English. Previous studies have reported high false-positive rates (FPRs) for non-native English writing, but population-level comparisons confound authorship with differences in topic, domain, and writing style. Professional editing provides a useful setting for examining this issue because it changes the linguistic form of manuscripts while preserving authorship and content. We examined 135,389 document pairs from a professional English editing service (2018-2025), comprising non-native manuscripts and their native-edited versions, to assess how editing affects detector responses controlling for content and authorship. For the 13 AI text detectors, FPRs for human-written texts varied widely, from 0.0% to 100.0%. Responses varied across detectors: the same edits increased AI scores in some detectors but decreased them in others. Notably, score changes correlated with the extent of editing. The findings identify professional editing style as a key confounding variable in AI detector outputs, rather than establishing a full separation of text origin from linguistic style, raising concerns about fairness and reliability in academic settings.

</details>


### [259] [Knowing When Not to Reuse: Conditional Experience Transfer in Autonomous LLM Post-Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26730)
*Tingyun Li,Wenfeng Feng,Weiqing Li,Abudukelimu Wuerkaixi,Guohua Liu,Yuewei Zhang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BCIT is a policy mechanism for selective reuse of past training updates in autonomous post-training, binding effects to source context, validating applicability, vetoing conflicts, and using bounded trials to confirm current-state evidence; it reduces harmful updates and improves final-model quality under equal compute across domains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Past update evidence often fails to generalize across different parent models, data, and training stages, risking wasted compute or degraded training trajectories if reused blindly; a principled authorization of experience is needed.

Method: Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer (BCIT) binds observed effects to their source context, enforces applicability conditions, vetoes named hard conflicts, and performs bounded training trials to obtain current-state evidence when necessary. It uses a memory of observed events and a shared adoption rule for fully trained candidates; only observed events extend memory.

Result: In a 4B model adapted to finance reasoning, text-to-SQL, and function calling, BCIT produced heterogeneous target and retention effects across contexts, but under matched candidates/evidence/compute it authorized fewer harmful updates and achieved higher equal-budget final-model quality than evaluated alternatives.

Conclusion: Treat experience authorization as a distinct problem in autonomous post-training, with BCIT showing potential to improve robustness and efficiency of update decisions by contextualizing, vetting, and experimentally validating past improvements.

Abstract: Large language models offer broad capabilities, but adapting them to evolving domains, tools, and requirements often entails repeated post-training. Autonomous systems automate parts of this process by proposing updates, training candidates, and using evaluation feedback to select subsequent proposals. As evidence accumulates, a central problem emerges: which past update evidence remains actionable after subsequent training has changed the parent model? An update's effect depends on its parent, data, and training stage. Treating past success as context-free permission can waste compute. If the resulting child is promoted, it can also degrade the subsequent training trajectory. We formulate this problem as conditional experience transfer and introduce Boundary-Calibrated Intervention Transfer (BCIT), a method that authorizes experience reuse before weight-changing training. BCIT binds an observed effect to its source context, checks applicability conditions, vetoes candidates with named hard conflicts, and obtains current-state evidence through a bounded training trial when needed. Fully trained candidates still face a shared adoption rule, and only observed events extend memory. On one 4B model adapted across finance reasoning, text-to-SQL, and function calling, candidate updates exhibit heterogeneous target and retention effects across the evaluated contexts. Under matched candidates, evidence, and compute, BCIT authorizes fewer harmful updates and attains higher equal-budget final-model quality than the evaluated alternatives. These results support treating experience authorization as a distinct problem in autonomous post-training.

</details>


### [260] [Graph-Guided Selective Unlearning for Language Models: Controlling Support Routes Beyond Forget Seeds](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26743)
*Waqas Khan,Tabinda Sarwar,Jingyue Cong,Xun Yi,Estrid He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Graph-guided selective unlearning expands deletion scope beyond explicit forget seeds by propagating deletion pressure across a weighted support-route graph, achieving lower soft leakage while preserving utility on TOFU/PISTOL benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current methods delete seeds only; residues can be recovered via paraphrases or neighboring data; enterprise unlearning requires broader deletion scope and routing control.

Method: Build a weighted support-route graph that captures how knowledge may be connected; propagate deletion pressure from forget seeds through the graph; apply graded forgetting strengths to high-risk neighbors; evaluate on synthetic TOFU and PISTOL benchmarks using GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct.

Result: GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across deletion settings, reducing leakage by up to 49.5 percentage points compared with a seed-only baseline.

Conclusion: Effective enterprise unlearning necessitates controlling information flow via support routes in addition to seed deletions; graph-guided deletion can substantially reduce remaining leakage while maintaining model utility.

Abstract: Enterprises fine-tune language models on proprietary data that may later require removal due to privacy, contractual, or compliance obligations. Selective unlearning removes requested knowledge while preserving model utility, offering a practical alternative to full retraining, but existing methods treat the explicitly identified forget examples as the complete deletion scope. This is insufficient when target knowledge remains recoverable through paraphrases, aliases, or neighboring training examples. We propose GRAPHSU, a graph-guided controller that expands the deletion scope beyond forget seeds by constructing a weighted support-route graph, propagating deletion pressure through it, and applying graded forgetting strengths to high-risk neighbors. On the Task of Fictitious Unlearning (TOFU), a synthetic author-profile question-answering benchmark, and PISTOL, a structural-unlearning benchmark built around interconnected factual samples, with GPT-2 Medium and Llama-3.2-3B-Instruct, GRAPHSU achieves the lowest utility-feasible soft leakage across all deletion settings, reducing leakage by up to 49.5 percentage points over a matched seed-only baseline, demonstrating that effective enterprise unlearning requires controlling support routes, not just forget seeds.

</details>


### [261] [AgentFold: Closed-Loop Agentic Search for Protein Folding Model Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26747)
*Mingquan Liu,Jiangyu Chen,Hanqun Cao,Xujun Zhang,Pengsen Ma,Xiangru Tang,Shuting Jin,Zhuo Yang,Tianfan Fu,Fang Wu,Xiangxiang Zeng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AgentFold treats protein folding model development as a closed-loop, multi-agent search over executable code variants, achieving substantial gains under matched compute and revealing design heuristics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address whether scientific LLM agents can autonomously improve tightly coupled scientific ML systems via executable code changes and expensive validation, using protein folding as a challenging domain requiring architecture search and domain-aware interpretation.

Method: Starting from ESMFold, AgentFold uses multiple agents to propose, implement, debug, and evaluate code variants; maintains structured memory of interventions; employs an MCTS-style policy to allocate compute across high-potential branches; evaluated on a 2,000+ line codebase, exploring ~80 variants with ~5,000 GPU-hours and ~170M LLM tokens.

Result: Under matched compute, AgentFold improves best lDDT by 7.5% over independent Codex proposals and outperforms random search; yields actionable design patterns for stable gains via priors and gated refinement; exposes risk of destabilization from direct geometry perturbations.

Conclusion: Autonomous, multi-agent code-level optimization can meaningfully advance scientific ML systems, with interpretable traces and actionable lessons for future architecture and training design; resources are publicly available.

Abstract: Scientific LLM agents have shown promise in literature reasoning, tool use, and experiment planning, but it remains unclear whether they can autonomously improve large, tightly coupled scientific machine-learning systems through executable code changes and computationally expensive validation. We study this question in protein folding, where progress requires coordinated architectural modifications, multi-objective evaluation, and domain-aware interpretation. We present AgentFold, a multi-agent framework that formulates folding-model development as a closed-loop search over executable code variants. Starting from ESMFold, AgentFold proposes hypotheses, implements and debugs code-level modifications, evaluates model variants, analyzes experimental outcomes, and stores both successful and failed interventions in structured memory. An MCTS-style policy allocates computational resources across high-scoring search branches. On an engineering-scale protein-folding codebase comprising more than 2,000 lines of code, AgentFold explores approximately 80 model variants using approximately 5,000 GPU-hours and 170 million LLM tokens. Under a matched computational budget, AgentFold improves the best lDDT by 7.5% over independent Codex proposals and outperforms a random-search control. Beyond model improvement, the resulting intervention traces reveal recurring empirical design patterns: stable gains tend to arise from early, soft, learnable priors and gated refinement, whereas direct geometric perturbations and geometry-conditioned feedback often destabilize training. The code and experimental resources are publicly available at https://github.com/lmqfly/AgentFold.

</details>


### [262] [Discovering Relationships in Data Lakes Using Large Language Models: An Industrial Case](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26750)
*Ahlame Diouan,Eric Ferey,Sabine Loudcher,Jérôme Darmont*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ColRel is a two-stage approach to learn column embeddings for data lakes from ingestion-time metadata and data, using business dictionaries to interpret coded schemata and produce short NL descriptions, improving column-relationship discovery in weak-signal settings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Metadata in data lakes is often insufficient or weak for identifying column relationships, particularly in ERP-derived datasets with coded/abbreviated labels, hindering discoverability and lineage.

Method: Stage 1 builds column embeddings from available metadata and ingestion-time data. Stage 2 uses short natural-language descriptions, derived with the aid of business dictionaries to interpret coded column names, to refine relationships. This two-stage process enhances semantic signal in challenging settings.

Result: Empirical evaluation on public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset shows ColRel excels in semantically related, weak-signal scenarios compared to baselines.

Conclusion: ColRel effectively improves column relationship discovery under limited/encoded metadata, with strong performance in weak-signal regimes and practical applicability for ERP-like data landscapes.

Abstract: Data lakes rely on metadata to remain usable, yet this meta data is often limited or weakly informative for column relationship discovery, especially in ERP-derived datasets with coded or abbreviated schema labels. We propose ColRel, a two-stage method that builds column embeddings from metadata and data available at ingestion time. In difficult cases, such as coded schemata, business dictionaries help better interpret column names and support the generation of short natural-language descriptions used in the second stage. Experiments on public benchmarks and an industrial ERP dataset show that ColRel is particularly effective in semantically related, weak-signal settings.

</details>


### [263] [DEEPCHART: How Far are LLMs from Faithful Data-Science Chart Generation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26757)
*Jiahui tang,Kuicai Dong,Dexun Li,Hongchao Gu,Haocheng Yu,Wei Han,Chen Zhang,Yong Liu,Hao Wang,Enhong Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DEEPCHART is a benchmark for faithful chart generation using an Extract--Reason--Visualize pipeline, revealing that data-level hallucinations persist even with plausible visuals and larger context windows.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Grounding charts in scattered, real-world evidence is essential; LLMs can produce plausible charts but struggle with data-level accuracy in long, multimodal workflows; a benchmark is needed to measure extraction, reasoning, and rendering fidelity.

Method: Assemble 1,482 task-conditioned chart-generation instances from real-world sources (papers, filings, ecosystem reports); expert-annotate; evaluate in three stages: source-data extraction, derived-data reasoning, and chart rendering (Extract--Reason--Visualize).

Result: Visual plausibility does not guarantee data fidelity; extraction and reasoning errors are common in realistic settings; larger context windows alone are insufficient for faithful chart generation.

Conclusion: DEEPCHART provides a benchmark and resources to study and improve faithful chart generation, emphasizing reliable evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning before rendering; code/data available at the project repository.

Abstract: Faithful chart generation in real-world data-science workflows requires grounding visualizations in scattered evidence, computing chart-ready quantities, and rendering them accurately. Modern LLMs can produce visually plausible, instruction-compliant charts, yet data-level hallucinations remain difficult to detect in long, noisy, and multimodal contexts. To measure this gap, we introduce DEEPCHART, an expert-annotated benchmark of 1,482 task-conditioned chart-generation instances drawn from real-world scientific papers, financial filings, and ecosystem reports. DEEPCHART formulates chart generation as an Extract--Reason--Visualize pipeline and evaluates source-data extraction, derived-data reasoning, and chart rendering stage by stage. Experiments with state-of-the-art models show that visually plausible charts often conceal data-level hallucinations, with extraction and reasoning errors common in realistic long and multimodal settings. These findings suggest that larger context windows alone are insufficient; faithful chart generation also requires reliable evidence extraction and quantitative reasoning before rendering. Our benchmark and associated resources are available at https://github.com/tangdouer1005/DeepChart.

</details>


### [264] [Categorizer Automata for Discounted-Sum Payoffs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26763)
*Nathalie Bertrand,Pranav Ghorpade,Senthil Rajasekaran,Sasha Rubin,Moshe Vardi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A categorizer automaton partitions discounted sums into finitely many bins with a state space linear in the number of bins, enabling efficient quantitative synthesis for discounted-sum utilities in MDPs; exact for piecewise-constant utilities, epsilon-optimal for piecewise-Lipschitz utilities, with PSPACE-hardness for piecewise-constant utilities.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Discretize continuous reward streams into bins without exponential state blowup from cross-product comparator automata, enabling tractable synthesis for discounted-sum utilities, even with discontinuities.

Method: Construct categorizer automata with linear state complexity in the number of bins, generalizing comparator automata; apply to Markov decision processes to synthesize policies maximizing expected discounted-sum utility for piecewise-constant and piecewise-Lipschitz utilities; analyze complexity and hardness; derive exact, pseudo-polynomial time and approximation results.

Result: Automata with linear-in-bin state space; exact algorithm for piecewise-constant utilities; pseudo-polynomial-time algorithm yielding ε-optimal policies for piecewise-Lipschitz utilities; PSPACE-hardness established for piecewise-constant utilities.

Conclusion: Categorizer automata offer efficient discretization for discounted-sum rewards, enabling tractable policy synthesis in a broader class of utility functions, while acknowledging inherent hardness in certain cases.

Abstract: Categorizing continuous data into discrete bins is a fundamental operation in artificial intelligence. We introduce the categorizer automaton, a deterministic automaton that reads an infinite sequence of rewards and identifies which of finitely many bins contains its discounted sum. Categorizer automata generalize comparator automata, the special case of two bins, which have already proven useful in quantitative synthesis. Our main technical contribution is the construction of a categorizer automaton whose state space is linear in the number of bins, rather than exponential as obtained by a cross-product of comparator automata. We then apply categorizer automata to Markov decision processes, where they allow one to synthesize policies that maximize the expected utility of a discounted-sum payoff for utility functions that may be discontinuous. For piecewise-constant utility functions, the resulting algorithm is exact and runs in pseudo-polynomial time. For piecewise-Lipschitz utility functions, a class that includes any utility with bounded slope between finitely many jumps, it again runs in pseudo-polynomial time and yields an $\varepsilon$-optimal policy. We also show that the synthesis problem considered is PSPACE-hard already for piecewise-constant utilities.

</details>


### [265] [AI Control Scientist: LLM-driven Agentic System for Automated Control Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26780)
*Haiteng Wang,Weihao Li,Jing Zhang,Lei Ren*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: AICS uses an LLM-driven, three-agent system to automatically design controllers from language requirements, outperforming automated baselines in design success and optimization efficiency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Traditional control design relies on expert knowledge, manual tuning, and lacks scalability; an LLM-driven agent framework could automate and accelerate controller design from natural-language requirements.

Method: AICS comprises three agents: Task Modeling Agent interprets requirements into engineering constraints; Controller Design Agent generates candidate controller structures and executable code; Parameter Tuning Agent optimizes parameters against closed-loop performance criteria. The workflow translates design prompts into implementable controllers and tunes them.

Result: Experiments show the system can automatically generate multiple representative control systems and outperforms existing automated baselines in design success rate and optimization efficiency.

Conclusion: The approach has potential to shift control system design from human-driven to agent-driven, enabling future integration with model predictive control and other advanced control paradigms.

Abstract: Control system design is critical for modern industry, such as chemical process temperature regulation and aero-engine control. However,traditional control design workflows rely heavily on expert knowledge and extensive manual parameter tuning, resulting in limited efficiency and scalability. To this end, this paper proposes AI Control Scientist (AICS), the first large language model (LLM)-driven agent capable of automatically generating optimized controller from language design requirements. Specifically, a Task Modeling Agent interprets user requirements to engineering constraints; a Controller Design Agent generate candidate controller structures and executable code; and a Parameter Tuning Agent refine controller parameters under closed-loop performance criteria. Experiments demonstrate that the proposed agentic system can automatically generate multiple representative control systems, outperforms existing automated baselines in both design success rate and optimization efficiency. This work has the potential to transform control system design from human-driven to agent-driven, paving the way for model predictive control and other advanced control systems design.

</details>


### [266] [SymbolLKG: Towards Verifiable Logical Reasoning via Logical Knowledge Graph and Symbolic Solvers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26836)
*Haizhao Fan,Yuchi Xiong,Jize Wang,Xinping Guan,Xinyi Le*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A neuro-symbolic framework combines an ontology-based Logical Knowledge Graph with dynamic solver routing to improve multi-step logical reasoning, transparency, and verifiability beyond standard CoT and RAG approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address the limitations of purely neural prompting strategies (hallucinations, inconsistencies) and the inability of standard RAG to capture complex logical dependencies by introducing explicit, topology-aware symbolic reasoning.

Method: Introduce an ontology-based LKG with topological nodes representing logical rules/constraints; implement a Logic Router to dynamically route tasks to the most suitable symbolic engine; employ a topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism to support the reasoning process.

Result: Empirical evaluation on logical reasoning benchmarks shows the framework achieves higher accuracy and provides verifiable reasoning paths, outperforming state-of-the-art prompting and RAG baselines.

Conclusion: The framework advances neuro-symbolic reasoning by explicitly modeling dependencies with an LKG and adaptive routing, addressing hallucination and dependency issues and enabling verifiable, structured reasoning.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) have demonstrated remarkable proficiency in natural language understanding, yet they struggle with strict multi-step reasoning, frequently suffering from hallucinations and inconsistency. Existing solutions like Chain-of-Thought (CoT) lack rigorous verification mechanisms, while standard Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) often misses the complex, structural dependencies inherent in logical tasks. To bridge this gap, we propose a Neuro-Symbolic architecture that integrates a Logical Knowledge Graph (LKG) with dynamic solver routing. Specifically, we introduce an ontology-based LKG that treats logical rules and constraints as first-class topological nodes, enabling explicit modeling of dependencies extracted from text. We further design a Logic Router to dynamically dispatch tasks to the optimal symbolic engine, which is supported by a topology-aware hybrid retrieval mechanism. Experimental results on logical reasoning benchmarks demonstrate that our framework significantly outperforms state-of-the-art prompting and RAG baselines, delivering higher accuracy and verifiable reasoning paths.

</details>


### [267] [LiveSim: Simulating Environment-Shaped Users in Multi-Agent Live-Stream Ecosystems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26849)
*Jiaqi Xu,Yiran Qiao,Jing Chen,Qiwei Zhong,Xiang Ao,Xueqi Cheng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LiveSim uses editable LLM-based user hypotheses and trajectory-grounded feedback to dynamically simulate live-stream ecosystems, building a transferable environment-behavior memory to boost fidelity and ecosystem-level insights.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Static, historical user profiles underrepresent evolving interaction dynamics in socially intensive environments like live streaming; there is a need for adaptive, trajectory-informed simulation that captures environmental shaping effects and supports risk analysis.

Method: Represent users as editable behavioral hypotheses; conduct trajectory-grounded interactions to reveal discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories; extract transferable environment-behavior patterns from these signals; accumulate patterns in a collective behavioral memory to refine user-level fidelity and enable ecosystem-level simulation; leverage LLMs to reason and update hypotheses and patterns.

Result: Empirical evaluation on real-world live-stream risk-control data shows improved user-level behavioral fidelity and enables ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and the effects of platform interventions.

Conclusion: LiveSim demonstrates that trajectory-grounded refinement and collective behavioral memory enable more faithful, scalable live-stream ecosystem simulations and facilitate risk-aware analysis and intervention evaluation.

Abstract: User behavior simulation with large language models~(LLMs) is increasingly used to support multi-agent ecosystem simulation. Existing simulators typically rely on static user profiles inferred from historical observations, which become inadequate in socially intensive environments such as live streaming where interaction dynamics continuously reshape user behavior. We propose \textbf{LiveSim}, an LLM-based framework for live-stream ecosystem simulation. It represents users as editable behavioral hypotheses and progressively refines them through trajectory-grounded interactions, where discrepancies between simulated and observed trajectories reveal missing environmental shaping effects. These signals are further extracted as transferable environment-behavior patterns and accumulated in a collective behavioral memory to improve user-level behavioral fidelity and support ecosystem-level simulation. Experiments on real-world live-stream risk-control data validate the effectiveness of LiveSim in improving user-level behavioral fidelity and enabling ecosystem-level analysis of risk evolution and platform intervention effects.

</details>


### [268] [BekchiAI: Measuring, Observing, and Controlling LLM Agents in One Click](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26867)
*Mesut Toruk*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BekchiAI introduces a benchmark and live platform to evaluate and observe agentic skills in LLM agents. It includes 13 tool-using ReAct agents across 7 task categories, 2,057 deterministic tasks with verifier-checkable gold answers, and additional behavioral metrics. It also provides BekchiAI-Platform for telemetry and live control, with evaluation scripts and public release.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address the inability of current leaderboards to reliably measure core agentic skills (tool sequencing, dependency planning, input trust judgments, grounding) and to provide a governance-capable, observable platform for deployed agents.

Method: Build BekchiAI-Benchmark (13 tool-using ReAct agents across arithmetic, SQL, security, URL grounding, planning, orchestration, tool-policy) with 2,057 deterministic tasks. Gold answers are computed via canonical SQL, DAG schedules, or closed-form lambdas, including adversarial samples. Define behavioral metrics beyond accuracy and cost. Compare four models (Qwen3.7-Max, gemma-4-31B-it, gemma4:26b, gpt-oss-120b) using evaluation scripts. Build BekchiAI-Platform for telemetry and remote control. Public release of benchmark, tools, and platform.

Result: A comprehensive benchmark with 2,057 tasks across multiple categories, tied to verifiable gold answers; behavioral metrics and per-model cost provided; four-model comparison reported; evaluation scripts and platform released for public use.

Conclusion: BekchiAI offers a dual solution: a robust, verifiable benchmark for agentic skill assessment and a live observability/control platform to manage deployed agents, enabling more reliable measurement and governance of autonomous agent behavior.

Abstract: Large language model agents reason, call tools, and act autonomously over many steps, but their agentic skills-correctly sequencing tools, planning under dependencies, judging untrusted inputs, and grounding generated arguments-are hard to measure with accuracy-only leaderboards. We present BekchiAI, which addresses both sides: a benchmark for measuring agentic skill and a platform for observing and controlling live agents. The BekchiAI-Benchmark, a suite of 13 tool-using ReAct agents across 7 task categories (arithmetic, structured/SQL, security detection, URL grounding, planning, orchestration, and tool-policy), totalling 2,057 deterministic, committed test tasks. Every task is verifier-checkable gold answers are computed by running canonical SQL against a real database, computing the exact schedule of a directed acyclic graph (DAG), or evaluating closed-form lambdas including adversarial security samples paired with deliberately imperfect signature scanners so a score reflects the model's own judgment, not the copying of an oracle. We define a small set of behavioral metrics beyond accuracy-tool-call adherence, URL hallucination and source-match, and per-model token cost and report a four-model comparison (Qwen3.7-Max, gemma-4-31B-it, gemma4:26b, gpt-oss-120b) whose story is in the per-family spread, not the aggregate. The benchmark runs are executed using the provided evaluation scripts. BekchiAI-Platform is a complementary web-based observability and control layer for deployed agents, providing full token and latency telemetry as well as remote run termination. The benchmark, evaluation tools, and platform are publicly released.

</details>


### [269] [C-Unseen: Weak Signal Detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs via LLM Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26870)
*Yassir Lairgi,Ludovic Moncla,Khalid Benabdeslem,Rémy Cazabet,Pierre Cléau*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: C-Unseen is a self-interpretable framework for weak signal detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs, using an LLM-based Rare Subgraphs Extractor to identify semantically rare subgraphs and a Weak Signal Alerter to track their persistence over time, outperforming keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Weak signals precede significant changes but are not well captured by frequency-based, topic-modeling, or untyped graph methods; capturing semantic and relational structure in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs) is essential for timely detection.

Method: Two-module architecture: (1) Rare Subgraphs Extractor uses a large language model with chain-of-thought reasoning to identify subgraphs whose content is in tension with the dominant snapshot narrative; (2) Weak Signal Alerter tracks the persistence of these rare subgraphs across consecutive DTKG snapshots to isolate true weak signals.

Result: Empirical evaluation shows that C-Unseen outperforms keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines in detecting weak signals within DTKGs.

Conclusion: Proposes a self-interpretable DTKG weak-signal detector that leverages semantic-relational cues via LLM reasoning and temporal persistence tracking, demonstrating effective early detection of weak signals.

Abstract: Weak signals are early, low-visibility indicators that precede significant changes before those changes become established. Existing detection methods, based on keyword frequency, topic modeling, or untyped graph topology, fail to capture the semantic and relational structure through which such signals manifest. In this paper, we propose C-Unseen, a self-interpretable framework for weak signal detection in Dynamic Temporal Knowledge Graphs (DTKGs). We define a weak signal as a rare, semantically coherent subgraph that proliferates across consecutive TKG snapshots. The framework operates through two modules: a Rare Subgraphs Extractor, in which an LLM identifies subgraphs whose content is in tension with the dominant snapshot narrative via chain-of-thought reasoning, and a Weak Signal Alerter, in which the persistence of these rare subgraphs is tracked across time steps to isolate true weak signals. Experimental results demonstrate that C-Unseen outperforms keyword-, topic-, and graph-based baselines.

</details>


### [270] [Evaluating human and LLM screening workflows in a conceptually complex scoping review: Recall--workload trade-offs and run-to-run consistency](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26885)
*Nikol Figalová,Lynn Huestegge,Anne Böckler-Raettig*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can support screening but performance hinges on workflow configuration; in a preregistered study, human-plus-LLM pipelines with file-batch processing achieved similar recall to humans but varied in workload; autonomous exclusion underperformed; high recall requires supervised, auditable processes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether LLMs can replace or augment human screening in evidence synthesis and quantify how workflow choices affect recall and workload.

Method: Preregistered study within a scoping review; initial title-screen; 1,131 records screened by a lead and four assistants; seven LLM runs across models and batch configurations; metrics: workload, recall (against 316 verified), agreement, consistency, burden; reported recall as operational.

Result: No workflow achieved 100% recall. Human and GPT-5.4 file-batch runs captured about 42-45% of records with ~82.3-82.9% recall. Gemini 3.1 batch had higher recall (83.9%) but retained 56.7% records. All-at-once configurations underperformed compared to file-batch. Two GPT-5.4 file-batch runs agreed on 91.7% but diverged on 94 records, including 29 true positives captured by only one run.

Conclusion: Workflow design, workload distribution, and human-LLM integration are critical; model identity alone does not determine performance; for high-recall needs, validated, auditable human-supervised workflows are preferable to autonomous exclusion.

Abstract: Background. Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used for screening in evidence synthesis, where false negatives can remove relevant studies before full-text assessment. We compared human and LLM title-and-abstract screening workflows in a preregistered study embedded in a conceptually complex scoping review.
  Methods. After a conservative title-only screen, 1,131 records were screened by one review lead, four trained assistants screening non-overlapping subsets, and seven complete LLM runs using different models and processing configurations, including a nominally identical repeat run. We compared retained workload, operational recall against 316 verified eligible records, agreement, run-to-run consistency, and procedural burden. Because eligibility was verified only for records advanced and assessed in the parent review, recall estimates were operational.
  Results. No workflow recovered all verified eligible records. The human workflows and two GPT-5.4 file-batch runs retained 42.2-45.0% of records while achieving 82.3-82.9% recall. Gemini 3.1 file batches achieved the highest recall (83.9%) but retained 56.7% of records. All-at-once configurations recovered fewer eligible records than corresponding file-batch configurations. Two nominally identical GPT-5.4 file-batch runs agreed on 91.7% of records but differed on 94 records, including 29 verified eligible records retained by only one run.
  Discussion. LLM screening performance depended on the implemented workflow, not model identity alone. Processing configuration, workload, record-level variation, and human-LLM decision integration are therefore substantive properties of deployed systems. For high-recall tasks, LLMs are better suited to validated, auditable, human-supervised workflows than autonomous exclusion.

</details>


### [271] [Learning-Augmented Online Allocation under Unreliable Advice: Robustness, Exposure Fairness, and Distribution Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26889)
*Fredy Pokou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A learning-augmented online allocation framework with finite candidate sets and irreversible decisions that enforces exposure fairness via a robust rule; under bounded-error predictions, it achieves consistency and robustness with an error-proportional loss; experiments show resilience to adversarial advice and reduced exposure disparity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Improve online allocation decisions by leveraging predictions while safeguarding against unreliable advice and fairness concerns due to exposure disparities in irreversible decisions.

Method: Develop a robust rule that blends predictions with a conservative fallback and a fairness correction term; analyze under bounded-error assumptions; prove that performance loss scales with prediction error; model exposure constraints and evaluate empirically.

Result: Theoretical guarantees of consistency and robustness; loss is proportional to prediction error; experimental results show stability under adversarial advice and a substantial reduction in exposure disparity.

Conclusion: Learning-augmented approach can deliver robust, fair performance in online allocation with irreversible decisions and exposure constraints; bounded-error analysis yields predictable degradation; empirical results support practicality and effectiveness.

Abstract: Learning-augmented algorithms improve online decisions using predictions, but unreliable advice may harm efficiency and fairness. We study an online allocation problem with finite candidate sets, irreversible decisions, and exposure constraints. We propose a robust and fair rule combining advice with a conservative fallback and fairness correction. Under bounded-error assumptions, we prove consistency and robustness with loss proportional to prediction error. Experiments show stability under adversarial advice and significant reductions in exposure disparity.

</details>


### [272] [AI agents in Algorithmic Electricity Markets: On the Emergence of Tacit Collusion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26896)
*Jakub Seredyński,Georgios Tsaousoglou*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Learning-based bidding agents in electricity markets can spontaneously sustain supra-competitive outcomes, suggesting tacit collusion is plausible; the study uses a repeated game with imperfect monitoring and multi-agent reinforcement learning, plus a multi-dimensional criteria set beyond Nash profits to detect collusion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: As markets digitize, autonomous agents optimize bids in oligopolistic, repeated-interaction markets. Prior evidence from other algorithmic markets shows tacit collusion can arise without explicit coordination. This work seeks to assess whether such tacit collusion can occur in electricity markets and how to detect it.

Method: Model the market as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring. Train bidding strategies via multi-agent reinforcement learning (MARL). Develop a multi-dimensional criterion set, beyond profit relative to Nash equilibria, to evaluate collusive-like behavior. Conduct experiments to observe emergent patterns.

Result: Experiments indicate agents can learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that align with tacit collusion indicators, even without any collusion instruction. This demonstrates a realistic risk of tacit collusion in learning-enabled electricity markets.

Conclusion: Tacit collusion in electricity markets is a realistic threat when participants use autonomous learning agents. The work provides detection criteria beyond Nash-based metrics and highlights the need for market design and monitoring strategies to mitigate such non-competitive outcomes.

Abstract: As electricity market participants increasingly adopt learning-based agents for their bidding strategies, electricity markets are becoming algorithmic. Evidence from algorithmic markets in other domains shows that tacit collusion can arise purely through independent learning. Moreover, electricity markets are typically oligopolistic and feature repeated interaction among a small number of participants, making them structurally susceptible to non-competitive behavior. In the face of these observations, this paper investigates the hypothesis that tacit collusion may emerge in electricity markets where participants' actions are controlled by autonomous learning-based algorithms. We model strategic bidding as a repeated game with imperfect public monitoring, and model the participants' emergent behavior using multi-agent reinforcement learning. We propose a multi-dimensional set of criteria (going beyond profit comparisons against Nash equilibria) to assess whether the resulting behavior constitutes tacit collusion. Our experimental results showcase that such a danger is realistic for electricity markets: there are cases where agents do learn to sustain supra-competitive outcomes that are supportive of tacit collusion indicators, even though the agents were never instructed to collude.

</details>


### [273] [Counterfactual Bias Testing for Application Tracking System](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26899)
*Sai Yashwant,Shruti Bansal,Anurag Dubey,Samaroha Chatterjee,Satyam Kumar,Shreyash Gupta,Gantala Thulsiram*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A generalizable LLM-agent-based workflow for auditing demographic bias in candidate-job matching, using synthetic, identity-neutral resumes with demographic treatments across multiple axes, a multi-metric fairness suite, and automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL reporting; reveals that relying on single metrics misses rank-stability issues, advocating multi-metric auditing and cost-effective LLM-assisted audits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Auditing automated candidate-job matching for demographic bias is costly with traditional correspondence audits and not scalable to fast retraining cycles. A reusable, scalable methodology is needed that can probe bias across protected attributes and provide actionable, multi-metric fairness diagnostics aligned with regulatory expectations (EU AI Act).

Method: 1) Use task-specialized LLM agents to synthesize identity-neutral base resumes and inject controlled demographic treatments across five axes (sex/gender, age, residence, language, disability), forming a K x (1+N) correspondence-audit matrix. 2) Qualitatively flag inferred protected characteristics via an EU AI Act-aligned prompt. 3) Rank candidates against a job description using a fine-tuned sentence-embedding model and cosine similarity. 4) Compute a nine-metric fairness suite spanning counterfactual (score delta, mean absolute rank change, flip rate), group-fairness (top-K retention, four-fifths/impact ratio), and merit-aware (Recall@K, nDCG@K, equal opportunity, equalized odds) with bootstrap CIs, significance tests, and Benjamini-Hochberg correction, producing an automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL report with a composite risk score.

Result: With a corpus of 5 job orders, 100 base candidates, and 10 demographic treatments (90 metric x variant evaluations), score shifts, top-K retention, and merit-aware rate gaps remained within tolerance for all treatments. However, a rank-stability metric (MARC) and nDCG@K surfaced borderline findings—including a case on the baseline itself—that a score- or retention-only view would miss.

Conclusion: The study supports multi-metric, multi-family auditing over single-score summaries and demonstrates that LLM-agent-generated audits provide a practical, low-cost complement to human-curated audits for any candidate-job matching pipeline.

Abstract: Automated candidate-job matching systems are increasingly classified as high-risk AI under emerging regulation, yet auditing them for demographic bias is expensive: classical correspondence-audit studies require hand-crafted resumes and manual submission, which does not scale to fast pipeline retraining cycles. This paper presents a general, reusable methodology that (1) uses task-specialized LLM agents to synthesize identity-neutral base resumes and inject controlled demographic treatments across five protected-characteristic axes (sex/gender, age, residence, language, disability), producing a K x (1+N) correspondence-audit matrix; (2) qualitatively flags inferred protected characteristics per an EU AI Act-aligned prompt; (3) ranks candidates against a job description via a fine-tuned sentence-embedding model and cosine similarity; and (4) computes a nine-metric fairness suite spanning counterfactual (score delta, mean absolute rank change, flip rate), group-fairness (top-K retention, four-fifths/impact ratio), and merit-aware (Recall@K, nDCG@K, equal opportunity, equalized odds) families, each with bootstrap confidence intervals, significance tests, and Benjamini-Hochberg correction, culminating in an automated PASS/INVESTIGATE/FAIL report with a composite risk score.
