On an example corpus of 5 job orders, 100 base candidates, and 10 demographic treatments (90 metric x variant evaluations): score shifts, top-K retention, and merit-aware rate gaps stay within tolerance for every treatment, but a rank-stability metric (MARC) and nDCG@K each surface borderline findings - including one on the neutral baseline itself - that a score- or retention-only view would miss. The results argue for multi-metric, multi-family auditing over any single aggregate score, and for LLM-agent-generated audits as a practical, low-cost complement to human-curated audits for any candidate-job matching pipeline.

</details>


### [274] [A Table Is Worth 64 Tokens: Pixel-level Compression for Multi-Table Document Question Answering](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26949)
*Iñigo Alonso,Mirella Lapata*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Pixel-level table compression via image-based representations can substantially reduce token usage in long-document QA with tables, when combined with a two-step, training-free approach to select relevant tables and reason at native resolution.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To reduce token cost in VLM QA for long documents containing interleaved text and tables, while maintaining table understanding, by exploring optical context compression (images) and its impact on tables.

Method: Evaluate five VLMs across two QA benchmarks and five visual-token budgets; represent tables as native-resolution images; explore downscaling effects; propose a training-free two-step method: (1) identify relevant tables from pixel-compressed context, (2) reason about those tables at native resolution.

Result: Native-resolution table representations achieve parity with or better efficiency than image-compressed inputs; aggressive downscaling degrades reasoning efficiency unless tables are only needed for relevance; two-step method saves 41% of tokens and gains 7 accuracy points vs single-step native-resolution QA; uses 15% fewer tokens than best single-step compressed setup with no accuracy loss.

Conclusion: Pixel-level compression is viable for long-document QA with tables when combined with a targeted two-step approach; significant token savings without sacrificing accuracy, depending on the ability to detect which tables are relevant.

Abstract: Answering questions over real-world documents requires processing long inputs that interleave text with tables. Optical context compression, which represents context as images, promises to reduce token cost, but its effect on table understanding remains unclear. We study pixel-level table compression for question answering over documents with multiple tables, evaluating five VLMs across two benchmarks and five visual-token budgets. Representing tables as images at native resolution matches text in both performance and efficiency, but downscaling them makes models compensate the loss in readability with longer, less effective reasoning traces that cancel the expected savings. Highly downscaled tables, however, preserve enough signal to identify whether they are relevant to a question. We exploit this asymmetry with a training-free, two-step method: the model first identifies the tables needed to answer a question from a pixel-compressed context, and then reasons over those at native resolution. On long documents, our method saves 41% of total tokens and gains 7 accuracy points over single-step QA with native resolution tables. It also uses 15% fewer tokens than the most efficient single-step compressed configuration, with no accuracy loss.

</details>


### [275] [From Atomic to Agentic: Towards Interpretable Evaluation of LLMs' Agentic Mathematical Capabilities](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26950)
*Jiayi Kuang,Yinghui Li,Yunze Song,Keyu Chen,Zhifeng Shen,Yangning Li,Yidong Wang,Di Yin,Ruizhi Qiao,Xing Sun,Kai Jin,Ying Shen,Liang Lin,Philip S. Yu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A process-level benchmark evaluates agentic mathematical reasoning in LLMs, linking problem-solving to a taxonomy of atomic capabilities, with planning, action, and feedback tasks across text and multimodal contexts; automated trajectory synthesis and fine-grained annotations reveal that similar end-to-end accuracy can mask differing agentic skill profiles.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current math benchmarks focus on final answers, offering limited insight into reasoning processes. A process-level evaluation is needed to diagnose failures in logic, guide the development of robust, agentic LLMs, and measure intrinsic planning and action capabilities beyond surface accuracy.

Method: Develop a taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities; design a suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks in textual and multimodal settings; implement an automated pipeline to generate high-quality problem-solving trajectories and produce fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting.

Result: Experiments show substantial variation in agentic capability profiles among models with similar end-to-end accuracy, indicating process-level metrics reveal capabilities that end-to-end metrics miss.

Conclusion: Process-level evaluation is essential to interpret the true potential of LLMs as mathematical agents and to guide the next generation of mathematical agents through diagnostic, trajectory-based assessment.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) are evolving from performing end-to-end mathematical reasoning to integrating agentic intelligence. However, most existing math benchmarks evaluate only final answers. This outcome-oriented evaluation provides limited diagnostic value for identifying process-level failures or rigorous logic, failing to guide the transformation of LLMs into robust agents. To bridge this gap, we present a process-level benchmark designed to evaluate the inherent agentic mathematical reasoning abilities of LLMs. Our framework aligns problem-solving agentic behaviors with a structured taxonomy of reusable mathematical atomic capabilities. We design a comprehensive suite of planning, action, and feedback tasks across both textual and multimodal contexts, supported by an automated pipeline that synthesizes high-quality trajectories and produces fine-grained annotations via controlled LLM rewriting. Experiments reveal that models with similar end-to-end accuracy can exhibit markedly different agentic capability profiles. This demonstrates that process-level evaluation is crucial for interpreting the true potential of LLMs and guiding the development of next-generation mathematical agents.

</details>


### [276] [GraphMemix: Query-Aware Evidence Forests for Long-Term Multimodal Agent Memory](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26983)
*Geng Li,Yuhao Wang,Dong Li,Jianye Hao,Yuxin Peng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GraphMemix presents a graph-based memory organization for multimodal agents that builds a query-aware evidence forest to store long-term memories. It expands seed memories through schema/semantic relations, separates evidence utility from activation costs to reduce redundancy, and optimizes a forest-format memory under an evidence budget, achieving better accuracy with lower lifecycle cost and a new Pareto frontier across four benchmarks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Organizing long-term memory for multimodal agents is hampered by expensive, task-agnostic offline summaries and naive embedding-based retrieval, which produce incomplete or redundant context. A query-aware, structured memory could improve relevance and efficiency.

Method: Three components: (1) candidate graph construction by expanding multi-view seed memories via schema and semantic relations to obtain query-relevant context; (2) evidence utility and activation costs that decouple memory support from anchor-conditioned relation verification to suppress redundancy; (3) forest optimization that jointly selects a forest-format memory under a maximum evidence budget and with reliable relational structure.

Result: Empirical evaluation on four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks shows significant improvements across different foundation models and demonstrates a new Pareto frontier between accuracy and lifecycle cost.

Conclusion: Memory organized as a query-relevant subgraph with a forest structure enables efficient, non-redundant retrieval and reliable relational context, reducing lifecycle costs while maintaining or improving accuracy.

Abstract: Organizing long-term memory for multimodal agents remains challenging because existing methods either suffer from expensive question-agnostic offline summaries or naive embedding similarity matching that introduces incomplete and redundant context. To address these issues, we propose GraphMemix, a combinatorial-optimization graph memory framework that models memory organization as query-aware evidence-forest construction. Specifically, our method consists of three key components:(1) candidate graph construction, which expands multi-view seed memories through schema and semantic relations to acquire query-aware original context; (2) evidence utility and activation costs, which decouples direct memory support from anchor-conditioned relation verification to suppress redundant or conflicting information; and (3) forest optimization, which jointly selects a forest-format memory context under a maximum evidence budget and its reliable relational structure. By organizing memory into a query-relevant subgraph, the method avoids substantial lifecycle cost and recovers low-similarity complementary evidence. Experimental results across four long-term multimodal memory benchmarks demonstrate significant improvements with different foundation models and establish a new Pareto frontier between accuracy and lifecycle cost.

</details>


### [277] [DSA: Evidence-Aware LLM-Agent Orchestration for Multi-Market Stock Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26990)
*Linsen Zhu,Yi Shi*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: DSA is an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research using LLM agents; it structures evidence gathering, context construction, model routing, optional Role Skill reasoning, and report generation with safeguards; it supports default and agentic profiles, and demonstrates implementation conformance via offline contract tests, not evidence of superior quality or returns.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To overcome limitations of using LLMs for finance: LLMs alone cannot assemble heterogeneous evidence, reveal unavailable data/model capabilities, or govern how generated opinions influence final reports.

Method: DSA partitions workflow into: evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context/diagnostics. It offers default and agentic profiles that share services but enforce profile-specific validation and governance. In the agentic profile, role outputs are parsed by role-specific parsers; Strategy Skill opinions undergo a signal-eligibility partition before synthesis; disagreements are fed to a decision agent with a conservative risk override. The reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen bundled Strategy Skills, hosted/local model routes, and multiple execution/delivery surfaces.

Result: At a frozen software snapshot, 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed; 596 cases were retrospectively mapped to six contract families central to the LLM-agent architecture. This demonstrates implementation conformance for the tested software contracts, but does not establish superiority in report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

Conclusion: DSA provides a concrete architecture and validated implementation contracts for evidence-aware LLM-agent stock research, emphasizing governance, modular orchestration, and risk safeguards. The abstract does not claim improved decision quality, only conformance of software contracts and architecture.

Abstract: Large language models can summarize financial information, but an operational stock-research system must first assemble heterogeneous evidence, expose unavailable data and model capabilities, and control how generated opinions affect a final report. We present DSA, an evidence-aware orchestration framework for multi-market stock research with large language model (LLM) agents. DSA organizes the workflow into evidence acquisition, structured context construction, model-routed analysis, optional role and Strategy Skill reasoning, and report generation with selected context and diagnostics. A default report profile and an optional agentic profile share evidence and model-routing services but use profile-specific output validation and risk safeguards. In the agentic profile, core role outputs are processed by role-specific parsers, whereas Strategy Skill opinions undergo an additional signal-eligibility partition before synthesis; disagreement is supplied explicitly to the decision agent, followed by a conservative risk override. The reference implementation includes six regional market paths, fifteen bundled Strategy Skills, hosted and local model routes, and multiple execution and delivery surfaces. At a frozen software snapshot, a selected manifest of 1,457 portable offline backend contract tests passed; 596 cases were retrospectively mapped to six contract families central to the reported LLM-agent architecture. This evidence establishes implementation conformance for the tested software contracts, not superior report quality, forecasting accuracy, or investment returns.

</details>


### [278] [ASIL: Replacing Screenshot-and-Click with Structured State and Semantic Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.26991)
*Rui Xie,Lu Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: ASIL provides an agent-native interface for software via structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, outperforming screenshot-based GUI for long-horizon tasks across 15 apps and 380 tasks; shows strong performance with few actions and favorable training gains.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Screenshot-and-click is state-incomplete and brittle for autonomous agents; GUI actions do not align well with long-horizon planning. An application-native, structured interface can enable more robust, scalable agent operation.

Method: Instantiate ASIL across 15 applications and benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks. Compare against screenshot-only control with a repaired runtime and a 50-step budget. Benchmark against application-native interfaces (LibreOffice UNO API and draw.io MCP). Evaluate training impact with small-scale supervised fine-tuning on Qwen3.5-2B and Qwen3.5-9B, and resource-limited on-policy RL.

Result: ASIL achieves above 80 strict success with closed models while executing fewer than five actions per task. Under a repaired runtime and 50-step screenshot budget, screenshot-and-click yields 6.6 and 26.6 strict success, rising to 15.0 and 53.3 on an easier OSWorld-comparable band. Against native interfaces on matched tasks, ASIL exceeds LibreOffice UNO API by 28–38 strict points but only matches draw.io MCP content contract. For training, small-scale SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, with resource-limited RL raising to 74.4 and 82.2.

Conclusion: ASIL offers a scalable, trainable, and effective means to expose software to agents, outperforming GUI-based control and approaching or surpassing native interfaces in many tasks. The structured modality supports stronger long-horizon planning and demonstrates clear training benefits.

Abstract: Powerful code agents can execute scripts, call tools, and manage files, yet many important applications remain accessible primarily through graphical user interfaces. We argue that screenshot-and-click is an inefficient interface for software-operating agents: screenshots are state-incomplete, and GUI actions are brittle, semantically weak, and poorly matched to long-horizon planning. We introduce ASIL (Agent-Software Interaction Layer), an agent-native interface that exposes software through structured JSON observations and code-executable semantic actions, realized through the deepest feasible access path for each application. We instantiate ASIL across 15 applications and a benchmark of 300 single-application and 80 multi-application tasks. ASIL reaches above 80 with closed models while executing fewer than five actions per task. Under a repaired runtime and a 50-step screenshot budget, the same tasks yield 6.6 and 26.6 strict success under screenshot-and-click control, rising to 15.0 and 53.3 on an easier OSWorld-comparable band. Against application-native interfaces on matched tasks, ASIL exceeds LibreOffice's UNO API by 28-38 strict points but only matches draw.io's MCP content contract. The structured modality also suits training: small-scale SFT raises Qwen3.5-2B from 58.0 to 72.1 and Qwen3.5-9B from 66.6 to 80.4, and resource-limited on-policy RL further raises them to 74.4 and 82.2.

</details>


### [279] [A Multi-Modal AI Framework for Real-Time Queue Prediction, Management and Optimisation in Intelligent Border Control Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27010)
*Varvara Mama,Eleni Veroni,Nikolaos Kapsalis,Christos D. Nikolopoulos,Anargyros T. Baklezos*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Multi-modal AI framework for dynamic border control combining LSTM queue forecasting with MPC-based control; evaluated on synthetic data showing substantial improvements in prediction accuracy, waiting time, and throughput over ARIMA/rule-based baselines.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address dynamic, uncertain border traffic with real-time data integration and proactive control, improving throughput and reducing delays.

Method: Integrates heterogeneous data sources into a unified representation; uses Long Short-Term Memory networks for queue forecasting, and Model Predictive Control with scheduling optimization to derive actionable control policies for border officers.

Result: On synthetic data, queue prediction error reduced by up to 35%; average waiting time reduced by 30%; average throughput increased by ~20% compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods.

Conclusion: The approach demonstrates the potential of combining AI forecasting with optimization for proactive border traffic management, but validation on real-world data and assessment of deployment challenges are needed.

Abstract: In the present work an efficient border control management procedure is proposed. Compared to operational queue management systems, whose operations are based on mostly static data, the proposed work takes into account dynamic traffic conditions, thus enabling optimal performance, even in cases of uncertainty. To this end, we are proposing a multi-modal Artificial Intelligence (AI) framework, tailored to th needs of border control systems, which enables real-time queue prediction, management, and resource optimization. The novel proposed approach integrates heterogeneous data sources and presents them through a unified representation by employing Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) networks for queue forecasting. Furthermore, it leverages Model Predictive Control (MPC) and scheduling optimization to derive actionable control policies, which in turn can be presented to border control officers. The proposed work has been evaluated using synthetic data simulating realistic traffic. The evaluation results demonstrate that the proposed method reduces queue prediction error by up to 35% and average waiting time by 30%. Accordingly, the average throughput increases by nearly 20%, compared to ARIMA and rule-based methods. The abovementioned results show the effectiveness and efficiency of combining AI architectures with optimization techniques for proactive and adaptive border traffic management.

</details>


### [280] [Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27044)
*Wei-Yao Wang,Kazuya Tateishi,Shuyang Cui,Christian Simon,Takashi Shibuya,Shusuke Takahashi,Yuki Mitsufuji*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes OmniUE, a unified, omni-interactive embedder for text, video, and audio, enabling any-to-any embeddings via context from an omni-LLM; introduces OmniCHOIR as a benchmark for omni-interactive audio retrieval; reports consistent SOTA gains across textual, visual, and audio tasks; aims toward universal embedders.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overreliance on two-tower architectures focused on language and image limits multi-modal alignment and user-conditioned interactivity. A unified, omni-modal embedding space with omni-interactive querying remains underexplored.

Method: OmniUE learns a unified embedding space for text, video, and audio using intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens. Visual and audio segmenters handle user interactions (text, visual regions, audio spans) and integrate with an omni-LLM to generate user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. OmniCHOIR benchmarks omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval across text, video, and audio with unimodal/multimodal prompts.

Result: OmniUE consistently outperforms state-of-the-art baselines across modalities. Quantitatively, +10.5% on MMEB-v2-video, +1.1% on MAEB, +83.7% on SCaR, and +24.1% on OmniCHOIR. Provides strong evidence for joint omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying.

Conclusion: Joint advancement of omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying advances toward universal embedders, enabling flexible, user-conditioned cross-modal retrieval and interaction.

Abstract: Multimodal representation learning has been shifting from traditional two-tower architectures to large language model (LLM)-based embedders due to their strong instruction-following capabilities. Despite this progress, existing approaches primarily focus on language and image modalities, which also remain the dominant modalities for user-conditioned interactions in current embedders. In this paper, we propose the first Omni-Interactive Universal Embedder (OmniUE), which not only learns a unified embedding space across text, video, and audio by leveraging intermediate-layer representations from dedicated learnable tokens, but also supports omni-interactive querying, enabling users to provide inputs in the form of text, visual regions of interest, and audio spans. Within OmniUE, visual and audio segmenters process diverse user interactions and integrate them with an omni-LLM to produce user-conditioned any-to-any embeddings via context aggregation. To evaluate OmniUE's omni-interactive capabilities, we introduce OmniCHOIR, benchmarking models for omni-interactive compositional audio retrieval based on the given text, video, and audio as well as unimodal or multimodal interaction prompts. OmniUE consistently surpasses state-of-the-art baselines across diverse modalities, with average improvements of 10.5% on textual-interactive video benchmarks (MMEB-v2-video), 1.1% on audio tasks (MAEB), 83.7% on visual-interactive benchmarks (SCaR), and 24.1% on our omni-interactive OmniCHOIR benchmark. We believe that jointly advancing omni-modal representation learning and omni-interactive querying paves the way toward universal embedders.

</details>


### [281] [A Contract-Centered Architecture for Scalable and Manageable Agentic Runtimes](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27086)
*Yaxiao Liu,Pengbo Liu,Yiwen Liu,Yihua Guan,Zhenghe Hou,Jiaxing Song*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Proposes four organizational contracts (Skill, Harness, Scaffold, data substrate) and a contract-bounded runtime architecture to enable enterprise AI deployment. Introduces a falsifiable hypothesis P1 (cost-aware capability-capacity separability) and a cluster-period randomized crossover measurement protocol with a four-state verdict. No implementation, dataset, or results are provided; emphasis on a measurement protocol and governance model rather than a finished system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address enterprise-wide coordination and governance challenges in AI deployment, where changes in capabilities, models, data, and runtime mechanisms must be managed across units under cost and compliance constraints. Provide a formalizable, falsifiable framework to reason about ownership, changes, and evidence within a bounded budget.

Method: Proposes a runtime core A = <S, H, X> with a separate data substrate, and four responsibility objects forming contracts. Defines P1: within an operating region, changing activated capability preserves capacity-response within margin; changing Scaffold preserves capability semantics within margins; design conditions become measurable obligations. Suggests a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment with controlled seeds, washout, and a four-state verdict; outlines architecture, data substrate, and measurement protocol but no implementation.

Result: No completed implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured results reported; the work presents a theoretical framework and measurement protocol rather than empirical findings.

Conclusion: The paper offers a contract-bounded runtime architecture and a falsifiable measurement protocol to study enterprise AI governance, but requires future implementation and empirical evaluation to validate P1 and the proposed design conditions.

Abstract: Enterprise AI deployment is a coordination problem across business units, application and AI teams, testing, platform engineering, infrastructure, security, operations, and data governance. Use-case benchmarks show whether one agent completes one task, but not how changing capabilities, models, runtime mechanisms, capacity, and enterprise data should be owned, changed, admitted, or evidenced together.
  We present four responsibility objects as shared organizational contracts: Skill (reusable, versioned capability and workflow asset), Harness (runtime compiler and governor), Scaffold (execution/control boundary and NFR owner), and a stack-external data substrate under independent CIO-governed semantics and telemetry. The runtime core is A = <S, H, X>, with the data substrate outside that stack.
  The central contribution is one bounded, falsifiable hypothesis, P1 (cost-aware capability-capacity separability): within a declared operating region, changing activated capability preserves the capacity-response interaction within a preregistered equivalence margin, while changing compatible Scaffold capacity preserves capability semantics up to a non-inferiority margin, and the required controls stay within a declared enforcement budget. Six design conditions become measured obligations whose coverage, violations, uncertainty, cost, and exclusions determine whether P1 is decidable.
  We propose a cluster-period randomized crossover experiment (balanced order, reset/washout, repeated seeds and failure regimes, cluster-aware uncertainty) with a four-state verdict: supported, falsified, conditional-engineering, or inconclusive. This paper contributes a contract-bounded runtime architecture, a source-preserving data substrate, and a falsifiable measurement protocol. It reports no completed implementation, experiment, dataset, or measured result.

</details>


### [282] [pro-team at LLMs4OL 2026 Tasks Flagship and Reuse: Retrieval-Augmented Generation and Vocabulary-Constrained Filtering for Ontology Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27101)
*Shivam Mishra,Dhannu Ram Meena,Muneendra Ojha,Krishna Pratap Singh,Kuldeep Singh*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline for ontology learning (LLMs4OL 2026) with Task A (End-to-End) and Task B (Ontology Extension Reuse). Uses Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct and all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval; left-truncated prompts; vocabulary-constrained filtering for Task B. Achieves Task B: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 0.9200, Taxonomy Discovery F1 0.8540; Task A: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.7416. Limitation: no non-taxonomic relations due to closed taxonomy vocabulary.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ontology learning from text is challenged by LLM hallucinations, format inconsistencies, and a bias toward hierarchical relations. The work addresses both end-to-end ontology construction and reuse of existing extensions under a retrieval-augmented few-shot regime.

Method: Offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting using Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct for generation and all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval. Task A uses top-5 demonstrations; Task B uses top-2. Left-truncated context window preserves instructions in long prompts. Task B applies deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering of generated triples, keeping triples if at least one endpoint matches the sample's closed vocabulary and removing duplicates of the initial ontology.

Result: Task B: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 0.9200, Taxonomy Discovery F1 0.8540. Task A: Semantic Graph Similarity 0.7416. No non-taxonomic relations were extracted.

Conclusion: The approach yields strong performance on taxonomy-driven tasks but is limited by a closed relation vocabulary that omits non-taxonomic relations. Future work could broaden relation vocabularies, explore open-world relation extraction, and improve handling of long prompts.

Abstract: Ontology learning from text remains challenging despite significant progress in Large Language Models (LLMs), which can hallucinate domain terms, produce inconsistent formats, and favor hierarchical over associative relations. In the LLMs4OL 2026 Challenge, we address both the End-to-End Flagship Task (Task A) and Ontology Extension Reuse Task (Task B) using an offline retrieval-augmented few-shot prompting pipeline. Our system employs Qwen2.5-14B-Instruct with all-MiniLM-L6-v2 for demonstration retrieval, selecting the top-5 examples for Task A and top-2 for Task B. A left-truncated context-windowing strategy preserves task instructions within long prompts. For Task B, generated triples undergo deterministic vocabulary-constrained filtering, retaining triples when at least one endpoint belongs to the sample's closed term/type vocabulary and removing duplicates of the initial ontology. The approach achieves Semantic Graph Similarity of 0.8692, Term-Typing F1 of 0.9200, and Taxonomy Discovery F1 of 0.8540 on Task B, while Task A achieves 0.7416 Semantic Graph Similarity. However, no non-taxonomic relations are extracted, highlighting limitations of closed, taxonomy-oriented relation vocabularies.

</details>


### [283] [LAAF: A Layered Accountability Architecture Framework for LLM Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27102)
*Prachi Chaturvedi,Shahnawaz Ahmad,Ehsan Nowroozi,Muhammad Waqas,George Loukas,Alireza Jolfaei,Lucas Cordeiro,Pierre Dantas*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Systematic PRISMA-based review of accountability for LLMs in high-stakes settings, proposing an integrated LAAF accountability architecture, mapped to major standards and with a four-layer classification; identifies persistent gaps and tensions; not a validated artefact.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Harm from ungrounded LLM outputs in critical domains necessitates sociotechnical accountability: traceability, human oversight, governance, and documentation, aligned with regulatory and standards regimes (EU AI Act, NIST RMF, ISO 42001).

Method: PRISMA-guided search of five databases (Jan 2022–Mar 2026); 4,512 records screened, 122 primary studies included plus 12 regulatory/standards documents; sociotechnical account of accountability as actor-forum relation; four-mamily mechanisms (technical controls, human oversight, organizational governance, documentation/traceability) with maturity assessments; four-layer classification (provenance, application logic, human oversight, governance/ redress) cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, continuous monitoring; mapped to EU AI Act, NIST AI RMF Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance.

Result: Identifies four persistent gaps (under-specification of human oversight, lack of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, limited empirical evaluation) and five structural tensions unresolved by surveyed instruments. Proposes integrated LAAF architecture with cybersecurity aligned to OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025); synthesis rather than validated artefact.

Conclusion: Provides a structured, multi-layer accountability framework aligned with major regulatory standards, with maturity assessments; useful for governance and risk management in high-stakes LLM deployments, but requires empirical validation and practical deployment work.

Abstract: Large Language Models (LLMs) operate in hospitals, courtrooms, banks, and public service desks, where fluent, confident outputs are treated as authoritative even when ungrounded or incorrect. When such an output contributes to harm, who is answerable, and through what mechanisms can responsibility be traced, explained, and acted upon? Following PRISMA guidance, five databases were searched from January 2022 to March 2026 against four review questions; of 4,512 records identified, 122 primary studies were included, together with 12 regulatory and standards documents analysed as primary sources. The review consolidates a sociotechnical account of accountability as an actor-forum relation resolved into five dimensions, and synthesises mechanisms across four families: technical controls, human oversight, organisational governance, and documentation and traceability, each with a maturity assessment. The corpus is read through a four-layer classification device spanning provenance, application logic, human oversight, and governance and redress, cross-cut by traceability, role clarity, and continuous monitoring. Both are mapped onto the EU AI Act, whose high-risk obligations have applied since 2 August 2026, the NIST AI RMF with its Generative AI Profile, ISO/IEC 42001, and sectoral guidance in healthcare, consumer finance, education, and the public sector. Four persistent gaps emerge: under-specification of human oversight, absence of shared accountability metrics, disciplinary disconnection, and limited empirical evaluation, alongside five structural tensions that no surveyed instrument resolves. The review closes by consolidating the classification device into an integrated accountability architecture, LAAF, with cybersecurity aligned to the OWASP LLM Top 10 (2025); it is a synthesis of the surveyed evidence rather than a validated artefact.

</details>


### [284] [TransMeme: A Multi-Agent Framework for Cross-Cultural Meme Transcreation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27127)
*Jingyi Zheng,Yule Liu,Zifan Peng,Tianyi Hu,Yuemeng Zhao,Xinhu Zheng,Xinlei He*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A multi-agent framework for cross-cultural meme transcreation (Chinese↔English) that preserves intent and multimodal coherence while adapting culture-specific meaning. Demonstrates superior performance over baselines in human evaluation and LLM-based ranking, with remaining bottlenecks in humor transfer and image-text alignment.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To enable mutual understanding in multicultural online communication by addressing limitations of ordinary translation for memes, which require aligning text, imagery, cultural context, and communicative intent.

Method: A coordinated, multi-agent system with specialized agents for cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment, plus feedback loops to handle difficult cases. Evaluated on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using human judgments and LLM-as-a-Judge.

Result: The framework consistently outperforms baselines across both evaluation settings: 33.1% average improvement in human evaluation, and highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% vs 26% for the second-best) in LLM-based evaluation; ablations indicate each component contributes to performance.

Conclusion: Cross-cultural meme transcreation benefits from coordinated, multimodal-aware architectures; future work should target humor reconstruction and improved image-text alignment to close remaining gaps in humor transfer and cultural adaption.

Abstract: Internet memes are a pervasive form of multimodal online communication; however, such communication often involves users from diverse linguistic and cultural backgrounds. Therefore, adapting memes across cultures and languages is a central challenge for enabling mutual understanding in online communication. Unlike ordinary translation or standalone text rewriting, cross-cultural meme transcreation must jointly preserve communicative intent, adapt culture-dependent meaning for the target audience, and maintain coherence between text and image. In this work, we first provide an explicit task analysis of cross-cultural meme transcreation and identify three core challenges: culture-specific knowledge understanding, intent and tone preservation, and multimodal consistency. Based on this analysis, we propose a multi-agent framework with specialized agents that are coordinated to address these challenges through cultural adaptation, target text rewriting, revision, and conditional visual adjustment. The framework strengthens target text adaptation with coordinated feedback to handle difficult cases that require deeper cultural or visual intervention. We evaluate the framework on bidirectional Chinese-English meme transcreation using both human evaluation and LLM-as-a-Judge. Our method consistently outperforms all baselines across both evaluation settings. In human evaluation, it achieves the best performance on all four dimensions and delivers a 33.1% average improvement over the strongest baseline, while in LLM-as-a-Judge, it attains the highest Top-1 ranking rate (60% versus 26% for the second-best baseline). Further analysis indicates that each component contributes to the performance. Our error analysis suggests that the remaining bottlenecks lie in humor reconstruction and image-text alignment rather than simple cultural knowledge gaps, pointing to future work on humor transfer.

</details>


### [285] [GRAIN: Bridging Name and Narrative Shifts in Real-World Graph Reasoning through Invariance-Rewarded Agentic RL](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27142)
*Zike Yuan,Han Zhang,Jianzhi Yan,Le Liu,Cai Ke,Huozhi Zhou,Jian Xie,Jiran Yin,Yukun Cao,Yue Yu,Hui Wang,Ming Liu,Bing Qin*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: GRAIN is a single-agent RL-based framework that robustly extracts graph structures from text by treating reasoning as semantic parsing and tool execution, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward; it outperforms multi-agent baselines (16.45% accuracy gain) with lower latency (−24%), and improves OOD generalization; GRIT benchmarks linguistic shift sensitivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: LLMs are brittle to shifts in node identifiers and task phrasing, making text-to-graph parsing fragile. Deterministic graph tools are robust but expensive when using multi-agent systems; thus a single-agent, RL-guided approach aims to learn robust mappings without incurring high latency.

Method: Model reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline. Train a single-agent LLM with reinforcement learning using a Structure Invariance Reward that validates intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies, encouraging robust, not surface-level, mappings. Introduces GRIT as a benchmark for linguistic-shift sensitivity.

Result: GRAIN achieves 16.45% higher accuracy than multi-agent baselines with ~24% lower latency; it halves the OOD gap of SFT models (from 15.77% to 7.80%) and shows robust performance on large-scale graphs beyond training distribution.

Conclusion: Single-agent RL with a structure-invariance objective yields robust text-to-graph extraction, reducing latency and improving generalization; GRIT provides a principled benchmark for evaluating sensitivity to linguistic shifts.

Abstract: Despite their potential in standardized graph tasks, Large Language Models (LLMs) remain brittle to real-world shifts in node identifiers and task formulation. While deterministic graph tools are invariant to such shifts, extracting topological structures from noisy text is highly fragile for LLMs, which often overfit to surface patterns. Moreover, mitigating these parsing failures via multi-agent systems incurs prohibitive latency. To address this, we propose GRAIN, a single-agent framework optimized via reinforcement learning. GRAIN models reasoning as a semantic parsing and tool-execution pipeline, guided by a Structure Invariance Reward. By validating extracted intermediate graphs against ground-truth topologies, this reward forces the LLM to learn robust text-to-structure mappings rather than memorizing linguistic artifacts. We also introduce GRIT, a benchmark evaluating sensitivity to such linguistic shifts. GRAIN outperforms multi-agent baselines by 16.45\% in accuracy with approximately 24\% lower latency. Furthermore, it demonstrates superior structural generalization, halving the out-of-distribution (OOD) gap of SFT models (from 15.77\% to 7.80\%) and maintaining robustness on large-scale graphs beyond the training distribution.

</details>


### [286] [Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering for Graph Anomaly Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27144)
*Xiang Wang,Zhijun Cheng,Zhenyu Meng*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: JPGFN introduces an adaptive, frequency-domain graph anomaly detection framework addressing static filters, feature importance, and label utilization by combining a Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN), adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering, and a node label constraint module, achieving superior results on real-world datasets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current GAD methods rely on static graph filters that do not adapt to graph data's frequency distribution, neglect per-feature importance within node feature vectors, and underutilize available node labels, limiting detection performance.

Method: 1) Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) performs feature separation across dimensions and applies nonlinear transformations to learn fine-grained node features. 2) An adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module captures complex frequency-domain features via Jacobi polynomials to adapt to data. 3) A node label constraint module integrates node labels to guide anomaly detection.

Result: Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets show that JPGFN significantly outperforms mainstream approaches.

Conclusion: The proposed framework effectively addresses the three identified challenges in GAD, delivering improved performance and demonstrating the value of combining feature-separation learning, adaptive spectral filtering, and label-informed constraints.

Abstract: In recent years, graph anomaly detection (GAD) based on frequency-domain filtering have achieved promising results. However, existing approaches still face three major challenges: First, they use static basic function to constructed graph filter which cannot effectively adapt to the frequency-domain distribution of graph data. Second, they fail to adequately consider the importance information of each attribute in the node feature vector, leading to the loss of fine-grained information. Third, they insufficiently utilize node labels for GAD. To address these issues, this paper proposes a novel graph anomaly detection method called JPGFN (Feature Transformation Enhanced Jacobi Polynomial Graph Filtering Network). First, a Feature Separation Transformation Network (FSTNN) is developed to better learn fine-grained node features by feature separation and applying nonlinear transformations to node features across different dimensions. Second, an adaptive Jacobi polynomial graph filtering module is constructed based on Jacobi polynomials to adaptively capture complex frequency-domain features of graph signals. Finally, a node label constraint module is developed to facilitate the use of node labels and enhance the performance of GAD. Experimental results on multiple real-world datasets demonstrate that the proposed method significantly outperforms mainstream approaches.

</details>


### [287] [When Tool Outputs Become Commands: Separating Action Induction from Runtime Authorization in Tool-Augmented LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27146)
*Xiaokun Guo,Zhen Xu,Dongdong Huo,Yanqiu Zhang,Wei Wang,Qinfu Yang,Dongjin Yu,Yu Wang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: SARA decouples action-induction from execution authorization for tool-augmented LLM agents, using a context-isolated Action Probe and No-History-Promotion to limit action leakage; achieves ASR ≤ 0.63% across AgentDojo/AgentDyn with competitive task utility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Untrusted runtime Observations can become action-inducing commands causing real-world side effects; need to separate how actions are inferred from how they are executed; ensure provenance and authorization are distinct and auditable.

Method: Introduce SARA architecture with separate observation and execution roles; Action Probe to expose action-inducing semantics and record provenance; execution calls are authorized only against user objective and audited evidence; No-History-Promotion to prevent historical data from laundering origins; evaluate across AgentDojo and AgentDyn in four settings, measuring ASR and task utility; extend across other backbones.

Result: ASR ≤ 0.63% across four primary evaluation settings; maintains competitive task utility; consistent reductions across additional backbones.

Conclusion: SARA provides effective mitigation of action leakage in tool-augmented LLMs while preserving performance; demonstrates generality across multiple agent architectures.

Abstract: Tool-augmented LLM agents must rely on untrusted runtime Observations to complete open-ended tasks; however, when tool outputs no longer merely provide data but begin to specify concrete actions, they effectively become ``commands'' that can drive real-world side effects beyond user intent. We argue that this risk arises from conflating action induction with execution authorization. To address this distinction, we propose SARA, which treats action induction and execution authorization as distinct runtime roles and separates action provenance from execution authority. On the Observation side, a context-isolated Action Probe exposes action-inducing semantics and persistently records action-origin provenance across steps as a review signal; on the execution side, actual tool calls are authorized only against the user objective and audited evidence from authorized successful executions, while satisfying goal, execution-chain, and argument-level support. To preserve this separation across multi-step execution, SARA applies No-History-Promotion to prevent historical recurrence from laundering action origins into execution authority. Across AgentDojo and AgentDyn, SARA limits ASR to no more than \(0.63\%\) across four primary evaluation settings while maintaining competitive task utility, and consistently reduces ASR across additional Agent backbones.

</details>


### [288] [Thomson: Continual Learning of Frontier Models for SovereignAI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27147)
*Shengzhuang Chen,Jerrod Parker,Yejin Bang,Andrew M. Bean,Nabeel Seedat,Stefan Winzeck,Daniil Glazko,Jannik Zgraggen,Fangyi Yu,Scott Arnott,Dietrich Trautmann,Luca Ciuffreda,Guglielmo Bonifazi,Davide Romano,Bradley Bell,Kirsty Fielding,Daniele Giofrè,Tom Zielund,Ipshita Chatterjee,Sneha Murthy Ghantasala,Manpreet Nanreh,John Scoville,Maciej Sakowicz,Wassim Seifeddine,Lukas Thede,Jonathan Richard Schwarz*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Continual learning on open-weight frontier models can enable SovereignAI across diverse institutions with lower compute and staffing, using a mid-/post-training stack and minimal high-impact parameter edits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Address information, economic, and power asymmetries in frontier AI by enabling a wide range of actors to independently build, deploy, and govern AI use without relying on a few large players.

Method: Employ continual learning on readily available open-weight models with a modern mid-/post-training stack, implementing safeguards to preserve plasticity and stability. Avoids full fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of frozen weights; applies minimal high-impact parameter changes.

Result: Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model, matches recent frontier models on agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax, multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Exhibits a distinctive π-shaped performance pattern: broad improvements across many capabilities, including untargeted ones, and substantial reduction of forgetting in domain adaptation.

Conclusion: Frontier-level performance is attainable by a broad set of institutions through continual learning on open-weight models, making it feasible for more actors to own significant parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tools, data/privacy) with lower compute and personnel demands.

Abstract: The development of frontier models is commonly perceived to be the exclusive remit of a small number of heavily funded players, creating an information, economic and power asymmetry between developers and the diverse user base of modern AI. Recent public discourse acknowledges this concern, calling for SovereignAI (an organisation's capability to independently build, deploy and govern AI use), but offers little concrete advice on how this can be achieved in the short term under a diversity of funding settings. We argue that frontier performance is achievable by a wide range of institutions through Continual Learning on readily available open-weight models. Unlike limited approaches such as small-scale fine-tuning, prompt engineering, or tool-augmentation of a frozen model, our approach exploits a modern mid- & post-training stack while introducing safeguards that preserve both plasticity and stability at each stage, making the minimal number of high-impact interventions on the parameters. This yields gains comparable to those typically seen across multiple successive model generations, at compute and personnel budgets substantially lower than commonly thought, making ownership of large parts of the SovereignAI stack (model, tool infrastructure, values & data privacy) viable for far more actors. We demonstrate this with Thomson, a general-purpose frontier model trained with an enhanced focus on high-stakes professional work. Thomson performs competitively with recent frontier models across agentic tasks, safety, legal, tax & multilingualism, and large-scale Deep Research. Evaluations show a distinctive $π$-shaped pattern: distinct improvements across a wide range of capabilities, including those not explicitly targeted, while almost completely eliminating the forgetting problem common to narrow domain adaptation.

</details>


### [289] [BPMN4CAI: A BPMN Extension for Modeling Dynamic Conversational AI](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27149)
*Björn-Lennart Eger,Daniel Rose,Barbara Dinter*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A standard-compliant BPMN extension for Conversational AI (BPMN4CAI) is proposed, enabling dynamic, context-aware AI interactions within business processes; developed via Design Science Research and validated by a case study, showing improvements in adaptive decision-making, context management, and interaction transparency.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BPMN currently struggles to model dynamic, context-sensitive Conversational AI behaviors in business processes, hindering standard-compliant representation and analysis.

Method: Design Science Research approach to analyze requirements and extend BPMN with AI-specific elements and components (specialized nodes, context management constructs, interaction artifacts); ensure standard compatibility; implement and validate through a case study.

Result: The BPMN4CAI extension enables adaptive decision-making, robust context management, and transparent Conversational AI interactions within BPMN-modeled processes; the case study demonstrates applicability and relevance.

Conclusion: BPMN4CAI provides a practical, standard-compliant extension to model Conversational AI in BPMN, contributing design knowledge and facilitating governance and adaptability; further validation and tool support are potential directions.

Abstract: Conversational AI systems, such as chatbots and virtual assistants, are becoming increasingly important to digital business processes. However, the established Business Process Model and Notation (BPMN) standard faces challenges when representing dynamic, context-sensitive interactions. This paper addresses this methodological and practical research gap by developing a standard-compliant BPMN extension (BPMN4CAI). Using Design Science Research methodology, this paper develops an approach that systematically extends existing BPMN elements and incorporates specialized components. The applicability and relevance of the BPMN4CAI framework are demonstrated and evaluated through a case study. The results show that the BPMN4CAI extension facilitates adaptive decision-making processes, robust context management, and transparent interactions for Conversational AI within business processes.

</details>


### [290] [Calibrated Enough to Know, Not Calibrated to Act: Fabricated Evidence Makes LLM Agents Commit to the Unknowable](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27167)
*Pranav Aggarwal*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A study shows LLMs’ propensity to commit to unverified, market-panel questions is driven by the presentation/packaging rather than data; a separable, trainable action gate governs commitment, and supervised fine-tuning can suppress or transfer this behavior across domains depending on how the task allows reasoning.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: to disentangle whether LLMs commit to answers due to information, belief, or gating mechanisms, and to assess whether the action gate can be trained and made context-sensitive.

Method: Evaluates 12 frontier models on panel-based (real and fabricated) questions; measures commitment vs accuracy; analyzes knowability gating; trains a 3B model on 540 synthetic dice/coins/jars/timers cases to alter commitment; tests transfer to unseen domains; examines effect of answer-format on the gate.

Result: Commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% with stronger evidence; fabricated panels yield 24.5% to 36.8% commitment similar to genuine data; packaging authority drives action more than content; on answerable questions, models perform near-perfectly; knowability gating is weak and misaligned with action; gate is separable and trainable; rigid formats reduce room to reason and alter the gate; training can suppress commitment in original tasks but may transfer to new domains.

Conclusion: The action gate is a trainable, context-sensitive mechanism that governs whether an LLM acts; packaging authority—not information—drives commitment; deployment must address both gate design and its training to ensure reliable behavior.

Abstract: An LLM agent shown a professional-looking market panel commits to a directional call on a provably unpredictable question far more often than one asked the bare question: across 12 frontier models, commitment rises from 6.5% to 54.0% as evidence is escalated. It commits just as readily when every number on the panel is invented: fabricating the entire display, so nothing the model can see is true except the question itself, still lifts commitment from 24.5% to 36.8%, statistically indistinguishable from the 37.6% produced by genuine market data. What unlocks confident action is not information but the authority of its packaging. The failure is narrow and locatable. Incapacity is not the answer: on matched answerable questions attached to the same panels, the same models answer essentially always, at near-perfect accuracy. Nor is it belief - stated probabilities barely move across the gradient that swings action by 48 points, and score worse than a climatological baseline. Missing judgment isn't it either: asked to classify a question's knowability before acting, models call it irreducible 90% of the time and then commit on just 0.4% of those. The act/don't-act gate is what fails, and the effect is concentrated in a few models rather than universal. Because the gate is separable, it can be trained. Supervised fine-tuning of a 3B model on 540 synthetic cases, predominantly dice, coins, jars and timers, drives commitment to 0.0% on the original cases and transfers to three unseen domains. It does not survive everything: the gate holds exactly when the response format leaves room to reason, and rigid formats that remove that room leave the model confident and wrong on questions it otherwise answers correctly. The gate is trainable and context-fragile, and deployment needs both halves of that sentence.

</details>


### [291] [What Makes Good Agentic Data? An ACE Lens on Data Generation for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27260)
*Xingshan Zeng,Zishan Xu,Boju Zhang,Yuzhou Wu,Lingzhi Wang,Jianghao Lin,Liangyou Li,Yasheng Wang,Lifeng Shang,Xin Jiang,Weinan Zhang,Yong Yu,Qun Liu,Weiwen Liu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Two-level framework for agentic data: factorized data object (E, q, τ, v) and ACE (Accuracy, Complexity, Diversity); analyzes generation paradigms, verifications, and behavioral coverage, and maps literature to trends toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and broader diversity; outlines future directions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Agentic data generation must be consistent across environments and tasks and provide useful, non-redundant experience; current domain-centric organization and heterogeneous evaluation obscure common mechanisms.

Method: Define a shared data primitive (E, q, τ, v) to represent environment, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. Classify generation paradigms by their anchor and dependency structure. Propose constrained distribution design via the ACE lens, examining Accuracy, Complexity, and Diversity; survey how prior work verifies data, calibrates difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage.

Result: A synthesized view of existing work showing a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and substantive diversity beyond surface variation. Provides a framework to analyze, compare, and guide future agentic data generation.

Conclusion: The central challenge is to allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve; the ACE lens offers a scalable, principled direction for data sources, training regimes, and adaptive learning.

Abstract: LLM agents increasingly rely on generated interaction data to learn how to interact with external environments. Agentic data generation must maintain consistency among environments, tasks, interactions, and success signals while producing experience that is useful rather than merely abundant. Existing work spans many agent domains, but domain-centered organization and heterogeneous evaluation often obscure common generation mechanisms and conflate candidate construction with verification and selection. This work develops a two-level framework for the field. First, we represent agentic data as a common factorized object $(E,q,τ,v)$, comprising an environment specification, task signal, interaction realization, and optional verifier. We organize generation paradigms by their primary anchor and dependency structure. Second, we formulate generation as constrained distribution design through the Accuracy-Complexity-divErsity (ACE) lens. Accuracy establishes the feasible support of grounded and internally consistent data. Within this support, Complexity places learning mass relative to the capability of a declared learner and execution configuration, while divErsity controls coverage and redundancy of data. Using this framework, we explore how prior work verifies generated experience, constructs and calibrates difficulty, and expands behavioral coverage. The literature reveals a shift toward execution-grounded accuracy, learner-relative complexity, and diversity beyond surface variation or dataset size. We further discuss broader directions and emerging trends in agentic data generation through the ACE lens, including their implications for scaling, data sources, training regimes and adaptive learning. Overall, the central challenge is not simply to generate more data, but to continually allocate valid, informative, and non-redundant experience as agents and environments evolve.

</details>


### [292] [Naive Prompt Optimization: Rethinking the Need for Complex Prompt Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27266)
*Yuan Chang,Xiaoqi Chen*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO) is a simple, single-lineage prompt revision method using teacher rollout feedback, achieving competitive performance with fewer rollouts than GEPA, and transferring across models, suggesting linear prompt optimization can rival complex search.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Reduce computational cost and complexity of prompt optimization while maintaining performance; address overengineering in prompt optimizers; evaluate transferability and applicability to interactive tasks.

Method: Iteratively revise prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback; single-lineage, linear optimization; compare to GEPA/GRPO; test in interactive games; test cross-model transfer.

Result: NPO matches or surpasses GEPA with fewer rollouts; stronger teacher models improve NPO; GRPO outperforms on certain tasks; NPO prompts transfer effectively to other student models within same family.

Conclusion: Simple linear prompt optimization can rival more sophisticated search; teacher strength partly substitutes optimizer complexity; NPO offers efficient, broadly competitive performance across tasks.

Abstract: Efficiently improving autonomous agents across diverse tasks is central to accelerating recursive self-improvement (RSI) in agentic AI, with prompt optimization emerging as a promising approach capable of delivering performance gains comparable to those achieved by fine-tuning model weights, while reducing computational costs in both optimization and serving. However, recent developments increasingly favor unnecessarily complex prompt optimizers. We introduce Naive Prompt Optimization (NPO), a lightweight single-lineage method that iteratively revises prompts using a teacher model with rollout feedback. NPO achieves comparable or better performance than GEPA with fewer rollouts, and its advantage increases with stronger teacher models, suggesting that stronger teacher reasoning can partially substitute for optimizer-side search complexity. In interactive games, NPO remains broadly competitive with GEPA, while GRPO performs better on some tasks less amenable to prompt optimization. We also show that NPO-optimized prompts elicit similar performance improvements when applied verbatim to other student models, especially across models within the same family. Overall, our preliminary results show that simple, linear prompt optimization can rival substantially more sophisticated and complex search procedures.

</details>


### [293] [BrailleBench: Investigating Multi-Criteria Braille Comprehension in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27268)
*Jinghan Zhang,Fengran Mo,Zhiyu Chen,Xiaoyan Han,Kunpeng Liu,Chang-Tien Lu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: BrailleBench benchmarks LLMs on Braille comprehension and production across English and Braille Grades 1/2; 5,570 aligned instances from five datasets; tests Braille understanding, Braille output, and end-to-end interaction; finds a large gap vs print-English; Grade 2 input fragility; fully Braille requests hurt performance; resources released.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Enable inclusive AI by evaluating whether LLMs can access and generate Braille content, addressing Braille-specific features such as indicators, contractions, and digital representations that affect model comprehension.

Method: Construct a deterministic, expert-curated benchmark (BrailleBench) aligning 5,570 instances from five datasets (math, commonsense, multi-hop QA) across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2; evaluate configurations for understanding Braille-authored content, producing Braille, and end-to-end Braille interaction; use a Braille Toolkit and an expert-reviewed pipeline to avoid data from LLMs; evaluate six representative LLMs.

Result: There is a persistent gap between print-English performance and Braille accessibility; Braille understanding and output are asymmetric; Grade 2 input is more fragile than Grade 1; fully Braille requests further degrade performance; results guide future Braille AI system development.

Conclusion: BrailleBench provides publicly available resources and a rigorous benchmark to drive the development and evaluation of Braille-aware AI systems for accessible information access.

Abstract: Although Large language models (LLMs) mediate access to knowledge and computational assistance, their capabilities should benefit vulnerable groups in the same way. However, it is unclear whether existing AI systems are inclusive enough for blind and deafblind users to access the same functionality through Braille, whose indicators, contractions, and digital representations introduce distinct requirements for model comprehension. To this end, we introduce BrailleBench, a benchmark for evaluating LLMs in Braille comprehension from different Criteria. BrailleBench aligns 5,570 instances from five datasets, including mathematics, commonsense, and multi-hop question answering across English and Braille Grades 1 and 2. Different configurations are designed to understand whether the systems can comprehend Braille-authored content, express answers in Braille, and complete end-to-end Braille interaction. To ensure the quality and prevent evaluation bias, the benchmark is built through a deterministic, expert-reviewed pipeline via a self-created Braille Toolkit without using any data instances generated by LLMs. We evaluate six representative LLMs from various aspects. The results reveal a persistent gap between print-English capability and Braille accessibility. Braille understanding and expression are asymmetric, where Grade 2 is especially fragile on the input side compared to Grade 1, and fully Braille requests further reduce performance. The experimental observations provide valuable guidance for the development of future Braille AI systems. All related resources in BrailleBench are publicly available for future research.

</details>


### [294] [LLMs Can Design Near-Optimal OR Algorithms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27296)
*Jackie Baek*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: LLMs can design competitive algorithms for well-specified OR problems, using two usage levels. The strongest model tested (gpt-5.6-sol) often matches or beats specialized methods, even when returning a fixed algorithm at level 2. Algorithm design with frontier LLMs is rapidly improving and can serve as a strong empirical baseline.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Assess whether frontier LLMs can serve as algorithm-design tools for well-specified operations research problems, reducing need for bespoke algorithms and enabling rapid prototyping of solution methods.

Method: Evaluate on inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. Two use levels: (1) level-1: solve individual instances from a single untuned prompt; (2) level-2: receive problem class description and parameter ranges and return an algorithm mapping instance parameters to solutions. A Python sandbox with fixed compute budget is available.

Result: gpt-5.6-sol matches or outperforms the best existing methods on nearly all evaluated instances. The advantage persists at level-2, where the produced algorithm is fixed prior to evaluation. Model performance improves quickly across releases (<8 months apart). A single untuned LLM prompt can yield algorithms competitive with specialized methods, suggesting frontier LLMs as strong empirical baselines for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.

Conclusion: Frontier LLMs are capable of generating competitive, generalizable algorithms for well-specified OR problems and can serve as a rapid, empirical baseline for algorithm design, with performance gains tracking advances in model quality.

Abstract: We ask whether large language models (LLMs) can design effective algorithms for well-specified operations research (OR) problems. We study inventory control, queueing network control, and assortment optimization. We evaluate two levels of LLM use: at level 1, the model receives one problem instance and returns a solution for that instance; at level 2, it receives only the problem class description and broad parameter ranges, and returns an algorithm that maps instance parameters to solutions. Human input is minimal: we give one untuned prompt that describes the problem, and the model has access to a Python sandbox tool with a fixed compute budget.
  The strongest model we test, gpt-5.6-sol, matches or outperforms the best existing method on almost all evaluated instances. This holds even at level 2, where the returned algorithm is fixed before seeing the evaluation instances. Performance also improves sharply across models released less than eight months apart, suggesting that this capability is moving quickly. Thus, for the well-specified operations problems we study, a single untuned LLM query can already produce algorithms competitive with specialized methods. These results suggest that frontier LLMs can be a serious empirical baseline for algorithm design in well-specified OR problems.

</details>


### [295] [Verify Smarter, Evolve Further: Efficient Harness Evolution through Behavior-Aware Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27311)
*Jinghan Xu,Yikai Zhang,Aili Chen,Weiyuan Li,Jiaqing Liang,Deqing Yang*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: HarnessLens is a budget-aware framework for automated evolution of agent harnesses for language-model agents. It jointly searches task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and uses an attributable-evidence gate to selectively verify candidates on behavior-relevant tasks. It yields 7.6–13.6% gains on held-out performance with substantially lower evaluation budgets across three harnesses and four benchmarks, demonstrating reliable, sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets; code at the project URL.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current propose-and-verify methods apply verification to many candidates on fixed task sets, wasteful and may miss regressions because aggregate scores obscure specific behavioral issues. There is a need for behavior-aware, budget-efficient harness evolution.

Method: A budget-aware framework that (1) jointly explores the space of tasks and user-configurable harness components; (2) derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories; (3) selectively verifies candidates on behavior-relevant tasks via an attributable-evidence gate, enabling attribution of observed behaviors to specific components.

Result: Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6–13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than baselines.

Conclusion: Behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets. Code is available at the provided repository.

Abstract: Agent harnesses shape how language-model agents use instructions, tools, and runtime components, but adapting these harnesses requires costly verification. Existing propose-and-verify methods typically score every candidate on a fixed task set, wasting rollouts on unrelated behaviors and allowing aggregate scores to obscure specific regressions. We introduce HarnessLens, a budget-aware framework for automated harness evolution. HarnessLens jointly explores the task space and user-configurable components, derives candidate modifications from execution trajectories, and selectively verifies each candidate on behavior-relevant tasks using an attributable-evidence gate. Across three agent harnesses and four benchmarks, HarnessLens improves average held-out performance by 7.6-13.6% while consuming substantially less evaluation budget than competing baselines. These results demonstrate that behavior-aware verification with explicit attribution enables more reliable and sample-efficient harness evolution under constrained interaction budgets. Our code is available at https://github.com/jhxu5214/HarnessLens.

</details>


### [296] [Not All Eval-Awareness Is Equal: Capabilities Framing Predicts Compliance](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27340)
*Allison Zhuang,Santiago Aranguri*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Eval-awareness in model safety evaluations is multi-dimensional: different verbal framings (capabilities vs safety) predict very different compliance, with causal links suggested by CoT-prefill; uniform suppression metrics are misleading as the same percentage can yield different behaviors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To unpack eval-awareness as a single safety metric and reveal its subcomponents and causal dynamics to improve evaluation pipelines and reduce misinterpretation.

Method: Classify verbalized eval-awareness in Chain-of-Thought as capabilities-framing, safety-framing, both, or neither; compare compliance under Qwen3-32B on the FORTRESS dataset across steering conditions; apply CoT-prefill interventions on eval-awareness-negative rollouts to test causality; examine aggregation effects on suppression rates.

Result: Capabilities-framing yields substantially higher compliance (+24 to +46 percentage points) than safety-framing across conditions; 10 of 11 CoT-prefill interventions shifted compliance in the predicted direction, supporting a causal link; eval-awareness suppression is not behaviorally uniform, and equal suppression percentages can map to qualitatively different outcomes.

Conclusion: Eval-awareness is multi-dimensional; evaluation pipelines should disentangle framings and components, recognize potential causal effects of prompting, and avoid relying on aggregate suppression rates that mask divergent safety-relevant dynamics.

Abstract: Steering interventions targeting eval-awareness, a model's recognition that it is being tested, are increasingly used in safety evaluation pipelines, where evaluation-awareness is treated as a single quantity to be suppressed. We show that verbalized eval-awareness in chain-of-thought can be identified as capabilities-flavored ("the user is testing my ability to follow instructions"), safety-flavored ("the user is testing my boundaries"), both, or neither: framings that predict compliance very differently. On Qwen3-32B over the FORTRESS dataset, capabilities-framing predicts compliance with a +24 to +46 percentage-point gap over safety-framing across all tested steering conditions. A CoT-prefill intervention on eval-awareness-negative rollouts suggests the link is causal, with 10 of 11 prefills shifting compliance in the predicted direction. Then, eval-awareness is not behaviorally uniform: aggregate suppression rates can move while the safety-relevant component does not, and the same "X% suppression of eval-awareness" can correspond to qualitatively different behavioral outcomes.

</details>


### [297] [Sophistication in GenAI Use: Field Evidence from a Large Firm](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27364)
*Nicholas J. Hallman,Zachary T. Kowaleski,Anu Puvvada,Jaime J. Schmidt*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Large firm study of 713k prompts from ~4k back-office employees across 15 functions over 8 months in 2025 finds seniority linked to more sophisticated genAI use; function-level variation with top in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management; no time-based improvement or lasting gains from formal AI training; implications for management and research.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Quantify how back-office staff adopt and use generative AI, identify determinants of sophisticated use, and inform managerial and research directions.

Method: Observational analysis of a proprietary dataset consisting of 713,564 employee prompts and corresponding LLM responses from ~4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025; measures of sophistication across individuals and functions; no causal claims.

Result: Senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use; substantial cross-functional variation with highest sophistication in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management; no observed improvements over time and no lasting gains from formal AI training.

Conclusion: Provides metrics and insights on sophisticated genAI use for managers and researchers; sophisticated use appears difficult to change, suggesting need for targeted interventions and continued study.

Abstract: We study how sophistication in generative AI (genAI) use varies among the back-office workforce of a large firm. Using proprietary data, we observe 713,564 employee prompts and their corresponding large language model responses from nearly 4,000 back-office employees across 15 functional areas over eight months in 2025. We document three main findings. First, senior employees exhibit more sophisticated genAI use, consistent with domain expertise complementing genAI capabilities. Second, sophistication varies considerably across functions and is highest in Strategy, Digital Innovation, and Project Management, three groups that share a focus on firmwide strategic initiatives and organizational change. Third, we observe neither improvements in sophistication over time nor lasting improvements following formal AI training, suggesting that sophisticated use can be difficult to change. Together, our study provides measures of and insights into sophisticated genAI use that managers can use to improve outcomes and that researchers can use in future research.

</details>


### [298] [CorporateBench: Large-Scale Q&A Benchmarking with Temporal Knowledge Bases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27391)
*Sil Hamilton,Albert Yu Sun,Oscar J. Romero,Carl-Leander Henneking,David Mimno,Bishan Yang,Igor Labutov*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: CorporateBench (CB) is a human-validated, large-scale multi-task Q&A benchmark for enterprise-scale document collections, combining 230k+ documents across four synthetic firms to evaluate information extraction and knowledge-base querying, revealing performance degradation of LLMs at near-realistic scales.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Evaluation of LLMs on corporate data is hindered by privacy concerns and the over-simplification of synthetic datasets. There is a need for a scalable, realistic benchmark that mirrors corporate communications and knowledge evolution.
