Method: CB builds four synthetically generated firms (12–10,000 employees) with temporally evolving, consistent knowledge bases. The corpus (>230k documents) allows cross-document reasoning and ensures logical consistency. The task suite covers two dimensions—information extraction and knowledge-base querying—across two tasks, evaluated on five LLMs.

Result: Five LLMs were evaluated; performance deteriorates as input size grows toward realistic scales, indicating current models struggle with corporate-scale reasoning. CB offers a new, scalable metric for corporate communication reasoning benchmarking.

Conclusion: CB fills a crucial benchmarking gap for enterprise-grade reasoning over large, evolving document collections and will guide LLM developers in improving corporate communication capabilities.

Abstract: LLMs are increasingly able to answer complex questions about enterprise-scale document collections. But evaluation is hard: companies don't want to share internal communications, and synthetic datasets have been overly simple. We present CorporateBench (CB), a human-validated multi-task Q&A benchmark whose scale approaches the conditions LLMs encounter in corporate communication networks, with evaluation corpora surpassing 230,000 documents. CB evaluates LLMs across two dimensions (information extraction and knowledge base querying) through four synthetically generated firms ranging from 12 to 10,000 employees. Each corpus is sampled from a temporally evolving knowledge base describing a consistent world, guaranteeing cross-document logical consistency even across hundreds of thousands of documents. We evaluate five LLMs on CB, revealing increasingly poor performance as input size approaches realistic scales. CB provides LLM developers a metric for corporate communication reasoning, filling a crucial gap in the benchmarking ecosystem.

</details>


### [299] [Learning a Continuous Sepsis Severity Score Without Hour-by-Hour Supervision: A Two-Site Retrospective Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27421)
*Kevin Zhu,Ryan Zhang,Baraa Abed,Tilendra Choudhary,Malvern Madondo,Mehak Arora,Yixuan Yang,Alasdair Gent,Aditya Nagori,Omer T. Inan,Krista L. Haines,Patrick Georgoff,Suresh M. Agarwal,Vijay Krishnamoorthy,Tetsu Ohnuma,Mihai V. Podgoreanu,Michael R. Pinsky,Gilles Clermont,Craig M. Coopersmith,Craig S. Jabaley,Rishikesan Kamaleswaran*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: A data-driven sepsis index from 43 variables over 72 hours uses mortality as a treatment-level signal; shows meaningful, hour-by-hour prognostic information and limited cross-site generalizability, with some independence from traditional indices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Current sepsis severity indices are based on legacy, discretized variables with fixed weights and may not reflect modern critical care. A learned index from patient trajectories could improve prognosis and decision support.

Method: Retrospective two-cohort study of 29,116 and 7,691 adult Sepsis-3 patients from Massachusetts and Georgia. Developed a 0–10 sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Mortality used as a treatment-level ranking signal to credit timesteps non-uniformly. Evaluation on a permanent 20% test holdout via clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation. Uncertainty via bootstrap of whole patients.

Result: Non-survivors scored 1.19–1.64 points higher than survivors on the 0–10 scale across baseline SOFA-2 strata; similar trends within lactate, MAP, and creatinine strata. Change in index per patient correlated with change in lactate (rho=0.39; n=1854); weaker correlations for MAP/creatinine. Cross-site model correlation reached 70–77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 (ceilings 0.92 and 0.90). Index correlated with established indices; null controls near zero. Demonstrated hourly prognostic information supporting potential clinical decision-supply use.

Conclusion: An hourly, trajectory-based sepsis index informed by mortality signal shows prognostic value and alignment with clinical expectations, offering potential as a decision-support tool alongside clinical judgment.

Abstract: Currently used sepsis severity indices rely on fixed variables and weights established decades ago, which are coarsely discretized and calibrated to a cohort that no longer reflects contemporary critical care. No alternative learned directly from patient trajectories is in routine use. We conducted a retrospective two-cohort study on a total of 29,116 and 7,691 adult patients meeting Sepsis-3 criteria from two hospital systems in Massachusetts and Georgie, respectively.
  We developed a sepsis index using 43 routinely charted variables over a 72-hour treatment window. Unlike previous studies, we use mortality as a treatment-level ranking signal rather than a per-state target, allowing credit to be redistributed non-uniformly across timesteps. Evaluation was done on a permanent 20% test holdout, using clinical vignettes and Spearman correlation. Uncertainty intervals were obtained by bootstrap resampling of whole patients. Under this ranking scheme, non-survivors scored 1.19-1.64 points higher than survivors on a 0-10 scale within all strata of baseline SOFA-2, with similar results stratifying within lactate, mean arterial pressure (MAP), and creatinine. Within-patient change in the index correlated with change in lactate (Spearman rho = 0.39; n = 1,854). Similar, weaker correlations were found for MAP and creatinine. On a cohort level, cross-institutional agreement measured by Spearman correlation between models trained on different sites, were 70-77% of same-site correlation. External within-patient correlations were 0.54 and 0.59 against ceilings of 0.92 and 0.90. Our index also correlated with established indices, while null controls stayed near zero.
  Our index demonstrated hourly prognostic information that meaningfully separates patient outcomes and is consistent with clinical expectation, indicating potential as a decision support tool complementing clinical judgement.

</details>


### [300] [Mechanistic Reaction Prediction via Discrete Flow Matching on Graph-Structured Electron Occupation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27429)
*Nguyen Xuan-Vu,Octavian Susanu,Daniel Armstrong,Philippe Schwaller*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: Models reactions as discrete electron-space flows using a CTMC over electron occupation vectors; uses discrete flow matching with Optimal Transport to generate mechanistic edit trajectories without elementary-step annotations; achieves competitive results on USPTO-480K and shows robustness to structural and reaction-type shifts; provides interpretable reaction pathways and side-product predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Overcome limitations of de novo product generation and purely topological edits by incorporating electron-space, mechanistically interpretable flow dynamics that align with chemistry and improve robustness to distribution shifts.

Method: Define a graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space over all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites. Formulate reactant-to-product mapping as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) on this space. Construct intermediate edit trajectories via generalized discrete flow matching of electron rearrangements using Optimal Transport, yielding a sequence of edit moves without requiring elementary-step annotations.

Result: MAELLE attains competitive performance on the USPTO-480K benchmark relative to leading reaction-prediction models. It demonstrates robustness in out-of-distribution tests focused on structural complexity and reaction type, maintaining performance where other methods degrade. It also recovers mechanistic trajectories that align with established chemistry and can predict additional side products.

Conclusion: MAELLE offers a mechanistically interpretable, robust alternative to topology-centric approaches by operating in electron space. It provides actionable pathways and side-product predictions, with competitive accuracy on standard benchmarks and improved resilience to distribution shifts.

Abstract: Chemical reactions are fundamentally transformations in electron space, yet most machine learning approaches model them either through \textit{de novo} generation of product molecules or through heuristic graph edits that operate directly on molecular topology.
  We introduce MAELLE (\textbf{M}ech\textbf{A}nistic \textbf{E}dit f\textbf{L}ow-matching on e\textbf{L}ectron r\textbf{E}arrangements), which instead models reactions as discrete flow matching over electron occupation vectors.
  Concretely, we formulate the reactant-to-product mapping as a Continuous-time Markov Chain (CTMC) over the graph-structured integer-valued electron occupation space defined on all bonding, non-bonding, and hydrogen sites.
  To construct the intermediate edit trajectories, we generalize the discrete flow matching mixture path to discrete electron rearrangements using Optimal Transport, yielding a sequence of mechanistically interpretable edit moves without requiring elementary step annotations.
  MAELLE achieves competitive performance on the USPTO-480K benchmark compared with leading reaction prediction models.
  Beyond in-distribution accuracy, we evaluate robustness across two out-of-distribution settings - structural complexity and reaction type - and find that MAELLE maintains strong performance where existing methods degrade.
  Finally, because the learned flow operates over the full electron redistribution, MAELLE naturally recovers mechanistic trajectories that align with known chemistry and can predict side products of a reaction.

</details>


### [301] [WikiSkill: Compiling Agent Experience into Persistent Knowledge for Skill Evolution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27454)
*Liyan Tang,Cyrus Rashtchian,Chun-Sung Ferng,Andrew Tomkins,Da-Cheng Juan,Tu Vu*

Main category: cs.AI

TL;DR: WikiSkill introduces a persistent knowledge base (wiki) to co-evolve agent skills with execution experience. By separating raw experience, knowledge, and executable skills and continuously consolidating experience into the wiki, skill updates can build on accumulated knowledge. This leads to consistent improvements over baselines, better transfer across models, and a strong role for knowledge accumulation in enabling reusable skills.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To address fragmentation of optimization histories and limited reuse of insights in skill evolution. The goal is to systematically accumulate and reuse agent experience to develop reusable, transferable skills across iterations and model families.

Method: Propose WikiSkill framework that separates raw execution experience, persistent wiki knowledge, and executable skills. Experience is consolidated into the wiki, informing subsequent skill updates. Evaluate across diverse benchmarks and models, with ablations to isolate the wiki's role and cross-model transfer.

Result: WikiSkill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution baselines and no-skill baselines in most settings. Larger models gain more from evolved skills; smaller models with skills can outperform substantially larger models without them. Skills transfer effectively across models and model families, and skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills. Ablations show persistent wiki accumulation is critical for effective skill evolution.

Conclusion: Systematic accumulation and refinement of agent experience into a persistent knowledge base enables reusable and transferable skills, complementing model scaling and enhancing cross-model transfer.

Abstract: Agent skills package specialized knowledge and workflows into reusable resources that extend AI agent capabilities. Recent work automatically discovers such skills from agent experience, which enables agents to progressively adapt through interaction. However, the insights that guide skill development typically remain scattered across optimization histories, limiting their systematic reuse across iterations. We introduce WikiSkill, a framework that co-evolves agent skills with a persistent knowledge base (wiki). At a high level, WikiSkill separates raw execution experience, accumulated knowledge, and executable skills, while continuously consolidating experience into the wiki, which subsequent skill updates can build on. Across diverse benchmarks and models, WikiSkill consistently outperforms state-of-the-art skill-evolution methods and improves over no-skill baselines in most model-benchmark settings. We find that skill evolution complements model scaling: larger models generally benefit more from evolved skills, while smaller models with skills can outperform substantially larger models without them. We also find that evolved skills transfer effectively across models and model families, and skills evolved by other models can outperform self-evolved skills. Finally, our ablation studies confirm that persistent knowledge accumulation in the wiki is critical for effective skill evolution. These results demonstrate the benefits of systematically accumulating and refining agent experience for developing reusable and transferable skills.

</details>


<div id='cs.CG'></div>

# cs.CG [[Back]](#toc)

### [302] [Quadratic Complexity of Voronoi Diagrams in $\mathbb{R}^3$ for Lines in a Single Ruling of a Regulus](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27114)
*Eunku Park*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Quadratic-time complexity for Voronoi diagrams of lines within one ruling of a smooth doubly ruled quadric in R^3; nearest diagram has Θ(n^2) worst-case complexity within this class; farthest diagram also Θ(n^2); exact O(n^2) enumeration algorithm via univariate representations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how restricting line sites to a single ruling (a structured geometric family) affects the combinatorial complexity of Voronoi diagrams and to develop efficient enumeration algorithms.

Method: Combine combinatorial geometry analysis of regular Voronoi vertices with algebraic-geometry tools: Plücker embedding to a conic, tangency conditions leading to binary quartics, Bézout bounds, and cyclic parameter sorting. Construct lower bounds on complexity via explicit configurations on a fixed hyperboloid. Derive an O(n^2) algorithm that enumerates finite vertices using real univariate representations.

Result: Nearest Voronoi diagram within the ruling has at most 4n(n-3) vertices and overall O(n^2) complexity under general position; farthest diagram has Θ(n^2) complexity (n(n-1) 3D cells) for general-position inputs. For every n≥4, there exists a ruling yielding Ω(n^2) regular nearest vertices, establishing Θ(n^2) worst-case for the nearest diagram in this class. There is an exact O(n^2) time enumeration algorithm for all finite nearest and farthest vertices via cyclicly sorted site parameters and constant-degree real univariate representations.

Conclusion: Confining lines to a single ruling of a smooth doubly ruled quadric collapses the potential complexity to quadratic, with tight bounds for both nearest and farthest diagrams. The Plücker-embedding viewpoint explains the algebraic structure (conic, binary quartics) and enables an efficient exact enumeration algorithm.

Abstract: We study nearest and farthest Voronoi diagrams of lines in $\mathbb{R}^3$ under the Euclidean metric when all $n$ lines belong to one ruling of a smooth doubly ruled real quadric. For arbitrary line sites, the combinatorial complexity of the nearest Voronoi diagram is known only to lie between $Ω(n^2)$ and $O(n^{3+\varepsilon})$. Under general-position assumptions, we prove that both diagrams in the ruling class have at most $4n(n-3)$ vertices and $O(n^2)$ total combinatorial complexity. Conversely, for every $n \ge 4$, one ruling of a fixed non-rotational one-sheeted hyperboloid contains a general-position set of $n$ lines with at least $(n-2)(n-3)/2$ distinct regular nearest vertices, where regular means that exactly four lines support the vertex and their three defining bisectors meet transversely. Thus the worst-case complexity of the nearest Voronoi diagram in this class is $Θ(n^2)$, while the farthest diagram has $Θ(n^2)$ complexity for every general-position input, since it has exactly $n(n-1)$ three-dimensional cells. Under the Plücker embedding, the ruling is a conic, and the condition for a line to be tangent to a Euclidean sphere restricts to a binary quartic. At a regular vertex, the four supporting parameters exhaust its roots, and sign alternation forces two arcs of the parameter circle to be site-free. This leaves only $n(n-3)/2$ possible cyclic support types, while Bézout's theorem bounds the number of centers for each type by eight. The same reduction yields an exact $O(n^2)$-time algorithm that, after cyclically sorting the site parameters, enumerates all finite nearest and farthest vertices as constant-degree real univariate representations.

</details>


### [303] [Lunar Generalizations of the Euclidean Minimum Spanning Tree in the Plane and their Expected Costs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.27118)
*Ondřej Draganov,Herbert Edelsbrunner,Sophie Rosenmeier,Morteza Saghafian*

Main category: cs.CG

TL;DR: Existence of a limiting constant for the expected cost of a lunar EMST on colored planar points, with cost scaling as C_s * sqrt(n); the constant exists but is not explicitly known.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extend chromatic persistent homology ideas to geometric graphs by generalizing EMST to multi-colored point sets; understand how random geometry with color constraints scales and behaves.

Method: Introduce lunar EMST defined via intersections of disks (lunes) of radius r for points of all pairwise distinct colors; analyze the evolution of the union of lunes as r increases from 0 to ∞ and how arcs/nodes of the spanning tree form, defining the cost as twice the difference of radii; study the expected cost under uniform random points in [0,1]^2 with random colors and prove the existence of a finite constant C_s with E[cost] ~ C_s sqrt(n).

Result: Proves that the limiting constant C_s exists for the expected lunar EMST cost and that E[cost] scales like C_s sqrt(n) as n -> ∞; the value of C_s remains undetermined.

Conclusion: The lunar EMST exhibits a scaling law analogous to classical EMST, with a color-parameter-dependent constant whose exact value is open; invites further work to compute or bound C_s and explore connections to chromatic persistent homology.

Abstract: Motivated by the recent introduction of chromatic persistent homology, we generalize the Euclidean minimum spanning tree (EMST) for $n$ points in $\mathbb{R}^2$ to the lunar EMST for the case in which the points come in $s+1$ colors. Calling the intersection of $s+1$ disks of radius $r$ centered at points with pairwise different colors a \emph{lune}, the generalized EMST reflects the history of the union of lunes as $r$ goes from $0$ to $\infty$, and its \emph{cost} is twice the difference between the radii when the arcs and nodes of the tree are formed. If the points are chosen uniformly at random in $[0,1]^2$ and colored randomly, the expected cost converges to some constant (that depends on $s$) times $\sqrt{n}$, as $n$ goes to infinity. The main contribution of this paper is a proof that this constant exists, however similar to the case of the classic EMST, its precise value remains elusive.

</details>
